### Stage Four — Essence and its related features

#### Chapter 1 — What is essence?

Each thing around us has an essence—what it is[^V02-1]—and an existence—the fact that it is. Essence is what answers the question “What is it?”, just as quantity answers “How much is it?” Under this definition, essence is a universal concept;[^V02-2] it cannot name something known only through direct observation. It is also defined as “that by which a thing is what it is,” a wording that covers every case. This is a difference in wording, not a circular definition.[^V02-3]

Consider essence simply as essence: that is, as what a thing is in itself. It is neither one nor many, universal nor particular. Human nature, for example, exists as a particular in the world and is understood as universal in the mind. This shows that being universal or particular is not part of what humanity is in itself.[^V02-4] Although humanity is never found without unity or multiplicity, universality or particularity, it does not follow that it must be one or many, universal or particular simply because it is humanity.[^V02-5] The same is true of other pairs of opposites that are neither the thing itself nor part of its nature. Denying that a feature belongs to something in one respect does not rule out its belonging to it in another. And the opposite of a thing requiring one feature is simply its not requiring that feature; it does not follow that it requires the opposite.

Likewise, if a possible thing has no existence at the level where we consider its essence, it does not follow that it has nonexistence there just because nonexistence is the opposite of existence.[^V02-6] A thing can lack both opposites at one level of reality. It is only impossible for it to lack both in reality as a whole, because reality is wider than any single level.[^V02-7] Consider things with no essential connection to one another: the existence or nonexistence of one does not occur at the level of the other’s existence or nonexistence.[^V02-8] More precisely, when we deny a thing’s existence at a certain level, we mean that the thing is absent from that level; we do not mean that the denial itself exists there.[^V02-9]

For this reason, they say that if we are asked to choose between contradictories, the right answer is to put the denial before the respect in which we are speaking.[^V02-10] So if we ask whether a human being, considered simply as a human being, is existent or nonexistent,[^V02-11] the answer is: considered in itself, a human being is neither existent nor nonexistent, nor any other feature that comes to it from outside its nature.[^V02-12] This means that none of these features is identical with humanity or part of it, even though in reality a thing is never without some of them or their opposites.

Putting the denial first does not mean that the feature is not required by the essence, as some have mistakenly thought.[^V02-13] Nor is the purpose to avoid an affirmative statement that uses a negative term, such as “not-A.” The difference between that form and a plain negative lies only in where the linking word “is” appears.[^V02-14] If we are asked to choose between two descriptions that are treated as opposites—“a human being is one or many,” or “A or not-A”—we do not have to choose either.[^V02-15] If we answer, we can say that, considered in itself, a human being is neither one nor many, neither A nor not-A.[^V02-16] This differs from asking through a true pair of contradictories: there, we answer by applying the denial to each description under the stated qualification, as above. But being described by a feature does not mean that a thing is identical with that feature.[^V02-17]

Universal humanity is not one numerically single humanity shared by many people,[^V02-18] as we have explained. Something that is one in number cannot exist in many places at once. If all people had one and the same humanity, it would have to be knowledgeable and ignorant, white and black, moving and still, and many other pairs of opposites. The relation between a universal nature and its individual cases is not like one father with many children, but like many fathers with many children. A concept that is universal in the mind can be found in each person, but no one is human merely by being related to one separate humanity set apart from everyone. Each person has a humanity of their own, numerically distinct from everyone else’s. The shared concept exists only in the mind.

#### Chapter 2 — Universals and particulars

In philosophy, a universal is something that can be shared by many individuals, or at least does not rule out being shared. A universal cannot exist as such among things in the world. If it did, it would exist as one particular thing, not as an idea that could apply to many. It could no longer be shared. Someone might object that a mental image also has its own individual existence: it belongs to one mind, has no size or position, and cannot be pointed to. Every mental image would then be particular, because an image in one person’s mind cannot be the very same image in many minds.[^V02-19]

But if a mental image is universal because it matches many things, then particulars also match one another, and would have to count as universal too.

The answer is that a mental image is universal because it can match many things, not because its individual existence in one mind is shared. It exists as an idea, not as a separate thing with existence of its own.[^V02-20] Its existence is like a shadow’s:[^V02-21] it depends on what it is connected to, whether that thing is in the mind or outside it, and whether it comes before or after that thing. Some universals come before the many particular things they refer to, as when a cause first conceives its effects. These are called universals before multiplicity. Others are drawn from the world after particular things exist, as our general knowledge is drawn from individual things outside the mind. These are called universals after multiplicity.

Here is what it means for a mental image to match many things. When you see one human being, the mind forms an image of humanity stripped of individual features. When you see another person, the mind does not need to form a second image unless the first has left your mind. It is like wax taking the impression of a seal: similar seals can be pressed against it, but they do not leave a different image each time.

When books say that a universal exists in the world or can be pointed to, they mean the nature that becomes universal when it exists in the mind. Things that share a universal idea can differ in four ways. If they share only an accidental feature, they differ in what they are, as blackness and a surface do. If they share a general kind, they can differ by the feature that marks out one kind within it. If they share a specific kind, they can differ through a feature that is not required of the whole kind: anything required of the kind belongs to all its individuals, though a feature may still be required of one individual. Finally, they can differ in how fully or incompletely the shared nature is present, as we have explained before, despite the weakness of the later philosophers’ rule that a complete and an incomplete instance must differ in what they are.

The truth is that a thing’s being an individual—its being impossible to share in thought—depends on something beyond its essence that prevents us from thinking of it as shared. But the feature that truly makes a thing an individual is its own existence, as the Second Teacher, al-Fārābī, held. Every existence is an individual in itself. If we set aside a thing’s particular way of existing, the mind can consider it as shareable, even if we add a thousand other features to it. Distinction in the world is not the same as being an individual in itself: distinction compares a thing with others that share something in common, while individuality concerns the thing on its own. A thing would still be an individual even if there were nothing else like it. Distinction may prepare a thing to become an individual: a material kind spread across many possible individuals does not receive existence from the highest source until its matter is ready for one of them.[^V02-22]

Some philosophers say that we know an individual through the senses or through direct awareness. This can be understood in line with what we have said: each particular existence can be known only by directly encountering it.[^V02-23] The Illuminationist Shaykh says in *al-Muṭāraḥāt* that what prevents sharing is a thing’s concrete identity. But sharing is really a matter of matching, and not every kind of match counts. It must be a match with something that has no independent individual reality. A concrete identity, in the end, is the thing’s own particular existence. Yet this same Shaykh strongly insists that existence is only a mental idea and has no reality in the world. That leaves a problem: if something other than existence makes a thing an individual, it must be either the shared essence itself or that essence together with matter and other features. None of these prevents sharing; even all general ideas taken together would still be general. What, then, is this concrete identity that prevents a thing from being shared?[^V02-24]

Another view is that an individual is made particular by one of its parts, understood by analysis. This can be explained in terms of existence, since existence and essence are not separate things in the world. The claim that a maker makes something an individual is also right: a maker gives it existence, and existence is what makes it an individual. Every existence depends on its maker, so every individual depends on the maker of its existence. But we are discussing what directly makes an already existing thing an individual.

Some scholars say that an individual is particular because it is connected to True Existence, the source of all things. Essences are connected to the true Maker through their existence, not through the ideas we form of them. By existing, each thing is connected to its cause, and through that cause to the cause of everything. In truth, all existences are shadows and rays of God.[^V02-25]

Others say that an individual prevents sharing simply by being what it is—not because of its parts, something necessary to it, or some feature that can come and go. The parts of a thing do not prevent sharing by themselves; something necessary to it is shared with others of the same kind; and a separable feature does not prevent sharing either. They therefore conclude that matter must be what makes an individual particular.

This argument is really about what distinguishes one thing from another, which is a condition for treating it as an individual. Matter’s role in making a thing particular is like that of other features. A kind with many individuals does not come into existence as one individual until its matter is set in a particular position and time. But matter alone does not distinguish one individual from another: the same forms and shapes can occur in the same matter at different times, and then time makes the difference.[^V02-26]

The same is true of Bahmanyār’s view that individuality comes from the conditions of matter, such as position and place, when time is the same. What he means is the feature that separates two things, not what makes a nature particular in the first place. When he saw that a thing’s position could change while the individual remained the same, he said that the individualizing feature was one of the positions the thing had during its life. Unless he meant that position is a sign of an individual and something that comes with its existence, this claim would make no sense. A material individual such as Zayd cannot be thought of as shareable without considering its position.[^V02-27]

The claim that two things can help distinguish one another does not create a circular argument. To distinguish a bird from a creature that gives birth to live young, we may refer to each in terms of the other, but the knowledge of each depends on what the other is, not on first identifying the feature that distinguishes it. The distinction depends on the things themselves, or on the cause that connects them. As we will see when discussing things defined through their relations to one another, no problem follows.[^V02-28]

#### Objection and reply

Someone objects: if two things of the same kind differ while they share the same subject and time, then how do the parts of time differ? Time is the measure of the heavens’ motion, and that motion has one body as its subject. The parts of time should therefore have the same subject too.

The answer is that the parts of time differ through their own nature. Time is a reality that is always passing and renewing; it has no nature apart from this ongoing change. Asking why one day comes before another, or how one differs from another when they are alike, is like asking why the heavenly sphere is a sphere. One day is what it is by coming before another day and differing from it. In the same way, two comes before three by nature and differs from it simply by being two.

A one-cubit length of line differs from half a cubit of that line by its own nature, not because of some feature outside it. The two lengths remain different even if we set aside the place and time in which they exist. We have therefore seen that things of the same kind can differ through their own nature. Avicenna says that among the categories, only position makes something an individual by itself.[^V02-29] He means that position distinguishes one thing from another when time is the same. With the position held the same, time can make the difference; with time held the same, position can. One position differs from another in the same way that one time or one amount differs from another: through its own nature.[^V02-30] Individuality in this broader sense can come from a thing’s own essence, as with the Necessary Being; from something that belongs to its essence, as a position belongs to the sun; or from a feature that comes with it from the start of its existence, as with position and time. A soul is an individual through its relation to a body, and bodily powers are individuals through the bodies they belong to.

#### Chapter 3 — Kinds of distinction

As we have seen, a thing’s being distinct is not the same as its being an individual. Being distinct is relative: it means standing apart from something else. Being an individual is not relative; it is simply the thing’s own way of existing and its identity.[^V02-31] A distinction is what sets one thing apart from others by a feature they do not share. Sometimes that feature is the thing’s own nature, as with the Necessary Being, who is distinct from everything else simply by being Himself. Possible essences and ideas in the mind are also distinct simply by what they are. In other cases, a thing is set apart by an added feature that it has and others lack, as writing sets a writer apart from someone who does not write. Or it is set apart by lacking a feature, as not writing sets a non-writer apart from a writer.

We can consider an added feature on its own, without also considering the absence of some other feature. For example, we can consider Zayd’s writing without considering that he is not a tailor. Or we can consider the feature together with the absence of another one. So an added distinction can be positive, negative, or a combination of both.

A single kind of thing can be distinguished in all these ways. A human being differs by nature from a horse. One person may differ from another through a positive quality, as the merciful Zayd differs from the all-conquering Amr; or through the presence of a quality in one and its absence in the other, as a knowledgeable person differs from an ignorant one. A writer who is not a tailor differs from a tailor who is not a writer by having one feature and lacking the other; the reverse is also true.

All added distinctions follow from existence.[^V02-32] Even different kinds of nonexistence are distinct only because the ideas of them exist in our minds, or because the positive features whose absence they describe exist. Nonexistences do not have separate natures or qualities of their own. The truth is that when added qualities set things apart, it is those qualities that are truly distinct. Each is distinct in its own right, not through some further quality; otherwise, the explanation would go on without end. A thing is distinct in itself only when its difference comes from what it is, not from some added feature. When an added feature marks it out, the distinction belongs to that feature; the thing is distinct only in a secondary sense.[^V02-33]

#### Chapter 4 — How a general kind differs from matter, and a specific kind from its subject

How we consider a thing’s nature matters here.[^V02-34] We can take it on the condition that it stands alone, with nothing else included. Anything joined to it is then outside it. If it forms a whole with something else, it is a part of that whole and serves as its material part.[^V02-35] It comes before the whole both in thought and in the world, so the part cannot be treated as the whole: the two do not exist in the same way.

We can also consider a thing’s nature without deciding whether it stands alone or is joined to something else. Taken this way, it can be said both of itself and of the whole it forms with the added part. A nature considered in this way may be incomplete in reality. It can apply to things with different natures, but becomes one of them only when something is added to it. This is what a general kind is: the added feature completes it and makes it one kind rather than another.[^V02-36] A nature may instead be complete in itself, yet still take on one of several distinct forms when other features are added. Each addition makes it one of the real kinds that fall under a general kind. A specific kind is like this.[^V02-37] The most basic matter underlying the world of elements provides an example: in itself, it is a kind, perhaps even a single individual of that kind.[^V02-38] This has led to debate over whether its unity is the unity of a kind or of one individual. There is no real conflict: by its nature, it is a kind with only one instance.[^V02-39]

Take “animal” as an example. If we consider animal on the condition that nothing else is included, then add rationality, the whole is made of animal and rationality. Animal is the material part; it is not the whole. If we consider animal together with rationality and completed by it, we have the specific kind “human being.” If we consider animal without deciding whether anything else is included, we have a general kind. The first sense of animal is a part of a human being and comes before it in existence; the second is the human being itself; the third is its general kind. It can be said of the human being, rather than being one of its parts.

We call the general kind and the feature that distinguishes a specific kind “parts” because both appear in its definition. The mind has to consider them to form an accurate idea of a specific kind that falls under a general one. In thought, this makes them prior to the specific kind. In the world, they come after it: until a human being exists outside the mind, we cannot identify a nature shared with other beings or a feature that marks it out and makes it what it is. This is the substance of Avicenna’s account in *The Healing*.

Some questions arise about this account. First, the nature being divided is the unrestricted nature, which seems to be just the nature taken without condition. So it appears to be divided into itself and something else.

The answer is that although the mind starts with the unrestricted nature,[^V02-40] it does not consider it as unrestricted while making the division. It considers it in three ways:[^V02-41] as the unrestricted nature itself, and as that nature taken under two other conditions. For example, the broad nature of animal is wider than “animal considered by itself” or “animal taken together with another feature.”

Second, if we take something on the condition that it stands alone, that means nothing else at all can be joined to it. How, then, can it be a part of a whole that includes something more? Avicenna uses body as an example: if we take it as a substance with length, width, and depth, and include nothing else in its nature, then sensation and growth would be outside it. Body would be matter for a whole that has those features.

The answer is that “standing alone” means complete in its own nature. It does not need anything else to become what it is. If something is added, the combination is a new whole. This is different from a general kind taken without condition: that kind is incomplete and needs a feature to make it a specific kind.

Third, the account first treats the general kind as something taken without condition, then later describes it as taken with a condition.

The answer is that “without condition” is broader. The same nature can be taken without condition in one respect and with a condition in another. What counts as a division in one respect can be the broader category in another.[^V02-42]

Fourth, a specific kind is the whole formed by the general kind and the feature that distinguishes it. So it is loose to describe it as the nature made complete by an added feature. Body, for example, does not become a human animal simply by adding a soul, sensation, and movement; the whole must be considered together.

The answer is that this objection treats the general kind, the distinguishing feature, and the specific kind as different things in the world. The point is that they are one nature considered in different ways. When we consider how the general kind differs from the feature that completes it, each can be named as part of the definition and said of the whole. When we consider only their unity, we have the specific kind itself. This is what it means to take the nature “with a condition.”[^V02-43]

Fifth, if matter is a part that exists outside the mind, why must it come before the whole in thought?

The answer is that to understand a composite kind such as a human being, we must first understand its general kind and its distinguishing feature. The mind therefore considers its parts before it considers the whole.[^V02-44] Put another way, the general kind is vague in relation to the specific features that complete it; in itself, however, it is already a distinct kind of nature. Seen in this way, it again serves as matter and as a part.[^V02-45]

Sixth, the body of an animal in the world is the body we see. How, then, can that body, taken as matter, exist before the animal?

The answer is that “body” is used in two ways. The body that serves as matter for the soul is one thing; the body we call an animal is the whole formed when soul is joined to it. These are two distinct bodies, one part of the other, as in every natural compound.[^V02-46]

Seventh, a general kind can be one of its specific kinds, and a specific kind can be one of its individuals. Why, then, is the general kind called incomplete and the specific kind complete?

The answer is that we are considering kinds as the mind understands them, not as they exist outside the mind or as the senses point to them.[^V02-47] The general kind is incomplete because it needs a feature to make it a complete kind. The specific kind is complete: it does not need a further feature to become what it is. This does not mean that actual individuals lack features that distinguish them from one another.

Keeping this difference in mind, we can take a distinguishing feature in three ways. If we take it on the condition that nothing else is included, it is a part and a form. If we take it without deciding whether it is joined to something else, it can be said of the whole and is still a distinguishing feature. If we take it together with what it completes, it is the specific kind itself. We can consider an accidental feature in the same three ways: as the feature, as something said of a thing, or as the whole made from the feature and the thing it belongs to. The first whole has a real unity in its nature; the second is one only in the way we consider it.

It would be clearer to call a specific kind, when considered as the thing that bears features, a “subject” rather than “matter.” The same applies to a general kind considered in relation to accidental features, rather than to features that complete its nature. Matter needs what is joined to it in order to become a complete thing; a subject already exists without the feature that belongs to it. The same thing can be matter for one feature and a subject for another.[^V02-48]

Here is a point to note. A general kind is incomplete and needs a distinguishing feature as part of what it is. It cannot need that feature in one case but not another. If it became complete without the feature, it would no longer be a general kind. A simple nature therefore cannot lose its distinguishing feature and have another one take its place. The feature is not needed just to set the thing apart from others, since accidental features can do that too. It is needed because the nature itself is incomplete. A kind cannot depend on a distinguishing feature in one case and not another, except as a difference in how the mind considers it.[^V02-49]

There is also a difference between a general kind in a compound thing and a general kind in something simple. In a compound, a general kind can be considered apart from its role as a general kind and taken as a complete kind in its own right, without adding one particular distinguishing feature. For example, body is a substance with length, width, and depth. Its nature is complete; otherwise, body could not change from something lifeless into a plant or an animal. Body is a general kind only when we consider it without deciding whether anything else is included. Taken this way, “body” applies to animals, plants, and other bodily kinds: they are all substances with three dimensions, even though they are more than just that.

But a simple kind such as color cannot have a separate existence outside the specific forms that give it its character. There is no color in the world alongside black or white that combines with them to make each color, as body exists alongside a living form to make an animal.[^V02-50] What is reported—that making the general kind and the distinguishing feature is one act, so making body is the same as making animal—if it is correct, means that making body as a general kind is not separate from making a specific kind.[^V02-51] Considered in itself, however, the general nature exists in a different way from the feature that distinguishes the specific kind.

It is also wrong to say that when an animal dies, its body disappears and a different body begins. Some matter must remain to receive the changing forms and features, whether it is a simple body or prime matter. That underlying matter is the most general material part of compound things. The animal’s bodily nature remains after death, though it is no longer an animal’s body. To say that its identity has been destroyed and replaced by another comes close to the wild claims that things can leap from one place to another without passing through the space between, that parts can separate and remain, or that qualities cannot last.[^V02-52]

One further point: you may read that if a general nature must be made one particular kind, it cannot exist as another; if it does not have to be made that kind, its joining with it may be by chance. This only makes sense when the general nature has a form in the world. It does not work for color in relation to black and white. Color itself has no form in the world separate from the power by which sight distinguishes colors. We cannot point to color apart from its forms and ask why it became black or white. But body and prime matter do exist in the world. If body had to take one particular form in order to exist, there could be no body without that form. So there must be a cause beyond body itself that makes it fire, a heavenly body, moving, located, round, or otherwise. This leaves room for matter to remain while forms and other features change.[^V02-53]

#### Chapter 5 — How to identify a kind’s distinguishing feature, how it differs from a mere mark, and how it joins the general kind

The words used for a kind’s distinguishing feature in definitions often name signs of the true feature, or things that follow from it. “Sensitive” and “moving,” for example, are not, by their meanings alone, the features that define an animal.[^V02-54] The true feature is that it has a soul that perceives and moves. The ability to perceive and the ability to move are not the animal soul itself; they are among the things that follow from it. But because we may not know the true features, or may lack names for them, we sometimes have to use these signs instead. So “sensitive” does not mean the concept formed from a feeling or a relation of awareness.[^V02-55] Otherwise, one thing would be made up of things from different categories, though one thing belongs to two categories only by accident. The true distinguishing feature is what gives rise to these qualities, and the same applies in similar cases. When one meaning is considered together with another, if they differ in what they are and how they exist, the second is not a distinguishing feature but a quality added from outside. If they differ only because one is vague and the other makes it specific, then the second is a distinguishing feature. Avicenna says in the metaphysics section of *The Healing*: “The mind may grasp a meaning that could be many things, each of which is that same meaning in existence. It then adds another meaning to make it specific, by including it in the first. This second meaning differs only in how definite or vague it is, not in its existence.”[^V02-56]

#### Further discussion and clarification

Some have denied that simple things have parts that the mind can distinguish. They say these parts are only features that go with the thing: the feature shared by several things is their general kind, and the feature unique to one is what distinguishes it. But this would mean that simple things with entirely different natures share an added quality, without anything in common to explain why it belongs to them. To avoid saying that one meaning can be drawn from different natures, these thinkers claimed that both the general kind and its distinguishing feature in a simple nature come from qualities that belong to it in the world. The broader kind is drawn from a feature we are not sure belongs only to that nature. The feature that distinguishes it, by contrast, is drawn from one we know belongs to that nature alone.[^V02-57] This view is plainly strained.

What led them to it was the thought that blackness, for example, could be divided into color and a quality that makes the eye contract. If both match blackness completely, there is no difference between them. But if color alone matches blackness, then it also matches whiteness, so blackness and whiteness would have to be the same thing. If only one of the two matches blackness, then blackness would be just that one. And if each matches a different part of blackness, then blackness would be made of parts in the world, though we have assumed it is simple. From this, they conclude that the general kind and distinguishing features of simple things are only ways the mind considers them. Blackness, for example, exists in the mind just as it does in the world, so it has no essential parts.

Here is how the difficulty can be resolved. If every meaning we drew from a nature were a complete and distinct reality, that nature would itself be made of parts. If one complete thing is identical with a second, and the second with a third, then the first must also be identical with the third. But the meanings we draw from a nature are not all complete in that way. Some are incomplete in themselves, or the mind considers them without yet giving them a definite form. Others make them definite. Bringing these meanings together is more like bringing a capacity to fulfilment, or something incomplete to completion. It does not mean that the nature itself is made of separate parts. Similarly, a general nature may be present in different things without making them identical. Animal nature belongs to both a human being and a horse, yet a human being and a horse are different.

To understand this more clearly, notice that not all the meanings we draw from a thing are drawn in the same way. Some come from a thing as it is in the world; others come from how the mind considers it. The mind can take a meaning that is mixed and joined with definite features in reality, think of it on its own as vague and undetermined, and then add specific meanings to it. This is not like joining two complete, separate things in reality, as matter and form join to make a third thing. Rather, the two meanings differ only because one is vague and the other makes it definite. The first kind of joining creates a real compound; the second creates a compound in thought, though this way of thinking can still be true at its own level of reality.

Someone might ask: if we take both the general kind and the distinguishing feature from a simple nature, then think of them as matter and form, each seems complete in itself. Wouldn’t joining two complete things mean that the simple nature is really made of parts? After all, different things cannot match one simple nature.

The answer is that the general kind and the distinguishing feature are not taken from a simple thing as two complete things, so that the general kind becomes mental matter and the distinguishing feature mental form. The mind alone makes the simple nature seem to be a compound of matter and form. There is no such compound in the thing itself. This is something the mind imagines through a way of considering the thing that does not match the world outside the mind.[^V02-58]

Someone might ask: if a definition tells us what a thing is, how can the definition contain several distinct meanings when the thing itself is simple, with no parts except those the mind imagines?

The answer is that a definition lays out, one by one, the meanings drawn from a thing’s nature. The thing itself holds them together as one. So the definition can have several parts without the thing itself being made of parts, even when the definition and the thing are one and the same in nature. We have already seen how the mind can draw different meanings from one simple nature.[^V02-59]

#### Chapter 6 — How the distinguishing feature completes the general kind

This completion does not take place in the outside world, since the two are one in existence there. Things that are one in existence cannot bring each other into existence. Rather, the mind divides a specific kind into two parts in thought and judges one to be the cause of the other.[^V02-60] This is because the parts of one essence depend on each other. The part that is needed, and so serves as cause, can only be the distinguishing feature.[^V02-61] The general kind cannot cause the distinguishing feature to exist. If it did, it would have to bring about every opposing distinguishing feature, so one thing would contain opposites that cannot coexist. That is impossible. So the distinguishing feature must cause the general kind to exist as a specific kind. It divides the general nature taken without restriction, brings about the particular share of that nature found in the specific kind, and forms one part of the whole along with what distinguishes that kind from others.

A possible objection is this: if rationality causes animal nature in general, it cannot divide that nature into kinds. But if it causes a particular kind of animal, such as a human being, that kind must already be specific before rationality can cause it. Once it is specific, however, it already exists and no longer needs a cause. The answer is that the distinguishing feature comes before the general kind because it causes the kind’s nature. The effect cannot first demand its cause, because it has not yet come into existence at the level of the cause.[^V02-62] It is the cause that brings the effect into existence. In the same way, animal nature does not call for one particular feature. It needs some feature, but does not choose which one. Rationality, by its own nature, brings about this particular kind of animal.[^V02-63] The general need comes from animal nature; the precise feature that meets that need comes from the distinguishing feature.[^V02-64] Nor is it strange for one general kind to have several causes, since its unity is weak.

It is strange that the author of *The Eastern Discussions*, though he understood this point, failed to use it in his argument for matter.[^V02-65] After giving his proof, he says he presented it to many sharp thinkers, and none found fault with its steps. His argument for matter in the celestial sphere goes like this: the sphere has a fixed size and shape because it cannot come to be or pass away. What makes this necessary? It must be the sphere’s bodily nature, a feature within it, or something separate from it. It cannot be its bodily nature: then all bodies would have the same size and shape. It cannot be a feature within it either, since we would then have to ask the same question about that feature. And it cannot be something separate, because that would relate equally to all bodies. So the sphere’s fixed size and shape must come from the underlying matter that receives its bodily form. That was meant to establish matter. But the sphere’s specific form comes before bodily nature in the order of existence. It gives the sphere its distinguishing feature and makes the general kind, body, specific. So it can cause the sphere’s particular size and shape. None of the problems he named follows. Keep this point in mind.

#### Chapter 7 — How form joins with matter

The word “form” can refer to a specific kind of thing, or to the nature of any thing.[^V02-66] It can also mean what gives a thing its underlying reality, what makes matter into a natural kind, or a perfection that can exist apart from a thing. If we look closely at all these uses, we find that they share one basic meaning: what makes a thing actually be what it is. That is why there is no contradiction in saying both that a thing’s form is its essence—what makes it what it is—and that its matter is what bears its form.[^V02-67]

To see why, start with this: matter in any thing is indeterminate. It has no definite nature in itself; it is only the possibility of becoming something. Form, by contrast, makes a thing actual. It is what makes the thing be something. For example, the matter of a bed is wood, but not wood considered simply as wood. Insofar as it has the nature and definite form of wood, it is already something in its own right, not matter for a bed. It is matter for a bed because it can become a bed, a door, a chair, or something else. Its refusal to take certain other forms comes not from its potential, but from its present actuality: it already has a specific form that prevents it from becoming something whose nature conflicts with its own. So the nature of wood has both an incomplete side and a complete side. Because it is incomplete, it can take on another completion; because it is already complete in one way, it cannot take on a conflicting completion. Together, these two sides explain why a bed has both matter and form.

Likewise, wood has its own form, while its matter is the elements—not considered as earth, water, or something else, but as elements mixed in a way that makes them ready to become a mineral, a plant, an animal, or another particular kind, but not just anything. This continues until we reach matter that has no matter beneath it: first matter, the basic underlying matter of all things. It has no definite nature or actuality apart from being a substance with the potential to become anything, with nothing in itself to make it one thing rather than another. It is only a receiver, with nothing but the capacity to become something; otherwise, the account would lead to a circle or an endless chain.[^V02-68] Calling it a substance does not make it definite; it is real only as an open possibility. Its readiness to change is not a completed form, but only the capacity to become one. Nonexistence is different: taken as nonexistence, it has no reality at all—not even an indeterminate reality or the potential to become something. First matter has this one weak kind of reality, and no other on its own. It is the lowest and weakest kind of being, almost at the edge of existence.

From this, a thoughtful reader can see that every composite thing is what it is through what serves as its form, not through what serves as its matter.[^V02-69] Matter, as matter, is absorbed into form just as a general kind is absorbed into its distinguishing feature. Matter stands to form as incompleteness to completeness and weakness to strength. A thing’s reality is established by its form. It needs matter so that form can produce its effects and the features that come with them and cannot be separated from them, such as quantity, quality, and place. If form could exist apart from matter, it would still be the same thing, because matter has no independent reality; it is only the potential for reality.

The world is a world through its form, not its matter; a bed is a bed through its shape, not its wood; a human being is human through the soul that guides the body, not through the body alone; and what exists is made real by existence, not by its essence. If the form of the world existed on its own, it would still be a world. If a bed’s shape existed without wood, it would still be a bed. Likewise, the human soul remains human when separated from the body, and existence apart from essence is still existence, as with God, the Necessary Being. The point is captured in the saying that when a person understands how things exist as they truly are, that person becomes an intelligible world, like the world that exists outside the mind. A wisdom poem puts it this way: “A heart full of cattle, donkeys, fields, and estates is just a village, not a heart.” This also helps explain why early logicians allowed a thing to be defined by its ultimate distinguishing feature alone, even though they held that a definition should reveal the thing’s full nature.[^V02-70]

An exalted principle is clear from what we have said in this chapter and from hints given earlier.[^V02-71] What makes any essence—simple or composite—be what it is and exist is only its ultimate distinguishing feature. The other distinguishing features and forms that go with it are like powers, conditions, tools, and preparatory causes. They ready the essence for the existence it has through that ultimate feature.[^V02-72] They do not belong to its final, settled nature or make up the core of its reality, although each may make up a different nature.

For example, some of the powers and forms in the human body prepare first matter simply to be a body, such as the form that gives it extension. Others give it plant life through the powers of nourishment, growth, and reproduction. Others give it animal life through sensation and voluntary movement. Still others make it human through the power of reason. Each earlier form prepares the way for the next. The later form arises from the earlier forms, and they receive their place in the whole through it. What had first been causes, conditions, and preparations later become powers, dependent features, and branches. The final form becomes the source and leading form for all the rest; the others serve it as helpers and offshoots.[^V02-73]

From these principles and what follows, it will become clear that a kind’s distinguishing features are not things apart from it. They are the particular ways its true nature exists as real individuals.[^V02-74] What exists outside the mind is existence itself. Through sensation or direct awareness, the mind draws broad or narrow concepts from a thing’s own nature, and concepts of its incidental features from other aspects of it. The mind then applies to these concepts what belongs to them in the outside world. A concept drawn from the thing itself is called essential; one drawn from another aspect of it is called incidental. An essential concept belongs directly to what exists, because it is one with that thing in reality. An incidental concept belongs to it only indirectly, as something added. This does not mean that a shared nature exists only in the mind, as one might think.[^V02-75] Existence belongs directly to it when the concept is essential: what truly exists is one with that nature in the outside world; the two are not separate realities.

#### Chapter 8 — How the general kind is taken from matter and the distinguishing feature from form

In things made of matter and form, the general kind is taken from the matter, and the distinguishing feature from the form.[^V02-76] Someone might object: A body, as we shall explain, is made up of matter and form, and both are substances, according to Aristotle and his followers. Yet the nature of a body is an extended substance. Why, then, should we take the concept of “substance” from matter rather than from form? It seems to apply to both equally, since both are kinds of substance.[^V02-77]

Here is the answer. Matter and form each have a simple specific nature. In the mind, each can be analyzed into a shared general kind and a distinguishing feature that makes it a specific kind and gives it its existence. This is the concept of substance. The distinguishing feature of matter is its capacity to receive forms; the distinguishing feature of form is extension. Matter is matter because it has the capacity to receive forms, and form is that form because it is extended. But matter’s capacity does not make it an actual, definite thing. It gives matter only the potential to become actual things.[^V02-78]

When we consider matter itself, all that makes it definite is that it is a substance. This gives it only the weakest kind of definition. Form is different: the idea of substance is contained within the idea of “extended,” just as a general kind is absorbed into its distinguishing feature. Matter in a body is nothing but substance with the capacity to take on any form or quality. Likewise, a general kind has only the idea of substance, which can in itself be joined with the conditions that make a thing a particular kind or individual. The potentiality in matter corresponds to the possibility found in a general kind. And the form of a body is its extension and continuity, just as its distinguishing feature is the simple concept “extended.” Nothing general or particular is included in that concept, as careful thinkers have explained. When grammarians explain a word formed from a verb, the word “thing” only refers back to the pronoun contained in it; it adds nothing else. Avicenna confirms this in *The Healing*: when “rational” is used in the broad sense of a distinguishing feature, it means “a thing that has reason.” Calling something “a thing that has reason” does not by itself tell us whether it is a substance or a quality. We know from other considerations that it must be a substance, or a body.[^V02-79]

This shows why, in the nature of a body, the concept of “substance” is taken from matter and the distinguishing feature from form. The same applies to other composite things like it. I can make this clearer, so listen closely to what I am about to explain,[^V02-80] and keep these secrets from those who would misuse them.[^V02-81]

Philosophers agree that a general kind, in relation to its distinguishing feature, is a necessary feature of it, while the distinguishing feature, in relation to the general kind, is a property. They also say that in things made of matter and form, the general kind corresponds to the matter and the distinguishing feature to the form. These claims together imply that the distinguishing features of substances are not themselves substances in the sense of falling under the general kind “substance.”[^V02-82] They relate to that general kind as something necessarily accompanying it, not as a kind falling under it. In the same sense, bodily forms and other forms are not substances, even though “substance” can be applied to them in a secondary way.[^V02-83] Their not belonging by their own nature to the category of substance does not mean that they must belong to one of the nine categories of qualities. Simple natures, whether outside the mind or in it, do not in themselves fall under any general kind.[^V02-84]

This does not challenge the claim that there are ten categories, as Avicenna explains in the *Categories* section of *The Healing*. The point is that anything with a specific definition belongs by its own nature to one of these categories. It does not follow that everything must have a definition; that would lead to circular reasoning or an endless chain. Some things are understood directly, not through a definition, such as existence and many things we know through immediate awareness.[^V02-85] Someone might object: A human being is made up of a body, which is its matter, and a soul, which is its form. The soul has been proved to be a substance, immaterial and able to survive the body’s destruction, as we shall show. But what you have said about forms applies just as well to the rational soul, since it too is a form and the source of the distinguishing feature in the definition of a human being.

I would answer that the human soul can be considered in two ways: as the body’s form and soul, and as a thing in itself. In the first sense, it exists for something else—the body. In the second, it exists in itself, whether for itself or for something else. For a form that exists in matter, its existence in itself is the same as its existence in the matter. The two ways of considering it therefore coincide. But a form that can exist apart from matter has its own existence in itself. The two ways of considering it, and the two kinds of existence, are then distinct. When a form that exists in matter leaves its matter, it is destroyed. A form that can exist apart from matter may leave it and still continue to exist. Its existence for the matter and its existence in itself are not the same.

Once this is clear, we can say that a thing’s belonging to a category in one respect does not mean it belongs to that category—or to any category—in another respect. The human soul is a substance when considered in itself, but as a soul it is understood through its relation to the body. Considered as part of the body, or as the form that completes it, it need not be a substance in that same sense. The same applies to other forms that exist in matter. It is true that the soul is an immaterial substance. But its role in making up the body does not have to fall under the general kind “substance.” The soul as a self-standing reality is one thing; as a state or form of the body, it is another.

The same answer addresses an objection to the philosophers’ rule that every new thing must be preceded by matter’s readiness to receive it. The rule may seem to conflict with the view that newly created souls are immaterial, a view held by Aristotle, the First Teacher.[^V02-86] The human body, through its particular condition, calls for a governing soul to direct it—that is, for something able to perform these tasks. The Generous Giver must therefore bring into existence a source of human guidance and action. Such a source can only be a reality that is immaterial in itself. So the soul does not come into being because the body calls for its essence, but because its essence cannot be separated from the role the body calls for.[^V02-87] Through its particular mixture, the body calls for something material; the generosity of the Giver brings about a sacred reality.

Just as one thing can be a substance in one respect and a quality in another, so one thing can be immaterial in its essence and material in its activity. The human soul is immaterial in its essence, but in its activity—guiding and moving the body—it depends on the body and is joined to it. As to its essence, it comes only from the generosity of the Giver.[^V02-88] In that respect, it is not preceded by the body’s readiness and does not have to exist together with the body. It is affected by the shortcomings of matter only indirectly. This is my answer to the objection against the rule. Consider it carefully: it may seem clear, yet it has a subtle point. Plato’s claim that the soul is eternal may also be understood in this light.

There is another way to answer.[^V02-89] If you reflect on the principles we have already discussed[^V02-90], and remember what the Illuminationist master, Suhrawardi, explains in his two books—that the soul has a simple, luminous reality in *The Philosophy of Illumination* and is pure existence in *The Book of Elucidations*—you will see that these mean the same thing. To appear is to be actual, and to be actual is to exist. Illuminationist principles show that light is simple and has no general kind or distinguishing feature. Individual lights differ only in how complete or deficient they are in their luminous existence. You will then understand that separate luminous realities do not fall under the category of substance, even though they exist without being in a subject. My dear reader, hold firmly to this principle: it has a depth that most thinkers have missed. If you study Suhrawardi’s books and the principles he uses to answer objections, an illuminating insight may open to you. From what I have explained, you may already have begun to see that the whole world is existence, all existence is light, and “incidental light is light upon light.”[^V02-91]

Consider the human body, with its forms and powers—the sources of its actions. It is like an army of the luminous governing soul in the world of opposites, and a place where the soul’s lights and effects appear. Those powers and tools, together with their commander, are all pure existence and light: like lamps of different brightness, arranged in order, one above another, all lit by a single light and under its independent command. A weak light cannot shine independently in the presence of a stronger one. The whole world of existence is like this: rays of light from the one Necessary Being, who exists through Himself. All existence comes from the rising of His light and the shining of His presence, as we see in the sun, whose earthly and heavenly image points to Him. But there is a difference between these two kinds of rays. The rays of the sun of intellect are living, aware, speaking, and active. The rays of the sun we see are qualities and lights that belong to something else; they are not alive, aware, or acting in themselves. The details of these ideas will come later, God willing.[^V02-92]

**In brief:** The more actual, noble, and luminous a form is, the more receptive, lowly, and dark the matter that receives it is.[^V02-93]

**In detail:** First matter is the source of lowliness and the center of evil and estrangement. It is an ugly old woman whom a luminous soul encounters only after clothing her in bodily and specific forms, and lighting her with powers and qualities. In this way, she leaves behind her bare potentiality, ugliness, and darkness. Once the soul is joined to matter, it brings about these forms, powers, and qualities. But if the soul departs and the flow of those powers and qualities stops, matter seems to return to its bare state, stripped of every feature and quality. It becomes exposed to fading away and decay, and naturally repulsive and frightening. We see this in the unease people feel when they look at the bodies of the dead, especially when left alone with one in the dark.

#### Chapter 9 — Platonic Forms and Ideas

Plato is said to have taught, in many statements that agree with his teacher Socrates, that things have forms in the divine realm.[^V02-94] These are sometimes called divine Ideas. They do not perish or decay; they last forever. Only the things that come into being here perish.

In the *Metaphysics* of *The Healing*, Avicenna says that some people thought this division meant there were two beings for every kind[^V02-95]—two human beings, for example[^V02-96], in the meaning of “human”: one a perishable, visible human, the other an eternal, separate human known by the intellect. They said that each had its own existence.[^V02-97] They called the separate existence an “Idea.” They also believed that every natural thing had a separate form,[^V02-98] which the intellect receives when it understands it, because what the intellect understands does not perish, while everything we see does.[^V02-99] They thought that knowledge and proofs aim at these Forms and reach them. Plato and his teacher Socrates are known for strongly defending this view. They said that “humanity” has one meaning shared by all people, and that it remains even when they perish. This shared meaning cannot be the many changing human beings we see. It must therefore be an intelligible reality that exists apart from them. That is Avicenna’s account.

With God’s help, I will first set out the different ways people have explained Plato’s words, and the objections raised against each explanation. Then I will explain what I take to be the truth about separate Forms and Ideas.

Avicenna explained Plato’s words as meaning that each thing has an essence stripped of its added features, an essence that can take on opposite states.[^V02-100] But Plato, whose student was Aristotle, the First Teacher, was far too great a thinker to confuse thinking of an essence apart from its features with an essence existing apart from them. Nor would he confuse considering an essence without deciding whether something is joined to it with requiring that nothing be joined to it. He would not confuse being one in meaning with being one individual in the world.[^V02-101] If he had, he would have thought that because all people are “human” in the same sense, there is one human being that exists as one individual in many people.

He would also have missed this: if “human” is considered only as what it is, without any added features such as being one or many, then saying that a human, simply as human, is one, eternal, or anything else that goes beyond the meaning of “human” is a contradiction.[^V02-102] And he would have thought that saying “humanity always exists” means that one and the same humanity remains forever. How could that be? These distinctions are clear even to students with some training in philosophy, let alone to thinkers of such stature.

Al-Farabi, the Second Teacher, says in *The Reconciliation of the Views of Plato and Aristotle* that Plato meant that things have Forms in God’s knowledge, where they remain unchanged. A later thinker developed this explanation.[^V02-103] He said that in the world we sense there are individual people, each with a body and particular features. This is the natural human being. But there is also “the human” considered simply in itself, without adding oneness, plurality, or any other feature beyond humanity. This is the meaning that can be said of many people, such as Zayd and Amr. When the mind says “human” of both Zayd and Amr, it turns to a meaning set apart from their particular features. It even sets aside the fact that this meaning is abstract or unrestricted. That meaning must exist somewhere: either outside the mind or in it. If it existed outside the mind, the features that make each person an individual would be added to the essence only afterward. So it must exist in the mind, where it is made into a distinct object of thought by the mind’s own way of considering it. The mind can focus on it without attending to that mental distinction. Since the concept “human” can be said of real substances in a way that identifies them, this thinker concluded that there are intellectual substances in the Intellect that are the essences of things in the world. This, he said, is Plato’s very view.

One might object that Plato is generally said to have taught that intellectual substances exist in reality and are universal essences of the individuals we see. The answer is that by “reality” he may have meant the Intellects, which are the true realities of the sensory world. In Plato’s view, the sensory world is only their shadow.[^V02-104] That is the end of this thinker’s account.

This explanation is very unlikely. The reports about Plato and the early philosophers, and the ridicule their Aristotelian followers directed at them, show that they meant the Forms to exist outside the mind and stand on their own, not as features lodged in something else. Plato is even reported to have said that, when free of the body, he saw spheres of light. Hermes is said to have described a spiritual being that taught him, and when he asked who it was, the being replied, “I am your complete nature.” If their words did not clearly point to a separate, individual reality for each kind in the creative world, why would the followers of Aristotle have mocked them with the claim—reported by Al-Farabi—that their view must put lines, surfaces, and spheres inside the Intellects?[^V02-105]

Their view would also require movements and orbits of those spheres, knowledge of the stars and of music, harmonious sounds, medicine, geometry, straight and curved measurements, hot and cold things, active and passive qualities, universals and particulars, matter and form, and many other things they found absurd.

Our teacher and master, to whom we owe our learning—may God raise his honor—says in one of his philosophical books that divine decree has two distinct senses: a knowing sense and a real, existing sense. It can mean that something appears in knowledge or takes shape in the intellectual world. It can also mean that it exists outside the mind. We have explained that an actual infinite is impossible in the realm of divine measure: divine decree is close to the source of things, while divine measure unfolds events over time without end. The divine realm can hold a complete understanding of an endless series without being limited by it, for God is all-encompassing and all-knowing.

Whatever exists through time and change completes its existence in stages here, but exists as a whole in the timeless order. Its existence there is complete, not spread across time. In that order, its amount must be limited, whether its existence unfolds in what we call the past or the future. Material things, considered in divine decree—that is, as they exist in the timeless order—do not come after their matter. The body and its matter exist at the same level there. So if you hear us say that material things are material only in the realm of measure and time, not in the timeless realm of divine decree and God’s direct knowledge, understand what we mean: matter does not come earlier there. We do not mean that matter is absent or that a material thing becomes immaterial in another respect.

The best name for things that unfold in time, when considered as they exist in the timeless order, is “real Forms” or “Forms of divine decree.” These are their complete realities in the timeless order. When considered as things that come into being and unfold in time, they are “created things.” This is a secret that the wise have hinted at. I cannot believe Plato meant anything else. But Aristotle’s followers misjudged him, trusted the stories their own imaginations told them, failed to investigate carefully, and repeated their attacks on the Platonic Forms. The result was that wisdom’s light went out and darkness spread. That is the end of our teacher’s words.[^V02-106]

The point is this: all material and temporal things, considered in themselves and in relation to one another, depend on places, times, and positions that keep them apart. But in God’s complete, direct knowledge, they are all present at once. None comes before another in that way of seeing and existing. Nothing there is changing, passing away, or beginning to exist in the presence of the First Reality. In this kind of presence, things do not need matter’s readiness or bodily positions. In this respect, they are like things free of place and time. The early philosophers meant only this by separate Forms, so that their view would not face the familiar objections against it.

You might say: Even if we accept that individual things which come into being have material existence, they could still be free of matter in another respect. But these individuals surely have many different existences. Plato’s followers say that each bodily kind has one separate, eternal individual. That would show not only that it exists apart from matter, but that it is one. And being separate itself implies oneness, as has been proved. So it is very far-fetched to explain their view in this way.[^V02-107]

Some people place Plato’s Forms in a separate world of images. This is also wrong for the reasons already given. First, the great thinkers who believe in both separate Forms and suspended images believe in both at once. Second, the Forms are great lights, firmly established in the world of intellectual light. Suspended images, by contrast, have bodily positions. Some are dark, and the wicked suffer when they see them: they are ugly, black and blue images that cause pain. Others shine, and the blessed delight in seeing them: they are beautiful, splendid, bright images, white as hidden pearls.

The Illuminationist philosopher who devoted himself to Plato, his teachers, and the Persian sages agreed with them that every simple kind in the heavens or among the elements, and every compound kind of plant or animal, must have one intellect free from matter. It looks after that kind and is its ruler. He offered several arguments for this view.[^V02-108]

**First argument.** In *The Discussions*, he says that the plant powers of nourishment, growth, and reproduction are dependent features. The earliest philosophers called them dependent features because they exist in something else;[^V02-109] later philosophers did so because their bearer can exist without them.[^V02-110] The forms of the elements are enough to make matter into real things; otherwise, elements and mixtures of elements could not exist. If those forms are enough, the three plant powers must be dependent features.

What bears these powers must be either the vital spirit or the body’s organs. If it is the vital spirit, then because that spirit is constantly breaking down and changing, the powers within it should change whenever their bearer changes. If it is the organs, heat—whether natural to them or coming from outside—acts on every organ. Plant bodies contain moisture and heat, and the heat continually breaks down the moisture. Their parts are therefore always changing and wearing away.

When a part of the body disappears, the power in it should disappear too. The remaining parts are then replaced by food. So what preserves the body’s balance as it changes, and keeps it going over time? It cannot be the power or the parts that have already vanished, since what no longer exists cannot act. Nor can it be the power and parts that will arise later: they exist because of the body’s balance, so they are results of what they would have to preserve; a result cannot preserve its cause.[^V02-111]

The power of growth arises when food enters the body and disrupts its balance.[^V02-112] This calls for movement: the food and the body move in different directions;[^V02-113] food replaces what has been used up and joins parts that differ in nature and position. These actions, with their complex structure, exact order, and beautiful arrangement, cannot come from a blind, unaware power.

Plants and animals remain stable kinds. The idea that a plant has a separate soul that governs it is mistaken; otherwise, that soul would be useless, unable to act or reach its perfection, which is impossible. The power called “formative” is said to be simple. How, then, could it shape organs with so many functions for preserving individuals and kinds, as anatomy books describe? Anyone who reflects on this, and on the wonders of living things, will see that these remarkable actions cannot come from a power with no understanding or control. They must come from a power free of matter, aware of itself and of other things. This power that governs plant bodies is called an intellect. It belongs to the level of separate intellects whose members govern natural kinds, sometimes described as the lords of images and talismans.[^V02-114]

“If you say: Could the power that acts on and governs our bodies be our rational souls? We answer:[^V02-115] We know plainly that we are unaware of these remarkable processes. We do not know when food is distributed among our different organs. Even when our minds are fully developed, we do not sense when it is divided among the body’s fluids, carried to different organs and positions, and shaped into varied forms and intricate patterns. We are even less aware of these processes at the beginning of our lives, when we are ignorant and undeveloped. So our souls are not what carry out these actions. Nor are they made of separate parts, one part to act and another to perceive.”[^V02-116]

**Second argument.** When you examine the kinds found in our world, you see that they do not arise by sheer chance. If they did, their kinds would not remain stable. A human being might come from something other than a human, a horse from something other than a horse, a date palm from something other than a date palm, or wheat from something other than wheat. But that is not what happens. These kinds continue in the same pattern, without change. What remains stable in this way cannot be based on sheer chance.[^V02-117]

The many remarkable colors in a peacock’s feathers do not come, as Aristotle’s followers say, from the different mixtures within each feather. They have no proof of this, and cannot identify what causes each color. How, then, can they make such varied claims without following a clear rule for that kind of thing?[^V02-118]

The right view is what the ancients said: every bodily kind must have a light that exists on its own, apart from matter, and that governs, cares for, and preserves the kind. They call this light the universal of that kind. By “universal,” they do not mean a concept that can be shared by many individuals. How could they mean that when they say this light exists on its own, understands itself and other things, and has its own distinct nature, shared by no other?

They also do not believe that the ruler of the human kind, the horse kind, or any other kind exists for the sake of the lower kind beneath it, as though that lower kind were a mold or model for the separate Intellect. They insist that what is higher does not exist for the sake of what is lower. If that were their view, every model would need another model, and so on without end. That is impossible.[^V02-119] Rather, because the ruler of a kind is free of matter,[^V02-120] it relates equally to every individual of that kind. It cares for them and continually gives them life. In truth, it is the whole and the original; the individuals are its branches.[^V02-121]

**Third argument.** They also argue from the principle that a nobler possibility must exist before a lower one: if a possible being of lower rank exists, then a possible being of higher rank must already have existed. The proof is given in his books.[^V02-122]

The world of bodies contains marvelous arrangements and subtle relationships—in the spheres, stars, elements, and things made from them. The world of souls also contains spiritual wonders and bodily marvels in the powers of souls and the way they are joined to bodies. The arrangements, relationships, and order in the luminous world of intellect must be nobler and better than those in these lower worlds. How could it be otherwise? The marvels of order in the bodily world are only shadows and traces of what is in the intellectual world. Those higher forms are the realities and originals; bodily kinds are their branches, derived from them.[^V02-123]

Those who believe in Platonic Forms do not say that animal life has one Form, being two-legged another, and having wings a third.[^V02-124] Nor do they say that the scent of musk has one Form and musk itself another, or that the sweetness of sugar has one Form and sugar another. Rather, every distinct bodily kind has something that corresponds to it in the sacred world. Each separate light among the rulers of images in the world of pure light has luminous forms from the rays of intellect, along with forms of pleasure, love, honor, humility, power, and other qualities. When its shadow appears in the bodily world, its image is musk with its sweet scent, sugar with its sweet taste, or a human or horse form, with its own pattern of organs and features. These bodily forms reflect the order found in the separate lights.

These are the views of this divine philosopher. They are very fine and subtle, but they do not settle some questions.[^V02-125] Do these intellectual lights have the same nature as their sensory images, so that one individual of each bodily kind is immaterial while the others are material? Or is each separate light a Form of a whole bodily kind, rather than a model for its individual members? Accounts of Plato’s views, and the criticisms made by Aristotle’s followers, clearly support the first reading: these intellectual rulers belong to the same kind as their bodily images.

The Persian sages’ practice of naming the ruler of each kind after that kind supports this too. They revered a plant called haoma as the sacred haoma spirit, Haoma Yazad.[^V02-126] They did the same with other kinds: the ruler of water was called Khordad, the ruler of trees Amordad, and the ruler of fire Ardibehesht. Suhrawardi, the Illuminationist master, understood the earlier philosophers’ names for these rulers as showing resemblance and causation, not identity of kind.[^V02-127] As he says in *The Paths*, when you hear Empedocles, Agathodaemon, and others speak of the rulers of kinds, understand what they mean. Do not imagine that they thought such a ruler was a body, or had a body, a head, and two legs.

And when you read Hermes saying that a spiritual being taught him, and that when he asked who it was, it answered, “I am your complete nature,” do not take him to mean that it was a model exactly like us.

Another problem is this: if legs, wings, and other organs are parts of an animal’s essence, how can a simple, luminous essence serve as its model, whether we consider those organs alone or together with their luminous forms? A model and what it represents need not match in every way, but what is essential in one must correspond to what is essential in the other, and what is incidental to what is incidental.[^V02-128]

A further problem is that these rulers are said to be pure light, while their images are either bodies or dark forms.[^V02-129] What do pure lights and their luminous qualities have in common with bodies and dark forms? All lights—intellectual, spiritual, planetary, and elemental—are said to be one simple reality. They differ only in strength and weakness, or through features that come from outside them. Yet these lesser intellectual lights, the rulers of images,[^V02-130] have effects with different natures. That follows from Suhrawardi’s view that a maker produces an essence, not existence, which he treats as a concept formed by the mind.

Different effects must result either from differences in what receives them or from differences in their causes. If an effect has no receiver, the difference must come from its cause. But the effects of the Intellects differ, and this cannot be explained only by differences in their receivers: the receivers and their capacities are themselves among the different effects, and they have no receiver of their own. Nor can the difference come from differences among the active lights since they differ only in greater or lesser completeness. Their effects must therefore differ in the same way. This further supports the view that the effects of the Intellects and higher principles—which are themselves luminous realities—must be natures that actually exist, not merely general ideas about them. The different natures we draw from them arise from different levels of existence: stronger or weaker, earlier or later, more complex or more simple. Different levels and relations bring different properties and effects, much as different numbers have different properties even though they are made from units and share the number one. Those who study numbers and the stars know the remarkable effects that come from such levels and relations.

#### A note

It is best to understand the early philosophers as saying that each bodily kind has one complete, perfect individual in the creative world. This is its original and source; the other individuals of that kind are its branches, effects, and products. Because it is complete, it does not need matter or anything else to support it. The bodily individuals here are different: because they are weak and incomplete, they depend on matter either for what they are or for what they do.

As you have learned, individuals of the same kind can differ in completeness. Some people argue that if one and the same reality exists on its own in some cases and in something else in others, then if one case needs no underlying subject, none of them should. This is not always true. It applies only when things exist in exactly the same sense. Some forms of existence do not need an underlying subject because they are more complete, substantial, and strong. The most deficient form is an attribute: it is weak and depends on something else. So the fact that one thing exists in a subject does not mean that everything sharing some common reality with it must also exist in a subject, when they differ in completeness and strength.

You also know that the kind of a bodily thing is made real and defined by its specific form alone, united with the final feature that distinguishes it.[^V02-131] Matter itself is indefinite; it exists only as the potential for something else. The general kind taken from matter is an incomplete nature, joined to that final distinguishing feature. In a compound thing, the remaining forms, qualities, and beginnings of its distinguishing features are like conditions, tools, and branches of one underlying reality.

That reality is itself an image of the ruler of its kind, just as the other individuals of the kind are images of their rulers. Their relation to it is like the relation of branches to one source. The patterns, proportions, and shapes in bodily things are likewise shadows of the intellectual forms and meaningful relations in their luminous rulers. The ruler of the human kind, called the Holy Spirit—the Intellect that pours knowledge into human beings—stands in relation to the rulers of other animal and plant kinds as images stand in relation to images.

These material forms—human, horse, bull, and other kinds—need sensory matter in our world. But in the higher world they do not need to exist in matter.
