There they are free of matter, exist on their own, and do not depend on something they are in. Think of mental images: they come from things outside the mind, but exist in the mind as dependent features, not on their own. They can come from substances that do exist on their own.[^V02-132] The bodily forms that exist in matter are likewise images of the separate Platonic Forms in the intellectual world.

The separate intellectual Forms are complete in themselves and in their natures, so they do not need an underlying subject. Their bodily images are incomplete and need one, because they complete something else. A mental image taken from a material thing is similar: it is free of matter while in the mind, but not free of matter outside it. In the same way, the intellectual Forms that rule over bodily kinds are free of matter in the world of intellect, while their bodily images—the specific forms of things—depend on matter.

Do not conclude from the word “Forms” that these great philosophers thought the rulers of kinds were created by the First Creator as models or molds for the lower things. A model must be higher than what it models, since it is the goal. But these philosophers insist even more strongly than Aristotle’s followers that what is higher does not exist for the sake of what is lower. They see bodily kinds as images and shadows of higher, luminous rulers, though the two differ greatly in honor and completeness. Besides, why would God need models to tell Him how to make things? And if He needed a model to make the first models, that would lead to models without end.

You might object: Are you not going against Aristotle, the First Teacher, who rejected this view? I answer: truth deserves to be followed. Besides, Aristotle may have been rejecting what ordinary people take Plato and the early philosophers to mean. They often spoke in symbols and figures of speech, especially about this subject, which can leave even eloquent speakers at a loss and exhaust the mind. Or perhaps Aristotle’s wish to lead and rule was shaped by his dealings with people and his time among kings and rulers.[^V02-133]

In any case, the book known as *The Theology of Aristotle* shows that Aristotle’s view agreed with his teacher’s on the existence of intellectual Forms for kinds: separate, luminous forms that exist on their own in the creative world. In its fourth treatise, he says:

“Beyond this world there are a heaven, an earth, a sea, animals, plants, and heavenly people. Everything in this world has a heavenly counterpart; nothing there is earthly. He also says that the human being we sense is only an image of the intellectual human being. The intellectual human being is spiritual, and all its organs are spiritual. The eye and the hand are not in separate places; all the organs are distinct, yet they are in one place.”

“In the eighth treatise, he says that what makes the fire here what it is is a fiery life—the true fire. The fire above it, in the higher world, is more truly fire. If it is true fire, it must be alive, and its life must be higher and nobler than the life of this fire, which is only an image of it. So the fire in the higher world is alive, and its life gives life to this fire. The same is true of water and air there: they are more alive than water and air here, because the life above gives life to them.[^V02-134]

“He also says that this whole sensory world is an image of that world. If this world is alive, then the first world must be even more alive. If this world is complete, the first world must be more complete, since it gives this world life, power, completeness, and lasting existence. If the higher world is complete in every way, it must contain all the things found here, but in a higher and nobler form, as we have said many times.

“There is a living heaven there, with stars like the ones in our sky, but brighter and more complete. They are not separate from one another as they appear here, because they are not physical.[^V02-135] There is also land without salt marshes, all of it fertile. It contains every kind of animal and the earthly nature found here, and plants rooted in life. It has seas and rivers that flow like living things, and all kinds of water animals. Its air also contains living creatures like those found in the air here. Everything there is alive.

“How could these things not be alive, when they are in a world of pure life, untouched by death? The kinds of animals there are like those here, but higher and nobler: their nature is intellectual, not animal. If someone denies this and asks how there could be animals, a heaven, and the other things we have mentioned in the higher world, we say: the higher world is a complete living being, containing all things. It was created by the First Creator, who is complete,[^V02-136] so it contains every soul and every Intellect. Nothing there is poor or in need. Everything there is full of abundance and life, as though life were boiling and overflowing.

“The life of all things there flows from one source. It is not just a single quality, like heat or wind, but one quality that contains every kind of quality. It contains every taste: sweetness, wine, and all other flavors; every pleasant smell; every color seen by the eye; everything felt by touch; and everything heard, including all melodies and rhythms. It contains all that can be sensed. These are all present within one single, expansive quality, as we have described. This quality is living and intellectual, and it contains every other quality without mixing them together or spoiling them. Each is preserved in it as its own distinct thing.

“In the tenth treatise, he says that every natural form in this world exists in that world in a better and higher way. Here it depends on matter; there it exists without matter. Every natural form here is an image of its counterpart there. So there are a heaven, an earth, animals, air, water, and fire there. If these forms exist there, then plants must exist there too.”

The passage continues:

“If someone asks: If there are plants in the higher world, what are they like there? And if there are fire and earth, what are they like? They must be either alive or dead. If they are dead, as they are here, why would they be needed there? If they are alive, how can they be alive there?

“We can say that plants there are alive too. A plant contains an active principle that carries life. If the principle in an earthly plant is alive, it must also be a soul.[^V02-137] All the more, then, the principle in the plant of the higher world—the first plant—must be alive, in a higher and nobler way. The principle in this plant comes from that one. The higher principle is one and universal; the plant principles here are many and particular. Every plant in this world is particular and comes from that universal plant. Whatever someone seeks in the plant world, they find in that universal plant.[^V02-138]

“If this is so, then if this plant is alive, the higher plant must be even more alive, because it is the first and true plant. This plant is second or third in the order of plants: it is an image of that first plant.[^V02-139] It lives through the life that the first plant gives it.

“As for the earth there, whether it is alive or dead, we will know once we know what this earth is, since it is an image of that one. We say that this earth has a kind of life and an active principle. If the earth we know through our senses, which is only an image, is alive, then the earth in the world of intellect must be alive all the more. It is the first earth; this one is a second earth, like it.

“Everything in the higher world is light, because it is in the highest light. Each thing there sees everything in the being of the others.[^V02-140] So everything is in everything, and the whole is in each one. Each one is the whole. The light that shines on them has no end. That is why each one of them is vast.”

That is the end of these luminous words and noble statements, transmitted by ʿAbd al-Masīḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥimṣī and edited by Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq al-Kindī.

These passages clearly affirm Platonic Forms. They say that created things have luminous intellectual forms in the world of intellect, and that a person receives these forms when understanding general ideas. But because we are tied to the body and its obscuring effects, our understanding of them is incomplete, like seeing something far away through dusty, cloudy air.

For this reason, we take realities that are pure existence and light—each distinct and clear in itself—for vague general ideas that might apply to many things. It is like seeing a shape in the distance and wondering whether it is a person, a horse, or a tree. Here, its location makes the shape a particular object, so we know it is one thing among several kinds, though we do not know which one. In the intellectual world there is no location, so what we understand may seem to apply to many individuals.

In short, something seems general when its existence appears weak or faint. That may be because it is weak in itself or because the person perceiving it cannot grasp it clearly. This is what happens with realities that are not material objects we know through the senses. Our power of understanding is too weak to grasp luminous realities and clear intellectual forms directly. Because we are tied to the world of matter and darkness, we see them through a veil or haze.

To sum up: ancient philosophers and their followers said there is a world of intellect or imagination that mirrors the sensory world in its kinds of substances and qualities. Some understood it as a shadowy, spatial world of images. Others said these were forms in God’s knowledge, existing in His own being, as Aristotle’s followers hold. On the ancient view, to say that the Forms exist on their own means that they exist in God, whose existence gives them a firmer basis than they have in themselves. In themselves, they are possible; in relation to the One who sustains them, they must exist.

Some say these words point to the ruler of each kind, the spirit that governs it. Each bodily kind has such a ruler: an Intellect that gives it order and a light that guides it. The rulers are not “universal” because the concept of each one can be said of many individuals; they do not apply to the separate bodies and images as a shared concept would. They are called universal because their care and outpouring of life reach every individual of their kind in the same way. Others say the words refer to bodily forms in this world, considered as they appear before the First Creator. They are fully present to Him and never hidden from His knowledge. In that respect, they are like forms free of matter, time, bodily features, and material veils. God knows them directly, as He knows general ideas and separate realities.

I think[^V02-141] the most likely view is that those great philosophers and the greatest saints—who had seen through nature’s veils and reached the goal of human life—taught that forms of things exist on their own, apart from matter. Thinkers and mystics come to know these forms through reason and spiritual insight.[^V02-142] These forms must exist. They either exist on their own or depend on someone who knows them. The second option cannot be right, or they would disappear whenever that person stopped attending to them, especially if that person were a soul free of the body. And no one has explained how they could exist inside the knower.

Someone might say that when the soul is not aware of these forms, they still exist in a separate intellect—a storehouse of forms the mind can know. The soul knows them when it connects with this intellect, turns its attention to it, and is ready to receive the forms that flow from it.

We answer: setting aside the problems with saying that true forms are placed in the soul,[^V02-143] this view would require a separate intellect to contain many different forms that stand at the same level. That is impossible. Higher realities cannot receive their forms from lower things, since that would mean that what is higher is changed and completed by what is lower. The early philosophers rejected this even more strongly than Aristotle’s followers. So the forms would have to flow from something higher. That leaves two possibilities: either many things flow from the one pure source, or the forms of things exist in God’s own being. Philosophers of the Illuminationist school reject both ideas.[^V02-144]

On this view, when the soul knows itself and examines its own nature, it must also know where these forms exist and how they exist there. To know a thing is to have it present to you, not hidden. So the place where these known forms exist cannot itself be hidden. Nor can the way they exist there—the very thing that is supposed to explain their existence.

So there must be general realities that exist on their own rather than as features of something else, and that correspond to material individuals. They can apply to many things because we consider them without deciding whether they are separate from matter or attached to it. When we mentally set aside the matter, individual boundaries, and sensory qualities of material things, we reach a shared nature. Like forms in the intellect, this nature is general. This account avoids the problems already raised. The familiar arguments against a universal existing as universal outside the mind apply just as much to one said to exist within the mind.[^V02-145]

#### A Difficulty and Its Answer

Avicenna gives this argument in the metaphysics section of *The Healing* against Forms and mathematical shapes that exist apart from matter. Suppose a mathematical shape exists separately from the shape we sense. Then either there are no mathematical shapes in the world we sense, or there are. If there are none, we could not see squares or circles. But if we had never sensed such shapes, how could we prove that they exist—or even begin to imagine them? We form these images from things we have sensed. If we imagined someone who had never sensed any of them, we would say, as the saying goes, that losing a sense means losing the knowledge it gives: that person could not imagine, or even understand, such things.[^V02-146]

But we have shown that many mathematical shapes can be found among things we sense.[^V02-147] So the nature of these shapes exists in the things we perceive. Considered in itself, that nature must either match the separate shape in its definition and meaning, or differ from it. If it differs, then the shapes we can understand are not the same as those we imagine or grasp through our senses. We would need a new proof to show that the separate shapes exist, and only after that could we ask whether they exist apart from matter.

We cannot avoid this by simply assuming there is no need to prove that separate shapes exist and moving straight to the question of how they are separate.

But if the nature we sense and the separate shape match in definition, there are two possibilities. Either the shapes we sense are material simply because of their nature. Then how could a shape with the very same nature exist apart from matter? Or these shapes become material because something is added to them, and their nature does not prevent this. In that case, separate shapes could become material, and material shapes could exist apart from matter. That contradicts the theory.

Also, the sensed shapes that have extra features must depend on separate shapes either because of what they are or because something has happened to them. If they depend on separate shapes by their very nature, then the separate shapes would also depend on whatever makes them exist. If they need separate shapes only because of some added feature, then without that feature they would not need separate shapes at all. How, then, could separate shapes exist?[^V02-148] And if the material shapes do not depend on separate ones, the separate shapes cannot be their causes or first principles in any way. They would then be less complete than the material shapes, since a material shape can have powers and actions that its separate counterpart does not. What a difference there is between a bare outline of a human and the form of a living, acting person![^V02-149]

That is Avicenna’s argument, word for word.

I say: the first argument rests on the claim that individuals of one and the same reality, with one definition and essence, cannot differ in being separate from or joined to matter, self-sufficient or needy, grasped by the intellect or known through the senses. His argument works only for natures whose instances are all equal, not for natures that can exist at different levels. It also assumes that an essence—or something essential to it—cannot differ in completeness across its instances.[^V02-150]

We have discussed this already, since it is the central question in this section. Many of the earlier philosophers’ rules allow for one and the same nature to be complete and self-sufficient in one instance, yet incomplete and needy in another. This difference comes from the nature’s own level; no separate cause has to come between what it is and its need or self-sufficiency. One instance is self-sufficient by its own nature; another is needy by its own nature. Whoever brings the needy instance into existence brings it into existence as needy; no further act is needed. It depends on a maker because of what it is, not because someone has simply applied the word “needy” to it.

The second argument assumes that two instances of one nature cannot differ so that one is a cause and the other an effect. If one instance were caused by another of the same kind, that other instance would also have to be caused by a third, and so on, until we reached an impossible circle or endless chain. This argument, too, depends on denying that one nature can have different levels: earlier and later, first and not first, self-sufficient and needy.

Know that Plato, the Persian sages, and the ancient Greeks are said to have taught that the natures of kinds exist apart from matter, and that the basic forms of natural bodies exist on their own. But they held that mathematical shapes and quantities always exist in matter, even if we can define them without mentioning matter. In their view, no spatial extension exists on its own, apart from matter. They argue that if an extension did exist apart from matter, it would have to be either limited or unlimited.[^V02-151] The second option is impossible, as will be shown when we discuss why quantities—whether at rest or changing—cannot be unlimited.[^V02-152]

Avicenna gives a different reason in *The Healing*. If an extension were unlimited because it existed apart from matter by its very nature, then every extension should be unlimited. Or, if it were unlimited because it was separate from matter, matter would have to be what gives it its limit and shape. Both ideas are impossible.

The first option—an extension that is limited—is also impossible. If a separate extension had a fixed limit and shape, something outside its nature would have had to change it. But, as you will learn, change belongs to matter itself. The extension would then depend on matter, even though we assumed it was separate. That is a contradiction.

Although Avicenna used these two arguments against those who say mathematical shapes exist apart from matter, they also work, by his own reasoning, against Forms that exist apart from matter. We have included them and answered them in a way that fits the view of those who believe in Platonic Forms.

Here is a hint from the world of light. If you are ready to receive divine secrets and true knowledge, you may see that every power, fullness, form, and beauty in this lower world is truly a shadow and reflection of something in the higher world. These realities came down and took on material form, though they were once pure and free of flaws, darkness, sickness, weakness, and death.

All created forms and beings are traces and lights of true existence, the sustaining Light. It is the source of absolute beauty and perfect greatness, which gives form to all that we love. The beauty of spiritual and bodily beings is only a drop beside that ocean, a speck beside that sun of greatness and glory.

Without its lights shining through the forms we see, we could not reach the Light of Lights, God’s absolute existence. When the soul is captivated by a worldly beloved, who is in one respect a true sign, it turns toward the true, absolute Beloved—the one on whom everything depends and to whom every living thing turns for refuge.[^V02-153] It turns toward His gracious presence, the source of lights and the origin of all effects. There the soul arrives in God’s presence and is lit from within by His light. It comes to know general realities and Forms beyond matter because it has become an intellect able to understand them.

Consider how the traces of His light appear in the visible world. They have descended from their spiritual, intellectual levels and now appear as individual things, beautiful, delicate, and graceful. Although physical darkness has weakened and thickened them, they still fill minds with wonder and leave those who seek them amazed and troubled. What, then, of absolute beauty itself, the shining divine light in all its greatness and perfection, which lies beyond seventy thousand veils of light and darkness? A report from the Prophet says that God has seventy thousand veils of light and darkness; if they were lifted, the radiance of His face would burn whatever His sight reached of creation.[^V02-154]

Learn, too, from the report that this fire of Hell was washed with water seventy times before it was sent down. Think of light in the same way: its brightness is hidden from us by veils.[^V02-155] Without those veils, the world could not exist or be experienced; it would be burned away and vanish in the blaze.

The heat of physical fire follows the kind of thing it is, and is a spark from God’s invisible power after that power has passed through many levels. One example is its appearance in the soul as anger. Intense anger can burn the body’s fluids, even though they are moist, more than a flame burns wood. This shows that not everything that warms something must itself be physically hot. In the same way, the fires and stars we see are shadows and images of God’s gentle, nonphysical lights, and traces of the lights of His unseen realm and the rays of His beauty as they descend through the worlds of command and creation.

#### An Awakening for the Mind

Anyone whose heart and mind have been lit by the principles discussed here can see that our attraction to bodies, beautiful forms, and material adornments has a deeper purpose. It is meant to wake us from forgetfulness and ignorance, and to train us to move from bodily things and material idols toward spiritual beauty, the virtues of the intellect, and divine lights. It guides us toward knowing our own nature, our noble origin, the beauty of our true world, and the good that awaits us when we return.

All the beauty, virtue, adornment, and desirable things we see on the surfaces of bodies are only colors, patterns, and impressions. The beings that govern each kind, and the angels that guide them, have formed these appearances in matter and the elements to draw the soul’s attention. When souls see them, they long for them and set out to find them. By looking closely, they learn to separate what is pure from what is mixed, and the kernel from its clouded shell.

By drawing general truths from individual things and discovering realities of the intellect through the senses, they learn to leave this world for the next.

All this trains the soul to picture these forms and beauties within itself and become joined to them. When bodily individuals disappear from the senses, the beloved images remain present within the soul: spiritual, clear, lasting, and joined to it in meaning, with no risk of separation or change. The soul no longer needs reports or descriptions; it sees for itself. It loses its desire to meet fleeting appearances and escapes the chains of change and time.

Anyone who has loved someone, lost sight of that person for a time, then met them again will understand. The person may have changed from the beauty remembered. But if the lover turns inward to the forms and images preserved in the soul from long ago,[^V02-156] they remain unchanged. The soul sees them as they were: not faded or spoiled, but lasting because their source and sustaining cause remain.

Then the soul finds within its own nature what it had been seeking outside itself. It turns away from the outward person and understands that the beautiful features it saw were not imprisoned in that person or confined to them. They had been pictured in the soul itself and remained there unchanged. That person was only a sign of them, like other bodily images that point to the lights of the intellect and reveal luminous meanings.

When a wise person reflects on this, the soul wakes from its sleep of forgetfulness, finds its own nature, and becomes independent of what is outside it. It rests from the pain and struggle of those who love bodies and material things: money, jewels, property, gardens, trees, fruit, and all that fades and passes away. God says: “The lasting good deeds are better with your Lord for reward and better for hope.”

When the soul wakes from forgetfulness, opens its inner eye, sees its own world, and knows its beginning and return, it understands that bodily pleasures and material beauty are only reflections of intellectual virtues and imagined forms of spiritual lights. They have no independent reality. They are like a mirage in a desert: the thirsty person thinks it is water, but when they reach it, they find nothing—and find God there instead.

#### A Merciful Illumination

In His mercy and kindness, the Creator made all bodily, sensory things signs that point to spiritual realities. He made the senses like steps and ladders by which the soul can climb toward knowledge of the intellect. This is the soul’s highest purpose in entering the world of the senses and rising beyond matter.

Just as sensory things depend on intellectual realities, as drops and shadows of their light, the soul is needy when it knows bodily things. Knowledge of intellectual realities is its wealth and joy. To know bodily things, the soul needs the body and its tools. But once bodily senses have led it toward spiritual realities, its own nature is enough to know them. When it has learned in this way and becomes a knower in its own right, it no longer needs the senses or the body.[^V02-157]

My dear, seek lasting wealth through this body and its tools while you can, before time runs out, life ends, and the body falls apart. Be careful not to leave your soul poor and dependent on a body for joy and completion. Otherwise, you may say: “If only we could return and do good, unlike what we did before.” Or your soul may remain in the intermediate world until the day it is raised, unable to sense, while it is still distracted, heedless, and drawn to bodily desires, natural adornments, and false hopes in this condemned life of the senses.

Those who do not know these realities of the intellect, or the beings that govern their images in the higher and lower worlds of imagination, never rise beyond what the senses show them. They know nothing else. God says of them: “They were content with the life of this world and felt secure in it, and they were heedless of Our signs.” That is, they ignore the afterlife and the home of joy, where the souls of the good rise after leaving their bodies. The Qur'an says: “To Him rises the good word, and good deeds lift it up.” The good word is the believer’s spirit; good deeds are the knowledge of the intellect, which draws the soul toward these realities and lifts it up to them.

### Stage Five — Unity and Multiplicity: Sameness, Difference, and Opposites

#### Chapter 1 — One and Many

Know that unity accompanies existence wherever it goes.[^V02-158] Both apply equally to things: whatever is said to exist is also said to be one. Unity also shares existence’s stronger and weaker degrees. The stronger a thing’s existence, the more complete its unity. In most ways, the two go together. This may lead us to think that they mean the same thing. They apply to the same things, but they do not mean the same thing. So it makes sense to discuss unity and what belongs to it, such as identity, sameness in kind, likeness, agreement, equality, and resemblance. We must also discuss multiplicity and its features, such as otherness and difference. In fact, more needs to be said about multiplicity, because unity is simple while multiplicity has many forms and branches.[^V02-159]

We have said that unity resembles existence in most ways. For example, like other ideas as broad as existence, it cannot be defined without either defining a thing by itself or reasoning in a circle. One definition says that a thing is one when it cannot be divided in the respect in which it is one. This defines a thing by itself, and it is circular. The idea of division in the definition already means multiplicity. Multiplicity, in turn, is defined as a collection of units. But “collection” already means multiplicity, and “units” already means one.[^V02-160] So this definition uses multiplicity to explain unity and unity to explain multiplicity. It has both flaws in reverse. The other definitions people have offered have the same problem. In truth, the ideas of one and many are so basic that they need no definition. Still, one point needs our attention: imagination grasps multiplicity first, while reason grasps unity first. Both ideas arise in the mind without instruction, but multiplicity first appears in imagination. What appears there comes through the senses, and what we sense is many. Unity is an idea grasped by reason. The first things reason handles are general ideas. As soon as reason begins to sort them, it sees each as one, then divides it into this and that, and into what it is and what it is not. We can therefore explain multiplicity through unity as a rational definition, taking unity as an idea the mind grasps first. We can also use multiplicity to draw attention to unity: we point to an idea reason grasps first by saying what is absent from it. In the first case, we explain an idea of imagination through an idea of reason. In the second, we draw attention to an idea of reason by using an idea of imagination. This way of speaking avoids circular reasoning.[^V02-161]

We can say that the one is what is not divided in the respect in which it is one. The words “in the respect” are needed to include things that are one only in some way. Without them, such a thing would not fit the definition, because it can be divided in another respect. With them, it does fit, because it remains undivided in at least one respect. The word “one” may refer to unity itself: a thing considered simply as one, just as we speak of existence simply as existence. This is the clearest sense of unity. Or it may refer to something other than unity itself.[^V02-162] That second kind can be genuine or relative. Several things can be one in a relative sense when they share something. What they share may be part of what they are, a quality they have, or simply a relation—like the relation of a soul to its body or a king to a city.[^V02-163]

The shared feature may be the general kind they belong to. This is called being one by kind: a human and a horse both belong to the animal kind.[^V02-164] Or it may be their specific kind. This is called being one by type: Zayd and Amr are both human and rational. The shared feature may also be something said of them, as when cotton and snow are both white, or the same subject they belong to, as when a writer and a person who laughs are both human.[^V02-165] When things share a feature, we use different words according to the kind of feature: sharing a type is likeness; sharing a general kind is being of the same kind; sharing a quality is resemblance; sharing an amount is equality; sharing a position is matching; and sharing a relation is correspondence. It is clear that the basis of relative unity is true unity: here, a thing that is one by itself, though the term “true unity” is best reserved for what cannot be divided at all, such as God. In the broader sense, this true unity may be unity by kind or by type.

There is also numerical unity, meaning one individual. It may be indivisible in the world outside the mind, or divisible. What is divisible may be one by continuity: it can be divided in principle into parts that share the same nature. A size or length is divided in itself; a simple body, such as water or air, is divided because a size belongs to it. Matter makes the division possible and lets us mark its parts. Or a thing may be one by composition: it has several parts that are already distinct. Such a thing is one by being gathered together. If it contains all the parts it can have, it is a complete whole; if not, it is many, and people call it “not one.” Completeness may come from arrangement, as in a single coin; from workmanship, as in a finished house; or from nature, as in a human being whose body has all its parts. A straight line can always be extended, so it is never complete in length. A circle, by contrast, surrounds its center on every side and is complete.

An individual that cannot be divided in the outside world—not potentially, like a continuous thing, or in actual parts, like a composite—may either have a position, like a point, or no position, like an individual intellect or soul. Every being is more noble as unity becomes stronger in it, though nothing that exists is entirely without unity. Even the number ten is one as ten. The farther something is from multiplicity, the more noble and complete it is. And as numbers grow larger, their share of unity becomes smaller. The thing that most deserves the name “one” is true unity. The most complete kind of it is what cannot be divided in any way—not in size or definition, not in possibility or actuality—and whose existence is not separate from what it is. Next comes what cannot be divided in size, either potentially or actually, even if the mind can divide its definition into parts, as in the intellects and individual souls described by the philosophers in Aristotle’s school. And what cannot be divided into separate individuals is more truly one, like an intellect, than what is divided into individuals, like the human soul.[^V02-166]

Unity by continuity includes a single line or a body of water. It can be divided into parts that share the same nature. Some continuous things cannot be split by breaking or cutting, like the celestial spheres. They deserve the name “one” more than things that can be split, such as bodies and sizes in the lower world. A celestial body also cannot be divided into individual examples of its kind, though it is made of matter and form. It is therefore more one than compound things in the lower world, which can be divided both into individuals and into matter and form.[^V02-167] Sizes are divided in the opposite way: they can be divided into individual examples, but not into matter and form.[^V02-168]

The strongest kind of unity by composition is natural unity, as in one human being, made up of a soul with many powers and a body whose elements, organs, skin, and bones are joined together. The unity of the body reflects the unity of the soul, just as the body’s existence reflects the soul’s. Numerical unity is more complete than unity by type, because unity by type exists in the mind; it is also more complete than unity by general kind, which is even more vague and only in the mind. The farther a general kind is from individual things, the weaker its unity becomes. So “one” applies to different things by degrees, just as “existence” does. The many is what stands against the one in all its meanings.

Unity, like existence, is not part of the nature of any thing. I do not mean that unity is separate from existence, since in our view unity is not separate from existence. But when you understand “human” and “one,” you do not automatically understand that a human being must be one. So being one is not part of what it means to be human; it is something that goes with being human. To understand how unity is additional to a thing’s nature, consider what we have already said about how existence applies to things. Keep this point in mind.

**A further indication.** The way one repeated gives rise to numbers is an example of how God brings creation into being by appearing in the forms of things. The levels of number are an example of how God’s existence, beauty, and perfection appear in created things. One is half of two, a third of three, a quarter of four, and so on. This is an example of the relations that necessarily connect God, who exists in Himself, with beings that depend on Him. A number appears through the things counted; in the same way, dependent beings appear through the natures they take on. Some of these beings are sensed, others understood by reason, just as some numbers are grasped through the senses and others through the mind. It is remarkable that, although number is so different from unity and every number has its own nature and features, when we look closely we find nothing in it or in its different levels but unity.[^V02-169]

No matter which number we consider, we keep affirming at one level what we deny at another. We say that one is not a number, as mathematicians and careful thinkers agree. Yet one is the basis of number: when repeated, it produces numbers, each with its own features and effects. We can also say that every number is only a group of ones, and at the same time say it is more than a group of ones. Each number is a distinct kind with features not found in the others, so it must have something beyond the ones gathered in it.[^V02-170] We keep affirming what we deny and denying what we affirm. This same mystery appears in what those who know God say: God is above the limits of created things, and even above the perfections of the created world, yet He is reflected in creation. Creation remains distinct from its Creator through its dependence and weakness, while its Creator is independent and exalted.[^V02-171]

**Objection and reminder.** Do not listen to those who say that unity is only an idea we form in the mind, and not something real. They argue that if unity existed, it would need another unity to make it one, and that unity would need yet another, without end. Answer them by remembering what we have already said: the reality of unity, in being one, does not need another unity to make it one. The mind can consider unity to be one at a later stage of thought; it can then consider the unity of that unity, and so on. The mind’s thoughts do not stop, but that does not mean there is an actual endless chain of things. These are different: the first is possible, the second is not.

In short, the word “unity” is used in technical writing for two different things. First, it can mean the state of being one. This is an idea in the mind, not something that exists outside it. Second, it can mean what makes a thing one in itself and keeps multiplicity from arising within it. This second meaning involves the absence of multiplicity; the first follows from the absence of multiplicity. Unity as an idea is a shadow of real, original unity. We draw it from unity itself when we consider unity on its own, and from other things because they are connected to and depend on unity. We have seen many times that real unity, the identity of an individual thing, and real existence—not just the idea of existence—are one in themselves, though we think about them in different ways.[^V02-172]

**Objection and reply.** You might say that unity and identity are different. A continuous body that has not been divided is one individual. Once it is split, it becomes many. Its identity remains, but its unity is gone. Since what remains and what vanishes cannot be the same, identity cannot be unity. We answer that the unity of continuity is the same as the identity of a continuous thing. When this unity leaves the body, the identity of that continuity ends and two new continuities begin. So the loss of unity in a continuous thing is the loss of that thing’s identity as one continuous whole.

If you reply, “Suppose that continuous form has ended. The bodily matter remains through division and joining. It can be one at one time and many at another, yet it exists in both states,” we answer: bodily matter is a substance that can receive forms. In itself, it is only able to receive them. Unity by continuity and the multiplicity that opposes it arise in matter, but matter itself is not defined by either of them. Its identity therefore remains when either unity or multiplicity ends, unlike the identity of an extended body. In itself, matter has only the potential to take on forms and a unity that comes from its ability to receive them. These remain through all its states. Its unity and multiplicity arise from outside its own nature, as features that happen to it. A person of sound judgment will see that a thing cannot, by its own identity, be the bearer of both unity and multiplicity: the identity of a thing does not accept being divided into many.[^V02-173]

**Clarification.** You may have read in books on this subject, such as *The Healing*, that unity differs from existence.[^V02-174] The argument is this: many things, considered as many, exist, but nothing considered as many is one. So not everything that exists is one simply because it exists. Unity must therefore differ from existence. The claim that a group of things has its own unity and a special feature, distinct from the group itself, does not change this argument: multiplicity applies to the group, while unity applies to its feature.

We answer: if by “the many” in these statements you mean the thing considered in terms of its definition, as in our earlier discussion of what belongs to a thing by its nature, then the first claim is false. The many in that sense is neither existent nor nonexistent. If you mean a group that exists in reality, then the second claim is false. Such a group exists, but it is also one in some respect, because everything has some unity. Someone might still say that unity applies to the multiplicity itself, not to the thing that is many. For example, ten applies to a body, while unity applies to ten as ten. Here there are two things: multiplicity and what has multiplicity. Multiplicity belongs to the body, while unity belongs to that multiplicity. So the unity of the multiplicity does not conflict with multiplicity, because they apply to different things. By contrast, the unity of the body that has multiplicity does conflict with its multiplicity when both are considered at the same time. But it does not conflict with the body’s existence. This shows a difference between existence and unity.[^V02-175]

Unity, like existence, has different forms.[^V02-176] Each particular kind of unity is opposed to a particular kind of multiplicity, and unity in general is opposed to multiplicity in general. A particular kind of existence—whether in the mind or outside it—is opposed to the matching kind of nonexistence, while nonexistence in general is opposed to existence in general. The point is that some kind of unity always goes with some kind of existence, however we consider it.

What we have said does not prove that unity in general differs from existence. The multiplicity opposed to unity in general does not exist on its own, since every existing thing has some unity, even if only in the way we think about it. Take a group of ten men, for example. Considered as a group of ten, it has no existence beyond the existence of the individual men, except as an idea in the mind. This is the correct view: every thing that exists outside the mind must have its own unity outside the mind. If this were not so, none of the usual divisions of things would hold. For example, a thing made of substance and quality would be neither substance nor quality, and would have to belong to a new category. The same problem would arise with other combinations, and the number of categories would keep growing. The same reasoning applies when a general kind is divided into types. We have therefore learned that “the many,” considered as many, exists only as an idea, just as the mind can also consider it one.[^V02-177]

You may object that we mean a statement in which “many” is itself the subject: something that is many, precisely because it is many, exists in some way, and cannot be one in the sense that opposes multiplicity. Unity conflicts with multiplicity, but existence does not. We answer that if you mean multiplicity in general, the first claim is false. If you mean a particular kind of multiplicity, your conclusion only says that this kind conflicts with its matching kind of unity. It does not conflict with every other kind of unity. So it shows only that one kind of existence differs from one kind of unity, which causes no problem. The same answer applies if you say that being many does not prevent something from existing. Keep this distinction in mind, and do not lose your footing after you have found it.

#### Chapter 2 — Sameness and Its Opposite

You have learned that one kind of unity belongs to many things because they share a meaning. Sameness[^V02-178] means that two things are joined in existence, though they differ in some way. They may share an existence in the world outside the mind or in the mind.[^V02-179] Sometimes both exist in themselves. For example, when we say “Zayd is human,” the existence we attribute to Zayd is the same existence we attribute to being human. Sometimes one exists in itself while the other exists only as a feature of it. For example, in “a human being is a writer,” the human being exists in itself, while being a writer belongs to the person as a feature. The two are joined through one existence, which belongs directly to the subject and indirectly to the feature. Or neither exists in itself: both may exist only as features of something else. For example, when we say “the writer is moving,” writing and movement both belong to a person. What joins them is the person’s existence, which belongs directly to neither of them. So the shared existence in sameness may belong to both things, to one of them, or to something outside both.

#### A Note to Clarify the Point

You know that unity through continuity is a kind of true unity. So something that is one by continuity must have one underlying subject. It cannot be divided into different forms, only into parts that share the same nature and exist only potentially, as we will explain later. When unity is actual, multiplicity is only potential. So it is a foolish claim that the parts of one continuous thing could have different forms.[^V02-180]

But someone may object: your definition of sameness seems to mean that one part of a continuous length must be the same as another part, or the same as the whole. You could say, for example, “This half of the cubit is the other half,” “This half is the whole cubit,” or “This half is part of it.” After all, if two things share an existence, that alone seems to make it true to say one of them of the other—even when their multiplicity exists only in our imagination or by assumption.[^V02-181]

Some answer this by saying that two things must differ in meaning but share an existence before we can say one of them of the other. A specific part differs from another part as an individual, they say, but not by its nature as a continuous quantity. That nature does not mark out one segment as a distinct part from the rest. This answer does not work, because we often say one particular thing of another: “Zayd is the son of Amr,” or “Zayd is this writer.”

Others say that, in general, saying one thing of another depends on their sharing an existence. But in ordinary speech, we use it only when there is no difference in their arrangement. Ordinary speech has also narrowed the broader idea of unity to unity in existence. For saying one thing of another, they add, we need both some difference and some unity. If there were only unity, we could not make a statement; if there were only multiplicity, the statement would not hold.[^V02-182]

A leading scholar tried to refute this answer. First, he said, this restriction is not suited to philosophy. Second, the issue is not how people use the word “predication,” but whether a continuous length and one of its parts are the same in reality because they share an existence. If so, we should be able to say that the whole is its part. For sameness is existence, and saying one thing of another is sameness in existence. So this restriction cannot be explained as a matter of words or convention. It must mean that other kinds of unity cannot, by themselves, make a statement true in reality.[^V02-183]

He then tried to untangle the problem. In statements about what a thing is, we must attribute its own existence to it directly, rather than treating it merely as a part of one larger thing. In statements about features, we must attribute the existence of the thing to the feature that describes it, rather than because the two are parts of one continuous whole. A part of a measured length may share the whole’s existence, but it exists only as a part of that whole. It is not a separate thing that happens to share its existence, as in statements about kinds and qualities. That is why no statement of this kind applies between the whole and its measured part.

There is a problem with this explanation. Being a part of something does prevent us from saying that the part and whole are the same, but not because they are too much alike, beyond the unity a statement requires. It is because they differ in a way that breaks that unity. Saying one thing of another requires both to share an existence and to differ in meaning, even if the difference exists only in how we consider them. A part and its whole differ in the opposite way: their existence differs, because the part must exist before the whole can be formed.[^V02-184]

The brief answer is this. A continuous thing that is one, so long as it has not been divided in any way—by cutting or breaking in the outside world, by imagination or thought in the mind, or by a difference between two qualities—has no real difference within it. Nothing else can be said of it, even as a matter of thought. Once any kind of division takes place, two identities arise and the earlier one ends, as we will explain later. Duality then enters existence. Where, then, is the single identity that could belong to two things? That shared identity is what makes predication possible.

#### Chapter 3 — Why Two Things Cannot Become One

For two things to unite would mean that two natures become one. But after the supposed union, either both still exist—in which case there are still two, not one—or only one exists, in which case one has disappeared and the other remains. Or neither exists, so both have disappeared and a third thing has come into being. In none of these cases have the two become one, as the claim requires.

People who allow that two things can unite are misled by things they see. Several similar or different bodies may seem to become one body through contact or mixture. For example, water may be gathered into one vessel, or vinegar and sugar mixed to make one sweet-and-sour drink. They also see water and air seem to become one air through boiling and natural change. Or they see a body that had two qualities come to have one. But none of these cases involves two things becoming one in their true nature. We speak of them as one by a loose use of words, treating the underlying matter as though it were the thing itself for some purposes.

What some ancient philosophers said about the soul joining the Active Intellect as it reaches its full development, and what Sufis say about a knower joining with God, refers to a spiritual state suited to beings beyond the body. It does not mean bodily contact or mixing, or that either being’s identity is destroyed. You will learn about this kind of state later in this book, in its proper place.[^V02-185]

#### Chapter 4 — Some Rules About Unity and Multiplicity

Unity is not a number, even though number is made from it. Number is a quantity that can be divided; unity cannot. If someone calls unity a number, they may mean anything that can be counted. If so, there is no disagreement: the difference is only in how the word is used. Unity is the starting point of number, because number can be made only from units, not from other numbers. If ten were made from numbers other than units, there would be no reason to choose one grouping over another. Five and five would be no more fitting than six and four, or seven and three. Nor can ten be made from all these groupings at once, because its definition would then repeat its essential parts. Each grouping would be enough on its own, so the others would be unnecessary. And if we say that ten is made from what all these groupings have in common, rather than from their different parts, we have admitted the point at issue: what they have in common is units.[^V02-186]

Here is further evidence. We can understand any number fully while not thinking about the numbers below it, so those lower numbers are not part of its nature. What makes each number is repeated unity. One plus one makes two; one plus two makes three. In this way, an endless number of kinds can be made by adding one each time. The process has no final step: there is no number with no larger number beyond it.

Most people say that the different levels of number have different natures because they have different properties: whether they are rational or irrational, how they share or differ in their factors, whether one divides another, whether they are squares or cubes, and related features. Different properties point to differences in what has those properties. This supports what we said earlier about existence: different realities differ because each occupies its own level. Just as being the number after two is what makes three what it is, with properties not found in the numbers before or after it, each level of existence has meanings not found at other levels. Unity considered without qualification corresponds to existence in general. Pure unity, which comes before all levels of number, corresponds to the Necessary Being, God, the source of all existence, directly or through other causes. The special features drawn from each level of number correspond to the natures joined to each level of existence. And just as numbers differ through the very units they share, beings differ through their own identities while sharing the fact that they exist.[^V02-187]

On this account, we can say that numbers differ in kind because each number has meanings and features that come from what it is—just as different natures come from different levels of existence. We can also say that numbers do not differ in kind, because they differ only in how many units they contain. A difference in the number of parts alone does not make two things different kinds. As for the claim that different properties prove differences in what bears them, this proves only that there is some difference. It may be a difference in kind, or a difference in strength and weakness, completeness and incompleteness, as we explained earlier.

#### Chapter 5 — Opposition

We have said that unity and multiplicity each have features of their own, as well as features shared with their opposites. These include unity,[^V02-188] sharing, being present in a subject, and relations to a subject, though the details differ on each side. One feature belonging to unity is sameness in the broad sense: anything that joins things or gives them a shared meaning. Its opposite, otherness, belongs to multiplicity. One kind of otherness is opposition, which has four forms: affirmation and denial, having and lacking, contrary qualities, and paired relations such as father and son. What do we mean by opposition? The broad opposite of sameness is otherness. Things can differ in their general kind, their specific kind, their distinguishing feature, or an incidental feature,[^V02-189] just as they can be joined in these ways. When a thing differs from another in an essential feature, we call it a contrary.

Difference in the identity or number of individuals is called “other” in one technical use.[^V02-190] Terms such as being alike, belonging to the same kind, resembling, and being equal also describe how several things share a feature. They depend on multiplicity, so it is more accurate to treat them as features of the many. In each case, the shared feature is a kind of likeness. Two things are alike when they share the same specific nature. A human and a horse are not alike as human and horse, but they share the general kind “animal”; the animal nature in each belongs to the same specific kind. A general kind is what several definitions share when we consider it without the features that distinguish them. If we take away those distinguishing features, the examples of that kind resemble one another. The same applies to the other forms of relative unity: resemblance is likeness in quality, equality is likeness in quantity, and so on. The shared feature in likeness exists in the mind: we form a general idea by setting aside the material details that distinguish individuals. This makes unity in likeness weaker than the many things that exist outside the mind.

Opposition is narrower than difference. Some different things can exist together in the same subject. But some differences between kinds make it impossible for them to exist in one subject.[^V02-191] Opposition is when two different things cannot exist in one subject at the same time and in the same respect.[^V02-192] The word “different” rules out things that are alike. The condition that they cannot share a subject rules out differences that can coexist, such as heat and whiteness. The phrase “in one subject” rules out cases where opposites exist at the same time but in different subjects, such as blackness in one person and whiteness in another. The phrase “in the same respect” rules out paired relations that can belong to one person from different sides: someone can be both a father and a son, but not in the same relationship.[^V02-193]

The condition “at the same time” also matters. Two contrary qualities may come one after another in a subject, even though they are together in the timeless order of reality. Their being together at that level does not mean that they exist in the subject at the same moment. In the same way, not being together in one place does not rule out being together in another setting, such as time. Some say that the time condition is unnecessary because things cannot come together except at the same time. That is wrong: this view still sees everything through the narrow lens of time and change.

I have chosen to define opposition itself, rather than define two opposing things as “things that cannot come together in one subject.” That wording suggests that both sides are real things in their own right. But affirmation and denial, or having and lacking, do not each name a separate thing. One could reply that denial and lack exist as ideas in the mind. Even so, there is a reason these four forms of opposition are different. If one side is the absence of the other, then, when that absence is understood in relation to a subject that can have what is missing, the pair is having and lacking. When no such subject is involved, the pair is affirmation and denial. If neither side is the absence of the other, then either each can be understood only in relation to the other—in which case they are paired relations—or not, in which case they are contrary qualities.

There is another way to divide the four forms. The two sides are either both positive things or not. If both are positive, then either each is understood only in relation to the other, making them paired relations, or they are not, making them contraries. If they are not both positive, then one is something present and the other its absence. If the absence is understood in relation to a subject that could have the positive thing, the pair is having and lacking; if not, it is affirmation and denial. This second division faces two objections.[^V02-194]

First, both sides might be absences, as in blindness and not-blindness, which seem to be affirmation and denial. Someone might answer that not-blindness simply means sight, making the pair one of having and lacking. But that is wrong. We can understand sight without thinking about blindness, while understanding “not blind” depends on the idea of sight. So the two meanings are not the same, even if they sometimes apply to the same things. The mistake comes from confusing what belongs to a thing in itself with what belongs to it only in some cases.[^V02-195]

Second, the absence of a feature can oppose the presence of something that necessarily brings that feature with it. For example, a body might move while lacking the heat that is said to follow from its movement. This does not fit either having and lacking or affirmation and denial, because the absent feature is not the positive thing’s direct opposite. We can answer by distinguishing direct opposition from indirect opposition. In this example, heat and its absence are directly opposed. Since the absence of heat also means the movement lacks a feature associated with it, the absence opposes movement only indirectly.

Opposition applies to its four forms by degrees. The strongest is affirmation and denial. Anything incompatible with a thing is either its denial or something that necessarily brings its denial with it. Other than these, things can coexist with it. The denial of a thing conflicts with it because of what the two are. The mind sees this as soon as it considers them, without needing to consider anything else. Something that brings about the denial conflicts with the thing only because it includes that denial. Its conflict is indirect. So direct opposition is the strongest. That is the usual account.[^V02-196]

There is a problem with this account.[^V02-197] On this definition, the two sides of a contradiction would not directly oppose each other in the same way. Denial directly rules out affirmation, but affirmation does not, in that sense, directly rule out denial.[^V02-198] What directly rules out denial is the denial of that denial, which necessarily entails affirmation.[^V02-199] It is better[^V02-200] to take “removing” or “denying” in its general sense as an act,[^V02-201] which may be considered from the side of what acts or from the side of what receives the action.[^V02-202]

#### Chapter 6 — The Kinds of Opposition and the Rules for Each

One kind of opposition is between affirmation and denial. Sometimes this means a contradiction between two statements, as logicians use the term. The statements cannot both be true or both false. For example: “Zayd is white” and “Zayd is not white.”[^V02-203] At other times, it means a contrast between single ideas: whiteness and not-whiteness considered in themselves, or the two ideas as applied to a subject, as when we say that Zayd is white or not white.[^V02-204] If we take any idea by itself and add “not,” we form another idea, very different from the first. In these cases, we need not ask whether either idea is true or false of anything.

When we say that a feature belongs to something, either directly or by using a related adjective, we affirm that the thing has that feature. To deny it is to say that the thing does not have it. The two statements cannot both be true, though both can be false if there is no subject. This is why Avicenna, in *The Healing*, says that simple terms such as “horse” and “not-horse” contrast in one way, while complete statements such as “Zayd is a horse” and “Zayd is not a horse” contrast in another.[^V02-205] Applying both to the same subject at the same time is impossible.[^V02-206] He also says that affirmation means the presence of anything, whether it exists in itself or as a feature of something else; denial means the absence of anything, whether it fails to exist in itself or as a feature of something else. We can see from this that opposition is broader than contradiction. Contradiction, in its strict sense, is between statements that affirm and deny the same thing. Opposition can also occur between single ideas. So it is a mistake to say that contradiction is simply the same as affirmation-and-denial opposition.

The claim in *The Tajrīd* that this opposition belongs to statements and judgments is also mistaken.[^V02-207] The statements do not oppose each other simply because they are statements, or because of their subjects. The opposition comes from affirming and denying the same thing. At its root, it is between affirmation and denial; statements are opposed only because they express them. One might explain this by saying that affirmation and denial are acts of understanding whether something is or is not the case. These are mental acts directed at a relation that is also understood in the mind. When they occur in the mind, they become judgments or beliefs; when we express them in words, they become statements. This account also resolves a dispute about whether opposition requires a subject or a place where something exists. Some say that substantial forms cannot be opposed because they do not exist in a subject; others say that the forms found in matter can be. The disagreement concerns contrary qualities, which take turns in a subject, not opposition in general. Here, “cannot exist together” means that two features cannot both belong to the same subject—not that two statements cannot both be true. Propositions can be opposed: “Every animal is human” contradicts “Some animal is not human,” and it contrasts with “No animal is human.” Avicenna calls the second contrast a kind of contrariety: the two statements cannot both be true, but they can both be false.[^V02-208] Statements, however, do not exist in a subject the way qualities do. The disagreement about subjects and places applies to contraries, which must take turns in one subject, not to opposition in general. If it did, “horse” and “not-horse” would not be opposed, since a horse is not a subject—and that is clearly wrong.

Affirmation and denial exist as opposites only in the mind or, by extension, in speech; they are not two things that exist outside us. Opposition is a relation, and a relation depends on what it relates. Here, one side is denial, which is an idea in the mind expressed in words. So the relation between affirmation and denial is mental. Having and lacking, by contrast, have some basis in reality: lacking is the absence of something from a real subject that could have it. That limited kind of existence is enough for the relation between having and lacking to hold in reality. Every thing has its own level of existence, and relations are ideas drawn from things that exist outside the mind, whether they are essential features such as being human or animal, or incidental ones such as walking or writing.

Another rule is that a subject cannot be without either affirmation or denial in reality, though this does not hold at every level of reality. Things with no essential connection to each other may be neither present nor absent at the level where the other exists. The same applies to incidental features when we consider essences just as they are.[^V02-209] For two statements to contradict each other, they must match in the eight familiar respects, with one more condition for natural statements: the predicate must be used in the same way. Some ideas do not apply to themselves in ordinary speech, even though their negation does; yet they may apply to themselves in a more basic sense. In statements about all or some, the two must also differ in quantity. Otherwise, both universal statements may be false. For example, “Every animal is human” and “No animal is human” are both false, so they are opposed but not contradictory. Their particular forms can both be true: “Some animal is human” and “Some animal is not human.” In modal statements, the two must differ in the right way as well. Otherwise, two statements about what is possible might both be true, or two statements about what is necessary might both be false, even when all the other conditions match. A contradiction has no middle: its two sides cannot both be true or both false. So every thing is either a horse or not a horse.[^V02-210]

A thing may be neither side of the other kinds of opposition. Also, among all opposing pairs, only affirmation and denial can apply to what does not exist. Another kind of opposition is between paired relations. These are both positive realities, and each is understood only in relation to the other, as with fatherhood and sonhood. They cannot both belong to one thing in the same respect, and neither can be understood without the other.[^V02-211]

This raises a question. How can paired relations be one kind of opposition, yet stand alongside contrary qualities as a separate kind? A relation can be broader than opposition or contrariety; it can also include likeness and other forms. Would that make one thing both a kind of a larger group and a separate kind alongside it? One answer is that the idea of “relation” is broader than the ideas of “opposition” and “contrariety,” even though the things described by each idea may relate to the larger group in different ways. In that sense, there is no conflict.[^V02-212]

Another answer is that the idea of relation, taken broadly, is wider than the idea of opposition. But when we consider relation as one kind of opposition, it is narrower. The same is true of a general idea: “universal” covers more than “general kind,” but a universal taken specifically as a general kind is narrower. The best answer is to distinguish a concept from the things it applies to. The concept of a paired relation is one kind of opposition, but the things that opposition applies to may themselves be relations. A concept can belong to one group while its examples belong to another. This happens often with ideas in the mind. For example, the idea of a universal is itself one particular example of a universal. The three answers rest on different distinctions: between a feature and what bears it; between relation in general and the narrower kind of relation that is opposition; and between defining an idea and applying it to examples. On the last account, “opposition” is opposition by definition, while an actual case of opposition may also be a paired relation.[^V02-213]

Another kind of opposition is between contrary qualities.[^V02-214] In the terminology used in Aristotle’s school, contraries are two positive things that are not understood in relation to each other. They take turns in one subject, cannot exist there together, and differ as much as possible. As noted earlier, broad kinds do not oppose one another; the most specific kinds do, as experience shows.[^V02-215]

Some people thought that contrariety could occur between broad kinds because they treated good and evil as contraries and as kinds that include many species. This view is mistaken for two reasons. First, good and evil are not contraries: one is existence or a perfection of existence, while the other is the absence of existence or of one of its perfections. Second, they are not kinds in this sense. If we mean good and evil as they are in reality, they reduce to existence and absence. If we mean what suits or harms human nature, then suitability and conflict are relations, not features that make up the nature of a thing. They cannot be kinds of the things they describe. If we consider suitability and conflict apart from the things they describe, each is a distinct kind of relation. So even if they were opposed, they would not be contrary kinds.[^V02-216]

#### Chapter 6 — continued

For two contrary qualities to oppose each other in the strict sense, the most specific kinds they belong to must share one nearest general kind. Courage is a virtue, while recklessness—the opposite said to it—is a vice. This does not break the rule. Courage and recklessness are, in themselves, qualities of the soul; being a virtue or a vice is an added feature, not part of what each one is.[^V02-217] Virtue and vice are not general kinds that contain the virtues and vices of the soul. Courage is also not contrary to either recklessness or cowardice, because it lies between them. The two extremes differ as much as possible, so they are contraries. Courage is opposed to each only indirectly: courage is a quality with the added feature of being a virtue, while recklessness and cowardice are qualities with the added feature of being vices. The real opposition is between virtue and vice; it applies to the qualities themselves only indirectly. Recklessness and cowardice oppose each other in another way, not because one is a virtue and the other a vice.

Another rule follows from the idea that contraries differ as much as possible: each thing has only one true contrary. A contrary is something whose existence requires the absence of its opposite. If a simple thing seems to have several opposites, they either oppose it in the same respect or in different respects. If they oppose it in the same respect, there is really only one contrary. If they oppose it in different respects, the thing is not simple. A person, for example, can be opposed to heat as cold, and to cold as hot; a person can also oppose many things by containing their opposites. The true contraries are heat and cold, black and white. Each has one opposite. A hot thing and a cold thing are opposed only indirectly, because each contains one of the true contraries. So when two qualities are truly contrary, their subjects are opposed only indirectly.

Some contrary qualities have a middle point, such as warmth between hot and cold. Others have no middle point, such as being neither light nor heavy, which some say of the heavenly bodies. Warmth still belongs to the kind of heat and cold; being neither light nor heavy lies outside both kinds. Sometimes one side is naturally fixed in a subject, as white is in snow and black in pitch. Sometimes neither side is fixed. In that case, the subject may be unable to lack both, as with health and sickness in a person, or it may lack both, as with heaviness and lightness in a heavenly body. Some physicians also recognize an intermediate state between health and sickness, such as the condition of a child, a young person, or an old person.[^V02-218] Under the strict definition used here, substances are not truly contrary to one another except through the contrary qualities they bear. In the older usage, however, material forms that take turns in one subject count as true contraries. That usage also allows one thing to have many contraries, since it does not require them to differ as much as possible. By that convention, black is contrary to red as well as to white.[^V02-219]

There is a serious difficulty here. The broadest categories can coexist in one bodily thing, so they are not contraries. Sharing a very broad kind is also not enough to prevent two things from coexisting: taste and blackness can belong to one thing, even though they belong to the same broad category. So contraries must share a closest general kind and differ in their most specific features. But then their opposition comes from those specific features, not from the shared kind. The specific features themselves would be the true contraries, yet they do not share a nearest general kind. Also, these features cannot take turns in one subject, because they do not exist on their own.[^V02-220]

The objection continues: these specific features cannot take turns in one general kind either.[^V02-221] We can answer that a general kind and its specific feature are one in their actual existence. They exist through one act of creation and are one thing outside the mind. Together, they make up a specific kind. So the qualities that belong to a specific kind in reality also belong to the specific feature that helps define it, when the mind considers that feature on its own. Since opposition is a feature of things as they exist outside the mind, it belongs directly to the specific kinds, not to their defining features considered by themselves.[^V02-222] The opposition between two contraries depends on the specific feature of each, but their taking turns in one subject depends on the fact that they are specific kinds. To exist as a feature in a subject is a kind of existence, and only things with their own standing outside the mind can exist in that way. Defining features do not exist independently. A specific kind and its defining feature can both directly have the external qualities of the other because they are one in actual existence; this does not apply to mental qualities that belong to them only because the mind treats them as different.

Another kind of opposition is having and lacking. One side exists; the other is the absence of that thing. This absence may concern an individual, at the right time or at another time, or it may concern a kind or a general kind, whether near or broad.[^V02-223] Examples include blindness, darkness, hair lost through a disease after it had grown, hairlessness before it grows, inability to see in a blind person, and the absence of a beard in a woman, which is possible for her kind.[^V02-224] In each case, there is a real lack, but it depends on a subject’s ability or potential to have what is missing. That is why the lack cannot be said of something that does not exist. In the familiar definition, having is a thing’s ability to possess a feature when it chooses or when the right time comes, such as the ability to see. Lacking is the absence of this ability at a time when the subject should have it, as with a blind person—not a puppy whose eyes have not yet opened. Logicians use the broader definition given first.[^V02-225]

Logicians also define contrary qualities simply as two things that cannot exist together in one subject. Metaphysicians add a further condition: the two must differ as much as possible. For having and lacking, they require the absence to be the loss of a feature that the subject could have at the time in question. So, in each pair, the first definition is broader than the second. But the broader definition of contrariety is called the familiar one, while the narrower is called the true one. For having and lacking, the names are reversed: the broader meaning is called the true one, and the narrower the familiar one.[^V02-226]

Someone may object that the four kinds of opposition do not include cases such as baldness and hairlessness before hair grows, or sight and its absence in a scorpion or a tree. These are not contraries under the narrow definition, and they are not having and lacking under the narrow definition either.[^V02-227] The reply is that the fourfold division uses the familiar, broader meaning of contrariety and the broader meaning of having and lacking. That includes these cases.[^V02-228]
