Here is a serious objection with two parts. First, in the usage of logic, contraries do not have to be two qualities that each exist in their own right. One side may be the absence of the other, as rest is opposed to motion, darkness to light, inability to speak to the ability to speak, femininity to masculinity, and oddness to evenness. Some writers say that ordinary contrariety includes true contraries and some forms of having and lacking, where the two sides take turns in one subject. But the ordinary meaning is broader still: male and female, and odd and even, do not take turns in one subject.[^V02-229] On closer inspection, oddness and evenness belong to affirmation and denial, so they are not a separate kind of opposition.[^V02-230]

Second, ordinary contrariety is also said to require the greatest possible difference. But then pairs such as black and red, or red and yellow, would not fit any of the four kinds of opposition, because neither pair differs as much as black and white.

Some accept that conclusion and call such pairs “mutually incompatible,” making them a fifth kind of opposition. Others answer that middle shades of color, such as red and yellow, are truly opposed in the same way as the extremes, black and white. A shade of black has both the general nature of black and its own particular degree. Compared with a weaker shade below it, it counts as black; compared with a stronger shade above it, it counts as white. Avicenna says that pure black does not itself grow stronger or weaker; rather, one shade is called darker or lighter only in relation to another. On this view, each middle shade of black is like an extreme because of the nature it shares with all black. The same holds for shades of white. So the opposition between red and yellow is true opposition insofar as one belongs to black and the other to white. Their particular colors oppose each other only indirectly. In this way, opposition between the middle shades comes back to the opposition between black and white, and there is no fifth kind.

This is the main point made by a respected later scholar. But it does not fit the earlier philosophers’ account of stronger and weaker degrees.[^V02-231] There is another problem with it. Every thing has a nature of its own, whether or not we compare it with something else. Only a true relation depends on another thing for its meaning and existence. Colors belong to the category of quality, not relation, so each has its own nature. Contrariety belongs to things because of what they are, not because we compare one with another. The opposition between red and yellow remains even if we do not compare them with black and white. It is clearly an instance of contrariety, not of any other kind of opposition.

#### Summary

Those who follow Aristotle answer that true opposition belongs to the natures of black and white, apart from the particular shades. Every shade of black is opposed to every shade of white because one contains the nature of black and the other the nature of white—not because of their particular degrees. A middle shade such as red or yellow is opposed in this same way: one is taken as black and the other as white. Their particular shades differ only indirectly. So opposition between the middle colors reduces to opposition between the extremes, and no fifth kind is needed.

The earlier philosophers give a different answer. They say that the degrees of one quality oppose each other as greater and lesser, just as different amounts of one kind of quantity do. On this view, a weak shade of black and a weak shade of white oppose one another because they belong to two different kinds, regardless of their particular degrees. But a question remains: are all colors really degrees of black and white? The answer works if they are, but not if they are not. The difficulty rests on a choice of terminology, which is a small matter in the study of reason and reality.

**A further point.** Every existing thing has some relation to other things, whether as a cause, an effect, or in another way. It also has things that can be denied of it—at the very least, its opposite. So paired relations and affirmation and denial apply to every existing thing, including God. God is the source of all things, though He is not a father, son, or neighbor. Nor can anything escape affirmation and denial: a stone, for example, either is or is not a stone. But some things have no contrary qualities and no lack of a feature they could possess. Intelligences that do not depend on bodies cannot receive contrary qualities or lack a perfection they are able to have, because whatever is possible for them in the broadest sense is already actual. So contrariety and having-and-lacking do not apply to every thing, either generally or in each particular case.

#### Chapter 7 — The Opposition Between One and Many

How the one and the many oppose each other is one of the most subtle questions in the study of divine things. It has puzzled many thinkers. Only those firmly grounded in knowledge can grasp it.[^V02-232] Their opposition is not between having a capacity and lacking it, or between affirmation and denial, because both one and many exist; neither is simply the absence of the other. Nor are they contraries. They do not differ as sharply as contraries must, since whatever number you name, you can always imagine a larger one. They also do not take turns in the same individual thing. The unity of basic matter, which can shift between being joined and divided, is only a faint shadow of unity. Multiplicity, in turn, is made up of unity.

One contrary cannot be built from anything of the other’s kind. And one and many are not opposed as a pair of relations, each defined by the other. But we can understand unity, and it can exist, apart from multiplicity. Multiplicity is many in itself because it arises from unity, not because we define it by comparing it with unity. The difference between these claims is clear. Multiplicity is related to unity because it depends on unity as its cause, not because being many is the same as being caused by unity.[^V02-233] We see opposition between them because each excludes the other, not because one causes or depends on the other.

Also, if a thing is defined by its relation to another, the other must be defined by its relation to it too. So if multiplicity were essentially relative, unity would be as well, as happens with pairs of relations.[^V02-234] The two would also have to depend on each other equally, both in existence and in thought. But they do not. Some people therefore say that their opposition is not intrinsic. They say it arises because unity makes up multiplicity, while multiplicity is caused by and made up of unity. But this view mixes up two different things.[^V02-235] We understand one and many as things that cannot exist together: when one appears, it removes many, and when many appears, it removes one. Yet the unity that many removes is not the cause of that many. Many is formed through another unity, and the unity that makes up many is opposed to a different kind of unity. For example, when one continuous body is divided in two, there are two unities. One belonged to the body before it was divided; it is not part of the twoness of the two halves. The other belongs to each half and is what makes them two. There is certainly a paired relation between the new unities and the resulting twoness. But the question is: what kind of opposition holds between unity and the twoness that comes about?

If someone says, “The very idea of opposition is a kind of relation, so the opposition between one and many must be a paired relation,” we reply: the idea of opposition is a broad concept that can exist only through one of its specific kinds. Our question is which kind of opposition holds between one and many. Until we establish that, the general idea of opposition does not itself count as a specific kind of relation in the way described above.

Some people claim that one and many are not directly opposed. They argue that two opposing things must belong to the same individual thing.[^V02-236] But unity and multiplicity do not belong to the same thing. Unity may belong to multiplicity itself, to several things that together make up one whole, or to something else that arises when multiplicity disappears and one continuous thing comes to be. In each case, the thing that bears one is not exactly the thing that bears the other. The same applies when multiplicity arises in something that was one, or in what bears it.

This argument has several problems. First, if it worked, it would also show that one and not-one, and many and not-many, are not opposed. That is plainly false. Second, the thing to which two opposing features belong[^V02-237] does not have to be the same individual. It can belong to the same kind, or to a nearby or more distant broader kind. For example, being male or female can apply to human beings and to animals; movement and rest can apply to bodies[^V02-238]; and good and bad can apply to things in general, however broad the case may be.[^V02-239] If the argument were right, a thing’s defining features and necessary qualities could not oppose their negations, since the individual thing does not remain once those features disappear.

Third,[^V02-240] even if the argument were sound, it would apply only to one particular thing and the multiplicity opposed to it, not to other kinds. Something can be one in what is said of it, in what bears the feature, or in how it relates to something else, and yet become many while remaining one in another way. Fourth, the underlying thing does not have to remain the same for every kind of opposition; this condition applies only to some kinds, such as contrary qualities. Avicenna says in *The Healing* that pairs of relations do not have to take turns in the same thing or belong to a shared thing. Nor must one side be able to become the other, as a cause can become an effect. Cause and effect are relations, but they do not alternate in the same thing.

Some people say that one and many are directly opposed as contraries. They argue that if we consider what the two mean, without considering that one causes or measures the other, they cannot belong to the same thing at the same time and in the same way. They say this is not the opposition of paired relations, because one and many do not depend on each other equally in existence or in thought. Nor is it either of the other two kinds, because in those, one side is the absence of the other. Unity is not the absence of multiplicity, since multiplicity is made up of unity; and multiplicity is not the absence of unity, since it depends on unity. Otherwise, a thing would cause its own absence. They also say that the claim that a contrary cannot be made up of its opposite is unsupported. But a mottled mixture of black and white can be opposed to either black or white, even though it is made up of both.

There is confusion in this argument. To call the opposition between one and many contrariety because it is not one of the other kinds is no more justified than to call it one of those other kinds. Contraries must be able to take turns in the same thing and must differ as sharply as possible. Neither condition has been shown here. The claim that a contrary cannot be made up of its opposite is not unsupported: contrary things destroy one another, so one cannot form a real nature from the other, or from both together. A mottled thing does not have one unified nature; its unity exists only in the way we imagine and describe it. So these arguments do not show that one and many fit any of the four familiar kinds of opposition. They would have to count as a fifth kind—unless we accept the familiar four kinds together with their usual conditions and results.[^V02-241]

### Stage Six — Cause and Effect

#

#### Chapter 1 — What a Cause Is and How Causes Are Divided

A cause has two meanings. First, it can mean something whose presence brings another thing into existence and whose absence means that the other thing is absent.[^V02-242] Second, it can mean something on which another thing depends: without it, that thing cannot exist, but its presence alone does not guarantee that the other thing exists. In this second sense, a complete cause is enough to bring about its effect,[^V02-243] while an incomplete cause is only one of the things the effect depends on.[^V02-244] These include form (what makes a thing the kind of thing it is), matter (what it is made from), purpose (what it is for), and agent (what brings it about). It is wrong to say that “cause” applies to these four in unrelated senses, especially when we say that causes divide into these four kinds. The word has the same second meaning in each case: each is something on which the thing depends.

Causes are also divided into those that are parts of a thing and those that are not. A part is either what makes the thing actual—its form—or what makes it possible—its matter. A cause that is not part of the thing is either what the thing is for, its purpose, or what brings it about, its agent. Sometimes the word “agent” is used only for an outside cause, separate from the thing. What comes from within the thing is then called its element or matter. Matter, too, can be considered in two ways: as what a thing is made from, such as its element, and as what is in it, such as its arrangement or features. These are sometimes all grouped under “matter” because they share the same role of making something possible and ready to take shape. On this account, there are four causes.

Other accounts separate these kinds of matter and count five causes.[^V02-245] Form also plays different roles toward the matter and toward the whole made from it. In its relation to matter, it is better to count form as an active cause: it works with a cause separate from matter, which helps matter take on the form it is ready to receive. We will explain this when we discuss how matter and form depend on each other. So although form gives matter its form, it is not matter’s formal cause; it is what brings that form about. This also shows why it is wrong to define an agent only as something external and separate.

Matter can also be a source of what it contains. It cannot be the source of form, since form must be in place before matter can receive it. But it can be the source of an accidental feature, because that feature first exists in something already made actual by form. Matter, considered on its own, is only a possibility. What is only possible cannot, just as such, bring anything about. Matter can, however, contribute to the nature of a whole made from matter and form, or to the existence of an accidental feature once form has made it actual.

As we have explained, matter and form can each be a direct or an indirect cause of the whole made from them, depending on how we consider them. When form is what gives a thing its basic identity, it makes matter actual, while matter is a cause of the whole. In that respect, form is a cause of the whole’s cause. But as a formal part of the whole, form is its direct formal cause. In this second role, form acts directly; nothing stands between it and the whole. When matter and an added feature together make up a particular kind of thing, matter is what bears that feature. The feature gives the whole its form, so matter is an indirect cause of the whole. But as a part of the whole, matter is its direct material cause.[^V02-246]

In short, matter and form are direct causes of the thing because they are its parts: one is its material cause and the other its formal cause. If either one causes the whole indirectly, it does so in a different way. Matter is not its material cause in that indirect role, and form is not its formal cause in that role.

The four causes also have many connections. In one sense, the agent and the purpose cause each other. The agent brings the purpose into reality; the purpose is what the agent acts for. If someone asks why you exercise, you may answer, “To become healthy.” If someone asks why you became healthy, you may answer, “Because I exercised.” Exercise is the active cause of health, while health is the purpose of exercise. The agent causes the purpose to exist in reality, not to be a purpose or to have its particular nature. The purpose, in turn, explains why the agent acts as an agent.[^V02-247] Matter and form also cause each other in another way, as we have noted.

Some of these causes can also be one and the same thing. As we will explain, the first cause of everything is also its purpose, both in reality and in thought. Sometimes the agent, form, and purpose are all one in nature. In a father, the source that brings the human form about in the seed is the human form itself, not some other form. What comes to be in the seed is a human form, and that is also what the seed’s development moves toward. As this form joins with matter and makes a human being, it is the form. As it begins the movement, it is the agent. As the movement reaches its end, it is the purpose. In relation to matter and the whole made from it, this one form is the form and formal cause in two ways. In relation to movement, the father’s form is the agent that starts it, while the form at which the movement arrives is its purpose, as explained in *The Healing*.[^V02-248]

#### Chapter 2 — Why Cause and Effect Must Be Present Together

First, a cause must exist when its effect exists. An effect is possible in itself: it can either exist or not exist. A thing that must exist, or cannot exist, does not need a cause. But if it can either exist or not exist, something must tip the balance in favor of existence. That cause must be present when the effect is determined; otherwise, the effect would not depend on it at that point. And since something that does not exist cannot bring existence about, the cause must exist too. So it must be present when the effect exists.

Second, when a complete cause exists, its effect must exist as well. The leading philosophers say that if God, who must exist, brings something else into existence, He does so either by His own nature or through something that belongs to His nature. In either case, the cause is always present, so its effect should always follow. If God’s nature alone brings possible things into existence, then apart from God nothing precedes them. If He does so through an attribute that belongs to Him, that attribute is always present too. So its effect should continue for as long as God and that attribute exist.

If the cause and its permanent attribute are present but the effect is not, then God is not the full, independent cause of that effect. When the power to cause an effect is fully present, the effect either follows necessarily or still depends on something else. If it still depends on another cause, then the first cause was not enough. The second cause would also need an explanation. Either this chain goes on without end, or we reach a cause that is sufficient by itself. Wherever the complete cause is present, its effect must exist and continue for as long as that cause remains.

If God causes the effect neither by His own nature nor through an attribute that belongs to Him, then something outside Him must explain why He acts. If that outside factor itself begins to exist, we ask what caused it, as we did with the first effect. The chain cannot go on forever; it must end with God. Then the same question returns: if the factor that brings about God’s action is always present, why would its effect not continue? It makes no difference whether we call the outside factor a time, a benefit, a motive, a will, or something else.

Someone may object: God is free to choose, and He may have chosen from eternity to bring something about at one particular time rather than another. We ask: could He have chosen a different time? If not, He was forced to act at that time and was not free.[^V02-249] Also, on that view, the act would already have to be fixed in eternity as happening at that particular time.[^V02-250] When the act occurs, the choice to bring it about at that time ceases. The choice cannot be necessary—otherwise, it would not end then—and it cannot be part of God's own nature, for the reason just given. So it would need another cause. But this is impossible: everything besides God depends on His choice, so His choice would then depend on itself. If He could have chosen another time, something must have settled which time He chose. If that was another choice, we must ask what settled that choice, and the same question goes on without end. The first effect still needs an explanation, whether it comes from a choice or something else.

At this point, thinkers split into different groups. Some say that a free agent can choose either of two equal options without any reason favoring one. They compare this to someone fleeing a lion who sees two equally good paths and freely chooses one without a reason to prefer it.

Others say that will itself can choose one of two equal options without any further reason. They say this is simply what will does: it gives one option priority without needing a reason or motive. This ability to favor one side belongs to will by its nature, they say, just as being an animal belongs to a human being or having three angles belongs to a triangle.[^V02-251]

Others say that God knows everything that will happen and everything that will not. What He knows will happen must happen; otherwise, His knowledge would be wrong.[^V02-252] If only that event can occur and the other is impossible, then He must will what He knows will happen and cannot will anything else, since willing the impossible is itself impossible.[^V02-253]

Others say that God’s actions always serve some good, even if we do not know what it is. God chooses to bring something about at a certain time because He knows that doing so then serves a good that would be lost if it happened at another time.

Others say that God’s action does not occur in eternity because of something on His side. Rather, the action itself must have a beginning, while eternity has no beginning. The two cannot go together.[^V02-254]

This last view has a sound point, if its meaning is understood correctly. We will see this when we discuss how the world of bodies began. Others hold that God has an endless succession of new acts of will. He does one thing, then wills something else and does that, and so on without end. We will now show the problems in these views.

First, some ask why a free agent cannot choose one of two equal options without a reason. There are two replies. The first is that if we allow this, we lose the way to prove that a creator exists. That proof rests on the rule that what can either exist or not exist needs a cause. If we reject that rule, we cannot prove that God exists.[^V02-255]

The second reply is that anything that can either exist or not exist needs a cause to settle which way it goes. We know this directly, even if we deny it in words. Until a determiner exists, neither side can become real. The determiner’s existence is not the same as our knowing that it exists. What matters is that the determiner itself is there. A thoughtful person can see this in their own experience: when the reasons for both choices seem equal, they remain still until something tips the balance.

The second view, that will can favor one side by its own nature, also fails. If both options are equal for the will, neither becomes actual without a reason. Calling this a natural power of the will does not explain anything. If the will chose the other option instead, the same supposed power would be present. Nor does it make sense to say that the will first exists without an object, then attaches itself to one. A will is always directed toward something; we cannot think of it as having no object, or as first willing just anything and only later becoming specific. When someone understands one option as better, the will turns toward it. That judgment comes before the will.

The third view says that God wills what He knows will happen. But if His knowledge that something will happen at a certain time depends on its happening at that time, then the event’s occurring at that time depends on His intention to bring it about then. If that intention, in turn, depends on His knowledge of the event, the explanation goes in a circle.[^V02-256]

We have already shown that things that do not exist are not real things, and that essences depend on existences. You will also see that God’s knowledge brings possible things into being and comes before them; it does not follow their coming to be.[^V02-257]

As for the fourth view—that God chooses when to act because He knows the action will serve people’s interests—we reply: the benefit that follows an act at a particular time is either bound up with the act’s very existence, or it is not. If it is, then the benefit follows whenever the act occurs. It cannot explain why the act happens at one time rather than another. If the benefit is not bound up with the act itself, then its coming from the act at this time rather than another is itself only possible and needs something to explain it. We would then have to ask what makes this time the one chosen. The only other answer is that time itself does so. But that would mean that time is something that exists, and every time is preceded by another.[^V02-258]

If times exist in an ordered series, are possible, and come from God, then God’s act of creating must be continuous. That is the point to be proved.[^V02-259] And how could a reasonable person accept that if the world were vastly larger, people’s interests would be ruined, or that if God had created it unimaginably earlier, those interests would be ruined too? This view also makes God’s action in general depend on something else.

As for the claim that an act does not happen earlier because it cannot happen earlier: that may make sense for particular acts and for specific natural forms that come to be in individual things.[^V02-260] But it cannot explain creation and bringing things into existence as a whole. A possible thing needs a cause because it is possible, not because it began to exist. Nor must every act, taken as a whole, be preceded by nonexistence. The world’s possibility of coming to be has no first moment: whatever time we suppose to be the first when it could have existed is preceded by another time at which it could have existed too.[^V02-261] A thing’s occurring at a particular time, and its being preceded by nonexistence at that time, can be understood only once time already exists. The same is true of its being in one particular place rather than another: that becomes possible only once place exists.

#### An Objection and Its Answer

Some say that whether the world has always existed or began at some point, it is never free of new events and changes. One thing passes away and another comes to be. If these events depend on God, why not say that the world itself depends on Him in the same way, even if it began at some point?

The answer is that some causes prepare the way for an effect, while others actually bring it about. A preparatory cause can come before its effect because it does not bring the effect into existence; it brings the effect closer and prepares the matter to receive it. But a cause that actually produces an effect must exist alongside that effect.

Consider a heavy body falling. Its weight causes it to fall. As it falls, reaching one point prepares it to move to the next. Its weight is the cause that brings about its arrival at each point; the movement that came before prepares it for the next one. In this way, events that begin in time can depend, through a preparatory motion, on an eternal cause. That cause brings about the world in all its parts, those that came before and those that come after. Whatever event we take to be the first must have been preceded by motion and change that prepared it to receive the cause’s effect.

Here we find the starting point for another proof that God’s activity never ceases and that His giving continues to reach possible things.[^V02-262] These events must have causes, and those causes must either be new events themselves or involve new events. Either the event comes about all at once because its producing cause begins to exist, or it comes about because a preparatory cause brings the producing cause closer to it. The first option would require an endless chain of causes and effects to exist all at once, which is impossible.

So an event comes about because it has been brought closer to its cause. That closeness comes from a preparatory cause that makes the event ready to receive the flow of existence from the giver of forms. These successive preparations cannot be separate, unconnected instants. If they were, there would be no link between one event and the next, and the earlier event would not have to lead to the later one. It could not prepare it. What remains is that the successive preparations form one continuous whole, with no actual boundaries. That whole is either a continuous existence that unfolds by its own nature, or something that depends on such a continuity, like time or motion.

We have therefore shown that events have an immediate cause.

That cause is something that continually passes and renews itself as one unbroken whole, not something made up of separate, indivisible instants. This is one of the starting points of natural philosophy. It establishes that bodily substance is continuous and cannot be made of indivisible parts. So no event can begin unless another event came before it, without any first event. Those who reject this view take one of two paths: they say that an act must be preceded by nonexistence, while eternity has no beginning, so the two cannot go together; or they say that motion and events cannot stretch back without a beginning. We will explain and answer these arguments later.[^V02-263]

#### Chapter 3 — Can Something That Accompanies a Cause Come Before Its Effect?

In the sixth section of *The Remarks and Admonitions*, Avicenna says that something accompanying a cause that comes before its effect does not have to come before the effect too. The cause comes before its effect through causation, not through time. Something that accompanies the cause but is not itself a cause has no such priority. And if it is not prior in time or through causation, then it is not prior at all.

One scholar objects:[^V02-264] priority is not always a matter of time or causation. There is also priority by nature: one, for example, is prior to two. So perhaps something that accompanies a cause can be prior in another way, even if it is not prior in time or as a cause.[^V02-265]

Avicenna is not denying every other kind of priority to something that accompanies a cause. He is denying only the kind that would match the cause’s priority over its effect. By “something that accompanies a cause,” he means something that exists alongside it in relation to that causal priority.[^V02-266] His general point is that something accompanying a thing that is prior in one way is sometimes prior in that same way, and sometimes not. What accompanies something that is earlier in time is also earlier in time. But when two things accompany a cause that comes before its effect, the second effect does not share the cause’s priority over the first effect.

A similar distinction applies to what is prior by nature. Animal comes before human being in this sense, but walking does not, even though animal and walking both come under a broader kind, such as substance.[^V02-267] Priority by honor and by rank works in similar ways. What counts as prior can depend on the point of comparison.

Al-Rāzī raises another problem. Avicenna says that the outer sphere and the cause of the inner sphere can come from one cause and so exist together. Yet the cause of the inner sphere comes before it. The outer sphere cannot come before it, because the inner sphere’s existence and the absence of empty space within the outer sphere go together. If the inner sphere depended on the outer sphere, then so would the absence of empty space. Anything that depends on something else is possible in itself. That would make the absence of empty space possible in itself, which is absurd.

Avicenna also says in *The Healing*, in its discussion of heaven and earth, that intellectual beings cause their spheres to have their specific places, and are present with them in this respect. Since a cause comes before its effect, he concludes that these intellectual beings come before the elemental bodies whose places they determine.

Al-Rāzī says this passage states that what comes before an effect is prior, while the earlier passage says that what accompanies something prior is not itself prior. The two claims seem to conflict. He says that a distinction is needed, but that it is hard to find.[^V02-268]

I see no conflict. The difference is clear. Two things are together by causation when they are both effects of one cause. Causal priority means being a cause, or having a place in the order because one is a cause. One thing cannot have two causes, but it can have two effects. So two effects exist together, and both come after their cause. Whatever accompanies one of these effects is also after the cause. But if one of two things that exist together is the cause of the other, then the other cannot also cause it; otherwise, one effect would have two causes. So there is no problem.

It is surprising that Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi missed this clear distinction and offered another answer that does not work.[^V02-269] He says that some things are together by their nature, while others only happen to be together. The word “effect” may not mean the same thing in both passages. Perhaps, then, the difference lies in what “together” means.

#### Chapter 4 — Why Causes and Effects Cannot Form a Circle or Stretch Back Forever

Both problems can be stated together. A chain of causes and effects could go on without end, with every cause also being an effect. If the chain has a limited number of things, it loops back on itself: a circle of two things, or of more than two. If it has no end, the chain stretches back forever. In the first case, one thing would come before itself, come after itself, and depend on itself. All of these are plainly impossible. If one thing causes another, it comes before it by one step. If the second thing also comes before the first, then the first would come before itself by two steps. The same problem grows with every added link.[^V02-270]

Another argument is sometimes offered.[^V02-271] Priority, posteriority, dependence, and need are relations that can be understood only between two things. The thing depended on is necessary in relation to what depends on it, while the dependent thing is possible in relation to what it depends on. But neither point proves the claim. A relation does not always require the two things to be distinct in reality; sometimes a difference in how we consider them is enough, as with the knower and what is known.[^V02-272] And necessity does not rule out possibility in the sense of what can exist. Possibility stands to necessity as an incomplete state stands to a complete one.[^V02-273] The two differ in how we consider them, so we should return to the argument already given.

You might object: if “a thing comes before itself” means that it comes before itself in time, this does not apply to causes. If it means that it comes before itself as a cause, then that is just the claim we are trying to prove. Saying that a thing cannot come before itself is simply another way of saying that it cannot cause itself.

I reply that “coming before” here means the kind of priority expressed by saying, “The cause existed, and so the effect existed.” A cause must exist before its effect can exist. We can say, “The hand moved, and so the ring moved.” We cannot say, “The ring moved, and so the hand moved.” This kind of priority plainly cannot hold between a thing and itself.

You might then say that a thing can cause what causes it without having to come before itself. You might offer two reasons.

First, if one thing depends on another, and that second thing depends on a third, it does not necessarily follow that the first depends on the third. Its immediate cause is enough. Otherwise, an effect could fail to follow even when its immediate cause exists.

Second, you might say that a thing’s nature causes something that, in turn, causes that thing to exist.[^V02-274]

I reply that the dependence is unavoidable, and neither reason succeeds. Without the more distant cause, the nearer cause would not exist; without the nearer cause, the effect would not exist. That is precisely what it means to depend on something. An effect could fail to follow from its nearer cause only if that cause existed without the more distant one. As for a thing’s nature causing what causes its existence, that would seem to require the effect before its cause. But it is not the circular dependence we are discussing when the two kinds of dependence are different: form depends on matter in one respect, while matter depends on form in another.

There are many arguments against an endless chain of causes. The first is Avicenna’s argument in the *Metaphysics* of *The Healing*. He has already shown that a true cause exists together with its effect. Suppose, then, that we have an effect, its cause, and the cause of that cause. These cannot continue without end.

Consider all three together, in relation to one another. The cause of the cause is the first cause of the other two, and both stand to it as effects—one directly, the other through an intermediate cause.[^V02-275] The effect at the end cannot be a cause of anything. The cause at the other end causes everything after it. A middle cause causes what comes after it and is itself caused by what comes before it. This remains true whether there is one middle cause or several, and whether the chain of middle causes is finite or endless.

If there are finitely many middle causes, taken together they have the same role as a single middle cause between the two ends. If there are infinitely many and so no first or last member among them, then the whole chain has the role of a middle cause: whatever part of it you take, it causes the final effect and is itself caused. The whole depends for its existence on its members. Since every member is itself an effect, the whole is an effect too. Yet the whole is also a condition for, and a cause of, the final effect.[^V02-276] No matter how far back you trace the chain, the same holds. So there cannot be a chain of causes in which every cause is itself caused and there is no first, uncaused cause. An endless chain would be a middle with no ends, which is impossible. This, Avicenna says, is the strongest argument on this question.

The second argument is the matching argument. It applies to any existing series whose members are ordered, whether they are causes and effects, lengths and dimensions, or numbers placed in order.[^V02-277] Suppose there were an endless series. Remove one member—or any finite number of members—from one end. You now have two series: one starts with the last member we assumed, and the other starts with the member just before it. Match the members of one series with those of the other.

If every member of the longer series matches a member of the shorter one, then the whole would equal a part of itself, which is impossible. If not every member matches, the longer series must contain a member with no match in the shorter one. The shorter series must therefore come to an end. But the longer series exceeds it only by the one member—or finite number of members—we removed. Since adding a finite number to a finite series still gives a finite series, the longer series must also be finite. This contradicts the assumption that it was endless.

The matching argument has been challenged in two ways. First, if it were sound, it would also show that numbers are finite. Compare the numbers from one onward without end with the numbers from two onward without end. Matching them would then make all numbers finite, which everyone agrees is false. It would also make God’s knowledge finite if we matched it with a smaller set that lacks one item. And it would make the motions of the heavenly spheres finite if we matched a series beginning with this revolution against one beginning with the previous revolution, though philosophers say these motions have no beginning.

Second, the argument assumes that the shorter series must come to an end if it has fewer members than the other. But endlessly doubling one gives a smaller result than endlessly doubling two, even though both go on without end. God’s power is less than His knowledge, since it concerns only what can exist, yet both are without limit. Saturn completes fewer cycles than the Moon, though philosophers say both go on without end.

The objection amounts to this: suppose every member of the longer series matches a member of the shorter one. We need not accept that the two series are equal; such a match can happen when both are endless. Even if we call them equal in this sense, it is not impossible for an endless whole and one of its shorter parts to match. We call one series larger only when it has a definite number of members beyond the other, and that need not be true of two endless series, even if one begins thousands of members later.

One answer is to say[^V02-278] that it is self-evident that any two series are either equal or differ in size, and that the shorter one must end.[^V02-279] The counterexamples can be answered by limiting where the proof applies.

For theologians, the proof applies to things that exist, whether they all exist together, as in a chain of causes and effects, or one after another, as with the motions of the heavenly spheres, which prepare for one another. It does not apply to numbers, because they are ideas, not things that exist on their own.[^V02-280] Only finitely many counted things actually exist.[^V02-281] The same applies to what God knows and what He can do: in reality, their number is finite. Their being called endless means only that there is no upper limit; beyond any number, known thing, or possible thing, there may be another.

For philosophers, the proof applies only to things that all exist at once and have an order in space or by nature, as lengths do, or as causes and effects do. So it does not apply to the motions of the heavenly spheres, which do not happen together, or to the separate members of one kind, such as rational souls, if they are endless, as some have thought.[^V02-282] These things are not arranged in a series. The matching proof therefore does not apply to numbers, the motions of the heavenly spheres, or rational souls.

Someone might object that limiting a reasoning proof in this way admits that it fails whenever the result does not follow. I reply that the proof does not apply to the counterexamples; it applies to other cases. For theologians, things that have no actual existence can be matched only in imagination. Imagination cannot picture an endless number of things and compare them one by one. The comparison stops where the imagining stops. But if the series exists in reality, each member of one series must match a member of the other, and the mind can judge this in a single broad view without inspecting every member. For philosophers,[^V02-283] matching in reality is possible only when things have a natural or spatial order. That is why the proof does not apply to numbers,[^V02-284] the motions of the heavenly spheres, or rational souls.

One theologian says that both series, and the matching of their members, exist only in the mind. If the mind’s general judgment that each member must have a match is enough, then the proof applies to numbers and to things that occur one after another or all together, whether they are ordered or not. The mind can imagine the whole series. But if the mind must inspect the members one by one, the proof fails even for ordered things that exist, let alone for other cases. The mind cannot hold an endless number of things in view unless it has endless time.

I say that the difference between these cases matters. Matching is always an act of the mind and always takes place in thought. But sometimes it reflects what is real, and sometimes it does not.

In the first case, the mind needs only one broad view to judge that each member of either series matches a member of the other. The match is already present in reality, so the mind does not need to inspect each member one by one. But if the members of a series do not all exist together, or are not linked by a natural or spatial order, one broad view is not enough. The mind must picture and compare many cases, one by one.

Consider a rope. Pull one end, and the other end moves because all its parts are joined. But if those parts were merely laid side by side, without being joined, you would have to move each one separately. The same is true in the case we are discussing.

As support for this point, and as a reminder, al-Tusi, one of the most respected later scholars, writes in *Naqd al-Muḥaṣṣal*:

Most theologians have relied on this proof to show that the world began in time. To show that past events cannot stretch back forever, however, we need another argument. I will first present what has been said, along with the objections to it, and then give my own view.

Earlier scholars argued that past events must be finite: since each event began, the whole series began.[^V02-285] An objection was raised that a claim about the whole does not always follow from claims about each member. They then argued that increase and decrease apply to past events, so the events must be finite. Their opponents answered with God’s knowledge and power: His knowledge includes more than His power, yet both are said to be endless.

The more careful scholars argued as follows. Take the past events, starting from this moment and going back without end. Then take them again, starting from the same moment one year ago and going back. Imagine matching the two series, with their starting points lined up as they extend into the past.

The two series cannot be equal. If they were, the events that took place between now and one year ago would both exist and not exist. The series that starts one year ago cannot be greater than the one that starts now either: what is removed from two equal things cannot be greater than either of them.[^V02-286] So the series that starts one year ago must be smaller. For it to be smaller, it must end before the series that starts now. It would then contain only a finite number of events. The other series exceeds it only by the finite number of events from that year, so it too must be finite.

Their opponents objected that this matching happens only in imagination, and only if both series can be pictured there. But an endless series cannot be pictured in imagination. Nor do the events exist all together in reality, so they cannot be matched there either. The proof, then, depends on something that exists neither in imagination nor in reality. They also objected that the increase and decrease are assumed at the finite end of the series, not at the endless end whose size is in dispute. So this does not prove what it is meant to prove. That is the argument and the objections given here.

I say[^V02-287] that every event can be described in two ways: it comes before what follows it, and after what came before it. These are different ways of considering the event. If we look at past events starting from now, treating each event once as earlier than what follows it and once as later than what came before it, the two series—earlier events and later events—match exactly in reality, though we consider them in different ways. We do not need to imagine matching them term by term.

Even so, the earlier events must be more numerous than the later ones on the side now in dispute: the past. So the later events must be finite, ending before the earlier events do. The earlier events exceed them by a finite number, so they too must be finite.[^V02-288] That concludes his argument.

One more issue remains. In any number, such as ten, there must be an order—not between its first, second, and third members, but among one, two, and three themselves. One comes before two by nature, even though it does not come before itself. Likewise, two comes before three, and three before four. So if an infinite number existed, as some have believed about rational souls, then its members would be together and ordered, and the matching argument would apply. Unless, of course, we deny that any number other than one comes before another number. Avicenna discusses this in the *Metaphysics* of *The Healing*.[^V02-289]

The third argument is this. If causes and effects stretched back forever, without ending in a first cause that causes but is not itself caused, then there would be a whole made up of all existing possible things, each caused by another member of that whole. That whole would itself be an existing possible thing.[^V02-290]

It exists because its members exist, and a whole does not cease to exist unless one of its parts ceases to exist.[^V02-291] It is possible because it depends on a possible member. Anything that depends on something possible is itself possible. By defining the whole as all existing possible things, we exclude what does not exist and what exists necessarily.

You might object that a group made up of existing parts can be only an idea, with no existence in reality—for example, a person and a stone taken together, or the sky and the earth.

To this we reply: They do not mean that the whole exists as one thing whose existence is separate from the existence of its parts. In fact, they have explicitly said[^V02-292] that a whole existing in the outside world may have no nature distinct from that of its individual members, as with ten men taken together.[^V02-293] A whole may also have a form that makes it a particular kind of thing, as in compounds made from the elements. Or it may have no such form and add only an arrangement of its parts, as with a bed made of pieces of wood. We will return to that example.

If the whole is itself an existing possible thing, then its independent cause—the cause that brings it into existence without relying on anything else—is either the whole itself, which is clearly impossible; one of its parts, which is also impossible; or something outside it. If the whole caused itself, one of its parts would have to cause itself and the causes on which it depends. Causing the whole means causing the parts that make it up, and an independent cause is one that needs nothing else. If something outside the whole causes it, that cause must bring at least some of its parts into existence. The chain of effects would then end at that cause, since anything outside the chain of possible things must exist necessarily in itself.

That part cannot also be caused by another part of the whole. Two independent causes cannot produce the same effect, and here we are speaking of a cause that brings its effect into existence on its own. This would contradict our assumptions in two ways: we assumed that the chain has no first cause, and that every member is caused by another member.

This account also answers the objections to the proof. First, if by “the cause of the whole chain” we mean its complete cause—the set of conditions that fully account for it—then it need not be impossible for the chain itself to be that cause. It would be impossible only if the cause had to come before the whole. But the complete cause of a composite does not have to come before it; indeed, it cannot, since the parts that make up the effect are included in that complete cause.[^V02-294] One might object that this would make the cause necessary in itself, since it would exist through itself. That does not follow. It would follow only if the cause did not depend on a part distinct from itself, whether or not we call that part “something else.”

If instead we mean the efficient cause—the agent that brings the whole into existence—then it could be one of the parts of the chain. That would be impossible only if it had to cause every part, including itself and the causes on which it depends. But it need not: a part of a composite effect may come from something other than its efficient cause, as the wood in a bed comes from a different source than the carpenter. Even if we grant that point, we still cannot assume that whatever lies outside the chain must exist necessarily. There could be infinitely many chains of causes and effects, each depending on a cause outside itself that belongs to another chain, with no chain reaching the Necessary Being.[^V02-295] Or, even if every chain must ultimately depend on the Necessary Being, that does not rule out an infinite series: the totality of infinitely many causes and effects could itself be a possible thing that depends on the Necessary Being.[^V02-296]

There is also a more general objection. The whole could be made up of the Necessary Being and all existing possible things. Its cause would be neither itself, nor one of its parts, nor something outside it. If the Necessary Being were outside this whole, it might seem to become caused if there were more than one Necessary Being. And if the cause were one of the parts, either it would cause every part, bringing two independent causes to the same effect, or it would cause only some of them.[^V02-297]

Our answer is that we have clearly defined the whole as all existing possible things, with each part caused by another part. The only thing outside it is the Necessary Being. The least that follows from its being an independent cause is that at least one part of the whole is caused by something outside it alone, not by another part. That is what it means for the chain to end.[^V02-298] The independent cause cannot itself be one of the parts, because then it would have to cause itself and its own causes. If something else caused some of its parts, then neither cause would be independent: each would depend on the other for the whole to exist. This differs from a whole made up of the Necessary Being and possible things, since one part—the Necessary Being—exists through itself and depends on nothing else. And a bed’s independent maker is not the carpenter alone, but the carpenter together with whoever produced the wood.[^V02-299]

There is, however, an objection to the claim that the independent cause of a composite made up of possible parts must cause every part. If this means that the very same cause acts independently on each part, it cannot be right. The parts of a composite may come into being one by one, as when the pieces of wood and then the arrangement that makes a bed are formed. If the independent cause were absent when the first part appeared, the effect would come before its cause. If the cause were already present, the final part of the effect would come after its independent cause, even though the cause was supposed to produce it immediately. We have already seen why that is impossible.

Alternatively, “cause every part” might mean that the cause of the whole produces each part either directly or through one of its own parts, so no part depends on anything outside it. If the effect is composite, then its independent cause would also have parts, each arising at the same time as its corresponding part of the effect. There would be no cause that comes before its effect and no effect delayed after its cause. But this does not prove what is needed: it does not show that the independent cause of the chain cannot be one of its parts. A part of the chain could be a cause in this sense without causing itself or its own causes. That part would be the whole made up of all the members that are both causes and effects, leaving out only the final effect—the one that comes after the rest in the order of causation, but before them in rank when we count from the finite end.[^V02-300] This whole is sometimes described as what comes before the final effect, and sometimes as what comes after the first effect. It is a part of the chain. When it exists, the chain exists, and each part of that whole corresponds to a part of the chain. So its causing the chain does not mean that anything causes itself.

One might object that this whole, which serves as the cause, is itself possible and therefore needs a cause. The answer is that its cause is the whole before the final effect within it; and likewise, each earlier whole is caused by the whole before it, without end.

One might also object that the part of the chain preceding its final effect cannot be an independent cause, since it is itself possible and needs a cause. The same would apply to every whole we propose. The chain would therefore exist only with help from those other causes. And that whole would not be enough to make the chain exist, since the pure effect would also have to be included.

We reply that this does not undermine independence. Independence means that a cause does not need help from a cause outside it in order to bring something about. We have supposed that the cause of each whole lies within it. And clearly, its final effect plays no part in bringing that whole into existence.[^V02-301]

Someone might say: If by “the whole” you mean either one chain or infinitely many chains, as you proposed, then this objection goes away too: there is no final effect and no composite whole before it. We reply that the objection still applies. The cause of this whole is the part made up of all the infinitely many wholes that come before their infinitely many final effects. If you say[^V02-302] that, from the start, we denied that the cause of the whole could be one of its parts—because no part has a better claim than another, or because any part you choose has a stronger claim to be the cause since it has greater causal power—we reply that this is not so. The part made up of everything before the final effect is the one fit to be the cause, since none of the other parts can independently bring the whole into existence.

#### Critique and Clarification

This way of arguing against an endless chain and proving the Necessary Being is very weak. We have already shown that the existence of each thing is the same as its unity, and that a thing’s unity is the same as its existence, in the sense explained earlier. So it is wrong to say that a person and a stone together exist as a third thing, separate from both. This also raises another objection to the original proof: we do not accept that the proposed whole needs a cause beyond the causes of its individual parts. That would follow only if the whole had an existence different from the existence of its parts, each of which has its own cause. Calling the whole “possible” does not prove anything. It is the possible things that exist, each through its own cause. Why, then, would the whole need another cause? Ten separate units, for example, do not need a cause beyond the causes of the units themselves.

To say that the existence of all the parts differs from the existence of each part is empty talk. The fact that all the parts together are different from any one part does not mean that the whole has an existence outside the mind beyond the existence of its parts. It is true that a real composite ceases to exist if one of its parts ceases to exist: each real composite has its own nature and its own unity, and losing a part destroys it. But this does not mean that any combination the mind imagines between two things becomes real as soon as both things exist, and then disappears only if one of them disappears.[^V02-303] Also, the fact that something does not exist when one of its conditions is absent does not always mean that it exists whenever that condition is present.[^V02-304]

This shows why a later claim—that a thing can cause itself—is false. The claim says that all existing things, both possible and necessary, form one possible whole because it depends on its members, and that this whole has no cause except itself. Its cause would have to be one of its parts, which is impossible because it depends on the remaining parts; or something outside it. But nothing lies outside it, so they conclude that it causes itself. There is no problem here, they say, because the whole’s dependence on each individual part does not mean that it depends on itself as a whole.[^V02-305] However, if by “cause” they mean an independent agent that brings things into existence, then that cause is one part of the whole of all existing things: either the Necessary Being, or what lies before the final effect and ultimately reaches the Necessary Being.

They also say that it is impossible for a thing to cause itself, or to do something equivalent to that,[^V02-306] when the cause must come before its effect. In that case, the thing would have to come before itself. But it is not always impossible for a thing to be the complete cause of itself. As explained earlier, this happens in the whole made up of the Necessary Being and its first effect, or of the Necessary Being and all its effects.

That argument is plainly weak. A sound mind cannot make sense of something that is possible in itself and yet does not need anything beyond itself in order to exist. If the whole they call possible had an existence different from the existence of its individual members, and a possibility different from theirs, then it would need a cause different from the causes of its members. To say that its existence is different while its cause is the same adds nothing.

One careful thinker says: We can take several things together as one whole, and use a singular word for them, such as “the group” or “the whole.” Or we can consider them one by one, using words such as “this one” and “that one.” The two ways of looking at them can lead to different judgments. For example, a narrow house cannot hold a group of people all at once, though it can hold them one after another. Knowing this, we can choose to explain what makes the two individuals exist together by looking at them separately: the whole depends on each of its two parts, and together the parts are enough for it to exist. So this one and that one are the two causes that make their whole exist. If we then consider the two separately, each is possible and needs a cause.

I say that this careful thinker got part of the matter right and part wrong. He was right that, taken separately, the two are neither one possible thing nor one necessary thing. They are two things: one is necessary, and the other is possible and exists through it. He was wrong to think that taking things together or separately changes what exists outside the mind. Whether the mind considers the sky and the earth together or one by one, their reality outside the mind does not change. Only our way of thinking about them changes. The mind can consider them as one thing that exists in thought, but that does not make them one thing in the outside world. The imagination can divide the sky into two, but that makes it two only in thought, not in reality. Taking things together or separately changes how we view them, not the things themselves.

The house example does not show a difference between looking at things as a whole and looking at them separately. The difference is in what we say about the group. The group, whether considered together or one by one, can be said to fit in the house if its members enter at different times. It cannot be said to fit if they all enter at once. That is the point here.

Some also object that this leads to an endless series of things as soon as we suppose that two things exist. The objection does not apply. Considering two things as a whole does not mean considering them again together with the feature of “being together”;[^V02-307] that would count the parts of their nature twice.[^V02-308] By “the whole” we mean the two things that bear the shared arrangement, without adding “being arranged together” as another part. So someone who says that the two things exist as something distinct from each of them need not say that their togetherness, or their being two, exists as yet another thing. One thing can exist even if “oneness”—the feature of being one—does not exist as a separate thing.

Someone might ask: Many philosophers say that number exists and is not the same as all its individual units taken together. How, then, can you say that a composite has no existence beyond the existence of its units? We reply: number exists in the sense that many units exist in reality. We do not agree that number is something different from all the units taken together; it is those very units.[^V02-309] But treating number and being many as if they were one thing with properties of their own—being ten, being a square, being odd, being a perfect number, and so on—is an act of the mind. These properties do have something in reality that they refer to: the existence of many units.

The fourth argument rests on two terms that go together: cause and effect. If the chain of causes and effects did not end in a cause that was not itself caused, then cause and effect would not match. But that is impossible, so the starting assumption must be false. Put another way: if cause and effect match, the chain must end in a cause that is not itself caused. Cause and effect do match, so the chain must end.

They match because, whenever one exists—in the mind or in reality—the other exists too, and when one is absent, so is the other. Why does this mean the chain must end? The final effect is only an effect; everything before it is both an effect and a cause. If the chain never reached a cause that was not itself an effect, there would be an effect that was not also a cause.

Someone might object that the cause paired with a pure effect—the final effect, which causes nothing further—is simply the cause immediately above it, not a cause that is itself uncaused. We agree. But the point is that every effect must have a cause to match it. That requires there to be a cause that is not itself an effect. Some express this argument in two ways.[^V02-310] First, if the chain went on forever, it would contain more effects than causes, which is impossible if cause and effect must match. Every cause in the chain is itself an effect, by assumption; but not every effect is also a cause. The final effect is the exception. Second, take all the causes in the chain and all its effects, and compare the two groups. If one group has more members than the other, cause and effect do not match. If they have the same number, then there must be a cause with no effect to match it, since at the finite end there is an effect that is not also a cause. Either way, the claim that the chain has no uncaused cause leads to a contradiction.

The fifth argument is close to the previous one. Remove the final effect from the proposed chain. Then count each remaining thing twice: once as a cause and once as an effect, since the same thing plays a different role when it causes something and when something causes it. This gives us two groups, considered in different ways: the causes and the effects. When we compare them, the cause group must have one more level. A cause always comes before its effect, so each cause matches the effect of the cause one level before it, not the effect at its own level. The final effect was left out, and it is not itself a cause. So the causes have one more level than the effects. If they did not, a cause would not come before its effect, as it must. A level of cause with no matching effect would mean that the chain ends.

The sixth argument uses the gaps between ordered things. It can be applied to causes and effects and to other things arranged in order. The interval between the final effect, or something like it, and each item in the chain must be finite, since it is bounded at both ends. The argument then says the whole chain must be finite: it can be only one item longer than a finite interval. For example, if the distance from the start of a road to every point along it is no more than a mile, the whole road cannot be more than a mile long, apart from a final segment at its end. If the starting point is not counted as part of the distance, there may be two extra segments.

This may persuade someone who accepts the intuitive step, but it does not force an opponent to agree. The opponent may reject the idea that a bounded interval in an ordered chain must contain only a finite number of steps. In a road, the distance from the start to any point is finite. But if the chain has infinitely many steps, the opponent can say that there is no last point before another point that would make the interval finite.

The argument tries to prove this by saying that a total made from finite numbers must itself be finite. That is weak: it simply repeats the claim, and in a less clear form. A total made directly from individual units is more obviously finite than a total made from groups, each of which contains a finite number of units. But the opponent can still object that this works only if there are finitely many groups, which has not been shown. This can mislead us into thinking that because each part is finite, the whole must be finite. That does not follow.[^V02-311]

The seventh proof is this. Any infinite series, whether it is a chain of causes and effects or something else, must contain groups of a thousand. The number of thousand-groups in it must either equal or exceed the number of individual units. Both are clearly impossible, because there must be a thousand units for each thousand-group. For example, one hundred thousand units make one hundred groups of a thousand.

The other possibility is that there are fewer thousand-groups than individual units. Then the individual units divide into two groups: one as large as the number of thousand-groups, and the rest. The first group must lie either toward the finite end of the series or toward its infinite end. Either way, the whole series would have to be finite. If the series is infinite in both directions, mark a point in it. This gives us a finite end, and we can use the same two possibilities.

In the first case, the number of thousand-groups is finite because it lies between two limits: the finite end of the series and the point where the second group begins. If the number of thousand-groups is finite, the whole series is finite, since it is made up of a finite number of groups, each containing a finite number of units. In the second case, the group made up of the excess units lies toward the finite end. It contains 999 times as many units as there are thousand-groups, and is bounded by the end of the series and the start of the thousand-groups. So this group too is finite. The number of thousand-groups must then be finite, and with it the whole series.

An objection to this proof, and to some of the earlier and later ones, is that the claim “equal, greater, or less” may not apply: equality and difference are features of finite numbers. If equality means only that each part of one group has a matching part in the other, we do not agree that this is impossible for infinite groups. For example, the numbers from one onward can match the numbers from ten onward, even though the second group starts later and the first has nine more numbers. Even if we accept that objection, we can deny that the smaller group has a definite end. If the series is infinite, the part toward the infinite end is infinite too, as are its groups of thousands, hundreds, or tens. The claim that there are two groups, with one ending where the other begins, is false.[^V02-312]

The eighth proof rests on the order of the series. In any ordered chain of causes and effects, if one member is removed, every member after it must also disappear. So an actual chain made up entirely of effects must contain a cause that comes first among the causes.[^V02-313] Without it, all the later levels that depend on it would disappear too. If we suppose there is an upward chain with no first cause, the whole chain would disappear. That contradicts our assumption that every member in the chain is an effect. In short, it makes no sense to say both that every member in the chain is caused by what comes before it and that no single cause supports the whole chain.

The ninth proof is the shortest, and the strongest, according to al-Fārābī.[^V02-314] Every member of an actually existing ordered chain that goes on without end is such that it exists only if another member before it already exists. So all the infinite members of the chain would have to wait for something before them to exist. Reason asks: Where, then, can anything in the chain begin to exist, so that another thing can follow it?

The tenth proof[^V02-315] is this. The proposed infinite chain of causes[^V02-316] and effects must be either even or odd. If it is even, the next number is odd and has one more member; if it is odd, the next number is even and has one more. Any number that is less than another must be finite, since it is bounded by two ends: its beginning and the next member beyond it.

One might object that not every number that cannot be divided into two equal groups is odd. That is true only of finite numbers, since even and odd apply only to finite numbers. Another way to put the proof is to say that any number can be increased, so it is always less than another number. The flaw in that claim is clear.

#### Clarification

The proofs above show that an ordered chain cannot extend without end in the direction of causes and causal priority. They do not show that it cannot extend without end in the direction of effects.[^V02-317] The reason is that, if an ascending chain of causes had no first member, reason could not identify any cause that must exist first and from which the whole ordered chain could begin to exist.[^V02-318] This is what makes such a chain impossible. A descending chain of effects is different.

The same point applies to all the proofs given earlier, including those based on one-to-one comparison, reciprocal relations, and the different ways things can be causes or effects. In each case, the chain is judged impossible only when two conditions come together: its members are ordered, and they all exist together in actuality at the infinite end of the chain. As noted earlier, the difference is that ordered causes exist in the world alongside the thing they cause, while their priority over it is established by analysis and by the mind's way of considering them.

If the ascending chain had no end, all its infinitely many causes would be supposed to exist at the same level as the effect and together with it.[^V02-319] So both order and co-existence would be present at the level of the effect.[^V02-320] In a descending chain, however, the ordered effects do not all exist together at the level of their cause. An effect's own incomplete, dependent existence does not become actual at the level of the cause's complete, causal existence. The cause is different: its existence is necessary at the level of the effect and encompasses it.[^V02-321]

So order and co-existence at a prior level occur only in a chain that rises toward its causes, not in one that descends toward its effects. The proofs establish that the chain must end in the first direction, but not in the second. This is the gist of what our teacher, Sayyid, explained.

#### Chapter 5 — Why the Chain of Causes Must Have an End

The proofs given so far show that causes are finite in every respect, that each chain has a first principle, and that there is one first principle for all of them. The efficient and final causes come before the material and formal causes. In cases where the agent and its goal are not the same, the goal comes before the other causes, because it is what makes the agent act. Once we have shown that the chains of efficient and final causes are finite, the chains of material and formal causes must be finite too, since they come before the other two in this respect. The form, in one sense, is also an efficient cause, so it belongs with the final cause in that class. The proof that the chain of agents is finite can therefore also show that every level of causes is finite.[^V02-322]

Since this proof has usually been applied to efficient causes, let us prove that the causes that are parts of a thing's existence and come before it in time are finite too. These are specifically called elements, because an element is a part that remains in a thing while it is still only potential.

First, if one thing is present in another in its entirety, we do not say that the second thing came from the first. A human being, for example, is present in full in a person who writes; we do not say that the writer came from humanity. When one thing depends on another in every respect, we do not say that it came from what it depends on. Nor do we say that white came from black, since no part of blackness remains in whiteness. So when something comes after something else in every respect, we do not say it came from that earlier thing.

But when some parts of one thing remain in another and other parts do not, we can say that the second came from the first. We say that air came from water, for example, because water is not present in air in its entirety, though part of it remains. We also say that white came from black, or that a chair came from wood, because the wood must change before it becomes a chair. So for one thing to be said to come from another, two things must be true: it must depend on some part of the earlier thing, and it must not remain together with another part of it.

With this use of the word in mind, matter can mean either the part that receives a form or what can become such a part—for example, water that can become air. In that change, what received the form of water becomes able to receive the form of air.[^V02-323]

The first kind of matter must be finite. If every receiver had another receiver before it without end, one essence would have infinitely many parts. That is impossible.[^V02-324] The second kind of matter can be explained as follows. The matter of air can receive the form of water, and the matter of water can receive the form of air. Each kind can change into the other. Neither kind, simply as a kind, must come before the other; a particular portion of water may come before a particular portion of air.[^V02-325] We do not rule out an endless sequence of materials in this second sense. Each particular thing may come from another particular thing before it, as the points in a continuous motion follow one another without existing all together. There is no contradiction in that sequence having no end.[^V02-326]

Formal causes, however, must be finite for two reasons. First, the final form is a cause of the form before it, so if forms had no end, neither would causes. Second, forms are parts of an essence, and one essence cannot have infinitely many parts.

#### Guidance

Matter—the subject in which a thing's existence is possible—has two kinds.[^V02-327] When a quality appears in a subject, it either causes something already present there to disappear or it does not.
