If it does not, the new quality is not a substantial form, the kind of form that makes the subject what it is.[^V02-328] If it were such a form, the subject would have needed another substantial form before it appeared. That earlier form would either remain or disappear.[^V02-329] If it remained, the subject would already be constituted by it and would not need the new form; the new quality would be an accident, not a substantial form. If the earlier form disappeared, the new form would have caused something to disappear, contrary to our starting assumption.

So any quality that appears in a subject without removing something already there is an accident, not a substantial form. We know that a thing's qualities, unless imposed on it or acquired by chance, arise by nature. There must therefore be a substantial form in the subject that leads it toward that accident. The substantial form is the first stage of its perfection, and the accident is the next.

By their nature, forms move toward their full perfection through accidents, unless something blocks them or a needed condition is absent. An accident that replaces another may be a block; seeds that do not sprout without sunlight are an example of a missing condition. Once a thing reaches its perfection, its nature cannot turn it back toward deficiency, because one and the same nature cannot both draw it toward something and turn it away. This proves that whenever a quality appears in a subject without displacing anything already there, the subject naturally moves toward it and cannot move away once it has attained it. A boy, for example, naturally grows into adulthood; once he has become a man, he cannot return to the seminal stage.

#### Objection and Answer

In *The Healing*, Avicenna raises an objection here. Someone who has no belief about a question may come to believe something false about it. Since that belief does not perfect the mind, does this not refute the claim that every quality that appears in a subject without making something disappear is a perfection?

I reply that a false belief can, in one respect, complete some simple-minded souls, because it is a positive state, and existence is better than nonexistence. It is harmful because it destroys the mind's readiness for the perfection proper to reason. In this respect it is like a poisonous quality: it completes an element but harms an animal. The same is true of blameworthy traits such as injustice and greed. They may complete some lower powers of the soul, but they diminish the higher power of reason.

False belief is a mental image and a positive state, so it can complete the intellect while it is still bound up with imagination. What offers no perfection at all is simple ignorance: it is not a quality but the absence of one.[^V02-330]

In the other kind of change, a quality appears in a subject and causes something to disappear. What disappears may be a substantial form, as when air replaces water; a quality, as when black replaces white; or a quantity or shape. These cases are clear. In general, anyone who allows reversal in this second kind does so because matter can change from water into air and then back again.[^V02-331] The first kind is different, because a thing's essence cannot change into another essence.

It follows, the argument says, that changes in the first kind cannot be reversed, while changes in the second kind can be. Someone might object that this division is incomplete. The transformation of elements into plants and animals cannot belong to the first kind, since such changes can be reversed: plants and animals can become elements again. But it cannot belong to the second kind either, because in that kind a new quality must remove an existing, opposite quality. The coming of a plant or animal into being does not seem to remove any such contrary quality.[^V02-332]

The answer is that a single element is not ready to receive an animal form, for example. It becomes ready only when a balanced mixture arises, and that mixture replaces the element's pure, strong qualities. The mixture's relation to those pure qualities is therefore an instance of the second kind of change, which can be reversed. Once the mixture exists, however, receiving an animal form perfects it. This is like a boy growing into a man: he naturally moves toward adulthood and never moves back from it. Animal life does not begin until the body has first become a suitable mixture, just as a man does not become a boy and an embryo again. The development of an animal therefore involves both kinds of change and is not outside the two kinds of matter.

There is another way to divide matter. A subject may receive a form on its own, as basic matter receives bodily form, or it may receive a form together with something else. In the second case, those things must come together in some kind of composition. The composition may involve a change, or it may not. When it does, one change may be enough to reach the result, or several changes may be needed. When no change is involved, examples include a syllogism formed by joining premises, or a number formed by joining units. The units may be limited in number, as in these examples, or unlimited, as with an army.

#### Eastern Wisdom

On careful reflection, we can see that when a quality appears in matter without replacing anything, that matter has no settled nature of its own. Anything with a settled nature becomes matter for something else only after its nature disappears. This shows that the elemental forms must be partly removed before the elements can become matter for a new mineral, plant, or animal form. A thing[^V02-333] does not naturally move toward something opposed to it, except when the difference is only one of greater or lesser completeness or strength. It moves only toward what completes and strengthens it.

Fire, for example, is opposed to animal life: fire burns and destroys an animal. Water, when it overwhelms an animal, drowns and kills it. The same is true of the other elements. So neither one element nor all the elements together move toward animal life. Rather, an active power frees the matter from their forms and moves it toward greater perfection. This power must be a substantial one. It cannot be, as some take Avicenna and others to mean, the quality produced by mixing the elements. An accident cannot produce a substantial change or move matter upward except by preparing it or serving as an instrument. But the question here is what actively moves it. Also, an accident depends for its existence on a substantial form. The elemental forms are therefore more fit to act and move than their qualities, which serve as their instruments. We have already shown that these forms, or any one of them, cannot be what moves matter toward animal life.

It follows that, within the elements, there is a form of the same general kind as the animal form,[^V02-334] though not the same specific kind. It moves toward animal perfection after passing through the mineral and plant levels. Nature does not skip a level of perfection; before reaching a higher one, it passes through the lower levels. This shows that change takes place in the category of substance, and that things naturally move toward the higher spiritual realm unless something prevents them. The discussion of goals will make this clearer, God willing.

#### Chapter 6 — Can Something Simple Both Receive and Act?

Most philosophers hold that one thing, in the same respect, cannot both receive and act. They add “in the same respect” to distinguish this from fire, which, as they put it, gives heat through its form and receives heat through its matter. We will return to this. Later philosophers say a thing can both receive and act without this qualification.

Here is the more careful view. If receiving means being affected or changed, a thing cannot be changed by itself. And if what it receives would make it more complete, it cannot make itself more complete. But if receiving means only that a thing has a feature that follows once its own nature is complete,[^V02-335] it may bring about a feature that necessarily follows from its nature and cannot be separated from it. This is especially true of features that belong to a thing's nature, particularly a simple thing's. In such cases, the thing that produces the feature and the thing that has it are one and the same: the thing is both active and receptive in the same respect. Avicenna says this plainly in several places in his *Notes*. This need not apply only to simple things. A thing made of parts may also have a feature that necessarily follows from its nature; the feature does not change or make that nature more complete. In truth, features that necessarily follow from existence, like those that follow from natures, have the same source and bearer in one respect: fire and heat, water and moisture, and earth and density.

The same is true of compounds and the features that belong to them by nature. They need matter only for qualities to arise or for them to grow more complete. Fire needs matter for its nature and form, but it does not need matter to stand between being fire and being hot. Nor is there a separate cause between a thing and a feature that necessarily follows from it. If we imagine fire existing without an outside cause or a separate receiver, it would still be hot. Two arguments have been used to show that one thing cannot both receive and act. The first says that receiving and acting are two effects,[^V02-336] so they cannot come from one thing. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī objects that acting and being affected are not real features that need a cause, as we have already shown; and even if they were, he says, one thing could still produce two effects. I reply that both objections fail. Common sense tells us that giving and receiving are real features. Anyone who denies this is denying what is plain. The reason some call them mere ideas is that, if causing were a real feature, it would need another cause between it and its source. We answered this when discussing existence, unity, and related ideas.[^V02-337] The second objection will be answered when we discuss whether one source can produce many things.

Another objection says that, if your rule were right, one thing could not receive one effect and produce another. You answer that this is allowed because it acts through its own nature but receives through its being affected[^V02-338] by what it produces. Then why not say that one thing can receive and act on the same thing in different respects? If you reply that a thing cannot be affected by itself, that is the very question under debate. Why could it not act and be affected in two respects, as when a person treats himself? If you say the argument assumes one and the same respect, then it proves nothing, since the two ideas have different meanings.[^V02-339]

I reply that philosophers have not said that a simple thing can receive one effect and produce another, so this is no counterexample to their rule. The example people imagine is an intellect standing between God and all possible things: it receives from God and acts on them. But an effect does not exist because the intellect receives existence or is acted on by it. As we explained earlier, there is no receiving or being affected in this account of existence. What is brought about is existence itself, not existence added to an essence as a feature in the world.

The mind may, however, divide a possible thing into its essence and its existence. It may then say that the essence can receive existence: first the mind considers the essence on its own, apart from existence, and then considers existence as joined to it. But when the essence is considered apart from existence in this way, how could it act on anything?[^V02-340] This shows that when receiving is only a distinction made by the mind, the multiplicity is only in thought. When receiving occurs in reality, the receiver and what it receives are really distinct. Yet if receiving does not mean being passively affected, it may be the very same as acting.

The example of a person treating himself does not apply. This is not merely two ways of considering one thing, as when an intellect knows itself. The person has different aspects: as a doctor, with the skill and plan for treatment, he is the source of the action; as a body with the ability to be treated, he is the recipient.

The second argument says that what receives relates to what it receives as something it may receive, while an active cause relates to its effect as something that must follow. A complete cause, precisely because it is complete, makes its effect necessary. If one thing were both receiver and cause of the same effect, its relation to that effect would be both possible and necessary. These cannot both be true. If the consequences contradict each other, then the thing itself involves a contradiction.[^V02-341]

I reply that this argument applies only to things ready to receive something, where what they receive is still only a possibility; these differ from active powers. This is what happens when things combine physically. The argument also applies in thought to natures that carry the possibility of existence, since a nature considered by itself differs from what brings it into existence. But when a thing has a feature that necessarily belongs to its nature, its relation to that feature is possible only in the broadest sense of “possible.” On the account we have given, features that necessarily belong to a thing do not overturn the argument.

Some thinkers—including al-Rāzī, the author of *al-Muṭāraḥāt*, and many later philosophers—allow a simple thing to be both active and receptive, and say this happens. They argue that natures give rise to features that necessarily belong to them and also bear those features. A nature must itself require a feature that necessarily belongs to it; otherwise, if the outside causes of that feature were removed, the nature could remain without it. Then the feature would not truly be necessary. The nature also bears its necessary features, since they are present in it and nowhere else. Thus possibility belongs to the natures of possible things,[^V02-342] evenness follows from the nature of four, and a triangle's angles add up to two right angles because of the nature of a triangle.

Someone might object that these essences are composite, so one part may be the source of the feature while another part receives it. I reply, first, that every composite includes something simple, and each simple part has some feature that necessarily belongs to it—at the very least, it is a thing or is possible in the broadest sense. Second, a composite nature has its own unity. A feature that follows from the whole when its parts are together cannot be caused by just one part, or it would have appeared before the parts were joined. Nor can one part alone bear it: a triangle's surface alone cannot have angles adding up to two right angles, nor can its three sides alone. The whole triangle bears that feature, and the whole triangle brings it about. So one and the same thing is both recipient and cause in one respect.

We can also see this in descriptions everyone accepts of God: that He must exist and that He is one.[^V02-343] His perfections—knowledge, power, and will—cannot be additions to Him. Descriptions such as “necessarily existent” and “knowing” are different: they are ideas the mind draws from His reality. So God's essence is simple, yet it is both the source of these descriptions and what they describe.

Also, according to Aristotle, the First Teacher, and his followers—including al-Fārābī and Avicenna—God’s knowledge of things consists of forms that match those things. The forms of possible things differ from God's essence, yet they are necessary consequences of it and are said to be in it. On this account, the same divine essence is both their source and their bearer.

This is where later philosophers, especially al-Rāzī, became confused. They treated “active” and “receptive” as if they meant the same thing in every use, without seeing the difference between the two cases. By treating different meanings as if they settled the matter, they set aside the proofs and fell into serious error about God's true attributes. They came to believe that these attributes are additions to the divine essence, as if the essence by itself lacked divine perfection. But the proof that God's true perfections are one with His essence and that He is one does not depend on this argument.[^V02-344] Even if this argument did not apply to necessary features, that would not justify saying that God's perfect attributes are added to His essence. God is far above any such shortcoming.

There is another objection to the argument based on the difference between possibility and necessity. A thing's being able to receive something, the objection says, means only that it is not impossible for that thing to receive it. This is possibility in the broad sense, and it does not conflict with necessity.

The reply is that readiness means more than “not impossible.” A thing is ready to receive something only if it could receive it or fail to receive it. This is possibility in the narrower sense: the thing has not yet received what it could receive. Even if we use “possibility” in the broad sense, we cannot treat it simply as one of its two forms, necessity and this narrower kind of possibility. We use the broad meaning in a way that, in this argument, still requires the narrower kind and therefore conflicts with necessity. In short, matter that can receive a thing's existence is not the same as the active power that makes that thing necessary. No thoughtful person should deny that one and the same thing, simply as one thing, cannot receive a perfection from itself.

#### Chapter 7 — How Thoughts Can Help Bring Things into Being

In brief,[^V02-345] earlier discussions of powers and changing natures have shown that whatever brings bodies and their natures into existence must itself be independent of matter. Anything that exists independently of matter must be an immaterial form. So the sources of things that come into being are forms—and even thoughts.[^V02-346]

More specifically, a soul's strong and settled thoughts can cause changes in the body without one body physically acting on another. A thought can produce heat without something hot, or cold without something cold. Several things show this. First, the power that moves a person—or an animal—is capable of producing either of two opposite actions. One cannot happen rather than the other unless something tips the choice. What tips it is the thought that one action will be pleasant or useful.[^V02-347] That thought is what makes the action more likely. If the thought could affect the body's instruments only through another physical instrument, then its effect on that second instrument would need a third physical instrument, and so on without end. That is impossible. So a soul's thoughts can affect bodies without physical instruments standing between them.

Second, as we will see when we discuss the heavenly bodies, their movements begin with their thoughts and desires. Third, we see this in ourselves. When we decide to write, we write if nothing prevents us; the thought begins the will.[^V02-348] When we imagine something pleasant and hope to get it, our face may redden and our body grow excited, even if the thing we hope for does not exist. When we imagine something frightening and expect it to happen, our face may pale and our body may shake, even if the thing we fear does not exist.

We also see[^V02-349] that someone who can walk along a log laid on the ground may hesitate to cross the same log when it bridges a deep drop. They imagine falling so strongly that their power of movement obeys the thought, as it naturally obeys thoughts. Night emissions during sleep are another example.

Fourth, if a sick person becomes firmly convinced that they will recover, they may recover; if a healthy person becomes firmly convinced that they are ill, they may become ill. The gaze of a person with the evil eye is said to affect others without a physical tool. Skilled doctors are also said to treat illness through mental images and thoughts. It is told that a king once suffered a severe paralysis. His doctor knew that physical treatment would not help, so he waited for a chance to be alone with the king. Then he insulted him with harsh and vulgar words until the king became deeply upset. The king's natural heat flared up, and his body grew strong enough to fight the illness. The only cause was the king's thoughts and feelings.

Once this principle is accepted, it is easier to believe in prophecy. A soul may reach such strength and dignity that it heals the sick, makes the wicked ill, or changes one element into another—turning something that is not fire into fire. Through prayer, it may bring rain and prosperity at one time, or earthquakes and the sinking of land at another. The elements share a common matter that can receive every form. The relation of weak, individual souls to the matter of their own bodies is like the relation of powerful, more universal souls to other matter. Just as our thoughts can begin small, particular events, the thoughts of those souls may begin great events, though this is rare and strange. Talismans and magic are of the same kind. As Avicenna says, the active powers above and the receptive powers below can come together in remarkable ways.

One point related to this discussion is that a general idea cannot by itself bring about one particular action. A general idea is shared by its individual cases and relates to each of them in the same way. If it caused one rather than the others, one possible outcome would occur without a reason. That is impossible.

#### An Objection and Its Answer

Someone might object that everything that enters existence is particular, though it has a general essence. So a particular thing can come into being only through a particular will. But philosophers say that God's knowledge and will are general,[^V02-350] even though they agree that these are sources of the existence of possible things. Put another way, they say that the thoughts of immaterial sources cause bodies and qualities in the worlds of creation and generation. But these thoughts are general, while the things that come into being are particular. The things imagined by the first principles cannot exist here, and the things that exist here are not what those principles imagined. So the philosophers' claim fails.

The answer is that “particular” has two meanings. In the first, a thing has many real examples of its kind. In the second, it has no other real example, even if the mind can imagine another. In the first case, no one example is singled out for existence without something beyond its essence and its necessary features. The mind cannot know which one it is without bodily tools,[^V02-351] and a general will cannot choose one over the others. In the second case, however, one imagined example can be singled out for existence by a feature that necessarily belongs to its own nature.[^V02-352] The mind can know it, and a general will can bring it into being, because its individuality does not depend on outside circumstances. There are no other examples like it that differ only in their individual features.

The general flow of existence,[^V02-353] the general will, and eternal providence extend to all things brought into being, whether outside time or within it. Yet some things come into being before others, or some do and others do not, because of causes that belong to their nature or conditions that come from outside. The causes that belong to their nature include the immaterial intellects; outside conditions include whatever prepares things to receive their effects.

For example, a person's decision to make a pilgrimage causes each step they take, but each particular step depends on the previous step having brought them to that point on the road. You already know that active causes produce particular effects through causes that prepare for them. These bring the active cause closer to its effect when it was not close enough before. This is because every event that begins in time follows another event.

If a kind could have many individual examples, the general will would not by itself single out one of them. A particular thing needs an added condition that makes it possible here and now, in this matter. But if a kind can have only one individual, the general will can bring that particular thing into being: its own possibility is enough for it to receive existence. This differs from one example among many of the same kind. The kind's possibility alone does not make that individual possible; the matter in which it will exist must also be prepared for it.

#### Chapter 8 — Is a Cause Stronger than Its Effect?

It is clear that a cause must be stronger than its effect in the respect that makes it a cause. We cannot decide at once whether it is stronger in other respects. Avicenna discusses this in detail. An effect may depend on its cause either for its nature or for its individual identity. In the first case, the cause must differ from the effect in nature; otherwise, the thing would cause itself. In the second case—like one fire being caused by another, or a son by his father—the effect cannot be stronger than its cause in that nature. Any extra strength in the effect would itself need a cause. That added strength is not already present in the cause for it to give, and it cannot come from the effect’s matter: matter receives an effect through its readiness; it does not produce the effect.

Can an effect be equal to its cause? We may mean equal in nature or equal in existence. If we mean equal in nature, the matter of the two may be equal or different.

If their matter is different, the two may be equally able to receive the effect, or they may differ. An example of the first is the matching movement of the surface of fire and the surface of the Moon’s sphere.[^V02-354] An example of the second is the light from the Sun and the light from the Moon.[^V02-355] The two lights differ in strength. Those who take this difference to mark a difference in nature count them as two kinds; those who see it as a difference in secondary features count them as one kind.

If the matter is equal, then the recipient may be free of anything that resists the effect, or its matter may contain something that resists it. Freedom from resistance is full readiness, which comes in three forms. The matter may contain something that helps the effect and remains with it, as when water cools something. Or it may contain something that resists the effect but disappears as the effect takes hold, as when black hair turns gray. Or it may contain neither help nor resistance, as when something without a taste takes on a taste.

In all three cases, the recipient can become fully like the cause. Fire can change water into fire, for example, and salt can turn honey into salt. The forms that make these things what they are, as people generally hold, do not come in stronger and weaker degrees. The recipient’s matter can take on the cause’s form because the two kinds of matter are alike, and nothing resists or competes with the effect. So the effect should come about fully.

But if the matter contains something that resists the effect, its readiness is incomplete. Water’s resistance to being heated by fire is an example: water’s nature holds it back from taking on heat. In this case, the recipient is always weaker than the cause. Its matter resists the effect, while the cause’s matter has no such resistance. A thing with an obstacle is never equal to one without it. That is why something other than fire, when heated by fire, does not become as hot as fire.

Someone might object that metals melted and cast by fire become hotter than fire: they burn the hand on contact, while fire does not. Avicenna gives several answers in *The Healing*. The molten metal is thick and sticky, stays in contact with the hand, and is hard to remove. Also, fire is not pure. It is mixed with other things, and has many surfaces that do not touch one another. It is mixed with particles of air and earth that break up its full heat. So the heat we feel from molten substances is stronger than the heat we feel from fire.

All this concerns cause and effect that share the same nature. Their natures may match, but their existence cannot be equal, because the cause comes before the effect: the cause gives existence, and the effect receives it. A fire caused by another fire may match it in fiery nature, but the fire that gives existence comes first—not as fire, but as an existing thing. A father likewise comes before his son, not in being human, but in existence.

If an effect shares neither nature nor matter with its cause, but shares only existence, then existence in the cause is stronger, earlier, more independent, and necessary. Avicenna, however, says that two existences do not differ in strength or weakness, because existence simply as existence cannot vary in this way. He says the cause and effect differ in three respects: before and after, independence and dependence, necessity and possibility. I think that by “necessity” he may mean the broad idea of existence that the mind applies to natures. We then describe a nature’s relation to existence as necessary, possible, or impossible.[^V02-356] This may be why he says existence “as existence” cannot vary in strength. But those who speak of real existence—the kind that rules out nonexistence—agree that it can be stronger or weaker. Avicenna himself says in many places that some things have stronger existence and others weaker, such as time and motion. A nature may also exist in the mind or outside it. Its existence outside the mind is stronger, because it produces effects that its existence in the mind does not.

#### Chapter 9 — When Can a Complete Cause Exist Alongside Its Effect?

Know[^V02-357] that we have shown that a thing’s nature is its form and the source of its final distinguishing feature. If the form existed without matter, or the final distinguishing feature existed on its own, it would contain all the meanings that make up the thing’s nature or existence. These would follow from the form or feature. For example, if the human form existed without matter, it would be the source of speech, life, sensation, nourishment, reproduction, and bodily existence. But because it is complete and does not need to carry out these actions, it does not perform them as it does when it is incomplete and still developing.[^V02-358] Likewise, the rational feature of a human being necessarily includes the idea of animal life and all that comes with it.

Forms need causes only for the way they exist in the world, because each form is one thing with its own natural unity. Once a form exists through its causes and conditions, the kind made up of that form and its nearby matter must also exist alongside it, without needing another cause. In this sense, people say that the complete cause of the compound exists with the compound. This is to treat something that exists only as a by-product as if it existed in its own right. As we have said, the compound exists only as a by-product, and it is caused only as a by-product. Something caused in this way may exist alongside what causes it in this way, because it does not depend on that cause in its own right. Keep this point in mind; it belongs to Eastern wisdom.

#### Chapter 10 — Six Distinctions Shared by the Four Kinds of Cause

There are six distinctions that apply to all four kinds of cause.[^V02-359] The first is the difference between a cause that acts in its own right and one that acts only incidentally. An agent acts in its own right when its nature brings about the effect. An incidental agent does not. This happens in several ways. First, an agent may directly remove one thing, and the opposite appears as a result. The purgative scammony, for example, is said to cool the body. Its direct action is to remove bile; when the bile is gone, coolness follows and is attributed to the scammony. Second, an agent may remove an obstacle without bringing about some opposite effect. Someone who pulls away a support is then called the one who brought down the roof. Third, a thing may have many features. It may bring about an effect through one feature, but be called its cause only incidentally when we describe it by another. So people say, “The writer builds,” “the builder writes,” or “the black thing moves.”

Fourth, an outcome that happens by chance may be attributed to a natural or chosen agent. A falling stone, for example, may injure someone. The stone’s direct action is to fall; it only happens that a person lies in its path.[^V02-360] Another example is the earth’s dryness, which preserves its irregular, non-spherical shape. Fifth, something that simply accompanies an agent, though it need not be there, may also be called an agent incidentally.

Matter, too, can be a cause in its own right or only incidentally. Matter in its own right is able, by its nature, to receive a particular form. Matter is called a cause incidentally in two ways. First, we may take a thing that receives a form together with the opposite of that form, and call the thing matter for the form it receives. Water, for example, may be called the matter of air. Second, we may take a receiver together with a feature that it does not need in order to receive a form. This is what happens when someone says that a doctor treats himself: he is being treated as a sick person, not as a doctor.[^V02-361] A form in its own right is like the shape of a chair; a form incidentally is like its color, black or white. The difference between a goal in its own right and one only incidental will be explained later.

The second distinction is between near and distant causes. A near agent acts directly, with no intermediary between it and its action, like a tendon that moves a limb. A distant agent is the soul, or something that comes before it in the chain of causes. Between them are the power that moves the tendon, the desire that moves that power, and the judgment that gives rise to the desire.

Near matter can receive a form without anything else being added to it or any further change taking place. The parts of a body are near matter for the form of the body. Distant matter cannot receive the form on its own, or it must first go through other changes. In the first case, one bodily fluid is matter for an organ’s form. In the second, food is matter for the body’s fluids, or semen is matter for the form of an animal, since many stages must come first. A near form is squareness in a square; a distant form is angularity. A near goal of medicine is health; a distant goal is happiness.

The third distinction is between particular and general causes. A particular agent causes one thing, like fire burning one object. A general agent causes many, like fire burning many objects. A general agent may act on everything, as God does, or only on some things, as other agents do. Particular matter can receive only one form, like a human body receiving its human form. General matter can receive many forms, like wood becoming a bed, a chair, or something else; the first matter is matter for everything. Near and particular are not the same: something can be near and general, like wood as matter for a bed and other wooden things. A particular form is the definition, distinguishing feature, or special property of a thing; general forms are its broader kinds. A particular goal can be reached by only one route, while a general goal can be reached in several ways.

The fourth distinction is between individual and universal causes. An individual cause may be a particular thing, a kind of thing, or a broad class, and it causes an effect at the matching level. A universal cause has no one-to-one counterpart, like nature’s power to heal through a remedy or the craft of the person who makes it. The same distinction applies to matter. For forms, universal and individual follow the same distinction as general and particular. A particular goal is, for example, Zayd’s arrest of a certain debtor in one specific act; a universal goal is getting justice from an oppressor.

The fifth distinction is between simple and compound causes. A simple agent is one unified thing; the First Principle is the clearest example. A compound agent depends on several things working together. These may be of the same kind, like several people moving a ship, or of different kinds, like hunger arising from the body’s power to seek food and its power to sense it. Simple matter is first matter as the basis of bodily form; compound matter is a mixture of substances used as a remedy. Simple forms include the forms of water and fire. A compound form is the human form, described here as a whole made up of several features, though that example needs more thought. A simple goal is being full after eating. A compound goal consists of several things we want, none of which would be enough on its own.

The sixth distinction is between what exists only as a possibility and what exists in actuality. An agent may be able to act without having acted yet. Fire, for example, is an agent in relation to something that has not caught fire but could. This ability may be near, like a writer ready to write, or distant, like a child who may learn to write. A receiver may be only able to take on a form, like semen in relation to the human form, or it may already have the form, like a human body. A form may be present in actuality, or it may exist only as the possibility of a form in matter that does not yet have it. A goal, too, may be only possible or already reached. In this way, a goal is like a form: the goal relates to the thing that reaches it much as form relates to the thing that has it. A goal also moves an agent to act, insofar as it acts for that goal.[^V02-362]

#### Chapter 11 — Can a Simple Thing Have a Cause Made of Parts?

Many scholars have said that it can, but the sound view is that it cannot. One careful thinker argues as follows:[^V02-363]

“A simple thing cannot come from a compound. If one part alone is enough to cause it, the effect cannot also depend on the other parts. If a part affects only some of the effect, not the whole thing, then the effect is compound, contrary to what we assumed. If no part has any effect on its own, then either the parts produce something new when they come together or they do not. If something new appears but does not itself exist, it cannot independently bring anything into existence. If it does exist and is simple, an endless chain follows: it too would have to come from a compound. If it is compound, the same regress follows from a simple thing coming out of it. If nothing new appears when the parts come together, they remain as they were before, so the whole cannot act as a cause.

“This would also mean that the cause of every new event must be compound. It must itself begin to exist; otherwise, there would be no reason for the event to occur at one time rather than earlier. If the cause were simple, its coming into being would require a cause of its own. Since it is simple, that cause would also be simple, and the chain would go on without end. But if the cause of the new event is a compound made from something lasting and something new, there is no such endless chain. The new part can be a condition for the lasting cause to bring about the new event. A condition may be the absence of something, so the whole chain need not consist of things that all exist at once.[^V02-364] This would mean that every new thing is compound; otherwise its cause would be simple. It would even mean that every simple thing is eternal, and therefore that the soul is eternal.”

The commentator on *The Wisdom of Illumination* objects that this argument has specific and general counterexamples, and that other arguments oppose it.

As for the first objection, even if none of the parts can act as a cause on its own, each may still contribute to the whole effect. That does not violate the assumption: the point is that no part acts independently, not that it has no effect at all. A part may contribute only when something else is present, as we have already seen in the example of several people moving one stone. The earlier argument says that when the parts come together, they do not need to acquire some extra thing that itself causes the effect. But the absence of an extra cause does not mean there is no added condition that lets the parts act. Here, the condition is their coming together. So the parts do not remain just as they were, and the whole is not ineffective. It acts because the needed condition is now present.

As for the second objection, if that were true, it would lead to an impossible endless chain. The formal part of every newly formed compound is itself new, since it exists at the same time as the compound. If that formal part is simple, that is the conclusion we need. If it is compound, the same question arises again. This cannot go on forever, because a thing cannot be made up of infinitely many parts; the chain must end in something simple. But if a simple thing comes into being, then, on this argument, both its simplicity and its coming into being require a simple cause that has also come into being. That leads to the impossible endless chain we described.

As for the third objection, what you have said may show that a simple effect cannot come from a compound cause. But we have evidence that it can. Once we have established, by the route already described, that there is a simple thing that comes into being, we can argue that its causes must eventually reach something compound. Otherwise, the impossible endless chain we mentioned earlier would follow.

That is the end of what the commentator said in his explanation. In notes I wrote on that book and its commentary, I said that this thinker’s argument is very strong in every respect except his claim that the soul, as a soul, is eternal. None of the three objections raised by that scholar defeats his argument.

The detailed objection fails. It says that a thing may affect the whole effect without acting independently, because its effect depends on something else, as in the example just given. This response does not hold. If each of ten people could independently produce the whole simple effect, provided the other nine were there, then several independent causes could act together. That is clearly impossible. Here is why: if every person is a cause on the condition that the other nine are present, and all ten have the same place and role in causing the effect, then each is an independent cause, contrary to the claim. If one particular person is the cause while the others are only conditions, choosing that person has no reason behind it; the cause would be that one person after all, contrary to the claim. If the ten together are the cause as a group, their unity is only in the mind unless they have a real form that gives them unity. But then the question about that form comes back, as the original argument says. Their being together, or anything like it, either exists only in thought—in which case it cannot produce a real effect in the outside world—or exists in reality, in which case it has itself come into being and the same question arises about its cause. The example of several people moving a heavy object will be explained later.

As for the general objection, we can grant that the formal part of a compound is itself compound and eventually leads to a simple part. But we do not accept that every part of something new must be a separately existing thing that has come into being through a further change beyond its own continuous renewal, as with parts of time and motion.[^V02-365] The simple part therefore need not require a simple cause that itself came into being, and no impossible endless chain follows. The counterargument also fails because it depends on a simple new thing whose coming into being is something added to its very being. We reject that claim, as will be explained.

The view attributed to that thinker—that a new thing’s cause is made of something lasting and something renewed, and that the renewed part’s absence after it has existed helps bring about the new thing—agrees with what philosophers say about how what comes into being is linked to what is eternal through motion. On their account, motion consists of continuously renewed parts, and the end of each part is a condition for the next part to come into being. This also agrees with what we have established and will explain when we prove that the whole world and all its parts came into being. We will do so by showing that substance itself is renewed in its very being, through a continuous reality like motion.[^V02-366] This is the nature that runs through bodies. Its reality remains in a state of renewal: it consists of connected parts, many in thought. Each part’s existence entails the end of the one before it, and its end entails the existence of what follows. This state belongs to that nature in itself; no outside maker gives it this state.

We will show in the discussion of the soul, God willing, that the claim that souls are eternal is wrong. The soul, considered as a soul—that is, as something whose being depends on a body—is not simple, contrary to what that thinker assumed. It depends on a natural body, and its nature, like that of physical nature, combines two aspects: what is actual and what exists only as a possibility.

**A possible objection and a further clarification.** One argument for allowing a simple effect to come from a compound cause runs like this. A part of a cause does not by itself produce a simple effect; the whole cause produces one effect. So the parts need not each have an effect, and there is no need for each to affect the whole effect on its own. Just as each of the ten is not an even number, while ten as a whole is even, the whole can have an effect—the simple effect itself—even if no part does.

Also, a part of a cause made of different kinds of parts may not be able to produce the effect by itself, and it does not follow that it produces only a part of the effect. The same is true of parts of one kind. If a thousand people move a stone a certain distance over a certain time, no one person needs to be able to move the stone just a share of that distance. Perhaps not one of them could move it at all alone. Yet because each has an effect when joined with the others, this shows that the contribution of any one person, when alone, is as good as nothing, even though it matters when the group comes together.

This is what one of the great thinkers said.[^V02-367] My reply, to clarify the issue, is this. Every compound either has a form or it does not. A thing’s form is what completes it and gives it its identity, existence, and unity. As we have said, a thing’s existence is its unity. When that unity is weak, as with a number whose unity consists in a collection of separate parts, its existence is weak too. A collection, simply as a collection, has no existence of its own apart from the existence of its members. And what does not exist cannot act as a cause. In such cases, the effect attributed to the collection is really the effect of its members.

Every fully real being with a true unity must have a unity stronger than the unity of its effect. So if a compound is supposed to cause something that is one, it must have a form that is, in reality, the cause. With that in mind, let us examine the claim that the whole produces one effect. The whole can be considered in two ways: as a collection, or as its individual parts. Considered as a collection, it is a single thing. But its unity is either only in thought, like the unity of an army, or it is real, like the forms that make compounds of the elements into particular kinds of things. If the collection is a cause, there are three possibilities.

First, the parts themselves are the cause. Then each must have an effect, and the collection’s effect must be the combined effect of all its parts. Otherwise, the collection would have no effect at all, since it is nothing beyond its parts. Their being joined is no extra thing that exists in reality; it exists only as an idea, and an idea cannot cause a real thing outside the mind.

Second, the cause is the imagined unity of all the parts together. This is like the first case: this unity is weak and depends on the many parts, so its effect can only be weak and depend on their effects. What really acts as the cause is the set of individual parts, not the group simply as one.

The third possibility works in the opposite way. In examples such as a magnet attracting iron or an antidote countering poison, the cause is truly one in its own right, not a cause because it has parts. So the cause of something that is one must itself be one in its own reality, even if it is multiple in another respect.

As for several people moving a heavy stone, or a ship full of grain sinking even though each grain contributes to its sinking, the truth is that each individual or part has only a weak effect. It may help prepare the material or make it more ready to move. But that effect fades if time passes before the next person or part contributes. When people act at different times, the effects fade as the intervals between them grow longer. Their separate effects do not add up, and the group has no effect as a whole, because each contribution is met and canceled by an opposing influence.

Suppose instead that each contribution, whether visible or not, remained in the affected material and was not erased by the passage of time. Then the same effect would build up from people acting separately over time just as it does when they act together. But each person’s effect can disappear when another acts, because every bodily action lasts for a certain time, long or short, and cannot last beyond it. Even the warmth produced in iron by a weak fire may disappear in an instant. If, in the example of the men moving a heavy stone, each push lasted and added to the next despite the gaps in time, the same amount of movement would eventually be produced when the last man pushed. One man would then seem to have moved a huge stone by his own strength, though in fact it had moved through the combined strength of all of them.

So people need to act at the same time not to create a unity that exists only in thought, but to preserve each person’s effect until the next one contributes. This keeps earlier effects from fading before the later ones arise, and stops each contribution from disappearing when the time of its action is over. Think this through carefully; it will help you understand many other questions, such as why bodily powers have limits in how much they can act on or be affected by other things. God is the source of right judgment and insight.

#### Chapter 12

The essence of a possible thing must exist when its complete cause is present, and cannot exist when that cause is absent. If we set those two conditions aside, it remains possible in itself. These two alternatives apply to a possible thing only under their respective conditions.[^V02-368]

A cause does not have to coincide with the effect’s prior nonexistence. Nor does a thing’s dependence on an agent require it to exist only after it once did not exist. When we say that a new thing is preceded by nonexistence, that sequence belongs to the thing’s own nature; the agent does not make it. First there is nonexistence, then existence, and the fact that this existence comes after nonexistence is a feature of the thing itself. The earlier nonexistence comes from the absence of the cause; the later existence comes from the cause giving the thing existence.

Being new in this way is not an optional feature that can be added to the thing by another cause. If it were, then, apart from that feature, the possible thing would still call for a cause. It would come from the agent without being new, and so would exist from eternity, since nothing would stand between it and the agent.[^V02-369] A separate cause would then give it the feature of being new, which is a contradiction. Or else the thing, by its own nature, would already be either necessary or impossible, and would be possible only because it is described as new. That is also false: a new thing would then be necessary or impossible in itself. It would also be eternal in its existence or nonexistence, and later the opposite would come into being through a cause. That repeats the earlier problem in an even worse form.[^V02-370]

Clearly, if a new thing had existed from eternity, it would not be the very thing that exists after not existing. So, with respect to this particular existence, it must come after nonexistence. This feature belongs to it in itself, without any agent causing it. The agent causes existence itself, whether that existence lasts or later ends. The new thing is not required to exist at all; but once it does exist, its existence must be after nonexistence.

There is no difficulty in saying that a thing may or may not have one feature, while once it has that feature, another feature necessarily follows. What is necessary in this way does not need a cause. So the fact that a thing may or may not exist[^V02-371] does not mean that its existing after nonexistence—or ceasing to exist after existing—must also be something that may or may not happen and therefore needs a cause. Nothing causes the fact that its existence comes after nonexistence, even though there is a cause for its existence, which does come after nonexistence.

Some people think a thing needs a cause only because it is new: once it exists, they say, it no longer needs a cause. That is also false.[^V02-372] If we break coming into being into three parts—prior nonexistence, later existence, and the fact that existence comes after nonexistence—and ask which one makes a thing depend on an agent, none of the three can be the answer. Prior nonexistence is pure absence, so it cannot cause anything. Existence depends on being brought about, which already presupposes the need for an agent; that need itself must have a cause. If existence caused the need for an agent, it would have to come before itself in several stages. Nor can the fact of coming after nonexistence be the cause of that need, since it depends on existence as one of its features, and we have already seen that existence needs a cause. If being new caused the need, it too would have to come before itself in several stages. There must therefore be a fourth factor, beyond these three, that explains why the thing depends on an agent. This[^V02-373] is what fits[^V02-374] the philosophers’ approach.

#### Chapter 13 — Why a Completely Simple Cause Cannot Produce Two Effects That Arise Together

If a thing is truly simple, with no real parts, and its very nature makes it the cause of something, then its whole nature is the cause of that effect. The mind cannot split it into “what it is” and “what causes the effect,” as though it caused the effect through some extra quality, condition, goal, time, or other factor. If it needed any such extra factor, it would not be a simple source; it would be compound.

By a simple source, I mean that the reality that makes it what it is also makes it the source of something else. Its being and its causing are not split between two separate aspects: one that makes it what it is, and another that produces an effect. We are human through one feature, the ability to speak, while writing is a separate skill. If a simple source produced more than one thing at once, its being the source of one thing would have to differ in meaning from its being the source of another.[^V02-375] Its nature would then contain two different aspects, contrary to our assumption. Keep this in mind, and do not get lost in long discussions that add little. Also, do not take “producing” here to mean a mere relation that appears only after two things exist. What matters is that the cause is such that the effect comes from it. The cause must have some particular feature that makes this specific effect come from it rather than another. That feature is what truly makes the cause its source. We call it “producing,” “being the source,” or “the cause’s being such that the effect follows from it.” These words only point to what we mean. Even “feature” here does not mean a relation formed in the mind. It means something real, specifically tied to this effect, which exists before the effect and before the relation that later appears between the two. Sometimes this feature is the cause itself, when the cause acts by its own nature; sometimes it is something added to it.[^V02-376]

If a cause is truly simple in the very respect that makes it a cause, its immediate effect must also be truly simple.[^V02-377] It follows, conversely, that a cause producing more than one effect, with none produced through another, cannot be truly simple. It must be divided either in its nature or in its existence. Think this through.

One of the weakest objections to this proof is that a point at the center of a circle is the endpoint of all the lines drawn from it to the circumference. The different meanings of “being the endpoint” of all those lines do not make the point a compound of infinitely many things. Almost as weak is the claim that a particular unit, when taken with one unit, makes a pair, and when taken with another unit, also makes a pair.[^V02-378] If the two pairs differ, this argument says, then the same unit must somehow count as two.[^V02-379] The person who made this objection seems not to have understood what it means for a truly simple thing to be a source, much less how to prove that such a thing exists.

When Bahmanyār asked Avicenna to prove this point, Avicenna wrote: “If a truly one thing were the source of two things, A and B, then it would be the source of A and of what is not A, since B is not A. That would be a contradiction.”

Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī replied that the opposite of producing A is not producing A, not producing what is not A—that is, producing B. A body, for example, can receive motion and blackness. Since blackness is not motion, the body receives motion and something that is not motion, without contradiction. Avicenna himself makes this point in *The Categories* of *The Cure*: saying “The wine has a smell” and “The wine does not have a smell” brings opposites together. But saying “The wine has a smell” and “The wine has something that is not a smell” does not: the first pair cannot both be true, while the second pair can.

Al-Rāzī says this reply is so plainly mistaken that even people with poor reasoning should see it. He cannot understand how those who claim to be sharp-minded could miss it. He is astonished that someone might spend a lifetime learning logic as a tool to protect the mind from error, then stop using it when faced with the noblest question and fall into an error that even children would laugh at.

I say that this reply also shows clearly that this eminent scholar did not understand what it means for something to be truly one and to be the source of an effect. His position is like that of a person who claims to use logic against Aristotle, the leading sage who founded it. As Avicenna puts it, that person is using logic against Aristotle’s own followers. Here, too, the scholar thinks he can use logic against Avicenna, the foremost of philosophers. Is that not foolish and misguided?

We have already established that, in the sense explained above, being a source is the very nature of a simple cause. A thing, considered simply as itself, is nothing but itself. So if a truly simple cause were the source of A and also of what is not A, its being the source of what is not A would differ from its being the source of A—even though being a source is its very nature. It would then differ from itself. That is a contradiction.

Al-Dawwānī tries to complete Avicenna’s argument this way: producing what is not A is not the same as producing A, so it is not producing A. Anything that produces what is not A is therefore also described as not producing A. If it has two aspects, it can produce A in one respect and not produce A in another, without contradiction. But if it has only one aspect, it cannot have both descriptions, since one and the same aspect would then have to be both ways. More generally, if being described in one way means not being described in another, then in the first respect a thing cannot also have the second description. The two descriptions cannot come together in one respect. There is a problem with this argument. First, contradictory claims cannot both apply to one thing, whether from one aspect or two. The familiar conditions for contradiction apply here.[^V02-380]

No one has required that a contradiction concern one single, truly unified thing.[^V02-381] Second, we can grant that being described in one way may amount to not being described in another.[^V02-382] At most, this means that the second description does not apply. It does not follow that the opposite description must apply.[^V02-383]

Third, this argument would also make any two different concepts contradictory when they apply to one subject in the same respect, such as existence and being a thing. The same reasoning would apply, yet no one says that these descriptions contradict each other.

Fourth, the opposite of a thing’s coming from a cause is its not coming from that cause.[^V02-384] The opposite of its existence is its absence.[^V02-385] Neither opposite is simply the idea conveyed by the descriptions “not coming from a cause” or “nonexistent.” Those negative descriptions can oppose the positive ones when they are themselves what we are talking about. But when we use them to describe a subject, they do not stand in the same opposition. That is why both can be said of the subject without contradiction.

Do not confuse this debate with the proof we have given. We do not mean by “producing” a relation that appears only after the cause and effect exist. We mean the real nature of the simple cause itself, which is what makes the effect come from it. Anyone who understands this will see the difference. We will return to the question in the section on divine things and explain it more fully.

#### Chapter 14 — Can One Effect Depend on Several Causes?

A single, specific effect cannot depend on two independent causes, whether they act at the same time or one after the other.[^V02-386] The reason is this: either a special feature of each cause—or of one of them—helps bring about the effect, or neither special feature does.[^V02-387]

If the distinctive feature of either cause—or of both—contributes to the effect, then the effect cannot come from the other cause alone; it must depend on both together. If neither special feature makes a difference, then the real cause is the feature they share, while their separate features do no work. In either case, there is one cause in the relevant sense, even if it is described in general terms.[^V02-388]

The claim that a cause must be more fully real and more unified than its effect applies to the active cause itself, not to added factors, conditions, or things that prepare the way for it. Avicenna makes this point in the discussion of how matter and form depend on each other in the *Metaphysics* of *The Cure*. He says that form considered simply as form—not one particular kind of form—shares in the cause that brings matter into existence. Someone might object that the matter and form together are not one particular thing, but only one in a general sense, and that a general cause cannot bring about one particular thing. Matter, after all, is one particular thing.

I reply[^V02-389] that a cause which is one in a general sense can still bring about one particular thing if a particular being preserves that general unity. That is what happens here: one separate intellect holds the cause together as one and brings matter into being. Matter’s coming into being is completed only when some accompanying condition is present. It does not matter which of these conditions is present.

There is another way to see the point. If either cause—or both—could act independently, then the effect would have to exist whenever either cause was present. But an effect that must exist cannot depend on something else. In the presence of either cause, it could not need the other. So it could not need both, even though the claim was that it does. This is a contradiction.

A single effect of a particular kind, however, can rightly come from many causes. Heat, for example, may come from movement, sunlight, anger, or contact with fire. The argument above does not apply to effects shared by a whole kind. Many different things can also share one necessary feature. General kinds, for example, exist through their more specific kinds; the general kind is present only through a specific form. The same is true of possibility, shared by things with different natures, and of evenness, which belongs to both four and six, two different kinds of number.[^V02-390]

Difference, too, is a single feature shared by things that differ. Like every accidental feature, it must be explained by the things it belongs to. Someone might argue that different causes must share a general feature, and that this shared feature is why they produce the same effect. But then we can ask what explains the shared feature. If nothing else explains it, that is the point we need. If another shared feature does explain it, the same question comes up again, leading to an endless chain of shared explanations.[^V02-391]

Someone may object that an effect either needs one particular cause by its very nature or does not. If it does, it cannot come from any other cause. If it does not, then it needs no cause at all. I reply that because the effect is possible in itself, it needs some cause or other, not a particular one. Its dependence on this particular cause comes from something on the cause’s side: the cause’s own nature calls for this effect. The effect’s general need for a cause comes from the effect; which particular cause it depends on comes from the cause.[^V02-392]

#### Chapter 15 — Some Principles about the Active Cause

We have learned that every cause that necessarily brings about an effect exists together with that effect. Confusion often arises when we overlook the respect in which something is being considered, or confuse what belongs to a thing in itself with what belongs to it only as a side effect. So when people say that an agent may come before its effect, they do not mean that it comes before the effect in the very role in which it causes it. They mean the same thing considered in another role.

An agent can act in its own right, as a doctor does when treating someone. Or it can be called an agent only indirectly. This may be because it is accompanied by what truly acts. For example, we might say, “The writer is treating the patient,” but the writer treats the patient as a doctor, not as a writer. Or the agent’s direct effect may be one thing, with another result following from it and being attributed to the agent only indirectly. Scammony, for example, is said to cool the body, but that is only a side effect. Its direct action is to purge bile, which then lowers the body’s heat. A doctor is called a cause of health in this way, though the source that restores health is higher than the doctor. Likewise, removing a support is called the cause of a wall’s fall, though the roof’s natural weight causes it to move downward.

The same is true of fire heating nearby things, sowing a seed, and thinking through premises. These are not causes in the deepest sense. The mistake about causes not needing to exist alongside their effects comes from treating indirect causes as direct ones. A son can outlive his father, a building its builder, and heat the fire that produced it. But the builder’s movement first sets a brick in motion; when that movement stops, the brick comes to rest. The end of the movement allows the materials to come together, and their arrangement gives the structure its shape. The material’s nature then helps it remain in that arrangement. In the same way, a father causes the seed to move into the womb, but the giver of forms causes it to take the shape of an animal and remain alive as one. Fire does not create heat; it removes the cold that was preventing heat from arising. The heat in water, and its change into fire, come from the agent that gives the elements their forms. We will show that every body ultimately has an intellectual, nonphysical cause. How could one physical fire be the true cause of another fire’s existence, when neither is prior to the other in the order of causes?

In general, each possible kind whose members share the same essential character must have a cause outside that kind. So the earlier causes in these examples are not causes in their own right. They prepare or assist, and are causes only indirectly. The true agent is the source that gives existence. In the language of the philosophers of theology, it is the giver of being. What natural philosophers call an agent may only move matter that already exists; as we have seen, such a cause merely prepares the way.[^V02-393]

A body contains matter, which is pure potential and dependence, so it cannot give existence. Nor can form do so by itself, since it has no existence apart from matter. Bringing something into existence must itself come from something that exists. If a body or its form were the source of existence, then what is merely potential would give being. These things do not create independently. If they have any role in bringing things into existence, they do so through support from above; they are links and conditions that make existence possible.

The effect’s existence is not equal to the cause’s. The cause exists through itself; the effect exists through the cause. The cause has necessity in itself, while the effect is necessary only when it is related to its cause. In this way, the cause has a stronger claim to existence than the effect. This shows that a quality, whose existence is weaker, cannot cause the existence of a substance, which comes before it. Nor can a composite thing cause something simple, since the simple comes before the composite. A body cannot cause an intellect or a soul; something perceived by the senses cannot cause what is known by the mind; and something dependent cannot cause what is independent. A cause must have a firmer share in existence than its effect.

One of the most false and destructive beliefs is that the world stands on its own and needs nothing from its Creator to preserve it, sustain it, hold it in place, or keep it alive.[^V02-394] As we have seen, this belief is wrong. It also harms the person who holds it in the afterlife and makes them think badly of their Creator. It turns them away from God: they forget Him, neglect prayer, and become absorbed in worldly aims, power, and possessions. They remember God only absent-mindedly, ask Him for things out of vanity or to be seen by others, or turn to Him unwillingly when hardship, disaster, or pain leaves them confused and lost. Many people live as if this belief were true, even if they never say so. Their whole lives pass with God hidden from them. They do not know their Creator as they should, and they live in blindness and confusion in this world—and even greater blindness and confusion in the next.

Those who hold the opposite belief—the belief of those who affirm one God—say that the world was brought into being and remains in its Creator’s grasp. It needs Him in order to continue and cannot do without Him for even an instant. His gift of existence reaches it moment by moment, as one continuous flow. If that gift, preservation, and support were withheld even for an instant, the heavens would collapse, the celestial spheres would vanish, the stars would fall, the foundations of the world would disappear, and every living thing would perish. The whole world would pass away at once, with no time in between.[^V02-395] This is what is meant by the verse, “God holds the heavens and the earth so they do not fall away; if they were to fall away, no one could hold them after Him,”[^V02-396] and by the verse, “The whole earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens folded in His right hand.”

This is one of the sound beliefs that saves the soul and gives it life. It keeps the heart turned toward God, trusting Him in every state and relying on Him in every action. A person who lives this way remembers God, asks Him for what they need, and entrusts their affairs to Him. This brings them near to their Creator, gives life to the soul and calm to the heart, and saves them from ruin.

Here is an analogy that makes the point clear. The world’s existence through God is not like a house built by a builder, or writing left behind by a writer—things that remain after their maker has finished and no longer depend on them. It is like speech coming from a speaker: when the speaker falls silent, the speech no longer exists. It is also like sunlight in the air: while the sun is shining, the light is present; when it sets, the light disappears. The Sun of Existence, however, cannot cease to exist, because that is impossible by its very nature. Speech is not part of the speaker; it is something the speaker does. And the light in the air is not part of the sun; it flows from it. The world’s existence through its Creator is like this: it is not part of His essence, but a gift and a flow of existence from Him.

We should not imagine that the world comes from God automatically, without His choice, as sunlight comes from the sun by nature, whether the sun can prevent it or not. As we will explain, God acts by choice in a way far beyond what ordinary people can picture. Think of a speaker who can speak when he wishes and remain silent when he wishes. God gives generously, shows His wisdom, and may also withhold His gift. The verses about His holding the heavens and the earth point to this.

Earlier philosophers often said that God alone is the source of all existence and that all existence flows from Him. The intermediaries—the conditions and factors needed for many different things to come from Him—do not give things existence. They prepare the way for them.

Some have argued as follows: Anything that is still potential, whether an intellect or a body, cannot give existence. Otherwise, its potentiality—its lack of actual being—would help bring something from possibility into actuality. Nonbeing would then be part of the cause of being, which is impossible. So only what is free of all potentiality, the Necessary Being, can give existence.

Many people have found this argument convincing, but it does not work. A possible thing is called “possible” when we consider its own nature. In reality, though, what is true of it is that it exists in actuality and is made necessary by its cause. The first way of considering it is one level of reality, but that does not mean the thing has that feature in reality as a whole; reality is wider than that one level. Possibility is a negative idea: when we consider a thing in itself, neither its existence nor its nonexistence is necessary. But the thing that exists in reality must be one or the other. Calling it “possible” at one level does not mean that it is actually marked by possibility in every sense.[^V02-397]

This is different from a positive feature. If something has a positive feature at one level, it has that feature in reality. For example, Zayd may be moving in the marketplace, yet we can describe him as not moving in his house. That does not mean that he is actually resting there. The same kind of reasoning applies to the claim that a general nature exists if at least one of its instances exists, and does not exist if all its instances are absent.[^V02-398]

So the argument has not shown that some possible things, such as intellects, cannot give existence, or that nonbeing and potentiality must share in giving existence. Even if possibility is a real feature of a possible thing, it does not follow that the thing acts because it is possible. It may act because it exists.[^V02-399] Color, for example, does not make an animal move or sense. Even if an agent needs possibility in order to act, possibility may be a condition outside the cause rather than one of its parts. Those who allow forms to have effects say that matter helps a form act by setting its place and limiting its effect; they do not mean that matter itself is the immediate cause.[^V02-400]

There is another problem for this argument. These philosophers say that possibility plays a part in the way the celestial spheres come from intellects. If possibility has no part in that process—not even as a condition—their own account fails. Yet possibility is a negative feature, so how can they give it a role in the coming into being of things?[^V02-401]

Possibility[^V02-402] may be a real description of a possible thing, but the thing’s nature is not nothing but possibility. It has other aspects through which it can act. This is especially clear for philosophers who regard the different kinds of existence as genuinely different realities, even though we call them all “existence.” Why, then, should showing that possibility does not act also rule out existence as a cause? This weak argument does not establish the claim.

Suhrawardi, the Illuminationist philosopher, offers another argument in *The Temples of Light*. He says that intellectual beings act, but only as channels for the generosity of the First Cause, who alone is the true agent. A powerful light does not let a weaker light shine independently; in the same way, the overwhelming power of the Necessary Being does not let the intermediaries act independently of His abundant flow and complete strength. In his *Wisdom of Illumination*, he asks whether an imperfect light could act independently in the presence of a light that overpowers it, while that stronger light rules over the very same act. The Light of Lights is present through every intermediary, brings its action about, and sustains every flow of existence. He is the absolute creator, whether there is an intermediary or not; nothing can act without Him.

This argument may be stronger than the previous one for readers who know the teachings of the ancient Persian philosophers. It can also be developed further from the ideas of Illuminationist philosophy. But as it stands, it only persuades; it is not enough for a formal debate. By God’s grace and guidance, we have a sound philosophical proof of this high aim, which will appear later.

Here is a further point. An incomplete agent needs movement and tools to bring what is within it into matter as something actual. A complete agent is one whose form comes to exist in matter because that form already exists in it. Once we establish that there is a First Agent and highest source, with no existence before His, we can see that He can have no prior matter or subject, no other agent, and no goal beyond Himself; those things would take away His first place and priority.[^V02-403] Everything that comes from Him exists through the pure flow of His being, with His knowledge and consent.
