What comes from Him adds no perfection, honor, pleasure, joy, benefit, or escape from blame to Him, because He needs nothing beyond Himself.

The existence by which He is what He is is the same existence by which everything else comes from Him. These are not two separate things or two different aspects of Him. We are different: we are human through one capacity, reason, and we write through another, the skill of writing. In short, the First Agent needs nothing beyond Himself—not a separate quality, movement, or tool—in order to bring something into being. Fire needs heat in order to burn, the sun needs motion in order to light the earth, and someone breaking down a door needs an axe. But the First Agent has no obstacle to overcome and no condition that must be met later.[^V02-404]

There are six ways an agent can act.[^V02-405] The first is acting by nature: something acts without knowing or choosing what it does, and its action fits its nature. The second is acting against nature: something acts without knowing or choosing what it does, and its action goes against its nature.

The third is acting under compulsion: something acts without choosing, even though it could choose either to act or not to act. These first three kinds share two features: the agent does not choose its action, and something else controls or uses it. That controlling power may itself be forced or may act by choice. The rational soul uses some bodily powers in this way. For example, muscles move the limbs because the soul directs them to move.

These powers in the small world of the human being are like the animals in the great world. The soul also uses other powers in a more natural way, such as those that nourish and grow the body, the beating of the pulse, anger, and desire. These powers act through the body’s subtle fluids and spirits, and begin in higher powers of the soul. In the small world, these beginnings are like the movements of the heavenly bodies, which are subject to the higher world of command. Just as those beings do not disobey God and do what they are told, these powers normally obey the rational soul. And just as some people disobey God’s commands, some powers resist what the soul tells them to do.

The fourth kind is intentional action. Here, the action follows a choice, and the choice follows knowledge of the goal the agent seeks. Without any added desire or reason to avoid it, the agent’s basic ability to act or not act is balanced between the two.

The fifth kind[^V02-406] is action guided by knowledge of what is good. The agent’s knowledge of what is truly good in the action is enough to bring it about; no further choice or outside motive is needed. The philosophers in Aristotle’s school call this action through providence.

The sixth kind is action that comes from an agent’s knowledge of itself, which is identical with its very being. This self-knowledge brings about its actions, which are themselves its knowledge of what it brings about. There is no real division in the agent, either in its being or in its action; the difference is only in the words we use.

These last three kinds are all chosen actions, though[^V02-407] the first of them—intentional action—is compelled in one sense. The choice begins in the agent after not existing, and anything that begins needs a cause. So the choice must have a cause, either the agent itself or something else. If the cause is something else, our claim is established. If the agent itself causes its choice, then it either chooses to cause it or does not. If it chooses to cause it, the same question arises again, leading to an endless chain of choices. If it does not choose to cause it, then the choice is brought about without choice, so the agent is compelled by something else. The chain must therefore end in a choice that has always existed: a choice that brings everything about as it is, without any added motive, fresh intention, or outside goal.

Now that you know these six kinds of agency, consider the different views about the First Cause. Some naturalists and materialists, may God leave them to their error, say that the source of everything acts by nature. Most theologians say that it acts by intention. Avicenna and most philosophers in Aristotle’s school say that God acts on things outside Himself through providence, and that the forms in His own knowledge come from Him through satisfaction. Following the ancient Persian and Stoic philosophers, Suhrawardi says that God acts in the sixth way, through knowledge of Himself.

Later, we will show that the First Cause cannot act by nature, against nature, or under compulsion. He is also far above acting by intention, since that would introduce several parts into Him, and even a kind of body. Exalted is He above all of that. He must therefore act either through providence or through satisfaction. In either case, He acts by choice: if He wills, He acts; if He does not will, He does not. This does not mean that He acts by necessity, as many people suppose. A statement such as “If He wills, He acts” is not judged true or false simply by asking whether either part is actually true—or necessary or impossible. Of the two views just mentioned, however, action through providence is the right one. As we will see, the First Cause knows everything before it exists, through knowledge that is identical with His own being. That knowledge gives things existence, so He acts through providence.

An example of these six ways of acting can be found in the human soul and its different actions. The soul’s own thoughts and imaginings arise from it through satisfaction. It also uses the particular powers that come from it, such as the estimating and imagining powers. The soul uses its thinking power to separate and combine particular images until it draws general natures from individual things and works out conclusions from premises.

Those powers cannot perceive themselves, because they depend on the body, and being bodily prevents perception.[^V02-408] The estimating power, which leads the others, even denies the soul itself; how much less can the other limited powers and senses perceive it? Yet the soul cannot use these powers unless it perceives, in a particular way, both the power it uses and what it uses it for. The soul perceives these instruments through its own awareness. It does not perceive them through the powers perceiving themselves, or through another instrument; there is no instrument for perceiving an instrument.

When the soul causes something through a picture or idea alone, it acts through providence. For example, imagining a fall can make someone fall from a high wall. Imagining something sour can make the tongue tighten and squeeze out moisture.[^V02-409] When the soul acts because of outside motives that lead it toward its aims and growth, it acts through intention, as in writing or walking. A good soul may be forced to do wrong, such as commit adultery, give false testimony, or falsely claim that God compels people. The soul acts by nature when it keeps the body balanced, provides its natural warmth, and preserves health. It acts against nature when it brings about fever, illness, excessive weight gain, or wasting away.

#### Chapter 16 — Why a Complete Cause Cannot Be Separated from Its Effect

Here is the argument. Either an agent brings about its effect through its own nature, or it does not. If it needs an added condition—a feature, a choice, a tool, a benefit, or anything else—then the thing we first called the agent is not the true cause. The true cause is the whole combination. We can ask the same question about that combination, until we reach something that acts through its own nature and being, not through a feature added to it. Every complete cause therefore acts through what it is, not through something added to it. Once we see that, we can say that the effect is an essential feature of its cause: it comes from the cause’s own nature and being.

Someone might object that if knowing an active cause means knowing its effect, then knowing the nature of any thing should also let us know its closest result, then the result of that result, and so on. Since every thing has a result, and every result another result, we would have to know an endless number of things all at once. That is clearly impossible.

There are several answers. First, even if knowing the nature of a thing lets us know its essential features, we do not agree that every thing has an endless chain of such features. We know a thing’s qualities, its latest results, and its farthest-reaching effects; we do not know its inner nature, its starting points, and its most distant causes.

Someone might reply that if a thing’s qualities follow from its nature, then its nature must also follow from those qualities. If knowing the qualities lets us know the nature, then knowing that nature should let us know every other quality as well.

But qualities can follow from the things that have them without the reverse being true. A triangle’s three angles always add up to two right angles. But angles that add up to two right angles do not always form a triangle: the two angles on either side of a straight line also add up to two right angles, even though there is no triangle.

Someone might then say that philosophers clearly teach that our knowledge of ourselves is our very self. Since we are always directly aware of what we are, we should know all our qualities, powers, and effects. These include our independence from the body, the fact that we began to exist, and the fact that we can survive the body’s death. We should know all this clearly, without reasoning or learning.

We answer that there are two kinds of qualities: those that exist only when the mind thinks of them, and those that exist whether or not we think about them. The first kind includes calling the soul self-sufficient, independent of a body, possible, something that began to exist, and something that continues after the body dies. Some of these qualities—such as being self-sufficient or independent—mean that something is absent or not present. If these absences were real features of a thing, every thing would have an endless number of features simply because an endless number of things are absent from it. Each absence would need its own cause, and there would be endlessly many causes. Other qualities in this group—possibility, having a beginning, and continuing to exist—would repeat endlessly if they were real features outside the mind. If having a beginning were a real feature, it too would have to have a beginning, and that beginning would have another beginning, without end. The same would be true of continuing to exist.

So these qualities do not exist outside the mind. A thing’s nature gives rise to them when the mind considers them, and even then only when we consider certain proofs or reasons.[^V02-410] Knowing the soul’s nature together with those reasons allows us to know these qualities. The second kind includes the soul’s power, desire, awareness, pleasure, pain, and other experiences that belong to it whether or not we think about them. So anyone who knows themselves knows these qualities and the effects, powers, and natural capacities that come from them.[^V02-411]

Most people, however, are not fully present to themselves. They are busy with things outside them, focused on what their senses take in, and caught up in worldly life. These things distract them from turning inward and seeing what they really are. They know themselves only faintly and pay little attention to themselves, so they overlook some of their own qualities and powers.

The existence of an incomplete soul, strongly attached to the body and its desires, is very weak. Since its awareness of itself is the same as its being, that awareness is dim and easily missed. It becomes unaware of its own qualities, special features, and effects. But bright, strong, complete souls that rule over their powers never lose sight of themselves or their powers. As they witness themselves, they also witness everything that follows from them within that same inner awareness. Their very being bears witness to everything connected with it, as we will explain later. Keep this in mind.[^V02-412]

#### Chapter 17 — The Basic Material of Things and Its Kinds

The basic material of a thing is what can bring that thing into being, either by itself or together with something else. If it acts alone, it may do so without changing in itself, as a board does when someone writes on it. Or it may change. The change may be a change in its condition, such as when metal is made into a statue or a child grows into an adult. In these cases, the material changes in some way—its place, size, or another feature. It may also change by losing something, as white turns to black. Or the change may be in the thing’s substance: wood is cut down to make a bed, or the seed gains new essential features as it develops into an animal, passing through changes of form along the way.

The basic material may also need to be combined with something else. Sometimes it changes in the process, as when yellow myrobalan is made into a medicinal paste. Sometimes it does not, as when wood and stone are made into a house. The individual units that make up a number belong to this group as well. Finally, the material may be the basis of all things, like the first matter, or the basis of several things, like grape juice, which can become vinegar, wine, or molasses.

We have already said that the first matter must have no form of its own. It must be pure potential: nothing but the ability to receive form, with no feature of its own that would make it fit one form rather than another. Someone might object that if “all forms” means every celestial and earthly form, no first matter could fit this description. The matter of the elements cannot receive a celestial form, and the matter of each celestial sphere can receive only its own form. If “all forms” means only the forms of the elements, then only the matter shared by the elements would qualify. So, they might say, the claim must be limited to that shared matter.

But “all forms” means all forms. The first matter can receive any form. It is limited to some forms rather than others only by something outside itself, not by its own nature. It has no actual feature of its own that would make it fit one form and reject another.[^V02-413]

In every kind of example discussed above, the basic material of a thing has no form or settled nature of its own. It is wholly open and undetermined. It may be first matter without any further specification, or matter made specific by something added to it. Either way, the source of its material nature is the first matter, which is the source of lack and limitation. In the opposite direction, true self-sustaining existence is the source of completeness and generosity. The closer something is to the First Reality, the more form, completeness, and actuality it has. The farther it is from Him, the weaker its actuality and the greater its lack and potentiality.

First matter stands at the farthest point from existence. In prophetic symbols and sacred language it is called the Abyss, darkness, emptiness, open space, the lowest of the low, and other names that point to its lowly and almost nonexistent state.

#### Chapter 18 — Names for the Basic Material

Names may be given to things because of what they are in themselves, or because of features and relations that belong to them. “Human” names what a person is; “writer” names a role a person may have. Sometimes a thing has no name for what it is in itself. This is true of the human soul: it has no name based on its own nature. “Soul” names it in relation to the body, which it moves and guides. Calling it a soul does not name what it is in itself, as “human” names what a human being is, or “Zayd” names a particular person. If “soul” means any self or being in general, then it names a broad idea in the mind, not the nature of this particular soul.

So some realities have no name for what they are in themselves; they are named for some added feature or relation. The basic material of things is like this. It has no name for its own nature, but it has names based on different aspects. As something that is only potential, it is called first matter. As something that actually bears a form, it is called a subject, though the word “subject” has other uses in philosophy and logic.

Because it can underlie many forms, it is called matter or clay. Because it is the simplest part reached when a compound is broken down, it is called an element. Because it is the first thing from which a compound is put together, it is also called an element. And because it is one of the basic parts inside a composite body, it is called a component. Philosophers sometimes use these terms loosely, without keeping the distinctions clear.[^V02-414]

They also sometimes call the part of a heavenly body that receives its form “first matter,” even though it is always actual. They call it “matter” too, even though each heavenly sphere has matter of its own. The first usage can be excused because the heavenly matter has its form through the action of higher causes; the material itself does not demand that form. The second can be excused because the differences between heavenly matters do not come from something already specified in the matter itself. Simple matter is not specified in itself. At its own level, it is wholly open; otherwise, it would already contain the features that divide one kind from another. The differences between heavenly matters come from the forms that make them actual, and from their union with those forms. Since these forms give them their essential differences, the matter, when considered apart from the forms that shape it, has a kind of general unity. Considered in another way, it is one particular thing, though no form or other condition is included in that view.[^V02-415]

#### Chapter 19 — Why Matter Longs for Form

The ancient sages taught that matter longs for form. What they left behind, and the results of their thought, show clearly that their symbols and secrets were not based on guesswork, hunches, or uncertain imagination. They rested on clear insight and firm proof. The sages cleared their inner lives through practices that purify the heart and remove what clouds reason. Their minds became clear, their inner sense refined, and their hearts like polished mirrors turned toward the truth.

They shared some of these teachings in examples suited to people ready to understand them. But later writers, who changed the way wisdom was studied, mixed it with rhetoric and preaching, and let personal aims, ambition, and the pursuit of worldly gain take over, dismissed the old teachings as mere metaphor without a sound basis. They did so either because they did not trust the reports about those great thinkers, or because they did not understand how far their insight reached.

Their objection is this: the longing the ancients ascribe to matter must be either a desire of the soul or a natural tendency. It clearly cannot be a desire of the soul. Nor can it be a natural tendency. Such a tendency would have to be toward one particular form or toward form in general. If it were toward one form, matter would naturally move toward that form, and every other form would have to come about by force. That is impossible. If it were toward form in general, the claim would still fail, since matter is never without a form, and longing is for what one does not yet have. So, they conclude, this teaching makes no sense.

**Comment and explanation:**

In *The Eastern Investigations*, Fakhr al-Din al-Razi raises this objection, drawing on Ibn Sina’s discussion in the natural sciences of *The Healing*. Ibn Sina writes:

“People sometimes speak of matter’s longing for form, comparing matter to a female and form to a male. I do not understand this. It is clear that matter has no desire of the soul. And the natural tendency that drives something toward its proper place, as a stone moves downward to complete what it lacks, seems just as far removed from matter.

“Matter could long for form if it were empty of every form, tired of the form it already had, or dissatisfied with what it received. It would also need the power to move itself toward a form, as a stone moves toward its proper place.[^V02-416] But matter is never empty of all forms, and it would not become tired of its present form and try to destroy it. If having a form made it weary, it should not long for that form. If it became weary only after some time, its longing would arise later, not belong to its nature, and would need some cause.

“Nor can matter be dissatisfied with its form, unless it longs to have opposite forms together. That is impossible, though an impossible wish might arise as a desire of the soul. A natural tendency, by contrast, is always toward an end that fits and completes a thing’s nature, and such natural ends are possible. So how could matter move toward a form? A new form comes from a cause that removes the old one; matter does not acquire it by moving itself. If the ancients meant that this longing is for later, secondary forms of completion rather than the basic form that makes matter what it is,[^V02-417] it is hard even to understand what they mean. How much harder, then, if they mean the basic form itself? These difficulties make the teaching hard for me to grasp. It sounds more like something said by mystics than by philosophers. Perhaps someone else understands it fully; if so, I would turn to them. If the ancients meant only that matter receiving a natural form can long for further completion—as earth tends downward and fire upward—then there would be some sense to it, though the longing would belong to the active form, not to matter in general. But understood as a claim about matter without qualification, it is something I cannot understand.”

This is the full passage from Ibn Sina on the subject.

Out of respect for my teachers in the sciences and my guides to truth, who are like spiritual fathers and ancestors of the mind, I do not feel free to contradict them when Ibn Sina himself admits that he cannot grasp this teaching. I would rather stay silent about what he leaves unsaid and admit how difficult it is, even though the matter is clear to me. But a brother in faith and a companion in the search for certainty pressed me to explain the longing for form that the greatest ancient sages and mystics ascribe to matter.

I agreed to help him. I will begin by recalling several principles we need for this question.

The first principle is that existence is one real thing, present in the world, not merely an idea in the mind as later thinkers claim. The differences between its instances and levels do not come from their being entirely different in nature, or from separate features added to them. They differ by rank, completeness, intensity, and weakness. Qualities such as knowledge, power, and will are the same as existence itself. Wherever existence is stronger, all its qualities are stronger; wherever it is weaker, they are weaker too.

The second principle is that the real nature of anything is its own particular way of existing. What exists outside the mind and flows from a cause is a thing’s particular existence. What we call its nature does not exist or come from a cause on its own; it exists because it is joined to the existence that truly comes from the cause. The relation between a thing’s nature and its existence is like the relation between a description and what it describes, or between a reflection and what is reflected. A thing’s nature is the mind’s account of it, an image of what we see in the world, and a shadow of that reality, as we have already shown.

The third principle is that existence, simply as existence, is active, loved, and desired. The flaws and harms we see in some things come either from what is absent or incomplete in them, or from a weak nature’s inability to bear a better kind of existence. They may also come from clashes between limited kinds of existence in a world where things press against, oppose, and conflict with one another.

When two opposing things meet, each may, because of chance conditions, overcome the other. Their conflict does not come from existence itself. It comes from each having a limited place and form that cannot include, surround, or join with the other. This conflict is part of bodily life, which is among the weakest levels of existence. Length, size, and other dimensions are especially limited: they extend only so far, and cannot go beyond a fixed boundary. Proof shows that dimensions and other continuous things, whether they last or pass, must be finite.

Because bodily existence is weak and spread out, the parts of a body cannot all be together in one place. Each part is absent from the others in its own space. This is why being tied to bodily size limits the ability to know. Knowledge means that one thing is present to another. The weaker a thing’s existence, the less it can know, and the more it is marked by ignorance, neglect, death, and harm. Time and motion are weaker still, because different moments cannot be present together, just as parts of a body cannot occupy one place at the same time. This is not the place to explain that point fully; perhaps we will return to it later.

For now, remember that existence itself is active, loved, and desired. Since it is a real good, anything that has it holds on to it; anything that lacks it seeks it. The fourth principle is that longing means seeking a perfection that is present in one way but absent in another. Someone who has nothing of a thing cannot long for it, since we cannot desire what is wholly unknown and absent. And someone who already has it fully cannot seek to gain it again. God, whose existence is complete and free of every lack, cannot long for anything or move toward greater perfection. Instead, everything else longs for and loves Him.

The active intellects are also made complete in the perfections suited to each of them. They stand before their source, behold the beauty of their maker, and receive as much of the good and existence as their nature can hold. The good that flows from them to the lower world does not add to their own perfection. It is a gift that flows from them to what lies below, without any personal aim or desire to improve the world. They do not long for what is beneath them. They are absorbed in the beauty of the eternal and in the sight of true existence. They are not said to long for what is above them either, except in a hidden way that belongs to their possible nature: when the mind considers their nature apart from the existence they receive from their Creator, it sees their limits and weakness.

Other levels of existence, however—whether they are the souls of the heavens, heavenly forms, natural kinds among earthly things, extended substances, or bodily matter—carry potential and longing for greater completeness.[^V02-418] The study of goals has already shown that moving things long for what completes them. This should make clear that all such things are born with a deep longing for their source, and acknowledge their dependence on the eternal Creator.

With these principles in place,[^V02-419] we can show that first matter longs for form. It has some level of existence and some share in being, as Ibn Sina and other careful philosophers in Aristotle’s school admit. We will prove this in its proper place. Its existence is weak: it is the potential for the things that come from it and are joined with it, as matter is joined with form and a general kind with its particular difference.

Since existence is one, and its qualities—knowledge, will, and power—belong to it wherever it exists, even first matter has some awareness of perfection, weak as its existence is.[^V02-420] It senses its own incomplete existence and therefore seeks complete existence, which is what everything ultimately acts toward and desires. As the fourth principle explains, anything that has some perfection but not the whole of it longs for what is missing. First matter therefore longs intensely for the forms of nature that complete it and make it a particular kind of thing.

I am not saying that longing becomes strong because the thing desired is more complete, or because matter clearly feels all the forms it might receive. Its awareness of existence is weak, because its own existence is only the potential for formed things. But it has the greatest range of longing in another sense: it is open to forms and goods without limit, each of which can complete some part of what it lacks.

A further reason supports this point.[^V02-421] On our account, first matter arises through a kind of limitation in immaterial beings, especially souls. It can therefore receive every kind of form that might complete it, even if that requires an endless succession of times, since all these perfections cannot be present together at one moment. They are all ways of existing, and matter already has a small share in them: the potential and readiness to receive them. When a thing has even a weak awareness of itself, lacking a perfection it could attain gives rise to longing for it. The strength of that longing depends on both its awareness and the perfection it still lacks.

The receptive powers of natural things account for their movement toward later stages of perfection. Through these changes they make up for their earlier limitations and seek to return to the source from which they began. These powers give matter its connection to longing for perfection, although the longing belongs directly to the thing joined with matter, not to matter considered on its own.

Ibn Sina’s claim that matter cannot have a desire of the soul is not correct. Considered by itself, apart from any form, matter is something negative and completely undetermined. But forms of plants and animals give matter a settled nature. As matter takes on a plant or animal form, it also has the desires of that form: the desires of a plant or animal soul, whether that soul belongs to a heavenly or earthly body. In plants, for example, matter has the desires of plant life; in animals, it has the desires of animal life. These include the natural tendencies suited to simple and composite bodies.

His claim about natural tendency also fails, because it relies on a premise that we have already shown to be false.[^V02-422]

Ibn Sina says matter could long for form only if it were completely without form, or bored with the form it had. We have already explained that matter, considered in itself, is without form and longs for a form. Once it receives one, it rests from that lack. But because the form it receives is incomplete, matter then longs for further forms that would make it more complete. One form meets one need; it does not remove every lack. It is like a woman who is not satisfied with one man and continues to desire another. Matter is similar: as long as it remains matter, it longs for more forms and further completion.

Each form leaves something still lacking, because the possibilities and perfections that matter can receive are without limit, while any one form makes only a limited number of them actual. Matter therefore continues to rise through further possibilities and added perfections, until it reaches the levels of soul and intellect, and then the highest perfection: pure form without matter, complete actuality without potential, goodness without harm, and existence without absence. At that point, movement ends, distress is calmed, and longing comes to rest.

The right answer is the first option: matter longs because, considered in itself, it is without form, or because it lacks a form it could receive.

Ibn Sina also asks how matter could move toward a form when a new form comes from an outside cause that destroys the existing form. We answer that matter’s longing and movement toward form take different paths. In itself, it longs for any form. Once it has a particular form, it seeks the further perfection that form lacks.

Matter’s longing and claim to fulfillment are shared by opposite forms. When one form exists, matter has a claim to keep it. But as matter, it is also open to another form that conflicts with the first. It cannot have both at once, so each must have its turn. The Creator gives each form its proper share: one form remains for a time, then passes away and gives way to its opposite.

Neither form has a greater claim to exist than the other. Each is due a place in the world, and each has its turn.

This is how matter longs in itself, when we consider it without any form. Once it has a particular kind of form, it longs for what will complete that form. Its desires can be arranged in two series.[^V02-423] One is a sequence over time: forms appear one after another, replacing earlier forms. This is the sequence of individual forms in earthly things that decay and change. The other series rises through levels of completeness and goodness. In it, each later form completes and fulfills an earlier one. These forms are linked as causes and goals, unlike the first series, where one preparatory condition gives way to another. The sequence of changing earthly forms may continue without end, but this does not mean there is no final goal. The upward series of perfection ends in the highest goal.[^V02-424][^V02-425]

So the ancient teaching is sound: matter longs first for natural forms, which give it a limited kind of good, and then for true goodness, higher glory, and complete perfection.

More than that, matter is the source of longing in everything that longs, for it is what makes possible a further state of completeness. If a being already had all the completeness suited to it, it would have nothing to long for. We long for what is missing, not for what we already possess; and we do not long for what we could never attain. As we have said, first matter is the source of that lack which can be overcome, and of readiness for a perfection still to come. Ibn Sina and other established philosophers do not deny this, as the later arguments for matter and its inseparable connection with form will show.

It is surprising that Ibn Sina himself, in his treatise *On Love*, gives a strong account of matter’s longing for form. After arguing that all things, living or not, have an inborn love, he gives a special explanation for simple, nonliving things:

“Every simple, nonliving thing is joined to an inborn love that never leaves it and helps it exist. Matter continually reaches toward forms it does not have. When it is stripped of one form, it quickly takes another, afraid of remaining in complete nonexistence. Every thing naturally turns away from absolute nonexistence.

“Matter is like an unattractive woman who fears that her appearance will be exposed. Whenever her veil is lifted, she covers what she wishes to hide with her sleeve.”

This is Ibn Sina’s own account of matter’s inborn love.[^V02-426]

#### Chapter 20 — Form as a Cause, and How Nature Differs from Form

Form is what makes a thing actual. Sometimes the underlying thing can exist without that form, considered in the broadest sense. In that case it is called a subject—the thing that receives a quality—as a body receives blackness. Sometimes it cannot exist without the form; then it is called matter. In the first case, the form is an accident; in the second, it is a substance.

“Form” has other meanings too. As we have seen, it is not an inner part of matter that explains what matter is, since form is not part of matter. It is instead an active cause that brings matter into actuality. These different meanings of “form” share one feature: each has to do with a thing becoming actual and existing. The different meanings of “matter,” in turn, share the ideas of potential, readiness, longing, and need.[^V02-427]

The word “nature” is used in three related senses, from broadest to narrowest. It can mean a thing’s essence; it can mean what gives that essence its specific identity; or, most narrowly, it can mean the first source of movement and rest in a thing, when these do not come about by chance or force. In technical usage, “nature” can name the second or the third of these senses.[^V02-428]

Form, as we have seen, is the part by which a thing becomes actual. In simple things, form and nature are the same in themselves, though we consider them in different ways. Take water, for example. Its form is what makes it the kind of thing it is. We call that same form its nature when we consider it as the source of the effects that belong to it, such as coldness and moisture. But compound things do not become actual through nature in that narrow sense. They become actual through a further form, received from the source from which existence flows. Their combined forms are therefore different from their natures.

Suppose you ask: if a compound needs another form, what makes it what it is? Is it the whole set of forms together, each form on its own, or only one of them?

Ibn Sina seems to suggest the first answer in one passage of *The Healing*. He says that compound bodies are not made actual by the powers that move them in a single direction, though those powers must still be part of what makes them what they are. These powers, he says, are parts of the compound’s form, and several meanings come together as one. Humanity, for example, includes natural, plant, and soul-related powers.

Taken at face value, this cannot be right. Several things that do not each make a thing what it is on their own cannot do so simply by being gathered together. Suppose more than one form is said to make up a thing. If each form is enough by itself, then the thing needs none of the others. Each would both make it what it is and fail to do so, which is impossible. If only one form is enough by itself, the others are not forms that make the thing what it is. The remaining possibility is that no one form is enough, and only the whole set makes the thing what it is. But that set is one thing when taken as a whole. So the forms that make the compound what it is would still amount to one form.

That last possibility is impossible too. Each part comes before the whole. But taken on its own, each part belongs to matter without making matter what it is; it depends on matter. Matter, then, comes before each part. If the whole set of parts made matter what it is, matter would depend on a whole made from parts that already depend on matter. That would make each depend on the other, which cannot be.

Since the first answer fails, one of the other two must be right. Most philosophers choose the second: nature and the other forms each play a part in making a compound what it is, but they do so in an ordered sequence. This, it seems, is what Ibn Sina meant. We choose the third answer: only one of the forms truly makes the compound what it is. The others are its branches and powers, or conditions that help it come about.[^V02-429][^V02-430][^V02-431]

You might object: this answer also fails. The rational soul is one of the things that makes a human being what it is. If the natural, plant, and animal powers play no part in making the human being what it is, they must be accidents, even though they are substances. This would mean that one kind of thing is both a substance and an accident. It would also mean that the simple forms that make the elements what they are make up the matter of the human body, and so make up something that helps make the human body what it is, even though they are accidents in relation to that body. That is impossible.

The principles we explained earlier resolve this difficulty.[^V02-432] A thing cannot be a substance and an accident in relation to the same thing. But it can be a substance in relation to one thing and an accident in relation to another. We must also distinguish between being a substance and being substantial to something, just as we distinguish between being an accident and being accidental to something. A substance exists in its own right; its status as a substance does not change when we compare it with something else. But being substantial to something is a relation. Relational features can differ depending on what we compare them with.

The simple forms make the simple elements what they are. They are not part of the nature of minerals, plants, or animals, though they are needed to preserve the balance of qualities that comes from their mixture. In the same way, a plant’s form makes the plant what it is, while its plant powers serve the animal soul and branch from it. They are outside the soul’s essence, but they are needed for an animal to exist. We have already explained this point in detail, so keep it in mind. Also remember that including necessary features in a thing’s definition does not make those features part of its essence. A definition may say more than the thing itself.[^V02-433]

It is surprising that, despite this, people say every compound natural body has a natural unity. What can they mean by that except that each has one nature that makes it what it is in actuality? On that view, even if all the forms and powers that come with it were removed, the thing would remain exactly what it is in its essence.

#### Chapter 21 — What a Goal Is and What People Have Said About It

A goal is what a thing is for—the reason it exists, as we explained earlier. Sometimes the goal is the agent itself, as with the First Agent, God. Sometimes it is something distinct from the agent but within it, such as the joy of winning. Sometimes the goal lies outside the agent. It may lie in the thing acted on, as when a movement guided by thought or by nature reaches its completion. Or it may lie in a third thing, as when someone acts to please another person. In that case, the other person’s satisfaction is a goal outside both the agent and the thing acted on, though the agent’s joy at pleasing them is another goal. This is only a brief outline; it needs a fuller explanation.

#### Chapter 22 — A Closer Look at Goals, Chance, and Aimless Action

They say that a thing depends on causes in two ways: for what it is and for its existence. Matter and form are causes of what a thing is; the agent and the goal are causes of its existence. No one disputes that every compound thing has matter, form, an agent, and a goal. But there is a question about whether every effect has a goal that explains its existence. Some effects seem aimless; some happen by chance; some come from a choice made without a reason or a preference for one option over another; and some have a goal that itself has a further goal, and so on without end. In that case, strictly speaking, they have no goal. It would be like saying that every beginning has an earlier beginning: everything would then be only a middle, with no true beginning. This may seem to be true of events in the elements, the movements of the heavenly spheres, and the successive conclusions of arguments, if they go on without end. We will examine these matters in turn.

#### Section 1 — How Even Aimless Action Has a Goal

Know that every voluntary movement has a sequence of sources. The nearest is the power that directly moves the body. In an animal, this power is in the muscle of the limb being moved. Before it comes resolve, then desire, and before desire come thought and imagination. When an image of something appears in the imagination or the intellect, the image itself stirs desire and leads it toward resolve, without any prior decision. The First Agent brings things into existence in a similar way, as will become clear later, God willing: the vision of the highest order causes things to proceed from Him, without desire or the use of an instrument.

Once desire has stirred and resolve is in place, the moving power in the limbs carries it out. This shows that voluntary movements take shape through the sources just described. Sometimes the image formed in the mind is itself the goal at which movement ends. For example, someone grows tired of where they are, imagines another place, longs to be there, and moves toward it. Sometimes the image is not itself the goal: someone may want to go somewhere in order to meet a friend. In the first case, the destination itself is what the person wants. In the second, the desired meeting comes about only after the movement ends. Sometimes the movement itself is what the mover seeks.[^V02-434]

It is now clear that the point a movement reaches is its immediate moving power’s primary and true goal. In an animal, that power is in the muscle, and the endpoint is its only goal of its own.[^V02-435] The earlier sources of movement may have other goals, as we have seen. If the immediate moving power and the two sources before it—the power of desire, and the thought or image that stirred it—are all directed toward the same goal, then the movement’s endpoint serves them all. The action is not aimless, because it is guided by a deliberate goal. But if the endpoint matches what imagination and desire seek, while failing to match what deliberate thought seeks, the action is aimless.[^V02-436]

Any goal that is not the endpoint of a movement, and whose source is not deliberate thought, must come from one of three sources. It may come from imagination alone; then the action is arbitrary. It may come from imagination working with nature or bodily temperament, as in breathing or the movements of a sick person; then the action is natural or necessary. Or it may come from imagination working with a settled trait or habit that prompts action without thought, as when someone plays with their beard; then the action is a habit. When a goal sought by one of these sources does not come about, the action is fruitless in relation to that goal.[^V02-437]

With these points in place, we can see that aimless action still has a goal: it is the goal of the power of imagination, as just described. So it is wrong to say that such action has no goal at all, or no goal that is good or thought to be good. An action need not have a goal in relation to a source that did not bring it about; it needs a goal only in relation to the source that did. In aimless action there is no source based on deliberate thought, and so there is no goal of that kind. The other sources, however, each have a goal in the action they cause, and that goal is good in relation to them.

Every action of the soul involves desire and imagination, even if the image that stirred desire does not remain. A person may no longer be aware of it: imagining something and being aware that one is imagining it are not the same. If every awareness required another awareness of itself, this would go on without end. So the desire that arises in someone who is asleep or distracted, or in someone playing with their beard, must have a cause. It may be habit, impatience with one’s present state, a wish to move into another state, or the senses’ urge for some new activity. Habit is pleasurable; so is moving away from a tiresome state, and so is seeking a new activity. Pleasure is a good for the senses or the imagination. It is a real good for an animal as an animal, though from the standpoint of human reason it is only an apparent good. So the action is not without a real good in relation to the source that produced it, even if that good is not a true rational good.

#### Section 2 — Chance

Democritus claimed that the world came about by chance. He said its basic building blocks were tiny bodies, too hard to be split apart. They were scattered through an endless empty space. They all had the same basic nature,[^V02-438] but different shapes, and they were always moving. By chance, some collided and came together in a particular arrangement, forming this world. But Democritus did not claim that plants and animals arose by chance.[^V02-439] Empedocles, by contrast, claimed that the basic elemental bodies came into being by chance.[^V02-440] Whatever arrangement allowed them to survive and reproduce remained; whatever did not, disappeared. He offered several arguments for this view. One was that nature cannot think things through, so how can it act toward a goal?

Another argument was that decay, death, deformities, and extra parts are not intended by nature, even though they follow a steady order, just as their opposites do. This seems to show that none of these outcomes is intended by nature. Withering runs in the opposite direction from growth, but it too follows a fixed pattern. Since withering happens because matter requires it, not because nature intends it, perhaps growth happens for the same reason: matter requires it, without nature planning or aiming for it.

Rain is an example. The sun turns water into vapor. The vapor reaches a cold region, cools, becomes heavy water, and must fall. Sometimes the rain happens to be useful, so people think it was meant to bring those benefits. But, this argument says, rain falls because matter requires it.

A third argument was that the same natural power can produce different results. Heat melts wax, hardens salt, darkens a laundry worker’s face, and whitens clothes. These are the arguments made by those who say things happen by chance. Before answering them, we need to set out a few points.

Some possible events happen every time; others happen most of the time. Each has a cause. The difference is that nothing interferes with an event that always happens, while an event that happens most of the time can be blocked. It happens when no obstacle gets in the way, whether it comes about naturally or by choice. For a person to carry out an intended action, they must be resolved, their body must be ready to move, no obstacle can block the movement, they must not change their mind, and they must be able to reach the goal. Given these conditions, they cannot fail to reach it.

Other events happen just as often as their opposites, like Zayd sitting down or standing up. Some happen only rarely, like a person having an extra finger. We do not call events that always happen or happen most of the time “chance.”[^V02-441] The other two cases—events that happen equally often and events that are rare—can be necessary under certain conditions.[^V02-442]

For example, while an embryo’s hand is forming, more material may remain than is needed for its five fingers. If the active power then finds the material fully ready, an extra finger must form. Given those conditions, the extra finger forms necessarily. Relative to this particular natural process, it is as regular as any other result, even though it is rare among members of the species.

Once we see that even rare events are certain when their causes and conditions are present, there is no doubt that equally likely events can become common or regular when we take their causes and conditions into account. What happens by chance does so only for people who do not know its causes. From the standpoint of the one who sets all causes in motion, with every surrounding cause taken into account, nothing happens by chance. As the philosophers say, every event is necessary once all its causes are in place. If a person knew every cause and condition, nothing would seem to happen by chance.

If someone digging a well finds a treasure, that is chance only to someone who does not know what led the digger to that spot or brought the treasure to light. To someone who knows all the causes, the discovery is not chance but necessary.

Even a chance event happens through a process that has its own goal. It is only an incidental result of that process, not its main goal. Sometimes a falling stone injures someone, then continues down to its natural resting place, which is the proper goal of its movement. Sometimes it injures someone and stops before reaching that place. In the first case, it reaches its natural goal, while the injury is incidental. In the second, it fails to reach its natural goal.

Chance, then, is an incidental outcome of a natural, chosen, or forced process. Those processes already have their own goals; without them, nothing could happen by chance. Chance enters when an unexpected event occurs, one that does not happen always or most of the time. Still, the process must be capable of leading to that outcome, at least occasionally; otherwise we would not call it chance.

For example, if a solar eclipse happens while Zayd is sitting down, we would not call his sitting a chance cause of the eclipse: sitting has nothing to do with bringing about an eclipse. But when we trace an event back to the causes that truly bring it about, we find that it has a natural or chosen goal of its own. So the world did not come into being by chance, though chance may play a part in some individual events. The claims made by Empedocles and Democritus are false.

Here is the detailed answer to the objections. First, the fact that nature cannot think things through does not mean that its actions have no goal. Thought does not give an action its goal; it helps choose one action from among several possible actions. Each action has its own goal by nature, and reaches it without anyone having to assign it that goal. If the soul had no competing motives or distractions, people would act in one steady way without thinking, as the heavenly spheres do. They are free of changing urges and disturbances, so their actions follow a constant pattern without thought.[^V02-443]

Another point supports the claim that nature acts toward goals: reasoning itself is an action with a goal, yet it does not need another act of reasoning to guide it. It is also clear that crafts have goals. Once a skill becomes a habit, a person can use it without thinking through each step. In fact, thinking about every step can get in the way. A skilled writer does not stop to consider each letter, and a skilled musician does not think about each note. If they did, their skill would suffer. So nature, too, can act toward goals without thought or planning.

The same is true when someone slips and grabs whatever they can to keep from falling, or when a hand moves to scratch an itch without thought. Even clearer: when the soul moves an arm or leg, it does so through a tendon, though the soul is not aware of the tendon.

In reply to the second objection: things sometimes break down because they lack what they need to reach their full form, and sometimes because an obstacle or outside influence interrupts nature’s course. As for these absences,[^V02-444] nature’s being directed toward a goal does not mean it must always reach it. Death, decay, and withering happen because natural processes fall short of their intended goal. There is a deeper point here that I cannot explain in this section.[^V02-445]

Withering also follows a goal. It has two causes: heat, which acts directly, and the body’s nature, which contributes indirectly. Each has its own goal. Heat draws out moisture, drives the material toward that result, and uses it up in an orderly way. The body’s nature, by contrast, tries to preserve the body for as long as it can by drawing on one supply after another. But each new supply is smaller than the last, as we will explain in the discussion of the soul. The shrinking supply leads indirectly to the orderly process of withering; the loss of moisture leads to it directly. Each process has its own goal.

Death may not be a goal for one particular body, but it serves the wider order that prepares the soul for eternal life. The same is true of bodily weakness and aging: the soul is disciplined through them, and its bodily powers are weakened in ways that prepare it for the next life, as we will explain in the discussion of the soul.

Extra parts also arise for a reason. When there is more matter than the body needs, nature gives it the form it is ready to receive instead of leaving it unused. So nature acts toward a goal in producing an extra part, even if that part does not serve the body as a whole. We have not claimed that every goal of nature must serve the whole body.

The argument about rain is also mistaken. Rain comes from heavenly arrangements meeting receptive conditions on earth. Together, these serve the order of the whole world and bring good and blessings. They are causes from God,[^V02-446] and nature brings them about always or most of the time.

In reply to the third objection: heat has one proper goal—to make the thing it heats take on a nature like its own. Its other effects, such as melting, hardening, darkening, and whitening, are necessary side effects. Later, we will see how a side effect can also serve as an indirect goal.

In *The Healing*, Ibn Sina gives arguments against Empedocles’ view, based on observation and clear examples. Some readers have therefore thought Empedocles’ remarks about chance and fortune were symbolic, or were falsely attributed to him. They say this because the other works they read show sound reasoning and great learning.

One clear example is this: if a grain of wheat and a grain of barley fall in the same patch of soil, the wheat grows into wheat and the barley into barley. This shows that the power in each seed makes the nearby soil take the form of that plant. It is not simply a result of matter’s needs, since the soil is much the same in both places. Even if the parts of the soil differed, they would not differ because of the nature of earth itself. The power in the seed gives that part of the soil its special quality. If the seed’s power got this quality from an earlier quality, the chain would go on forever. If it did not, then the power in the wheat seed is naturally directed toward a particular goal. Otherwise, why would an olive tree not grow from wheat, or barley from a watermelon seed?

Another point is that the goals nature reaches when nothing blocks it are all good and complete. When nature produces the opposite, that happens only rarely. This is why people look for some outside cause and ask: “What happened to this animal that made it sick and weak? Why did wheat or barley fail to grow?” Nature, then, is directed toward what is good whenever nothing stands in its way. When we see nature falling short, we help it through skill, as a doctor does. The doctor believes that once the obstacle is removed and nature’s power grows stronger, nature will bring about health and good. This supports the point.

#### Section 3 — The Goals of Chosen Actions

Some people who deny God’s attributes say that God’s actions serve no wisdom or good purpose. Yet you have seen that nature acts toward goals, and that even the actions of people who are asleep or distracted serve the needs of some power within them—the power that actually causes the action—even when reason or thought does not guide it. These people rely on arguments weaker than a spider’s web. For example, they try to show that actions need no motive by pointing to two equal roads for a person fleeing, two equal loaves for someone hungry, or two equal cups of water for someone thirsty. They do not realize that our failure to see what tips the balance does not prove that there is no such cause. Some of the things that guide our actions are hidden from us, such as the positions of the stars and higher divine causes.[^V02-447]

They also fail to see that if we deny motives for action and allow choices to happen without any reason, we close the way to proving that there is a Creator.[^V02-448] The proof begins from this point: something that could exist or not exist needs a cause that makes one outcome happen. If we reject this rule, we cannot prove that God must exist. And if we accept their view, there is no firm basis for thought or inquiry, or even for trusting what is plainly true, because we could never be sure that the opposite of a conclusion would not follow. Their view could even make God create in a person a state that makes things appear other than they are, since they say God can will without any reason. In Muslim history, these people resemble the ancient Sophists.

Another argument is that will itself tips the balance between alternatives, as an essential feature of will. Essential features and what belongs to a thing’s nature need no further explanation, just as knowledge is knowledge and power is power.[^V02-449] But this is empty talk. If the two possible actions are equal,[^V02-450] what makes one happen rather than the other? The special feature they appeal to is nonsense: it would still be there if, as they suppose, the other equally possible action had been chosen.[^V02-451]

Another claim they repeat is that will exists before an action without being directed toward anything in particular, and then becomes directed toward one thing rather than another. This alone shows the weakness of their view. Someone who wills does not simply will whatever happens to come along. Will is a relation, so it cannot exist without being directed toward something; it cannot first exist on its own and only later attach itself to one thing.[^V02-452] Rather, when we imagine something before it exists and one side of its possibility is favored, a will directed toward that side arises. The preference comes before the will, as we have said. The strongest arguments they offer are points raised by the author of *The Eastern Investigations*.

First, a heavenly sphere is a body whose parts are alike, yet two points in it are singled out as its poles, one circle as its equator, and one line as its axis. Since every point, circle, and line is alike in nature, the poles, equator, and axis could have been elsewhere.

Second, each sphere moves in a particular direction, though it could move in any direction. Each also moves at a particular speed, though it could move faster or slower and either option seems equally possible.

Third, each planet occupies a particular place in its sphere, though nothing in that place makes it different from the others. Since all places have the same nature, reason allows that the planet could have been somewhere else.

Fourth, the universe has a particular size rather than being larger or smaller, though either seems possible to reason.

The answer to the first point is that the point became special through the particular motion.[^V02-453] That motion sets the two poles; once they are set, the axis between them is set as well. Without the motion, no circle would be fixed as the equator, and so no pole or axis would be fixed.

The answer to the second point is that differences in the spheres’ directions and speeds follow from differences in their intellectual principles, which cause the spheres and their motions in a way that brings about the best order.[^V02-454]

This also answers the third point. A planet’s place became definite through the planet itself, not before it existed; otherwise, the sphere would be solid, with no place for the planet. Once the planet exists in that place, it cannot move to another.

As for the fourth point, every body, including the outermost sphere, has a nature that requires it to have a particular size. It cannot be otherwise. What reason allows us to imagine may not match reality, because before a proof we may not know the specific feature that makes the cause produce this result. I have a separate treatise on these problems, where I set out basic principles that remove doubt. Anyone seeking reassurance should consult it.[^V02-455]

In short, if an agent has only a possible relation to an effect and no motive or cause that leads it to act, the effect cannot come from it. One of two equal possibilities cannot become more likely than the other. A rational person who says otherwise is only saying the words, not believing them.[^V02-456] This motivating purpose is the goal of bringing things into existence. For God, that goal is His own essence, because He is a complete agent. If He needed something outside Himself in order to act, He would not be complete in His agency. You will see that He is the cause of all causes, and that His action has no ultimate goal beyond Himself.

Goals, like all other causes, depend on Him. If His action had a goal beyond Himself, then either that goal would not depend on Him—which contradicts what we have assumed—or it would depend on Him. In that case, we would ask again what goal led Him to bring about that goal, and continue in the same way until we reached a goal that is His own essence. His essence, then, is the goal of all things, just as He is their cause.[^V02-457]

We will show, God willing, that the Necessary Existent takes the greatest joy in His own essence, and that His essence is the source of all things. Whoever takes joy in something also takes joy in what comes from it, insofar as it comes from that thing. God wills things not for their own sake, considered by themselves, but because they come from His essence. The goal of His creation of the world is His own sacred essence. Any agent whose action follows this pattern is both the agent and the goal of that action. Even if pleasure in us were aware of itself, and its own essence were the source of an action, it would will that action for its own sake and because the action came from it. It would then be both agent and goal.

#### An objection and a reminder

They often say that what is higher does not will or attend to what is lower. If it did, it would be perfected by what is lower, since that thing’s existence would be better for it than its absence; but a cause is not perfected by its effect. This does not undermine our argument. What is denied is direct love and attention, aimed at the lower thing for its own sake—not love that follows as a consequence. If God loves and wills His action because it is an effect of His essence, a trace of His outpouring and existence, it does not follow that this action is itself a source of goodness or joy for Him. His joy is in what He loves directly: His exalted essence, from which every perfection and beauty flows.[^V02-458]

It is said that a reader once recited before Abu Saʿid ibn Abi al-Khayr, may God have mercy on him, the verse “He loves them, and they love Him.” The master replied: “God loves them because He loves only Himself. Nothing exists but Him; all else is His work. When a maker praises his work, he praises himself.”

This makes clear what is meant by the saying: “Were it not for love, neither heaven nor earth, land nor sea would exist.” God’s love for creation returns to Him. In truth, what He loves and wills is His own essence, for its own sake. It is like loving a person and then loving the traces he has left: the true object of your love is that person, as the poet says:[^V02-459]

> It was not love of the houses that captured my heart,
> but love of the one who lived in them.

#### Section 4 — The Purpose of Beings That Follow One Another Without End

Before we explain this, we need to make one point clear. Some outcomes are not the main goal, but are necessary along the way. They come in three kinds. The first is something that must exist before the main goal can be reached. For example, iron must be hard for cutting to work. This is called useful, either in fact or only in our estimation. Death and similar things belong here. Death serves the order of living things as a whole, and the soul too, as we have mentioned.[^V02-460]

The second kind is something that must accompany what the goal depends on. For example, cutting requires an iron knife, which may be dark. The darkness is not needed for cutting; it simply comes with the iron that is needed.

The third kind comes after the main goal. It may follow necessarily, as earthly events follow the motions of the heavenly spheres, whose goal is something higher than the motions themselves. Or it may not follow necessarily, as a parent’s love for a child may follow marriage, whose goal is having children. All three kinds are indirect goals, also called necessary outcomes. The evil we find in our world belongs to this last kind of necessary outcome. Divine providence, which is generosity, requires that every good exist. That includes the elements that combine to form other things. But fire cannot exist without having the power to burn, and the order of the world depends on fire serving its proper role. So some of the things made from these elements are destroyed. The motions of the heavenly spheres, which follow divine governance and the order it requires, bring fire to the things it destroys. What is necessary in each instance of evil serves a goal in relation to something else or to the order of the whole, as we saw in the discussion of good. We will explain this further when we discuss divine providence, God willing.

Once this is clear, we can say that the proper goal of the natural power that governs the world is not the continued existence of one particular individual of a kind. Its proper goal is for each kind to continue to exist.[^V02-461][^V02-462] If one individual can last, then there is no need for individuals to replace one another; only one individual exists, as with the sun and moon. But if one individual cannot last, as with things that come to be and perish, then new individuals must follow one another. This series of individuals is not wanted for its own sake. It is needed because the proper goal cannot be reached without it. So the endless succession of individuals is an incidental goal, not a true goal in itself. True goals are finite.

That is the goal of the natural power that guides each kind. An individual’s nature aims to preserve that individual; it has no other goal. As for the unbroken motion of the heavenly sphere, its purpose, as you will learn, is to bring every possible arrangement into reality, so that the souls of the spheres can become more like what is perfect. This can happen only through a succession of particular arrangements. So those changing arrangements are incidental goals, like the earthly events mentioned above.

As for the reasons and conclusions in an argument, the claim that a goal must be finite means that one agent cannot pursue one goal, then another, and another without end in a single action. It does not mean that many actions cannot have many goals. Each argument has its own conclusion, and in following it the mind seeks only that conclusion. There is no problem here.

#### Chapter 23 — The Difference Between a Goal and a Good

A goal may lie within the created world, or it may be above it. If it lies within the created world, it may exist in what receives it—for example, the shape of a house in the clay and bricks a builder uses—or it may exist in the agent’s own mind, as the shelter the builder hopes to provide. In every case, the final cause—the goal toward which an action is directed—is the first cause on which the other causes depend. Unless an agent first pictures a goal, it cannot act. Yet when a goal exists in the outside world, it is itself brought about by the other causes.[^V02-463][^V02-464]

Consider first the kind of goal that exists in what receives it. In relation to the agent, when the agent imagines it and is moved to act by that thought, it is the purpose. In relation to movement, it is an endpoint, not a purpose: a thing is completed when it reaches its goal and remains once reached, while movement ends when it reaches its endpoint. In relation to the agent’s being completed by it—when the agent could not yet do something but becomes able to do it—it is a good. Whatever brings a possibility to completion is a perfection; absence is bad. So attaining something and bringing it into reality are good.

When we consider the goal in relation to what receives it, and how that thing becomes what it is through the goal, the goal is its form. The goal that exists in the agent is a form or quality within the agent. If we consider it as something that completes the agent, it is a good; if we consider it as what starts the agent’s movement, it is a goal. So every goal is a goal in one respect and a good in another, either truly good or only thought to be good. This applies, for example, to some actions prompted by imagination alone, without careful thought or natural impulse.

You should also know that the goal of an agent that directly acts on matter is a form within that matter. An agent whose goal is not a form within matter is not acting directly on it. Sometimes the same person acts both directly and indirectly, and in different roles: close to the material or farther away, directly shaping it or not. In that case, the form made in the material is the person’s goal in one way directly and in another way as a side effect.

For example, someone builds a house to live in. As someone seeking shelter, he is the prior cause of the building: his wish to live there leads him to build. As the builder, he acts because he seeks shelter. From the builder’s point of view, the goal is something beyond the house’s shape; but in the role of directly shaping the material, his goal is the house’s form and structure.

#### Chapter 24 — The Difference Between Good and Generosity

A goal may move an agent to act, and so be the purpose of its action. In another sense, the goal is something brought about by the action. In this second sense, we can ask how it relates to the agent that is completed by it and to the agent that produces it. If the result does not affect the agent or anything that depends on it, what it brings about is simply existence. If the agent is changed or improved by the result, what it receives is a good. In general, good is what every thing seeks: existence, or a fuller existence.[^V02-465]

Generosity means giving what is fitting without asking for anything in return. Someone who gives what does not suit the receiver is not generous—for example, giving a knife to someone who plans to kill an innocent person. Nor is someone generous if they give a benefit in order to receive something back, whether thanks, praise, a good name, or pleasure. A generous agent gives another being a perfection in its nature or circumstances without receiving anything in return.

So an agent who acts for a goal that serves as a kind of repayment is not generous. That agent is making a deal and expecting something back. This makes the agent incomplete and needy: it gives one thing to gain something better and more pleasant for itself. If an agent ought to do something but does not, it lacks a perfection and is incomplete. If its perfection depends on something else, it is needy—even if that other thing completes someone else, removes their need, or brings them good.

If another’s good and the agent’s own gain or loss matter equally to the agent, there is no reason for it to bring good to the other. Its action remains only a possibility, because an aim is what moves an agent to act; something that does not tip the balance is not an aim. If the two do not matter equally, the explanation eventually returns to something connected to the agent itself. The question “Why?” keeps coming back until it reaches the agent’s own nature—some good it gains or some harm it avoids. Then the questioning stops. Every thing seeks its own good and the removal of what harms it. An agent acts from love of what it values, and each thing seeks what it values most: itself. So anyone who acts to gain something is incomplete; in short, they seek something they do not have. This is the view we find in their books.

**Further comment and clarification:** The study of goals is truly part of wisdom, and perhaps its best part. But books on the subject contain loose statements and claims that have not been fully worked out. These can be settled only by careful discussion and deep study. We should examine them closely and answer the doubts they raise as far as we can.

If you look carefully at a goal, you will find that it is always the same as the cause that acts. The difference lies in how we view it.[^V02-466] For example, when a hungry person eats to become full, he eats because he pictures being full. He tries to bring that fullness about in reality, moving from imagining it to having it. As he imagines being full, he is the one who eats so that he can become full in fact. The imagined fullness completes the cause that makes him act; actual fullness is the goal that follows from the action.[^V02-467] Eating thus comes from fullness and brings about fullness, but in two different senses. As an idea in the mind, fullness is the cause that makes him act; as a reality, it is the goal. The goal in the agent’s mind is never separate from the agent, and the result of the action also returns to the agent by completing it.

This shows why the usual divisions of goals do not hold. They say a goal may be in the agent, as joy is; in what receives the action; or in someone else, as another person’s approval. But the last two kinds really return to the first. A builder does not build, and someone does not win another person’s approval, unless some benefit comes back to them. This is true whether “goal” means what moves the agent to act or what naturally follows from the action.

They also say that a goal may be the very place where a movement ends, or something else—for example, moving to escape boredom with one place or to meet a friend. But unless the agent sees one option as better, or seeks some happiness or benefit that returns to them, voluntary movement could not happen.

We can make sense of this last distinction, but not by saying, as some do, that in both lists “goal” simply means the endpoint produced by an action. As we have already seen, even that endpoint must bring some good back to the agent, at least as the agent imagines it, if nothing blocks the result. Rather, the distinction is about what the goal is in itself: either the very thing at which movement ends, or something else.[^V02-468][^V02-469]

The philosophers say that God’s actions are not done for aims or goals. Yet they often call Him the “goal of goals” and say that He is both the beginning and the end. The Qur'an says that all things return to God, and that the return is to Him, among many other such statements. If saying that God’s actions have no goal means that they are not directed toward anything outside His own essence, that is true. As we said earlier, the First Cause is complete in His agency and does not depend on anything else in order to act. But that does not mean that His action has no goal or purpose at all. We may understand His knowledge of the good order—which is identical with His essence—as the purpose and goal of creation.

If you say, “The final cause, as the philosophers explicitly state, is what makes an agent act. It must therefore be different from the agent itself, since what brings something about must differ from what it brings about,” I reply: The philosophers often use words such as “requires” loosely.[^V02-470] They may use them simply to mean that one thing cannot be separated from another. How else could they claim that an agent and its goal must be different in reality without proving it?

An agent is what gives existence; a goal is that for whose sake existence is given. The goal may be the agent’s own essence, or something higher than it. If the goal were something that exists in its own right and produces an action from its own nature, it would be both the one acting and the goal.

So when the philosophers deny that God acts for a goal, they mean a goal separate from His own essence: honor, praise, thanks, bringing benefit to someone else, or any other result of His action that does not enter into His purpose.
