But if we mean that His knowledge of the good order—which is identical with His essence—gives rise to His bringing good into being, then careful inquiry and proof lead us to affirm this. The greatest minds have recognized it.[^V02-471]

Avicenna states this in his *Notes*: “If someone knew the perfection that is the Necessary Existent by itself, then arranged all the things that come after it according to its pattern so that they reached the highest order, that person would truly have aimed at the Necessary Existent by itself, which is perfection. If the Necessary Existent by itself is the agent, then it is also the goal.”

We have said that the First Principle is the goal of things in this sense. He is also their goal because every thing seeks its own perfection and, as far as it can, tries to resemble Him in attaining it. Each has a love and longing for Him, whether by choice or by nature. The philosophers who recognize God teach that love and longing run through all beings, though in different degrees. All things, created and brought into being, draw from this vast sea of longing and bear witness to the oneness of the eternal Real. Each turns toward its own direction, seeking to reach it and to take from it the light of longing. The Qur'an points to this when it says, “There is nothing that does not glorify Him with praise.”

Every being naturally seeks its own perfection: the goodness of its existence, which it receives from the First Good. It turns away from its own particular lack, which comes from matter and nonexistence. So every being has an inborn desire for the good. Goodness is loved for its own sake. For some beings, the good they love is the particular good suited to them; for others, it is the unqualified good—the Necessary Good, pure existence untouched by any lack or limitation. Each being finds what suits it pleasing and longs for it when it is absent. Love comes from what has been or will be received from the beloved. The greater the goodness and fullness of existence, the more it is loved and the stronger its love of the good.

The holy reality untouched by possibility or lack is the fullest good, and so is both the most beloved and the greatest lover. Since the divine attributes do not differ from His essence, love in Him is wholly one with His essence and existence. As for all other beings, either their existence is itself a form of love, or it comes from love.

Among created beings, the strongest love belongs to the active intellects. They receive the divine light directly, without bodily senses or imagination, and their activity brings about the lower things that follow. Next come the higher souls, which the active intellect brings from possibility into actuality and gives the power to form images, hold them, and rest in them. After these come the animal powers, and then the plant powers.

Each of these has a kind of worship suited to it, and imitates its higher source as far as it can.[^V02-472] The worship of the higher beings will be explained in its proper place. Among lower beings, worship takes the form of yielding to what is higher, submitting to it, and passing its good on to what is lower.

Look at minerals: they accept impressions and shapes, and yield to melting and hammering. In this way they acknowledge their source and show their submission. The more readily a mineral can be shaped, and the brighter and more beautiful its form, the higher it is. The duller and less useful it is, like rock and stone, the lower it is.

Then look at plants. Their branches move to and fro in the breeze. A Persian verse compares a cypress tree swaying in the north wind to someone waving their hands and bowing. The plant praises and honors God as its leaves brush together and its branches move, and it gives its fruit to animals. A plant that resists this order and yields nothing useful is fit only for the fire.

Animals, in turn, serve human beings. They go where people lead them and carry loads to places that would otherwise be reached only with great hardship. Some animals, however, resist human service, like wild beasts and other untamed creatures.[^V02-473]

Then there is human worship: people imitate the higher source through knowledge and action, understand what can be known, and rise beyond bodily concerns. Human beings have the power of speech, freedom to act, and a complete form. Their worship is the highest among earthly beings, and their knowledge the greatest among animals. But a person consumed by wrongdoing sinks below animals, plants, and minerals. Minerals accept the forms given to them, while that person refuses them. The tree bows and praises its Lord, while that person does not. Animals obey human beings, while that person does not obey or know their Lord. May God protect us from such heedlessness and forgetfulness.

People imitate the higher source through the thinking power, which understands general truths, and through practical reason, which governs the body and its powers. They use the senses to draw general ideas from particular things, and the imagination to help them think, so that they can reach what reason seeks. They restrain sexual desire—not for pleasure itself, but to imitate the First Cause by preserving the kinds of living things, especially the human kind. They train anger to face danger and take up arms to defend a just city and a good community. From the rational power come acts such as understanding intelligible things, readiness to face death, love of the next life, and longing to live near the Merciful Lord.

Understand these words in their true sense, not as invented poetry. Some of what I have said may sound like persuasion or rhetoric, but these are symbols; when examined closely, they lead to proof. In short, all things—intellects, souls, heavenly bodies, and earthly bodies—imitate the higher source. By their nature, they love and long to obey the First Cause. They have an inborn way of turning toward it and moving around it.

Avicenna says in several places in his *Notes* that earthly powers are like the souls of the heavens: what they seek through their actions lies above them. Nature and earthly souls do not move matter in order to produce something lower than themselves, such as a particular mixture or condition, even though these results necessarily follow. They move matter so that it may reach the best state possible for it and so imitate what is above it, just as the souls of the heavens move their bodies.

From this, a thoughtful reader can see that the goal of every mover, high or low, is the First Agent. The things it moves are directed toward Him, not toward what lies below them.[^V02-474] This also explains the saying, “If the higher did not love, the lower would disappear.”

The same is true of what brings rest, such as the nature of the earth, and what brings motion, such as the natures of the heavens. What they seek is not something lower than themselves, such as a place. Their goal is to reach the best state possible for them, the state that suits them.

As the Second Teacher, Abu Nasr, says in *The Gems*: “The heavens pray through their turning, and the earth through its trembling. In everything we find a natural longing for what it loves and a tender desire for what it seeks.” Nothing is without such longing.

One of the people of insight says: “By my life, the heavens, in their swift turning and deep yearning, and the earth, in its stillness, are alike in this. The beauty of the First has reached them both: the heavens dance with joy, while the earth, overcome by what has come to it, lies stretched on the carpet. The pleasure of this vision has made them its servants; the sight of the Eternal’s kindness has taken their hearts.” As the verse says: “That is gratitude for boundless kindness; this is intoxication from the wine of longing.”

You may say: “Although a goal comes before an action in what it is, it must come after the action in existence, as its result. If God were both agent and goal, He would have to be prior to all possible beings and also later than them. The same thing would be both first of all and last of all.”

I reply: The goal comes after an action in existence only when the goal is something created. That does not apply when the goal is higher than creation. In acts of creation, the goal comes before what is brought about, in knowledge and in existence, though in different respects. God is first of all as the agent who causes everything, as we will prove. He is also the last of all as the goal and good toward which all things naturally turn and long, because He is goodness itself and the true beloved.[^V02-475]

What makes Him the agent is His own essence. What makes Him the goal is that things proceed from Him in such a way that they naturally love Him: they preserve the perfections they already have and long to gain further perfections, so that they may resemble their source as far as possible. You now know the difference between a goal that belongs to a thing in itself and one that belongs to it only as a consequence.

#### Questions and Clarifications

We have established that anyone who acts to reach a goal outside himself is incomplete and depends on something else to make him whole. What completes him must be higher than he is. So when someone acts for a goal, that goal must be above him—even if he only thinks it is. He cannot truly act for something below himself or for the sake of a result that is less complete than he is. What he aims at must be higher than the act of aiming. If he truly aimed at a lesser result, his aim would bring into being something more complete than himself. That cannot be.[^V02-476]

Consider the physician. The doctor treats a patient to restore health, and health may follow. It is easy to suppose that the doctor’s intention produced the recovery. But the intention does not itself give health. Health comes from a source higher than the physician and the physician’s purpose: the giver of good to things when they are ready to receive it. The intention only helps prepare the body. What actually gives health is always higher than the one who seeks it; the person who intends the result is a cause only indirectly.

You may object that people often aim at things less worthy than themselves. That is so, but they do this because they are mistaken.[^V02-477]

You may also ask: “If a goal is what makes someone act, then the goal and the one who acts are linked by nature. Shouldn’t the one who acts truly become more complete through the goal?” Sometimes the person is noble in his own nature, yet his contact with matter and with bodily powers of sense and imagination leads him to aim at something lesser.[^V02-478]

Another question: “If God has no goal in creating possible beings and does not aim to benefit them, why did He bring them into being in such a perfect order? No one can deny the remarkable design in the parts of the world and the benefits they provide. We see it in the senses and the parts of the brain: the front for imagination, the middle for thought, and the back for memory; in the throat for speech, the nose for breathing, the teeth for chewing, the lungs for breath, the body for the soul, and the soul for knowing its Creator. We see it, too, in the movements of the heavens, the paths of the stars, and the benefits of the sun and moon. No book or speaker could list them all.”

I reply: God does not act for a goal outside Himself or because of any benefit, whether known to us or unknown. There are far more benefits than we know. Rather, His own nature brings all things into being in the best order and with the greatest good they can have. Some benefits are necessary, such as reason in a human being or a prophet for a community. Others are not necessary but are fitting, such as the growth of eyebrow hair or the hollow under the foot. And God knows everything, hidden or open; not even an atom in the heavens or on earth escapes Him.

As we have said, every cause cares for what follows from it in the same way. A cause does not act to benefit what is below it, does not become complete through its result except as a side effect, and does not choose an action for the sake of that result—even if it knows and accepts what follows. Water, fire, the sun, and the moon cool, heat, and give light to preserve their own powers, not to benefit others.

The good that reaches others flows from their actions as an overflow, as the saying goes: “The earth, too, has a share in the generous cup.”

The world itself cries, “There is no god but He,” while the heedless person thinks one thing is an enemy and another a friend. The sea raises waves by its own nature; a scrap of straw imagines that all this turmoil is about it.[^V02-479]

God’s own knowledge of Himself—which is the source of all good—brings things into being in the best order. The resulting benefits may be called side goals if by “goal” we mean what makes someone act. But if we mean what an action naturally brings about, then they too are goals.[^V02-480][^V02-481] The same applies to the sources of harm and loss that arise indirectly in the material world.[^V02-482]

Someone may ask: “These results, and the sources that produce them at their highest level, must in some way be pictured by those sources, either directly or indirectly. But some of these sources are bodily natures that have no awareness at all.”

I reply: We cannot simply say that nature has no awareness. Careful thought suggests otherwise. If a thing did not have an inner drive to act, it would not act from its own nature. And if the source of an action existed only in the result that came last, then that source would have to be present in some way beforehand. This suggests a kind of awareness, though not conscious thought or choice.[^V02-483]

The Qur'an says, “There is nothing that does not praise Him, but you do not understand their praise.”[^V02-484] Under the view of the oneness of existence that we are about to establish, every thing has some degree of awareness, just as it has some degree of being. God is living, knowing, powerful, and willing; these are not separate qualities added to Him, but are one with His essence. And God is present to all things as the source of their existence: they reveal Him and reflect His qualities. Those qualities appear more or less clearly according to how fully each thing exists.[^V02-485][^V02-486]

Someone may say: “We often infer from the order and care in an action that its source planned it.”

“Why, then, should the actions that arise directly from nature not also involve thought and choice?”

I reply: That argument can help explain things to people who cannot see the true goals and sources behind nature. We have already shown that every action has a goal and a result, whether or not it involves deliberate thought.[^V02-487]

**Further Insight**

Everything we have said shows that the First Principle is where all things begin and where existence finally leads. He is the highest goal in both senses: He gives rise to action, and He is where action ends. So He is both the source of all things and their final goal. These two ways of speaking differ in two respects: one is direct and the other indirect; and one concerns God’s own existence, while the other concerns how those who seek Him come to know Him.[^V02-488][^V02-489]

God is first in existence, because all things come from Him in order, one after another. But for those who seek Him, He is last: they rise from one stage to the next until they reach His presence. He is last in their experience and first in being.

God tells us that the world’s goal, in this second sense, is to be known: “I was a hidden treasure, and I loved to be known, so I created creation that I might be known.” This shows that God is the world’s highest goal as the One who is known, just as He is its source and goal as the One who exists. He also points to a goal along the way when He says, “Were it not for you, I would not have created the heavens.” The final goal of the world, in all three senses, is God’s existence and meeting Him in the life to come. The world was made for this; its order was shaped for this. All things move toward God, and to Him all things return.

#### Addendum

We have seen that, by their original nature, all beings in the world turn toward true goals and sound ends. There is one goal above them all: the highest good. But some people chase other goals that only seem good.[^V02-490] These goals are dressed up to attract them, and most people follow them blindly, without understanding. Only God’s sincere servants escape this. They are at odds with the source of all existence. They are not truly God’s servants, and God is not their guide and master. If they serve something else, it is a false power—a tyrant or devil. We may call them servants of desire or servants of false rulers. The Qur'an uses both kinds of language.

Those who turn to God, long to meet Him, and follow the true order are under His care, for He cares for the righteous. But those who rebel, turn to false powers, and follow their desires have a false power for each desire. Each person follows the thing they have made their own god, as the Qur'an asks: “Have you seen the one who takes his own desire as his god?”

You know that imagined systems and lesser goals fade and do not last. If someone gives their loyalty to a false power born of the material world, then as that world sinks toward nonexistence, the false power fades with it.[^V02-491] It draws the person down through lower and lower states until it ends in ruin. May God protect us and our brothers and sisters in faith from following our desires and clinging to the world’s glitter. May He make us among His good servants, whom He cares for on the Day of Judgment.

#### Chapter 25 — More on Cause and Effect

We have already said that one thing may affect another, or be affected by it, either in itself or only by association. Whenever I use “by association” here, I mean that a subject is described in a way that is metaphorical rather than literal because it is linked or united with what truly has that property. A body, for example, is called equal or unequal because it is joined to a quantity; it is called similar because it is joined to a quality. Someone riding in a ship is called moving because he is connected to the ship. As you have learned, when an essence is described by features of existence—such as being prior or later, a cause or an effect—this too is metaphorical and incidental, because essence and existence are united. And in our discussion of creation, we showed that creation does not act on an essence at all.[^V02-492][^V02-493]

Only a level of existence, not an essence, can be made, caused, or brought into being. Essences in themselves are pure possibility and mere potential, hidden by their nature; they have not entered the open realm of actuality, existence, and appearance. Those who say that possible essences are already “established” before they exist go wrong by separating being established from existence. Before existence, no judgment can be made about an essence—not even that it is established in itself. It has not yet appeared or become distinct from anything else. Existence is a light that reveals essences, dark in themselves, to the mind, just as physical light reveals trees, stones, and other solid bodies hidden from sight by their own darkness. Each level of existence reveals an essence by being joined to it and united with it. Until that mode of existence is present, nothing can be said about the essence linked to it. Only through that existence, whether grasped by the mind or sensed, can we judge that the essence is just what it is. In itself, an essence is neither existent nor nonexistent, visible nor hidden, eternal nor created, if these words mean that such features belong to it. But if some of these words simply deny others—if “nonexistent” means only “without existence,” not that nonexistence is something real, and “hidden” means “not appearing,” not “unable to appear”—then such denials are true of essences always.

An essence has no separate self to which any feature can be ascribed. It is impossible for both opposites to be absent at once from something that exists, but this applies to it only insofar as it exists, not insofar as it does not exist. Until a thing is considered through some mode of existence—even as if that existence had colored it—we cannot call it existent in itself or make any judgment about it. So we can speak of an essence, even through its defining features or through ideas we assign to it, such as possibility, hiddenness, darkness, and concealment from all eternity, only after existence has colored and illuminated it. When people of spiritual insight and certainty say that essences, which they call the “fixed entities,” have never appeared and never will appear in themselves, and that only their properties and effects appear—so that they never even smell the scent of existence—this is what they mean.

To judge an essence as existent, even at a particular time, comes from a veil over our sight and a failure to distinguish a thing from what accompanies it or necessarily follows from it. The philosophers and careful thinkers agree, as their own words plainly show, that the necessary features of essences do not need to be produced by a maker or caused by anything, whether by the essence itself or by something else. The same applies to the sources of evil and nonexistence: in the philosophers’ view, they do not come about through a creative act or a gift from the highest source, who is free of every shortcoming. In the same way, since we have shown that what a creator produces, and what follows from it, is only a mode of existence and a level of manifestation, not an essence—and that an essence appears by the light of existence without itself being made or bestowed—what truly exists and proceeds from the true Creator is existence, not essence. So calling an essence an effect is entirely metaphorical, just as calling it existent is.

Do not think that describing an essence as existent is like describing a body as white. We truly call a body white because whiteness really exists in it. A body may exist before whiteness does, but once whiteness exists in it, the body truly has that quality. A quality exists in a subject only after the subject exists, and it depends on the subject. But existence does not inhere in an essence. An essence does not exist before existence, and existence is not something we can draw from the essence itself. So there is no basis for calling an essence existent even in an abstract sense, much less for saying that it exists in reality. The mind draws essence from existence, not existence from essence. It is more accurate to say, “This particular existence is a human being,” than to say, “The essence of humanity exists.” We can describe a subject either because a quality is joined to it in existence or because the mind draws that quality from its way of existing. Both require the subject to exist in the context of the description. Until something exists in the external world, no quality can be added to it or drawn from its particular mode of existence, as one of the later scholars has explained. And as you know, what exists is existence, not essence. So we can now see how a cause is a cause in itself, truly, and how it is a cause only by association, metaphorically.

Everyone agrees that a cause makes and affects what is possible. What it makes must be one of three things: existence, essence, or the joining of essence and existence. We have shown that the last two cannot be right, so existence alone is what a cause brings into being. It follows that the external world contains only individual existences. As we explained in our discussion of essence, each particular possible existence itself gives rise to the features that distinguish kinds of things. The mind draws general kinds, distinguishing features, species, and essential or incidental traits from individual existences. It then judges these to apply either by virtue of what a thing is or because of some feature it has, which is itself another mode of existence. For this reason,[^V02-494] the mind forms distinct ideas of individual or general features, whether essential or incidental, by looking at an individual. The ideas it forms depend on the mind’s capacities and on how many similarities it notices among particular things, some alike and some different. Looking at Zayd, for example, the mind may form an idea of him as an individual unlike anyone else; it may then form an idea he shares with Amr and Bakr, or a broader idea he shares with a horse and other things.

Someone may object that this works only for simple kinds such as blackness. In such a case, there is no separate thing called “color” and another thing that makes blackness different from the other colors. So it would make no sense to say that color was made first and blackness afterward; their existence is one. But in other cases, the essential features that the mind distinguishes are also distinct in the external world and are not made in a single act. An animal, for example, shares bodily existence with a plant but differs from it through its animal soul. The making of the body is not the making of the soul; when the soul leaves, the body remains, as with a dead horse whose body continues to exist.

We reply: As we have already explained, a thing considered as matter is not the same as that same thing considered as a broader kind. We are not discussing how the matter of a thing differs from the whole in external reality when we set aside the mind’s ideas. We are discussing the broader kind, which can be said of the whole and is called an essential feature.

All existing things—whether an intellect, a soul, or a form that defines a kind—are levels among the rays of the true Light and the displays of God’s self-sustaining existence. When the light of truth shines, the mistaken idea that possible essences exist in themselves fades away. Their features and effects appear only through the levels of existence, which are rays and shadows of true existence, the one Light. This principle is among the wisdom God gave me through His timeless care. With it, I have tried to bring philosophy to completion and wisdom to its fullness.

This principle is subtle, hard to reach, and deep. Most philosophers overlooked it, and many serious students stumbled because of that, as did their followers. Just as God let me see, through His grace and mercy, that possible essences and fixed entities have no eternal existence of their own, He guided me by a clear proof from the highest realm to a straight path: everything that truly exists is contained in one single reality, with no partner in real existence.

There is no second being alongside it in reality; no one else dwells in the house of existence. Whatever appears to be other than the Necessary Being we worship is a display of His essence and a manifestation of His attributes, which in truth are His essence. One of the people of spiritual insight put it this way: “Whatever is called ‘other than God,’ or ‘the world,’ is to Him as a shadow is to the person who casts it. It is God’s shadow.” This is how existence relates to the world.[^V02-495]

The places where this divine shadow, called the world, appears are the possible essences. The shadow stretches over them, and we perceive it as far as His existence extends over them. Yet we perceive through His own light, because these essences have no light of their own. They have no existence in themselves, even though they may be called established by association. Existence is light; everything else is dark in itself. We know the world only as far as we know the shadow, and we know God only as far as we know the One from whom the shadow comes.

To the extent that we do not know what lies within the shadow—the image of the one from whom it comes—we do not know the Real.[^V02-496] “Have you not seen how your Lord extends the shadow? If He willed, He could make it still”—that is, He could stop the flow of His generosity and the shadow of His existence.[^V02-497] “Then We made the sun a guide to it.” The sun is He Himself, considered as light to Himself and in Himself. Reason and the senses bear witness to the long-reaching shadows of existence: no shadow can be seen without light. “Then We draw it back to Us gently.” It is His shadow, so He draws it back to Himself. It appeared from Him, and all things return to Him.

Everything we perceive is God’s existence in the essences of possible things. Seen from the side of God’s own reality, it is His existence. Seen through the different meanings and states that our minds and senses take from things, these are the essences of possible things, which have no being of their own. Changing images and meanings do not stop a shadow from being called a shadow; in the same way, the world remains known as “the world” and as “everything other than God.”

If this is so, the world has no true existence of its own; it is only imagined to be separate. This is what the divine mystics and realized saints teach. God willing, I will give the promised proof of this lofty idea later. For now, here is a passage from someone of direct insight and certainty that may make the point clearer:

“Know that universal concepts and possible essences, though they have no external existence in themselves, are certainly understood and known in the mind. They remain hidden from external existence. Yet they have a rule and an effect in every thing that exists outside the mind—in fact, they are those very individual things, not something else.[^V02-498][^V02-499] They remain ideas in themselves, yet appear through individual beings, just as they remain hidden in the mind’s grasp of them. Every individual thing depends on these universal concepts. The mind cannot remove them, and they cannot exist outside the mind in a way that would stop them from being understood. This is true whether an individual exists for a limited time or without limit.

“A thing that lasts for a time and one that does not are related to a universal concept in the same way. Still, the universal receives different descriptions from individual things, according to what each thing’s nature calls for. This is like the way knowledge belongs to someone who knows and life belongs to something living.[^V02-500] Life and knowledge are concepts we can understand, and each is distinct from the other. We say that God has knowledge and life, so He is knowing and living; we say that an angel has knowledge and life, so it too is knowing and living. The reality of knowledge is one, and so is the reality of life. They relate to the knower and the living thing in the same way. Yet we call God’s knowledge eternal and a human being’s knowledge something that began in time. Notice what the relation adds to this one intelligible reality. Knowledge makes the one it belongs to a knower; the one described also gives knowledge its status: it is new in a being that began and eternal in the One who is eternal. So each can be what is described and what describes another.

“These universal concepts are ideas; they do not exist externally in themselves. Yet they carry effects and receive descriptions when related to things that do exist. They can receive such descriptions, but they cannot be divided into parts. That would be impossible. Each thing has its whole nature within it, just as humanity is wholly present in each human being. That nature does not split or multiply as the individuals multiply. It remains an idea in the mind.[^V02-501]

“If a link can be established between something that exists outside the mind and something that does not—even though that link itself has no existence—then it is easier still to understand links between things that both exist outside the mind. In the first case, the things have no shared basis, yet a link holds between them. In the second, they share external existence, so their link is stronger and more direct.”[^V02-502][^V02-503]

This is what that wise person said—may God sanctify his noble spirit. It strongly supports what we are trying to prove. His words show, directly and indirectly, that universal essences, unlike individual things, have no external existence. The mind draws them from particular existences, which are the real things, and unites them with those things. We can see this by comparison: from God’s own existence, which sustains all things, we draw the ideas of knowledge, power, life, and other attributes. In the same way, from human existence we draw ideas such as speech, life, sensation, movement, and the ability to walk or write.

God’s existence is perfect and complete, indeed beyond all completeness. The existence of all other things flows from His lasting goodness. He needs no existence beyond Himself to possess His attributes. These qualities express His glory and fullness in His simple being. Other existences are also rays of His greatness and shadows of His light and beauty. But the features we assign to them—whether they are drawn from the existence itself or from something that comes to it later—cannot be drawn or applied to them until they have come from the true Maker and the One who sustains all things. In themselves, they are levels of His appearance and self-disclosure. From this passage we also learn that each thing becomes distinct and individual, and so exists as a particular, through its own external existence, as we explained earlier following the Second Teacher.

Another point we can understand from this person’s words, which he explains more fully elsewhere, is that a fixed essence and the external existence of a thing reflect one another. An essence takes on some features of existence, while existence takes on some features associated with essence. This helps explain many deep questions, including the “secret of destiny”[^V02-504] that leaves reason puzzled, and how some evils can arise from certain levels of existence even though existence itself is good.[^V02-505] His words also show that existence, not essence, is what links things. Cause and effect between two things do not arise from what they are in themselves, but from their connection through existence. The same is true of the way two things are joined: what unites them is the existence they share.[^V02-506] The force of unity comes from the force of existence. The Sheikh hints at this in his commentary on the two short protective chapters of the Qur'an, al-Falaq and al-Nas.

He says: “The one who splits the darkness of nonexistence with the light of existence is the First Cause, the Necessary Being. This follows from His perfect goodness in Himself. The first thing to come from Him is His decree, and it contains no evil at all, except what is hidden beneath the radiance of the First Light: the dimness that necessarily accompanies the essence brought into being from His reality.”

Here he calls the existence of the intellect the radiance of the Light of Lights. Possible beings are flashes of God’s beauty and majesty, like rays from the sun of His glory and perfection. The darkness that necessarily accompanies essences is their possibility: possibility means that, once an essence is colored by existence, it is neither forced to exist nor forced not to exist. The Qur'an calls this God’s coloring: “God’s coloring—and who gives a better color than God?” There is no darkness darker than denial.

When the Sheikh speaks of an essence brought into being from God’s own reality, he means that what the First Cause brings into being is a thing’s existence, which is its very reality—not a universal essence. Essences do not exist on their own. The mind draws them from different kinds of existence and then describes existence through them. This is why existence comes before essence in the external world, while in thought we consider essence before existence, as we explained earlier.

#### Chapter 26 — The Highest Goal of the Earlier Discussions

Know, seeker who uses reason and strives to obey God, and hopes to join those absorbed in witnessing His greatness and immersed in the sea of His beauty and majesty, that a true creator is not one thing that then receives the label “creator.” The very nature of who He is makes Him a source of giving. What He is in Himself is the same as what He is in acting: He is pure action, not a separate thing that happens to have the quality of acting. The same is true of what He creates. In its very being, it is an effect, a gift. It is not a separate thing that is later described as an effect.[^V02-507]

If we considered the cause and its effect apart from their roles, we might treat them as two things: one thing and its effect. But in itself, the effect is simple, just as the cause is simple. When we set aside everything that does not belong to a cause as a cause, and everything that does not belong to an effect as an effect, we see that each cause is a cause through its own reality, and each effect is an effect through its own reality.

Once this is clear, the thing called an effect has no separate identity from the cause that gives it being. We cannot point to two independent realities, one giving and one receiving. If the effect had a separate reality, we could understand it without understanding its cause and its link to that cause. But something considered precisely as an effect can be understood only in relation to its cause. This would undo the rule we set earlier for what it means to be a cause or an effect, which is a contradiction. So an effect, considered in this way, has no separate essence. It is a relation, something that follows from and depends on its cause, with no independent subject underneath it. The cause, by contrast, is the source and starting point entirely through its own reality: everything depends on it.

When the chain of causes and effects ends at one simple reality of light and existence—beyond multiplicity, lack, possibility, limitation, and hiddenness—and we see that it gives through its own nature, shines by its own reality, lights the heavens and the earth, and gives rise to the created world, we understand that all beings have one source and one underlying reality. All else is His aspects, names, and qualities. He is the source; everything else is His way of appearing. He is what truly exists; everything else is a feature or relation of His existence.

Do not mistake these words to mean that possible things are contained within God, as one thing is inside another. That would require two separate existences: one containing and one contained. When the light of truth dawns in a human mind lit by guidance, it becomes clear that real existence has no second. The imagined split fades, and the truth stands revealed. Everything called “existent,” in any sense, is a way the One who sustains all things appears—a quality of His being, a flash of His attributes. The sharp account of cause and effect with which we began has therefore led, through spiritual insight, to this: the cause is the true reality, and the effect is one of its aspects. What we called causing and being caused is the same reality appearing in another way, not the separation of one independent thing from another.[^V02-508]

Hold firmly to this point, where even the greatest minds have gone astray. Give your life to understanding it; then, if you are ready for it, you may catch a glimpse of what you seek.

#### A Problem and Its Solution

Someone might object: On your account, the reality of the Necessary Being—and of every effect—belongs to the broad category of relation. You say that a true cause is essentially a cause, and a true effect is essentially an effect. If either were not, it could not be a cause or effect in itself. But a cause is defined by its relation to an effect. We cannot understand one without the other. You will also say that relation is one of the broadest categories, and that any particular kind within it needs a distinguishing feature. Then the Necessary Being would be made of a general kind and a distinguishing feature, even though you have shown this to be impossible.[^V02-509]

We answer: Relation, like the other broad categories, is a kind of essence that is added to existence. This is why these categories are defined as essences: substance is one kind of essence, quality another, and relation another. In short, any idea that cannot be understood without first understanding another idea belongs to the category of relation. But God is not an idea that can exist in the mind. He is existence itself, pure light and actual being. We can grasp Him only through the rays of what He gives. After sustained work, reason is driven to recognize that the one God causes through His own sacred being and self-sustaining existence—not through some extra feature beyond His own essence. Such an added feature would break His unity and make His reality composite. We cannot form a mental picture equal to His essence.[^V02-510]

It is now clear that God has no essence apart from His all-embracing being, whose light fills the heavens and the earth. It is not the mind, by itself, that declares Him one and self-sustaining. Rather, this truth is shown by a proof that comes from Him and by a light He casts into the mind. The mind can only follow, surrender, believe, and be certain. God is the proof of His unity and the witness to His oneness.[^V02-511]

Once you understand that a thing’s individual identity can involve a relation to something else because of its external existence, you can see that this does not make the thing part of the category of relation. This resolves similar questions: God is knowing, powerful, willing, hearing, and seeing in Himself; basic matter is ready to receive forms; an accidental feature depends on a subject; and an animal soul governs and acts through its body. None of these things belongs to the formal category of relation, though the mind can apply the idea of relation to them. This idea applies to anything that exists, especially the source of all things.[^V02-512][^V02-513]

The same answer resolves the problem of what it means for one part of time to come before another. Things that are related should exist together, but that seems to conflict with the fact that one moment comes before another. Outside the mind, the parts of time do not remain together: each passes as the next arrives. Their order belongs to the way they exist outside the mind. The relation, however, applies to the mind’s understanding of those parts. In thought, they can be present together: the mind considers which comes first and which later, then judges them by their external order. From the point of view of thought, both parts are present at once.

Different levels of existence have different rules. The same reality, such as motion or time, can unfold one moment after another in the outside world, both as it begins and as it continues. In imagination, it can unfold moment by moment as it begins, yet remain whole as it continues. In the world of intellect, it can begin and continue all at once. We have another way to answer this problem about the parts of time, which we will explain in its proper place, God willing.[^V02-514][^V02-515][^V02-516]

#### A Note

Take care not to let your reasoning stumble here and lead you to say: “If the existence of every possible thing depends on something else and is not independent, then God must be subject to origination and change. He must be a place where possible things—or, more specifically, things that come to be—exist.” Remember what we pointed out earlier. An accident or form in a subject or matter is one way a thing exists in itself through its relation to something else, the thing it belongs to. Considered in that way, it must have an existence of its own, distinct from the existence of the subject in which it is found. But here we say that nothing besides the One Real has any existence—not independent existence, nor even dependent existence. The existences of all things are only the Real unfolding through His own modes and states.[^V02-517]

Also, anything called an attribute—whether it is a form or an accident—must contribute to the perfection of what has it. It may complete the thing’s existence and make it a full member of its kind. This is its first perfection, its form. Or it may add excellence to an already complete individual. This is its second perfection, an added accident that gives more than the basic structure and first completion provide. So anything that has an attribute must have lacked something before receiving it: either something needed for its original nature, or something that would make its individual existence more complete. The Real, exalted is He, is free of both kinds of lack and of every trace of imperfection. In Himself He is complete and beyond completion; in His essential attributes He is excellent and beyond excellence. His power has no limit or end, so no perfection can be greater than the perfection He has in Himself.

Nor can there be any excellence beyond the excellence that belongs to Him in His first and essential perfection. Everything else is a flow of His generosity and a flash of His light, appearing after He is already complete in Himself and in His holy attributes. What flows from Him does not become a form for His essence, and what comes from Him cannot add an attribute or excellence to His already boundless perfection. Far above that is He. Any form or quality completes something else, and acts upon a receptive subject; it must itself be completed by something beyond it. Nothing of this kind applies to the Necessary Being. Nothing completes Him or rules over Him. He rules all things with supreme power. Who could give Him power or perfection, or shape His essence so as to bring it from potentiality into action? Would the God of the worlds then be a material potentiality, open to change?

Again, if God could acquire some possible perfection, then His essence would now lack that perfection, even though it could have it. Such a lack would have to be due either to a barrier or to the absence of a cause, since every lack in a possible thing comes from one of these two. Neither is possible in God. A barrier must oppose the perfection it blocks and must act upon a subject, but the Necessary Being has no opposite and is not a subject. As for the cause of that perfection, it would have to be either God Himself or something else. If it were God, the perfection would last as long as He does. If it were something else, that other thing would have to be possible, necessary, or impossible in itself. Each option fails.

First, a possible thing comes after the Necessary Being. If the lack were in God’s very essence, as we have assumed, the perfection that would answer it—and the cause of that perfection—would have to come before Him. That would place a possible thing above the Necessary Being, which is plainly false. Second, there cannot be more than one Necessary Being. Third, what is impossible in itself cannot cause something that is possible in itself. A giver of perfection must possess at least as much reality as the perfection it gives; what is impossible has no reality at all, let alone perfection. We have already seen that it exists only as a word or idea, not in reality. This also answers an objection raised by a distinguished scholar in his commentary on *The Temples of Light*, against the proof for the “possibility of the nobler” associated with the School of Illumination and ultimately with Aristotle. The objection assumes that a nobler possible being may fail to exist because its cause would require something absolutely impossible: a perfection in the Necessary Being beyond what He has. But a possible being cannot depend on what is impossible in itself. We will discuss this argument at length in the section on divine things.[^V02-518][^V02-519]

Now, although there is little room here to explain the point fully—and the goal is beyond what the wings of the mind can reach—you can see that possible things do not relate to the One Real as attributes relate to their subjects, or as accidents exist in the things they belong to.

When you hear[^V02-520] the mystics and people of spiritual insight say that the world is an attribute of God’s beauty or a feature of His majesty, they mean the unfolding we have described. They use such words because language cannot fully express what they mean. Their insight is too clear for them to overlook the imperfection involved in an attribute that inheres in God or a feature added to His existence and making Him different from what He is in Himself. They see all duality as dissolving into the reality of existence and say that only the One who overpowers all things is truly in the house of existence. Inherence itself implies two things.

So every comparison used to explain how the One Reality relates to possible levels brings the idea closer in one respect but can mislead in another. The closest comparison[^V02-521] is the one and the many, as we hinted earlier in the chapter on unity and multiplicity. One[^V02-522] gives rise to number through repetition: without one being repeated, there could be no number. And number contains the reality of one, taken without any added condition. I do not mean one taken on the condition that nothing else exists. The difference between these is like the difference between existence considered without restriction—the all-pervading nature of existence—and existence considered on the condition that nothing else accompanies it. The latter is the level the mystics call Oneness (*ahadiyya*) and the philosophers identify with the full reality of the Necessary Being. The former, existence without restriction, is what the mystics call the reality of the Real:[^V02-523] existence with no limit, even the limit that would come from defining it as “not an essence.”[^V02-524] Understand this distinction.[^V02-525]

Number then divides the one into levels: two, three, four, and so on without end. These levels are not extra qualities added to number, as defining features are added to a general kind. Each number is a simple kind in its own right, though it differs from the others. That is why it is said that a number’s form is its matter, and its defining feature its broader kind: its kinds are distinguished by the very reality they share. In itself, the reality of one has many states and modes without any added defining feature, accidental feature, or individual mark. From each level of its perfection, essential meanings and intelligible qualities arise, which the mind draws out. The mind does the same with the different levels of existence: it forms essential meanings and intelligible qualities, called “essences” by some and “fixed entities” by others. As we have said repeatedly, these are not actual things in the world, nor are they additional to existence except as distinctions made in thought.[^V02-526]

The way one gives rise to number through repetition is an example of how the Real brings the world into being by appearing in the signs of the universe. The levels of one are like the levels of existence, with their qualities and necessary features. The levels of number, and their properties, are like the way some levels of existence are joined with essences and described by them. This is unlike ordinary predication, where the subject and its quality must be distinct in reality. Number’s division into levels of one is also like the way the fixed entities display the effects of the divine names and attributes. The relation between one and number is like the relation between the Real and creation. And the fact that one is half of two, a third of three, a quarter of four, and so on, is like the necessary relations that belong to the Real as attributes. This is the subtle sense in which mystics speak of attributes and the one who has them—a sense many learned people have missed when they discuss the universal levels of existence.[^V02-527]

When existence is considered on the condition that nothing accompanies it, the mystics call this the level of Oneness. All names and attributes are absorbed in it. They also call it the “gathering of gatherings,” the “Reality of realities,” and the “Cloud.”[^V02-528] When existence is considered together with all the things that belong to it, both universal and particular—its names and attributes—it is the divine level, called “Unity” (*wahidiyya*) and the “station of gathering.” In its role of bringing the manifestations of the names, the fixed entities and realities, to the perfections suited to their capacities in the external world, it is called Lordship. When existence is considered without either condition, it is the identity that runs through all beings.

This points to the difference between necessity and possibility. Understand that this division arises from distinguishing existence from essence, and the Lord from the servant. In pure existence and true unity, there is no such thing as being necessary through another. Anything necessary through another is possible in itself. Possibility arises when thought distinguishes a particular mode of existence from existence itself. In short, possibility comes from a certain way of looking at things in the mind, as we have explained.[^V02-529][^V02-530]

Here is how this approach understands substance and accident. If you look closely at things, you find that some are followed by accidents and described through them, while others are the subjects that bear those accidents. The subjects are substances; the dependent features are accidents. Both are existence appearing in one of these two ways. All substances share a single reality—in their being and their concept, their intelligible core. This core is an intelligible model from which the kinds of substance in the world of intellect derive, as in Plato’s theory of the luminous Forms. Each intellect casts only a shadow into this material world. The more remote something is from this world because it is closer to God, the subtler and less complete its material shadow is. The shadow cast by the intellect that governs material substance, considered just as material substance, is matter itself—the source of the general idea of substance in the mind. Each of these three levels of substance—the mental, the intellectual, and the material—is a manifestation of God’s self-sustaining power.[^V02-531]

Just as God’s self-sustaining essence is hidden in itself, the general kind of substance is always clothed in accidents and distinguishing forms. And just as God’s essence, considered together with one of His attributes, is one of His names, substance, joined in the mind to a general meaning or in the external world to a principle that gives it a specific form, becomes a particular substance. In this account it is a manifestation of a specific name; add a particular time or place, and it becomes an individual substance.[^V02-532]

Just as universal names joined in a unified way give rise to compound names, simple substances joined in a unified way give rise to naturally composed substances. And just as some names encompass others, some substances do too. The primary divine names are finite, as are the main kinds and species of substance. But the derivative, relational names are without end, as are individual material things. In the language of the mystics, this possible reality of substance is the Breath of the All-Merciful and universal matter. When it takes on a particular form and becomes an actual being, it is called a divine word. Since God’s manifestations and modes are many, accidents too are without end, though their main kinds are finite.[^V02-533][^V02-534]

This account should make clear that attributes, considered as concepts and names, are distinct in the world of names—that is, when the mind considers them one by one—even though their reality is simple and their existence one. Their manifestations are likewise distinct realities, though in their existence they depend on substance and share the feature of being accidents, whose existence is additional to that of the substance. Everything in the world is a sign of what is hidden from us. So do not imagine, with your limited understanding, that the aims, terms, and coded expressions of the great mystics lack proof or are mere guesswork and poetic fantasy. If their words do not seem to fit sound reasoning and true philosophical principles, the fault lies in the limits of the reader’s understanding, not in their insight. Their direct insight gives greater certainty than proof. In things that have a cause, proof itself is the path to direct insight, because the cause is the proof of what it causes. They also hold that certain knowledge of things with causes comes only through knowing their causes. How, then, could a true proof conflict with what direct insight reveals? When one of them says, “You denied us through proof, though we saw,” he means a proof falsely presented as proof. A true proof never conflicts with spiritual insight.

Some of what we have discussed may seem to conflict with the outward form of theoretical philosophy. In truth, it is that philosophy’s inner spirit, drawn from the light of prophecy and the lamp of divine guidance, which knows the levels of existence and what follows from them. We are not reluctant to state these ideas, even though philosophers and their imitators may reject them. If you want to understand what we mean and see how these claims agree with sound proof, listen as we show the agreement in a few cases that may seem to conflict with it. You can then judge the rest by the same standard, and avoid mistrusting those who know the truth.

Now understand what “name” means to the mystics. A name is the divine essence considered together with one of its attributes or manifestations. Spoken names are names of names. The philosophers’ claim that God’s attributes are identical with His essence does not conflict, on close inspection, with the mystics’ claim that they are distinct from His essence in one respect. The philosophers mean that God’s existence in Himself, without any added meaning, quality, or state, is what the concepts of these attributes refer to. His being described by them does not depend on a real quality inhering in Him, as whiteness inheres in a body; on a negative idea, as blindness is said of a person; on a relation, as “above” is said of the sky; on the idea that His essence is caused, as when essential attributes are said of a thing; or on a created being’s dependence on a cause, as when existence is said of possible essences. The mystics also hold that the attributes are identical with God’s essence in this sense. Still, no one can deny that the concepts of the attributes differ in meaning. Their sameness concerns identity and existence; their difference concerns concept and meaning. So when they say that a name is the same as the one named—or different from it—they are speaking from these two points of view.[^V02-535]

Do not think that because the divine names differ in meaning from God’s sacred essence, they must therefore be possible beings, or that there must be several Necessary Beings, or some possible aspect or composition in God, as if His essence both received and acted. Far above all such things is He. We have repeatedly shown that creation and the giving of existence apply directly and essentially to modes of existence, not to universal concepts such as names and fixed entities.

The same is true of what is uncreated and what is not given existence: these apply to existence itself, not to universal meanings. Just as being created depends on existence and applies to concepts only indirectly, so too being uncreated applies to them only indirectly. God’s names are therefore neither created nor uncreated as separate beings. Their multiplicity at the level of thought does not imply possibility, multiple existences or necessary beings, composition, or change in God’s essence. Nor does it imply different sources of action that would undermine the philosophers’ account of how the chain of beings proceeds from the intellects. The source of multiple acts is the multiplicity of modes of existence; where there are no such distinct modes, there are no multiple acts.

Although the attributes’ meanings differ, their real source is one, a simple and undivided essence. Proof and direct insight show that the divine essence is shared by all the Most Beautiful Names. Their multiplicity belongs to hidden levels[^V02-536]—the “keys of the unseen.” The names are intelligible meanings in the hidden reality of God’s existence. Though many in thought, they are folded into that reality with perfect simplicity. The divine essence, the existence that comes before all other existences, is simple; yet the mind describes it through many attributes of perfection and relation, each marking[^V02-537] one of God’s modes and manifestations. As we have said, these are not separate beings in the external world and do not enter into God’s one existence. They are fixed in the mind, not present as distinct things outside it, though they have effects in an indirect sense. In reality, only existence itself has effects. But their multiplicity in thought gives rise to a multiplicity of effects in the world, a multiplicity that ultimately returns to true unity. In one respect, this multiplicity also belongs to God’s knowledge of Himself: His knowledge of Himself includes His essential perfections in their unified state. Then His pure generosity and “most holy outpouring”[^V02-538] bring His essence into view under each name separately,[^V02-539] first as distinct forms in His knowledge[^V02-540] and then in the external worlds, level by level.

#### Chapter 27 — Why Possible Beings Are Truly Many

Many readers of the mystics’ writings have not reached their level or grasped what they mean. They assume that the mystics’ teaching about the oneness of existence means that only one individual reality exists, and that the identities of all possible beings are merely ideas, with no real things behind them. Some have even said that the noble, holy beings do not exist: the First Intellect, the angels close to God, the prophets and saints, and the great celestial bodies, which differ in kind, size, and effect. They deny the ordered worlds we see around us and the worlds above them, even though their beings differ in kind, identity, and number, and have many different effects and properties. But by “real” we mean what gives rise to effects in the world, and by “many” we mean things with different effects and properties. How, then, could possible beings be nothing in the world?

When mystics say that possible beings are “ideas” or “mental constructs,” they do not mean what an ordinary reader might assume from a quick look at their books. That would be like someone who learns the rules of poetic meter and thinks that this alone will make them a poet, without the ear to tell a sound rhythm from a broken one. If you have the natural ability to recognize these truths, what we have said already can help you see the point. Every possible being has two sides. In one respect, it exists and is necessary through something else. In that respect, it shares the reality of existence with every other being, without difference. In another respect, it has its own distinct identity: it occupies a certain level in existence, with a certain degree of strength or weakness, completeness or lack. A possible being is possible because it falls short of the Necessary Being’s perfection, boundless power, and highest majesty. At each level where it falls short of unrestricted existence—existence untouched by lack, nonexistence, or possibility—the mind recognizes particular features and determinations. These are what some call essences and others call fixed entities. So, when we analyze a possible being, we find two aspects: existence in general and its particular degree of limitation.[^V02-541]

We can consider this in three ways. First, we can consider a possible being as a whole, without separating those two aspects. In this respect, it is a real possible being at a particular level in the order of existence.[^V02-542]

Second, we can consider it as existence without assigning it to any particular level or limit. This is what the mystics call the reality of the Necessary Being. It is present in the Necessary Being and in possible beings alike, because in this respect one existent is not different from another. Existence itself, considered without restriction, does not pass away, change, or come to be. Those features belong to the levels of existence when we consider them along with their particular limits. This unrestricted existence is the same as the level of Oneness. And the unity that spreads through all beings is this same reality, taken simply as existence, not as a general idea applied to many things. It is one actual reality with different levels.[^V02-543]

Third, we can consider a being’s particular determination apart from existence itself. This determination has no reality of its own; it is only an idea formed by the mind. This is the aspect of possible beings that the mystics call “nonexistent.” Their claim is sound: once the mind separates existence itself from a possible being, nothing real remains except the mental distinction it has drawn. The actual realities of the world are many, but the source and basis of their existence is one: the all-pervading reality of existence, which is real in itself and is not made by another. So possible beings really do exist, but the mind’s idea that they are many is different from their real existence. Their existence is real; their multiplicity belongs to the way the mind distinguishes them.

Because these ideas are subtle and hard to express, readers often confuse them. They then accuse the great mystics of contradicting clear reason and sound proof, and of rejecting the philosophy that shows us the different intellects, souls, forms, and bodies, each with its own kind of existence. How weak is the defense some make for the mystics: “At a level beyond reason, reason’s judgments no longer hold”—as if reason’s judgments also failed at reason’s own level![^V02-544] They do not see that sound proof cannot be denied by a healthy mind. Some truths may be too lofty for even a sound mind to grasp, because we are still confined to this world and have not reached the world of mysteries. But this does not mean that sound reason declares any true claim to be false. Some careful thinkers among the mystics have said plainly that reason is a judge. The natural powers and needs of living things, when they act without outside interference or a barrier, cannot be false. There is no falsehood or failure in the natural things that flow from God’s pure generosity. False ideas arise instead from imagination, teaching, or the tricks of a confused mind. The human intellect is a word from God, and its sound judgment confirms that distinct beings truly exist by their original nature.[^V02-545]

The respected al-Ghazali says: “At the level of spiritual insight, one cannot accept what reason judges impossible. But a truth may appear there that reason alone cannot reach. Anyone who cannot tell the difference between what reason rules out and what it cannot grasp is too ignorant to address.” The scholar ʿAyn al-Qudat al-Hamadani says in *The Essence*: “Know that reason is a sound measure. Its judgments are true and certain; it does not lie. It is just, and cannot be unjust.” Their words show that we must not turn away from sound reason. How, then, could these great mystics, who have disciplined themselves and risen beyond ordinary human concerns, judge true by what a just judge—sound reason—declares impossible? The truth is that those firmly grounded in mysticism and spiritual insight do not deny that possible beings exist.[^V02-546]

Al-Ghazali gives a clear statement of the fourth level of unity. He says: “The fourth level is to see only One in existence. This is the vision of the truthful, and the mystics call it ‘being lost in unity.’ Since they see only One, they no longer see themselves: they have lost sight of themselves.” Someone might ask: How can a person see only One while also seeing the sky, the earth, and all the other physical bodies, which are many?

Al-Ghazali answers: “This is the highest form of spiritual knowledge: the truly existent is one. Things appear many to someone whose attention separates them. The person who knows unity does not divide their attention among the sky, the earth, and other beings. They see all of them as one.” The secrets of this knowledge cannot be set out fully in a book. But one example may help. A person can seem many when we consider their soul, body, and limbs. From another point of view, they are one: we call them one human being. We can look at someone without thinking about the many parts of their body, or the difference between soul and body. In a state of deep absorption, the person is wholly taken up in that unity; ordinary attention, by contrast, notices many things. Everything in existence can be seen in different ways: as one in one respect, and as many in another. Some things appear more divided than others. The example of a human being is not an exact match, but it can point us toward this hidden truth. It may help you stop rejecting a state you have not reached. Believe that it is true, and your belief gives you a share in it, just as belief in prophecy gives you a share in it even if you are not a prophet. This vision, in which only the One Real appears, may last or may flash by like lightning. The brief vision is more common; the lasting one is rare.”[^V02-547][^V02-548]

Al-Ghazali says elsewhere in *The Revival of the Religious Sciences*: “A person whose insight is strong and whose resolve is steady sees only God when their mind is balanced. They know no true existence but God and His acts. Those acts are traces of His power and depend on Him, so they have no independent existence of their own. When such a person looks at an action, they see the one who acts through it. They do not focus on it as a sky, an earth, an animal, or a tree; they see it as something made. Their attention does not pass beyond the maker to some separate thing.

“It is like looking at a person’s poem, handwriting, or book. You see the poet or writer in it, and see it as their work—not as ink, gall, and copperas marked on white paper. You are not looking at something separate from its maker. The whole world is God’s work. Whoever sees it as God’s act and loves it as God’s act sees only God, knows only God, and loves only God. They do not even look at themselves as themselves, but as God’s servant. This is what people mean when they say that they are lost in unity and have lost sight of themselves. One of them put it this way: ‘We were present with ourselves, then vanished from ourselves, and remained beyond the self.’

“These truths are clear to people of insight. They are hard to explain because many people cannot grasp them, or because scholars who know them are busy with their own work and see no benefit in explaining them to others.”[^V02-549][^V02-550][^V02-551]

I have quoted al-Ghazali, the great scholar known as the Imam and the Proof of Islam, to reassure those who follow the path of faith and to answer a concern some of them may have: that this special teaching about unity conflicts with reason and religious law. They may say that reason shows the many possible beings plainly, while religious law depends on different levels of existence, different worlds, human actions, and the promise of reward and warning of punishment. How, then, can we say that only God exists?

As we have explained, and as al-Ghazali’s words show, this unity includes multiplicity. It is a unity that gathers many things together. When we consider existence as existence—the all-embracing reality of what exists—we are looking at the level called Unity (*wahidiyya*).[^V02-552] When we consider pure existence, with no other meaning or determination in its essence, we are looking at the level of Oneness (*ahadiyya*).

#### Chapter 28 — How Existence Flows Through Particular Beings

Know that beings exist in three different ways. The first is pure existence: it does not depend on anything else and is not limited by any condition. The mystics call it the hidden reality, the absolute unseen, or the divine essence in its oneness. It has no name or attribute, and we cannot grasp or know it directly. Anything that can be named or described is a concept in the mind or imagination. Anything we know is connected to something else. But this reality comes before all things and remains as it is, without change or transition. It is utterly unseen and unknown except through its effects.

In itself, God’s holy essence is neither restricted by a particular feature nor “absolute” in the sense of being conditioned by limits and distinguishing marks. The things that belong to His essence are conditions for how He appears, not causes of His existence. To say otherwise would imply some lack in Him. Here, “absolute” means that every description, rule, and attribute is denied of the innermost essence. It is not limited or changed by any name, quality, or determination. Even these denials are ideas formed by the mind, so the essence is not bounded by them either.

The second way beings exist is through something else. Their existence is limited by an added quality and described by specific features. This includes intellects, souls, the spheres, the elements, and all the beings made from them: people, animals, plants, and lifeless things.

The third is all-pervading existence.[^V02-553] It is “universal” in a different sense from a general idea. Existence is fully real and actual, while a general idea—whether it is a natural kind or a mental concept—is vague and needs something added to make it concrete. The oneness of this existence is not numerical unity, the one from which numbers are formed.[^V02-554] It is a reality spread through the forms of possible beings and their essences. It does not fit within any one category or limit: timeless or newly originated, prior or later, perfect or deficient, cause or effect, substance or accident, separate from matter or embodied.[^V02-555]

In itself, it does not need anything added to it to appear in all the different levels of existence. Rather, the particular realities of the world flow from its levels and ways of unfolding. It is the root of the world, the source of life, the Throne of the All-Merciful, and what the mystics call “the Real through whom creation is made.” It is the Reality of realities. It is many while remaining one: as particular beings differ through their essences, it is timeless with the timeless, changing with the changing, understood with the intellect, and sensed with the senses. For this reason, some may mistake it for a general idea. But it is not.

Words cannot fully describe how this existence spreads through essences and includes all beings. We can only use comparisons. In one respect, it is to the beings of the world as basic matter is to particular bodies; in another, it is like a general kind, such as the most general kind, in relation to the individual things and species beneath it. These comparisons bring the idea closer in some ways and lead us away from it in others.[^V02-556]

Understand that this existence is not the familiar general idea of existence that the mind abstracts from things. That idea is a mental concept. As we have seen, it belongs to a class of ideas that the mind forms by reflecting on other concepts.[^V02-557] Many philosophers, especially later writers, missed this distinction.[^V02-558]

The mystics state this plainly. The careful thinker Ṣadr al-Din al-Qunawi, after describing existence in this third sense and comparing it to matter, says: “Existence is the matter of possible beings, and the readiness of each being is prepared by the wisdom of the Creator, the All-Knowing and All-Wise, in the way He knew it to be prepared.”[^V02-559] The common feature of these beings is weakness: each is limited by possibility, distant from the fullness of existence, and caught in multiplicity. The mystic Ibn Arabi calls this existence the “Breath of the All-Merciful.”[^V02-560]

This distinction clears up the confusion around “the Dust” and “the Phoenix.” When the mystics call the Necessary Being “Absolute Existence,” they mean existence in the first sense: pure reality with no other thing alongside it. They do not mean this third, all-pervading existence. Confusing the two has led to serious errors: unbelief, moral license, the claim that God dwells in created things, and the idea that God takes on the limited qualities of possible beings and becomes subject to defects and change. Once we distinguish the levels of existence, the pure transcendence and holiness upheld by careful philosophers and most religious scholars remain intact.

As one saying puts it:

> Whoever understands my words has not lost their inner sight;
> only a person with clear vision can understand them.

To point to these three levels and the general ideas the mind forms from each, ʿAla al-Dawla writes in his notes on *The Meccan Revelations*: “True existence is God; all-pervading existence is His act; and restricted existence is its effect.” By “all-pervading existence,” he does not mean the familiar abstract idea of existence, but the reality that spreads through all things. Al-Qunawi calls it “general” because this is the broadest and most obvious feature the mind grasps first, not because this name exactly describes what it is.[^V02-561]

Al-Qunawi also says in *The Key to the Unseen* that the apparent existence spread through the identities of possible beings is called “general existence,” and “the manifestation that runs through the reality of possible things.” This name points to its broadest and most obvious feature, the first one the mind can grasp; it is not an exact name for what this reality is in itself. In his commentary on the opening chapter of the Qur'an, al-Qunawi points to the first level of existence as “something the mind can understand by seeing its effect, though it cannot witness its essence.” He also writes:

> Gathering is a state, not an individual thing;
> it has power over all individual things.[^V02-562]

#### Chapter 29 — What First Flows from True Existence

Once you have understood the three levels of existence, as far as you can, you can see what first flows from the Necessary Being: all-pervading existence. In His oneness, God's essence has no description beyond itself, yet it holds every quality of beauty and majesty. All-pervading existence is also called the Cloud, the all-gathering level, the Reality of Realities, and the oneness that gathers all things. It is sometimes called the level of divine unity. When we consider the Necessary Being in relation to His names in the mind and to possible beings in the world, we speak of the level of unity and the divine presence.

This flowing forth is not causation in the usual sense. Causation requires a difference between cause and effect. It applies to particular beings, each distinct in its own way and marked by its own fixed identity. But here we are speaking of unrestricted existence. Its unity is unlike the unity of one individual, a kind, or a broader class. It is what makes every kind of unity and distinction possible.[^V02-563]
