The Necessary Being, considered through the name Allah, which gathers all the divine names, is the source of all-pervading existence. This is so because of His all-gathering reality and the particular meanings contained in His beautiful names. The mystics call this name the All-Gathering First and the Leader of Leaders. Through it, God acts on particular beings, each of which is contained within all-pervading existence. This is the sense in which we can affirm a relation between God and creation.

When philosophers say that the First Intellect is the first thing to flow from God, based on the principle that “from one, only one can proceed,” they are speaking generally about distinct beings that differ in nature and effect. In that sense, the First Intellect is first among the separate beings. But when we examine it more closely, we find all-pervading existence joined with a particular nature, limitation, and possibility. At the most basic level, then, what first flows forth is all-pervading existence itself. At each level, it is joined with a particular nature and limited in a particular way.

As with the Necessary Being, whose essence is beyond description in its oneness but includes every divine name and quality in the level of unity, all-pervading existence is distinct in itself from particular natures and fixed identities. Yet at each level it is joined with a particular nature and its accompanying features. These natures are not made separately or added to existence. What is brought about is each particular level of existence itself, not the fact that it is this particular level or its union with a particular nature. The Necessary Being's oneness is the source of all-pervading existence, and the unity of His names is the source of the world, both its existence and its particular natures. Glory to the One who joined oneness to oneness and multiplicity to multiplicity. Without this, there could be no connection between what acts and what is acted upon, and no action or creation.[^V02-564]

#### A Note on Upholding God's Transcendence

As we have said more than once, the word “existence” is used in different senses. In one sense, it means a thing's reality: what rules out nonexistence. Philosophers use it in this sense for God. In another sense, it means the mental idea of existing. No reasonable person treats that idea as a thing's own reality, much less as God, who is the source of all realities and beings.

This second sense is called “relative existence,” “being there,” or “existence as a statement,” as when we say, “Homer is a poet,” or “Zayd is a writer.” This kind of existence can go together with nonexistence when we speak in different respects: Zayd may be at home and not at the market. In fact, such a statement can be called nonexistent, since it has no separate existence in the world apart from the way we describe it.[^V02-565]

Just as philosophers use “existence” in the first sense for God, many leading mystics do as well, including Ibn Arabi, Sadr al-Din al-Qunawi, and the commentator on *The Meccan Revelations*. Ibn Arabi and his student often use “absolute existence” for all-pervading existence—the shadow, the Cloud, or the all-gathering level—not for God as the Necessary Being. The commentator sometimes uses “absolute existence” for God. I suspect the disagreement between him and Ibn Arabi comes from using the same word in different senses.

Among those who use “existence” for God are the Persian poets Attar, Firdawsi, and Jalal al-Din Rumi. Attar writes:

> The Lord whose very being is His own;
> all things are pages bearing signs of Him.

Firdawsi writes:

> You are the heights and depths;
> I do not know what to say—whatever is, is You.

And Rumi writes:

> We are nothings appearing as beings;
> You are absolute existence; our being is yours.[^V02-566]

Theologians who hold that God's names may be used only when scripture allows them see no doubt that “existence,” or even “the Existing,” cannot be used as a name for Him. Whether it may be used to describe Him is disputed. Some say no; others say yes, and this is the sounder view, since the word “existence,” like the word “thing,” can apply to both God and created beings, though they exist in utterly different ways. When the commentator says that God's essence is beyond existence and nonexistence, he seems to mean the abstract idea of existence, not real existence itself. He takes this to be the reason that the word should not be used for God and that those who use it are unbelievers. But this reading of the great thinkers is mistaken.[^V02-567][^V02-568] All the great philosophers and mystics agree that God is beyond every defect and that His essence cannot be fully grasped. The mystics speak of a special way of knowing God: the seeker recognizes God through God when the self has fallen away in the experience of unity. Anyone who studies their books carefully will see that they do not disagree about God as the reality of existence.

The objections that the commentator raises against Ibn Arabi and his student, Sadr al-Din al-Qunawi, are really about words; they agree on the main ideas. For example, Ibn Arabi says that “absolute existence” is the Real, described by every quality. The commentator objects that the Real is God, not absolute existence, whether unrestricted or restricted. But Ibn Arabi may mean all-pervading existence, which spreads through all natures and can therefore be said to bear every quality, including the qualities of created things—while God Himself remains beyond them. Or he may mean God's pure existence, in which case “every quality” means every perfection and divine quality that belongs to God Himself, not something added to His essence. It may also refer to God as He is known through the appearances of His names and qualities, which are expressions of His vast mercy, generosity, and care.[^V02-569]

Elsewhere, Ibn Arabi writes, “In reality, there is only the Real.” The commentator replies, “Yes, but through His generosity, He has brought forth His manifestations. The outpouring has unrestricted existence, the manifestations have restricted existence, and the One who pours it forth has true existence.” Ibn Arabi also writes, “The Real is nothing but existence.” The commentator again replies, “Yes: He is true existence; His act has unrestricted existence, and its effect has restricted existence.”

After explaining that possible beings receive existence and that their natures have no existence of their own, Ibn Arabi writes, “If you understand this, you will grasp a great truth: He appears in everything, but He is not the essence of things. Glory be to Him: He is Himself, and things are themselves.” The commentator agrees: “That is right. Hold firmly to this view.” These and other objections come down to different uses of words and different ways of speaking. Words such as “essence,” “reality,” “identity,” and “self” can mean pure existence or a thing's particular nature. The same confusion arises with “existence”: it may mean God's true existence, all-pervading existence, or restricted existence. Anyone who carefully reads the commentator's notes on *The Meccan Revelations* will see that he and Ibn Arabi do not truly disagree about existence.

The special kind of unity taught by the mystics lies beyond what the mind can reach by reasoning alone. Until a person is guided by God, it is hard to explain in terms that fit the expectations of formal thinkers. So their words can seem to conflict. Differences of this kind appear even in scripture and the sayings of the Prophet: different religious communities appeal to the same texts while holding different beliefs. “For each of you We have appointed a law and a way.”[^V02-570]

Shaikh Abdullah al-Ansari writes in *The Stations of the Wayfarers*, speaking of the unity known to a few:

> The third kind of unity is God's own, which He alone deserves. He has let a glimmer of it shine into the secrets of some of His chosen ones, then left them speechless when they try to describe or explain it. The scholars of this path have tried to point toward it with elaborate names and distinctions, but words only hide it further; descriptions make it harder to approach, and explanations make it difficult to grasp. Those devoted to spiritual practice and those who experience spiritual states turn toward this unity. Reverent worshippers seek it, and those who speak of the unity that gathers all things have it in view. Yet no tongue speaks of it, and no words can point to it. True unity lies beyond anything a created being can point to.[^V02-571][^V02-572][^V02-573]

I have answered the mystics' account of unity in these lines:

> No one can make the One into one by speaking;
> whoever claims to describe Him has missed the point.
> Every description of His unity is borrowed,
> and the One Himself makes it fall away.
> His unity is His own oneness;
> whoever describes Him has turned away from it.

#### Chapter 30 — Why Possible Things Have No Existence in Themselves

From what you have heard, you may now accept the special unity of God: existence is one reality, and that reality is the Real. The natures and identities of possible beings have no real existence of their own. They appear to exist by taking on the light of existence. We understand them through the ways existence appears and unfolds. What appears in every manifestation, nature, state, and distinction is the reality of existence itself—or rather, the Real, appearing in different ways through His manifestations, names, and relations.

A particular possible nature, such as being human or being an animal, is like abstract ideas such as possibility, thingness, and nature in one respect: none has an independent existence in itself. But the difference[^V02-574] between these two kinds[^V02-575] lies in how we use them. When we call an existing person human, we refer to that person, whether the person exists in the world or in the mind. Words like “possibility” and “thingness,” by contrast, do not point to a separate thing in the world. In short, particular natures represent particular beings; abstract ideas describe those natures. Neither kind is a separate concrete thing that we can see or that can act on our minds and senses. Possible beings are empty in themselves. What exists always and forever is God alone. Existence is one; multiplicity and difference belong to how we know it. From one mode of existence, the mind can understand many meanings.

True existence appears to itself. This is called the Hidden of Hidden. It also appears through its act, lighting the heavens of spirits and the world of bodies. This is its manifestation as existence, called Light, through which natures and fixed identities show their features. Because these natures differ, existence appears as many in a secondary sense, though it is one in itself. Each nature becomes a mirror in which the features of the others appear, but the act of existence itself is not multiplied or repeated. The Qur'an says, “Our command is only one, like the blink of an eye.”[^V02-576]

Multiplicity lies in the mirrors, not in the light or the act that makes it appear. The one act is a single light that reveals different natures without making them. The light of the sun seems to multiply as it shines through many windows, though the light itself is one. This, the mystics say, is why possible natures have no existence in themselves. They are not “nothing” in the sense of being just a negative idea in the mind, or merely an idea formed by thinking about other ideas. They have no existence of their own in the world. What is not existence, or something that exists in itself, cannot be made real by something else. Only existence and its different forms are real. Natures seem to exist because the mind connects them with the levels and forms of existence.

So possible natures remain without independent existence forever. They do not receive existence as a quality added to them. Instead, they become mirrors through which true existence appears, as different levels of existence take on different forms. As the poet says:

> Existence flows through all things in its fullness;
> its distinct forms are matters of thought.

And:

> The possible’s face is dark in both worlds;
> its poverty never leaves it—God knows best.

The great thinkers have made this point plainly.[^V02-577] In explaining the verse “Everything perishes except His Face,” Abu Hamid al-Ghazali describes those who know through direct experience:

> They saw directly that nothing exists but God, and that everything perishes except His Face. They did not mean that things will perish at some future time. Each thing is already perishing, from beginning to end. Considered in itself, every thing is pure nothing. Considered in the way existence flows to it from the First Reality, it exists—not in itself, but in the way it turns toward its Maker. Each thing has two faces: one toward itself and one toward its Lord. From the first, it is nothing; from the second, it exists. So nothing exists but God, and everything perishes except His Face, forever.

The books of Ibn Arabi and his student, Sadr al-Din al-Qunawi, are full of this teaching. They say that when they compared what they had seen directly with the Qur'an and the Prophet’s sayings, they found that it matched the plain meaning of those texts without needing reinterpretation. They therefore rejected readings by theologians and other scholars that conflicted with what they had repeatedly seen for themselves. They took the verses and sayings in their first, apparent sense, while avoiding any bodily meaning or resemblance to created things in speaking of God and His attributes.[^V02-578]

One scholar says that religious reports should be accepted as they are, without reinterpretation or denial. By “reinterpretation,” he means giving a statement a meaning other than its usual one; by “denial,” he means refusing to accept that usual meaning. A Persian verse puts the problem this way:

> To speak of Him risks likening Him to creation;
> to keep silent is to deny Him.

Some scholars have even condemned those who interpret verses and reports. Most religious scholars, however, hold that their apparent meanings are true, even if they also have other, deeper meanings. The Prophet is reported to have said that the Qur'an has an outer meaning, an inner meaning, a limit, and a point where it opens up.[^V02-579] If these verses and reports did not carry their first, apparent meanings, they would be of no use to ordinary people. Instead, they would only confuse them. People understand the unclear passages in three different ways.

The first group are those firmly grounded in knowledge. They accept the first meaning without drawing from it anything that would make God deficient or contradict what is known about Him. The second group are scholars who rely on rational argument, including Islamic philosophers. They interpret the verses and reports to fit their theories, because their thinking has not moved beyond formal reasoning. The third group are strict literalists, including some Hanbali scholars and people who focus on language and reports. They remain within the bounds of the physical world and conclude that God is a body or something bodily. God is far above what they say.

For most people, especially those who study only the practical rules of religion, the third approach may seem the most suitable. Al-Ghazali says as much in *The Jewels of the Qur'an*: “Either be completely literal or completely transcendent and holy—as the saying goes, ‘Be wholly Jewish, or do not play with the Torah.’” But most people cannot hold to pure transcendence, so they are left with the literal approach.

The truth is that both extremes—the literalist’s and the interpreter’s—depart from the balanced path of those firmly grounded in knowledge. Each looks at the manifestations of existence with only one eye. The literalist looks with the left; the interpreter with the right. The complete knower sees with both. Every possible being has two sides: one turned toward itself and one toward its Lord. With the right eye, the knower sees God as the source of existence, appearing in every thing. All goodness, completeness, and beauty return to Him. With the left eye, the knower sees creation: it has no power except what God gives it, and can only receive His gifts. In itself, it has no existence and is marked by weakness and lack. Every flaw, loss, and failure belongs to this side.

> The glass grew thin, and the wine grew thin;
> they seemed alike, and the matter became unclear.
> It was as if there were wine but no cup,
> and as if there were a cup but no wine.

Some ignorant imitators[^V02-580] of the mystics have taken this too far. They lack the learning and insight of the great mystics, and their weak understanding and wavering beliefs have led them to think that the One, beyond all forms and appearances, does not truly exist. They think only the world of forms and its spiritual and physical powers are real, and that God is simply this whole visible world. They call this world the “Great Human”[^V02-581] or the “Clear Book,” and say that each individual human being is a small copy of it. This is open unbelief and false teaching. No one with even a little knowledge would say this. To attribute it to the great mystics is a blatant lie; their deepest teachings reject it.

The confusion[^V02-582] may come from the many ways the word “existence” is used. Sometimes it means God; sometimes it means all-pervading existence; sometimes it means the general idea of existence in the mind. The mystics often use “existence” for the shadow-like existence of particular beings, and then speak of it in ways that can sound as if they are speaking of God Himself. For example, Ibn Arabi says that whatever enters existence is limited. Al-Qunawi speaks of a hidden reality beyond the circle of existence and creation. In one of his letters, Ala al-Dawla says that above nature lies a world of absolute nonexistence, and that the darkness of nonexistence surrounds created existence, though the source of life is found within it. Elsewhere he says that above the world of life is the world of existence, and above that is the realm of the Beloved King, with no end to His realm.

In such statements, “nonexistence” may mean what is beyond this shadow-like, created existence. The word may also mean what cannot be known or described by the mind. The pure oneness of God lies beyond anything a created mind can see or grasp. “Existence” can also mean what is found or encountered. In that sense, God cannot be found as an object apart from His manifestations, even though He exists fully and in Himself. His essence appears to itself in pure oneness, the “Hidden Treasure” mentioned in a well-known saying. Then He appears in another way, through His acts and in the world. This further appearance unfolds through the forms in which He is known.

Through this later appearance, God's enduring reality is seen in the mirrors of intellect and soul, according to each observer's ability. It appears with greater or lesser clarity, depending on the level of existence and the strength of the observer's understanding. This variety of appearances does not divide God's essence, lessen its perfection, or change His eternal existence. He remains as He always was, even when nothing else existed. That is why the poet says:

> The face is one, though it seems to multiply
> when you count the mirrors.

#### Chapter 31 — Why Evil Does Not Belong to Existence Itself

Know[^V02-583] that a possible thing can be called a “thing” in two senses. One is its existence: it appears at some level, in some world. The other is its nature—what it is—which the mystics call its fixedness. This second kind of thinghood means that the mind knows a nature through the light of existence. The mind draws the nature from existence and understands it as belonging to that existence. This is true whether we mean a thing in the world or in the mind. The nature is not made or caused separately, and it is never detached from existence. This is not what the Muʿtazilite theologians[^V02-584] mean when they speak of fixed essences.

The more careful view is that a nature is not a quality added to existence. Rather, the mind understands it through existence, and the two belong together in the thing itself. We see existence; we understand a thing's nature. This kind of “thinghood” lets us distinguish a possible nature from an impossible one. The possible nature can receive God's gift of existence and hear the command “Be,” entering the world by God's permission.

Someone might object: “You have said that each possible nature is identical with its existence in the world, and follows it. How, then, can the nature come first and receive existence?”

We answer: it does not come first in the world. But the particular forms of existence have an earlier, undivided level. This level comes from God's knowledge of Himself and of the levels of His names and qualities. Before these forms descend, divide, and become many, their names and qualities are present within God's oneness. From them come the natures and fixed identities of possible beings. These natures follow the existences God knows, because His knowledge of them is perfect and is one with His essence.[^V02-585]

God's eternal knowledge of these natures is what allows their existences to appear later, each at its proper level. When existence flows from God and becomes distinct in the world, each form is joined to a nature. No separate act of creation is needed. So no nature comes before its existence in the world.

In God's knowledge, too, the fixed identities follow His existence, which is His knowledge of things. He knows their existences together in one simple awareness, yet knows their natures in detail. To know the complete cause is to know its effects. The same is true in the world: what flows from God and is made is the many forms of existence. Natures follow them only in a secondary sense. So when mystics say that fixed identities exist and receive God's gift of existence, they mean that each one's features appear in the light of existence. They do not mean that existence becomes a quality attached to it.[^V02-586]

As for the verse, “Was there not a time when the human being was not even mentioned?”, the “not a thing” refers to a particular human existence, not to the human nature. The same is true of the saying, “God was, and there was nothing with Him.” For the mystics, possible natures are “things” only in the sense that they are known to God, not because they have their own existence. That is why Abu al-Qasim al-Junayd, when he heard this saying, replied, “And now it is as it was.” ʿAla al-Dawla makes the same point about those who reach unity without believing that God enters or joins created things: they see their Lord as He was when nothing was with Him, and know that He is now as He was. This is the state of unity.[^V02-587]

Once these points are clear, we can say that natures and fixed identities do not exist on their own. First, they are gathered within unity; later, they appear among the many forms of existence. Yet when the mind considers them in themselves, apart from existence, they become the source of many judgments: possibility, limitation, and other defects. Evils and hardships that belong to these natures come from them, without being separately created. In this sense, they shield God from having defects attributed to Him.[^V02-588]

But to ignore natures altogether leads to confusion, unbelief, and the denial of wisdom and religious law. Recognizing the way natures have their own features helps answer many difficult questions.

These questions include why evil occurs in the world and why some people sin. Such things arise from the limitations of a being’s nature, defects within it, or its lack of readiness. Here lies the secret of destiny. Some knowers of God count the fixed identities among His hidden aspects: they are forms of His self-knowledge, known before they appear. But this subject can confuse weak minds.[^V02-589]

Few scholars can consider it without being harmed or led astray. A person needs firm knowledge, strong spiritual practice, and a steady mind to stay on the right path. Al-Qunawi says that these hidden aspects also belong to what is contained in God's oneness, but that the complete knowers understand the difference. There are deep waters here with no shore.[^V02-590][^V02-591]

Al-Qunawi also says that existence appears in two ways. In one, the features of things appear everywhere; in the other, existence itself appears in each level. These are not the same: existence takes on a different form at each level, and what belongs to a thing at one level may differ from what belongs to it at another. Whether a feature is present or absent depends on its conditions. These may be a level, a state, a place, a time, or something else. The details of how existence appears are endless, even if the basic patterns are limited.[^V02-592][^V02-593]

#### Chapter 32 — How Evil and Suffering Can Arise Without Undermining the Goodness of Existence

From what we have said, you may have understood this: when the one, true existence appears in one of the many natures—each different in meaning, in what makes it a thing, and in its implications—it casts truth upon what is false and makes that nature real through His existence. It appears in each nature according to what that nature can receive, taking on its color and a particular quality at each level where it appears.

You have already learned that these essential qualities, which philosophers call natures and mystics call fixed identities, come before particular existences in the mind but follow them in the world. What is given and brought into being is existence, not nature. So the differences among natures in themselves correspond to differences among existences in strength and weakness, priority and lateness, height and lowliness. In short, the one reality of existence appears in each thing according to what it can receive. It is like the same water in different places: here fresh and sweet, there salty and bitter. Or like sunlight, which takes on the color of the glass it passes through, though it has no color in itself.[^V02-594]

Ṣadr al-Dīn al-Qūnawī says in a treatise on some sayings: “Whatever is, in itself, free of different limiting qualities and perfectly subtle, appears and takes a distinct form in each being, level, and world according to that being’s capacity to receive it and the way that level calls for it.”

This shows that every action or quality ascribed to a manifestation belongs to it in one respect and is denied of it in another. Each particular being has a nature and an existence, a way of being what it is and a way of appearing. God gives existence to natures; praise and thanks are His for giving goodness to things.

Every possible being has two aspects: its nature and its existence, its own possibility and its necessity through another. What we affirm of it or deny of it is true under one aspect or the other. You also know that what beings share, and what makes them good, is existence; what sets them apart and accounts for evil is their natures. So the language of declaring God beyond all likeness and describing Him in terms that apply to creatures—in God's words and in the words of His prophets—refers to these two aspects. Both are to be understood in their plain sense, without contradiction or reinterpretation.

Creation, giving, action, actuality, completion, formation, lasting existence, gentleness, and mercy come from God and His power. The capacity to receive, limitation, defect, weakness, passing away, decay, change, loss, overpowering, and anger come from creatures and from what they can receive. A Persian poet put it this way:

> From that side come creation and completion;
> from this side, change at every moment.

Differences among the things that can receive existence, among possible realities, and among natures arise partly from their own character and partly from the most holy outpouring, called the eternal divine decree. This decree is the way things are present in God's knowledge in the best and most fitting order, following His names and qualities, which are one with His essence. Their existence in the world comes when God gives them existence at their appointed times and according to their readiness. This is called the holy outpouring and is the decree as it takes effect in the world. The measure follows God's knowledge, and neither decree is separate from His essence.

This does not mean that things do not come into being, change, or pass away, one after another. We will discuss this when we explain time, the timeless order, and eternity, and how these are ascribed to the Originator of all in a way that involves no change in His essence, qualities, or actions as His actions. When we explain how He encompasses things in time and space in a transcendent, all-embracing way, it will become clear how God speaks with His angels and messengers—not in the way the Ashʿarites say.

To sum up, the limits and defects found in the existence of possible beings come from what receives existence, not from existence itself.[^V02-595][^V02-596] This answers the dualists’ objections and removes the apparent contradiction between two verses of God's Book. One says, “Whatever good comes to you is from God, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.” The other says, “Say: All is from God.” How fitting that the verse goes on to ask, “What is the matter with these people? They can hardly understand what they are told!” This points to how subtle the issue is. The question of cause and effect has confused people because it is so difficult and deep: effects are veils over the faces of their causes. Many have gone astray over it. Scholars remain puzzled; some affirm causes, while others deny them. That is why it is said that people are either puzzled or ignorant about this question. Whoever finds a way through this grave difficulty, a slippery path from which only those purified by God can escape, becomes a true believer in God's oneness without denying the causes they see.

To sum up: as we have said, every thing has a particular face turned toward the Lord of Lords, the cause of causes. Through it, the thing praises Him and declares Him free of imperfection. The power we see at work in created causes comes from one of God's beautiful names, of which that cause is a manifestation. The cause praises God through that name, in the language of its own state—not through its created self, which passes away. In one respect, differences among beings reflect differences among God's names. But evils and defects come from what each thing can receive and from its readiness, as things meet and collide at lower levels while existence descends.

All true religions teach that every good, perfection, and state of well-being comes from God, while every evil, affliction, and limitation, from whatever point of view, comes from creation. As Abraham—peace be upon him and our Prophet—said, “When I am ill, He heals me.” He attributed the illness to himself and the healing to his Lord.

God's words also point to this: “If You punish them, they are Your servants; if You forgive them, You are the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful.” The punishment of ignorant, wretched souls does not come from the First Reality as an act of revenge. It follows from their own deeds and actions and from their bad character. Because of an earlier sin, they are like someone who gives in to an appetite until he falls seriously ill: on the Day of Resurrection, they themselves carry the fuel for their fire. Forgiveness and joy, by contrast, follow from the First Existence, His mercy, and the existence He gives things according to what they can receive.

In a prayer, our master Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him and his family, says that all good is in God's hands, while evil is not attributed to Him.[^V02-597][^V02-598] In another saying, he says, “Whoever finds good should praise God; whoever finds otherwise should blame no one but himself.”

It is clear, then, that God gives existence, brings natures from nonexistence into being, and carries them from potentiality to actuality, from incompletion to completion, and from hiddenness to appearance. May God guide us on the straight path.

#### Chapter 33 — How Possible Beings Reflect the Presence of God

As we said earlier, all natures and possible beings—things that depend on God for their existence—are mirrors in which God's existence appears, and places where His sacred reality is revealed. A mirror, simply as a mirror, reflects the image that appears in it. But things we can sense have many layers and limits. They can reflect the First Reality only from a great distance, as Aristotle says in *The Theology of Aristotle*, also known as *On Divine Lordship*.

Here is the point: God reveals Himself to things in one way and appears in all possible beings in one way. This appearance in things is itself God's second appearance to Himself, at the level of His acts.[^V02-599] His perfection is so complete that His essence overflows from itself and flows outward. This second appearance to Himself cannot be like the first. Two identical things cannot exist, and what follows cannot equal what comes before it in the fullness of existence. A ray, for example, is not as bright as the light that shines it.

People use different terms for this second appearance: the descent of God's necessary existence, or its outpouring; the Breath of the Most Merciful in one way of speaking; cause and effect in another; God's active love among people of spiritual insight; or God revealing Himself to something other than Himself. The variety of ways He appears reflects the many names and qualities in His simple, undivided knowledge.[^V02-600][^V02-601] The one divine essence, whose existence depends on nothing, appears in each mirror of nature according to what that nature can receive. It does not have many kinds of appearance in itself, as some have thought. That would break God's true unity—far above Him is that.[^V02-602]

In Chapter 63 of *The Meccan Revelations*, Ibn Arabi writes: “When someone sees their image in a mirror, they know for certain that they have seen it in one sense and have not seen it in another. The image may look very small because the mirror is small, or very large because it is large. They cannot deny that they saw their image. Yet they know that their image is neither inside the mirror nor between them and it. So they are neither wholly right nor wholly wrong when they say, ‘I saw my image’ or ‘I did not see my image.’ What is this image they see? Where is it? What is it like? It is absent and present, there and not there, known and unknown.[^V02-603]

“God showed His servant this example so that they would understand: if a person is confused and unable to grasp the nature of this image—although the image itself belongs to the created world and they cannot grasp its true nature—how much more confused and ignorant must they be about the One who created it.”

I say: Ibn Arabi draws our attention to how much subtler God's self-disclosures are than the image that puzzles the mind and defeats our understanding. We can become so confused that we even ask whether the image we see is real. The mind cannot say it is sheer nothingness, because it knows that it is not nothing; but it cannot say it is pure existence either, because it knows that the image is not a separate thing, distinct from what it reflects, nor is it pure possibility.

The mirror and the image it shows are meant to guide us toward understanding how God's light passes through things, how He appears in the mirrors of natures, and how He appears in each thing according to what it can receive. A possible thing's existence is not identical with its nature—what it is. Nor is it identical with God's own existence, which depends on nothing, since the possible thing is limited, incomplete, and dependent. Yet it is not wholly separate from God, because it cannot exist on its own, as we have already proved.

We have also shown that God's self-disclosure to things is one, and that He gives existence in one way. Its many forms come from the many natures and their differences. The same mirror does not receive a repeated act of self-disclosure.[^V02-604] This shows that knowing a reality requires that the reality itself be present, not a second image of it. If the image's appearance were the same as the reality's own appearance, God's self-disclosure would be repeated.[^V02-605] The people of spiritual insight have already shown why this cannot be.

Another subtle point now becomes clear. Philosophers disagree about how the human soul knows the truths of things when it is freed from the body and joined to the source that gives existence. Does it know by receiving forms from that source, or in the opposite way—by seeing those truths within the active source itself?[^V02-606] Both views have been defended in books on the subject. But careful reflection shows that the answer is neither. The soul becomes fully joined to its source by letting its own sense of self fall away and finding its life in the Real. Absorbed in seeing God, it sees things as they truly are in the world. It does not see a second copy of these realities, different from the things themselves. That would mean God's self-revelation had been repeated, which we have already shown cannot happen.

The mystics and the Stoic philosophers also rejected the idea that knowledge is only a mental image. They say that things exist in the world just as they are known to God; their existence comes from Him, not from themselves. God's knowing things is the same as His giving them existence through the light of His being.[^V02-607] So when a person with direct insight sees the forms of things through contact with the sacred world, that person sees the realities themselves as they exist—not copies or likenesses of them. Someone with limited understanding, however, sees God through the mirror of things and forms a belief about Him from what they see. God appears to that person in a form that matches their belief. If, on the Day of Resurrection, He appears in a different form, that person rejects Him and seeks refuge from Him.[^V02-608]

This is why people's beliefs differ: each person sees God through different things. It is what is meant by God's words, “I am as My servant thinks of Me.” Each person accepts the ways God appears that fit their condition and rejects what their nature cannot receive.

The traveler who has reached God and let go of the self sees Him without anything created standing between them. But this state can also keep the traveler from seeing created things: absorbed in God, the traveler is too narrowly focused to see anything else. Before letting go of the self, created things hide God. Afterward, the traveler may lose sight of God's different levels and the ways He appears through His names and qualities.

The person who knows God fully returns from this unity to the many details, guided by what they have seen in unity. They see God through His names and qualities, and see creation through God. They travel through the world of realities, lit by their Lord. At this level, they know things by knowing their source: the source of their existence, the source through which they appear, and the source of their natures and fixed identities. They can then truly say they see things as they are, in God's noble face, which holds the secrets of the heavens and the earth.[^V02-609]

Every mirror other than God Himself reflects something because, considered by itself, it is empty of the images and qualities it shows. This is true of natures that reflect existence; of the universal imagination, which displays the world of images; of a person's imagination, which displays imagined forms; and of ice, water, crystal, and iron, which display visible images. Hearing, taste, smell, and touch also display the things they sense.

But their ability to reflect is not the same as their own nature or existence: each mirror exists in its own particular way. So what we see in a mirror is not wholly the same as the thing itself, but neither is it wholly different from it. If you focus on the mirror itself—on its being made of iron or glass, for example—it blocks your view of the image. Its particular nature takes over, and it no longer shows the image as before.

If you stop looking at the iron or glass as a thing in itself and instead notice how it is connected to the image, you see the image across from you. The mirror remains what it is, even when you do not pay attention to it. So what you see is not simply the same as the thing's own existence. The mirror's particular nature hides the thing's true existence in the way that nature allows. This is why images look different in different mirrors—larger or smaller, straight or bent, clear or faint—because their surfaces may be curved, hollowed, polished, or cloudy, even when we do not notice these features.[^V02-610] The same is true of all other possible mirrors.

God is different.[^V02-611] His essence gives rise to things and to our understanding of them. His essence is the mirror through which things appear just as they are. His essence, by itself and without any added quality, is the source of things; so seeing His essence is the source of seeing things. Full knowledge of a complete cause gives full knowledge of its effect. God knows Himself only through Himself: His essence and His awareness of it are one, without any difference or division. These are also the source of the world and of our awareness of it. In the same way, we can know created things only through their own existence. Their existence and their being known are not two separate things. Because cause and effect are joined without a gap, the existence of created things and our awareness of them are joined in the same way. Just as the existence of things depends on God, so does our knowledge of them as they truly are.

God's essence, then, is the mirror through which all things, general and particular, are known as they really are, without error or falsehood—unlike the mirrors of possible beings.

**A consequence:** We cannot truly know any thing without knowing the one who brings it into being, as we have already explained. Each thing has one way of existing, and that existence comes from one source. This follows from what we know about the impossibility of one thing's appearing twice and the oneness of God's self-revelation. It also shows why the philosophers are right to say that certain knowledge of things with causes comes only through knowing their causes. Think carefully about this, if you want to understand it fully.

**A further point:** You can now understand a familiar claim in general philosophy: knowing a particular cause gives you knowledge of its particular effect, while knowing a particular effect gives you knowledge only of its cause in general, not of that cause in its full detail. The usual explanation is that a particular cause produces a particular effect, while a particular effect calls for a cause of some kind. But that is only a claim, not a proof. The real reason is that an effect is a particular form of its cause, a specific way the cause appears. If you know the cause itself, you know its different forms and ways of acting. If you know only the effect, you know the cause only through that one form. It is like seeing a person's face in a mirror that makes it look larger or smaller, bent or straight.

Ibn Arabi explains this point in the chapter on Seth in *The Bezels of Wisdom*. He writes: “God's self-revelation always takes the form that the one receiving it is ready for. The one who receives it sees only their own image in the mirror of the Real; they do not see God Himself. Yet they know that their image appears only in Him. It is like an ordinary mirror: when you see images in it, you do not see the mirror, though you know that you see those images only in it.

“If you understand this, you have reached the highest point a human being can reach. Do not strain to rise higher: there is nothing beyond this, only sheer nothingness. God is your mirror when you see yourself, and you are His mirror when you see His names and how they take effect. Those names are not other than His essence.”[^V02-612][^V02-613][^V02-614]

**A further point:** You can now see that people's different beliefs about God come from the different ways they see Him appear. People reject what they cannot accept from their own point of view. When God appears to pure intellects through statements about what He is not—He is not a body, He is not limited, and so on—those intellects accept this and declare Him free of likeness, imperfection, bodily form, and multiplicity. This is the way of minds that rise above the body, including some philosophers. Minds still tied to matter, such as imagination and the powers of embodied souls, reject this view. The same is true of people whose judgment is ruled by those powers, such as many literalists and those who picture God in human form: they can understand God only by comparing Him to created things.

When God appears through qualities that describe what He is—knowing, powerful, and so on—hearts and thinking souls accept Him. They are connected to bodies, yet their inner nature can also rise above them. Pure intellects, however, reject this way of speaking, because their nature allows them only to turn away from bodily things. Each kind of soul accepts the forms of God's appearance that suit it and rejects the ones it cannot receive. Everyone sees God only through their own way of existing and knows Him through their own identity. God appears to each person only in the mirror of that person's self.

Every power is hidden from God by its own limits and thinks nothing is better than itself.[^V02-615] The angels argued over Adam; reason and imagination each claim mastery and refuse to yield to the other. Reason claims that it can grasp the truths of things through thought, but in fact thought gives it only mental ideas and the features that follow from things' existence—not the things as they are in the world. At its highest, while it relies on thought alone, reason knows in a general way that it has a Lord, free of defects and worldly qualities. But it does not directly see God, His self-revelation, or the detailed ways His light reaches every part of the worlds.[^V02-616]

Imagination also claims rule and rejects reason's claims whenever they go beyond its own range, because it grasps meanings tied to particular things. Each power, then, has something of the devil's pride. Likewise, everyone apart from the fully developed human being has some share of hidden or open idolatry: they serve one of God's names, but not God through all His names. This is what the Qur'an says: “Some people worship God on the edge: if good comes to them, they are content; but if a trial comes, they turn away.”

The fully developed person accepts the Real, follows His light through all the ways He appears, and serves Him through all His names. Such a person is truly God's servant.[^V02-617] That is why this is the fullest kind of human being. They see the First Reality through every divine and created appearance, without dividing God's essence or His act of appearing. As we have said, God's self-revelation is one. Its many forms come from the many ways He relates to things and the different conditions under which He appears; these are called natures and fixed identities.[^V02-618]

These natures have no existence of their own and are not separately created or acted upon. They are joined in some way to the different forms of existence, which are shadows of the one light and flows of God's self-sustaining existence. Their features can then be said of them. Those who are complete know realities without doubt. They are servants of the All-Merciful, who walk humbly through the world of truth. When the ignorant speak to them, they answer, “Peace.” The ignorant are weak and limited in mind, unable to understand God's appearances in every place and at every level.

Defiant and rebellious souls, by contrast, do not honor what is sacred to God. They are far from Him and shut out from His light. They accept only what their own nature shows them. Of them, the Qur'an says, “You and what you worship apart from God are fuel for Hell.” Here, Hell means being shut out from seeing God's self-revelation and His nearness. These people mistake the many forms of existence—which are gifts from God and ways He appears—for separate things with their own power. They worship those things and credit them with creating what happens at lower levels, instead of worshipping God through all levels and all His names. They do not understand that God appears in every thing and is also beyond every thing. Glory to the One who is free of evil; glory to the One whose kingdom contains nothing that He does not will.[^V02-619]

**A brief summary:** Traveler on the path of truth, look at unity and multiplicity, together and apart. If you see only unity, you are with God alone, and the multiplicity that belongs to creation disappears. If you see only multiplicity, you are with creation alone. But if you see unity hidden within multiplicity and multiplicity lost within unity, you have joined both kinds of perfection and reached the best of both states. Praise be to God, Lord of greatness and majesty, whose are the most beautiful names.

#### Chapter 34 — Another Proof That the Necessary Being Is One

The Necessary Being is complete in reality; nothing lies outside Him.[^V02-620][^V02-621]

Know that the Necessary Being is utterly simple in reality. Anything whose reality is simple in this way is all things. So the Necessary Being is all things, and nothing is outside Him. Here is the general proof. Suppose some thing lay outside His reality. Then His very essence would make it true to say, “He is not that thing.” If His essence did not make this denial true, we would have to say, “It is not true that He is not that thing.” Since these are opposites, one must be true. Denying the denial means affirming the thing, so that thing would be part of His reality after all. But we assumed that it was not. That is impossible.[^V02-622]

And if His essence makes it true that some thing is not Him, then His reality must include both what it is and the absence of that other thing. It would be made up of parts, at least in the mind's analysis. But we began by saying that His reality is simple. This, too, is impossible.

Here is the detailed proof.[^V02-623] Suppose we say, “A human being is not a horse.” The reason for denying that a human is a horse must come from something other than humanity itself. A human, simply as a human, is human and nothing else. Humanity itself cannot be what makes a person “not a horse.” Otherwise, understanding what a human is would be the same as understanding “not a horse.” Anyone who understood humanity would also have to understand that contrast. But that is not what happens: we often understand what a human being is without thinking about horses. Still, in reality a human being is not a horse. That truth does not come from the idea of humanity alone. Considered simply as an idea, a human being is only human.[^V02-624]

The same is true of every nature: considered on its own, each is only itself. But in reality a thing must also be either some other thing or not that thing. A human is either a horse or not a horse, a star or not a star. A star, in turn, is either human or not human. If a thing cannot be something else, then the denial of that other thing must apply to it. So in reality a human is made up of its humanity and the fact that it is not a horse, not a star, and not many other things. Its nature includes both what it is and these absences.

Anything to which a true denial applies must be made up of parts. In thought, you can picture the thing and what is being denied of it, compare them, and say that one does not belong to the other. What makes a thing what it is differs from what makes the denial true of it. If you say, “Zayd is not a writer,” the image of Zayd by itself cannot make the statement true. Otherwise, Zayd, simply as Zayd, would be nothing at all. The statement must include Zayd and some lack that means writing is absent from him, such as a lack of ability or readiness, or some other limit.[^V02-625]

Pure action has no potential waiting to be fulfilled. Pure perfection has no readiness waiting to be realized. Absolute necessity and completeness leave no room for possibility, defect, or lack. Absolute existence has no trace of nonexistence in it. Otherwise, at least in the mind, it would combine action and potential, perfection and lack.[^V02-626] The Necessary Being is existence itself, existing through Himself, without any trace of multiplicity or possibility. Nothing real is denied of Him; only negations, nonexistence, defects, and possibilities are denied of Him, because these are all forms of lack.[^V02-627]

To deny lack is to affirm existence. So He is the fullness of all things, the completion of everything incomplete, and the one who makes up for every flaw, hardship, and defect. What is absent from Him is only the weakness, limits, and evil found in things. He is the goodness within all good things and the fullness of all existence. A thing is more fully and truly itself when complete than when it is incomplete. This is what the Qur'an points to when it says, “You did not throw when you threw; rather, God threw”; “He is with you wherever you are”; and “He is the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden, and He knows all things.”[^V02-628]

#### Chapter 35 — Why possibility and potential are not direct causes of existence

First, possibility is a kind of nonexistence, as we have already explained. What does not exist cannot cause or bring about anything. So possibility cannot be a cause, or even part of one. A cause brings something into existence. To do that, it must have a definite character that makes it the cause of this thing rather than another. Otherwise, there would be no more reason to call it a cause than to call something else a cause. Anything with a definite character in itself exists. So every cause exists, and anything that does not exist cannot be a cause.

This also shows that possibility cannot be part of a cause. If it were, it would have to play a causal role in making that cause effective, which brings us back to the same point. Whether we count possibility or leave it out makes no difference. Like countless other forms of nonexistence, it may be tied to the acting cause without producing its effect.

Here is a second proof. Possibilities in possible things must either differ only in number or differ in kind. If they differ only in number, then the possibility of one thing cannot cause another thing to exist. Things of the same kind share the same features that belong to them by their nature. So one possibility would have no greater claim to produce an effect than another. If one did produce that effect, they all would. For example, if the possibility of an agent's existence caused a heavenly sphere to exist, then the possibility belonging to every existing thing would produce a sphere. Each sphere would produce another, without end.

The other option—that possibilities differ in kind—does not hold either. Possibility is the opposite of necessity, and necessity is one. The opposite of one thing is one thing. Yet possibility can be divided into the possibility of a substance and the possibility of a quality. The possibility of a substance can then be divided into the possibility of a body and that of something without a body. What is divided must have something in common. And what we understand by possibility is the same in every case; the differences lie outside the idea of possibility itself. This makes possibility one kind of thing, with differences that come from other features. Besides, possibility is a kind of nonexistence, and nonexistence has no differences in itself. So possibility cannot bring anything into existence, whether its own subject or something else.[^V02-629][^V02-630]

There is another proof. If possibility caused something, it would do so either with its subject or without it. If it acted without its subject, it would be independent of the subject in its action. But anything that acts independently of something also exists independently of it. Possibility would then be a separate substance, which is impossible. If possibility acted together with its subject, it would be part of the cause. A part of the cause would help make the whole cause effective. That effect—making the cause effective—would itself happen either with the subject or without it. The second option is impossible for the reason just given. The first would mean that this same possibility had to be part of the cause that makes it effective. We could repeat the question without end. This would create an endless chain of causes acting together, which is impossible.[^V02-631]

You may ask how philosophers can say that the possibility of the First Intellect gives rise to the heavenly sphere, and that the possibilities of the other intellects give rise to the heavenly bodies. This gave their opponents a chance to mock them. One critic wrote that the claim that the First Intellect's possibility causes the outermost sphere, while its necessity causes the second intellect, was nonsense unfit even for ordinary people, let alone for anyone who claims to seek the truth.

I reply that saying “possibility causes something” means something like saying, “The absence of a cause causes the absence of its effect.” We do not mean that nonexistence acts in the world. We mean that when a cause is absent, its effect does not exist. In the same way, when philosophers say that possibility gives rise to a sphere, they mean that the First Intellect's existence has a possible limit, so what comes from it is a limited being, such as a body.[^V02-632] In truth, existence causes existence, and nonexistence leads to nonexistence in the sense just explained. Nonexistence is not, in itself, a cause of something that exists; it can only play an indirect role.

The same applies to the possibility of an essence and to the possibilities that prepare matter to receive a form. They do not bring things into existence, whether those things are created outright or come into being through change. We have already shown that possibility itself causes nothing. As for the powers that prepare matter, if they acted without bodily matter taking part, they would be independent of matter in their action and would exist without matter.

As we have seen, anything independent of something in its action is independent of it in its existence too, since existence is part of the ability to act. That would make these powers immaterial, contrary to what we have assumed. If matter took part in their action, it would be receiving and being affected. Matter receives; it does not act or bring things about. A thing cannot be both necessary and possible in relation to the same thing, so matter cannot be the agent or a partner in bringing something about. Thus bodily powers, even when they are active, cannot give existence; how much less can powers that only prepare matter do so.[^V02-633][^V02-634]

When philosophers say that the possibility of an essence causes it to exist, they mean only this: necessity and impossibility make a thing unable to receive the effect of a cause, while possibility does not. Possibility removes a barrier to the cause's action. It makes an essence able to receive existence, but does not itself bring existence about.

When they say that near and distant possibilities cause existence, they mean that they prepare matter to receive a form. In the world of matter and readiness, opposing forms get in the way of one another. When matter takes on one form, it cannot receive certain others. Some forms block a new form more strongly than others. Matter in the form of water is far from receiving the form of fire. Matter in the form of air is closer to receiving it. The hotter the air becomes, the more suited it is to fire. At a certain point it is equally open to either form. If it grows hotter still, its readiness to receive fire becomes stronger than its readiness to remain air. It then receives the fiery form and becomes fire.

So the possibility that comes with readiness means that a barrier or opposing form has been removed, either entirely or in part. When the barrier is entirely gone, the potential is near; when it is only partly gone, the potential is distant. A body's balance of qualities, though it is itself a real quality we can feel, can be called a readiness for the forms of animal, plant, or mineral life—in other words, a possibility of receiving them. Pure opposing qualities keep matter from receiving a higher form. As those qualities lose their force and opposition, matter becomes more able to receive a higher form. When it is fully prepared for the highest form, its opposing qualities have almost disappeared. It can then receive a perfection like the one received by the matter of the heavens, which is free of opposing forms and qualities: the thinking soul. The highest source is always giving, and once the barrier is gone, matter receives from it.[^V02-635]

Near and distant possibilities, then, make matter ready to receive by removing barriers. We have now shown that possibility, potential, and nonexistence do not produce the existence of anything. They prepare the things that can receive existence.[^V02-636]

Every thing that comes into being in our world must be preceded by nonexistence. To say that this nonexistence is one of its causes means that matter has shed its earlier form and can now receive a later one. The same applies to things whose nature is to unfold over time, such as time and motion, and whatever depends on them: for each new part to arise, what was already present must pass away. The same is true of continuous things at rest and of mathematical quantities. For one part or point to be present in a place, another must leave it, because these limited things cannot fill the same place all at once.[^V02-637]

#### Chapter 36 — Why powers in bodies need a spatial relation to act

We have established that anything that depends on something else for its existence also depends on it for its actions. This nearly proves the point we are making. Matter exists in space, and anything whose existence depends on matter also depends on a spatial position, at least indirectly. Its power to act depends on that position too. A bodily cause cannot act on something with which it has no spatial relation.[^V02-638]

Here is a fuller explanation. Any power that produces an effect works in one of two ways. Either its effect must first occur in a particular place, so that its effects elsewhere depend on what it has already done there;[^V02-639] the closer something is to that place, the more readily it receives the effect. Or its action does not depend on first affecting a particular place. Fire is an example of the first kind: its effect varies with distance.

The nearer something is to the fire, the sooner and more strongly it is heated.

When a power's effect depends on a particular body in this way, the power is related to that body either because it needs the body in order to exist, as fire does, or because it needs the body only in order to act, as a soul does. In either case, we can say that it acts through a spatial relation. But a power whose effect depends only on the effect being possible in itself, and whose action is not directed to one body rather than another, has no connection to matter in its existence or in its action. It is wholly independent of bodies and belongs to the realm of immaterial intellects.

This shows that powers in bodies cannot affect the existence or qualities of immaterial beings. Near and far apply only to things that have a place and position.[^V02-640] It also follows that bodily powers cannot bring about prime matter or the substantial forms that make a body what it is. So they cannot bring any body into existence.

Someone might object: if a bodily power cannot affect an immaterial being because it has no spatial relation to it, then an immaterial being cannot affect a body either, since it has no spatial relation to bodies. If so, we should not say that immaterial beings bring bodies into existence.

I reply that for an immaterial being to affect something, it is enough that the effect be possible in itself. Once that possibility is present, the effect comes from the immaterial cause, whether or not the effect has a place in space. But for a bodily power to act, the possibility of an effect is not enough. The thing receiving the effect must also have a spatial relation to the body whose power is acting. An immaterial being cannot have that relation to a body.

When matter receives a form or perfection from an immaterial being, matter itself is what receives the effect. It is not merely a channel between the recipient and some other thing. In this case, matter is not the acting cause; it is the recipient. These are two different roles.[^V02-641]

You may ask: do philosophers not say that the body's coming into being causes the soul to come into being, even though the soul is immaterial and has no spatial relation to the body? I reply that you will learn how the soul comes to be through the body. Its cause is an immaterial being, while the body carries the possibility of the soul in a certain way, as I will explain later.[^V02-642][^V02-643] So the body is a condition that allows the soul to flow from its cause; it does not produce the soul itself.

Likewise, a quality or power that exists in a subject does not cause the subject. It is a condition that lets the subject receive the effects that follow from that quality or power, as I will explain later. A subject and what exists in it have no spatial relation to one another.

#### Chapter 37 — Why existence alone can act as a cause or be caused

Only existence can act as a cause. Consider anything other than existence apart from its existence: in itself, it stands equally open to existence and nonexistence. So it is not actually existent in itself. Its essence cannot cause anything to exist—not even itself, much less anything else. Whatever causes something must affect that thing's existence. Existence, then, can act as a cause. If we imagined existence apart from essence, it would be even more able to cause things, since essence itself is only possibility and dependence. We already know that what does not exist has no effect. Likewise, if we imagined a material power apart from matter, it would be more able to act because it would be free of matter's defects and limitations. But that is a separate question. Our concern here is that existence can act as a cause in general.

Fakhr al-Din al-Razi raises this objection: existences either differ only in number, or they differ in kind. If they differ only in number, the existence of one thing cannot cause the existence of another. No individual of a single nature has an inherent priority over another; all stand on equal footing. But the other option—that existences differ in kind—cannot be right either. Existence is divided into the existence of substances and the existence of accidents, which exist in something else. The existence of substances is divided into that of bodies and things without bodies; the existence of accidents is divided into the different categories of properties. What is divided must have one meaning in common. The idea of existence is self-evident and shared by all existences; their differences come from features outside that idea.[^V02-644] If existences differed in kind, each would be made up of a general kind and a distinguishing feature.[^V02-645] Then the existence of the First Effect would be composite, and a single cause would produce more than one effect—a conclusion they reject.[^V02-646]

The principles we have already established are enough to set aside arguments like these. We have shown that existence is one simple reality, though it has degrees of strength and weakness, priority and posteriority. Existence can be caused because essences, by themselves, cannot be caused. What can be caused is either existence itself or an essence's being joined to existence. But that joining comes later than the essence and depends on the existence of the essence that receives it, as we have explained. So what is caused in itself is existence alone.[^V02-647]

Al-Razi objects that existence is one essence. If so, a cause acting on existence should be able to cause any effect. Here is his argument. When water warms up, warmth is one essence among many. Its actual existence, which flows from separate principles, either depends on a condition or does not. If it does not, warmth must exist forever, since an essence can receive existence and the cause is always giving it. If warmth does depend on a condition, what depends on that condition: the existence of warmth or warmth itself as an essence? The first cannot be right. Contact with water is a condition for coldness, and the existence of coldness is no different from the existence of warmth. So contact with water should also be a condition for the existence of warmth, since whatever is a condition for one thing is a condition for things like it. But then warmth would have to arise whenever water made contact: the essence is ready to receive it, the cause is always giving, and the condition is present. That would mean anything could arise whenever anything else did, with nothing having its own specific cause or condition. Our senses show that this is false.

The second option—that warmth itself, rather than its existence, depends on the condition—leads to the conclusion al-Razi wants. If an essence depends on a condition, it depends on something else. Whatever depends on something else must have a cause, and the chain must eventually end with the Necessary Being. So essences themselves are caused, not just their existence. That is the end of his argument.

I say that the argument fails because it assumes that existence is one essence applied to all things in exactly the same way, rather than a reality that varies in degree. On that assumption, it also follows that God's existence has no effect on anything. Al-Razi has said repeatedly that God's existence is equal to the existence of possible things. Then anything that comes from God's existence could also come from the existence of anything else, so God would have no special power to cause any effect. But a cause must have a particular connection to its effect. Otherwise, its existence would make no more difference than its nonexistence in bringing that effect about. And if its existence makes no difference to whether something comes about, it is not the cause of that thing. God's existence, then, would cause nothing. God is far above such a claim.[^V02-648]

Earlier we distinguished between existence as the existence of blackness and blackness as something described as existent. We showed that need cannot belong to existence simply as existence. Need arises only when an essence is considered in relation to its existence: the essence is possible, and so it depends on something else. The essence depends on existence for its being; existence itself is not what depends. As for the claim that essences are not caused, we have already explained how to understand it.

Al-Razi and those who think like him assume that an effect must first have its own identity before the cause acts on it. Only then, they think, does it acquire a need that is not part of its essence, and the cause gives it existence. They think this applies to essences, since an essence relates to existence like a receiver to what it receives, or like matter to form when we consider them separately. So they conclude that a cause produces an essence's being existent, not existence itself. But they have missed this: without existence, how could an essence arise at all and then be possible, dependent, necessary, and finally existent? We have explained how an essence is described in these ways, so there is no need to repeat it. We have also explained how existences themselves depend on the giver of existence even more deeply than essences do, and what it means for existence itself to come to be through dependence on its cause.

When al-Razi says that essences are not caused, he means that being caused is not part of what an essence means. This is like saying that an essence's attributes do not belong to it when we consider it just as it is in itself. That means only that the attribute does not belong to it under that description; it does not mean that the attribute never belongs to it in reality. In the same way, essences are caused in the sense that being caused truly applies to them, though they are not caused in the sense that being caused is part of their essence.

This is what al-Razi means by his reinterpretation. Our approach has shown that the first of these two senses leads to the second.[^V02-649]

#### Chapter 38 — Why an effect need not be preceded by nonexistence

This question is like the one in the previous chapter. It belongs in a discussion of cause and effect, but it also fits with what we will say about what comes before and after.[^V02-650] Since some debaters have taken a strong stand against this view, we need to make the case more firmly. I will give several proofs.

First, the universe is either possible in itself at all times, or its possibility is not eternal. The second option is impossible. If its possibility were not eternal, the universe would have to be impossible in itself: it cannot be necessary in itself, and what is impossible in itself cannot become possible. Its becoming possible must be due either to what it is or to something outside it. If it is due to what it is, then its possibility always held.[^V02-651] If it is due to something outside it, that cause is either eternal or not. If it is eternal, the impossibility it brought about could never end. If it is not eternal, we must ask the same question about that cause. This line of reasoning must eventually reach something necessary in itself, and then the impossibility could never end.[^V02-652][^V02-653]

Someone may object: even if this impossibility depends on the Necessary Being, its effect may depend on a condition. When that condition disappears, so does the effect. I reply that if the condition is necessary in itself, it cannot disappear, so the impossibility cannot disappear either. If the condition is not necessary in itself, we must ask the same question about it. The reasoning does not go on without end; it ends with something that exists necessarily in itself. So we cannot claim that possible things were unable to exist from eternity.[^V02-654]

But there is a difficulty. If we consider an event as something preceded by nonexistence, then its possibility cannot be limited to one time rather than another, given the arguments above. Its possibility must always hold. Yet that does not mean the event[^V02-655] stops being an event. We have taken it as something preceded by nonexistence, and being preceded by nonexistence is part of what it is. That feature cannot be removed. If the event can be possible at all times and still remain an event, this proof does not hold.

I reply that we are not discussing things whose very nature is change and coming-to-be. We are discussing things whose nature can have coming-to-be as a feature. Many things, such as parts of motion and time, cannot last forever; they necessarily come into being. They need a cause because they are possible, but their possibility is only the possibility of this kind of existence: lasting existence is impossible for them. A thing's being possible means that some kind of existence is open to it, not that every kind of existence is open to it. A substance cannot exist as an attribute, just as blackness cannot be whiteness. Motion and things like it cannot exist by lasting unchanged.

Here is a second proof. If an effect needs a prior nonexistence, it must be either because the effect itself needs that nonexistence in order to exist, or because the agent needs it in order to act. The first is impossible. If the effect needed nonexistence in order to exist, that nonexistence would have to be present along with it. But nonexistence at the same time as the effect contradicts the effect. Something that contradicts an effect cannot be a condition for it. The second is impossible too. The effect's existence contradicts its nonexistence, and a condition that contradicts what it must accompany cannot be a condition at all. So neither an effect's existence nor an agent's power to act requires a prior nonexistence.

Here is another proof. When events come into being and continue to exist, they either need a cause while they exist or they do not. If they do, they need a cause from eternity to eternity, because they remain possible in themselves.[^V02-656] If they do not, then either they have ceased to be possible, or they have remained possible but no longer need a cause. They cannot have ceased to be possible: what is possible in itself cannot become necessary in itself. And if possibility belongs to a thing by its own nature, it is always possible. If possibility comes from something outside it, then the fact that it is possible must also be possible. That would require another possibility, and that one yet another, leading to an endless chain.[^V02-657][^V02-658] So events remain possible while they continue to exist, and therefore still need a cause, because possibility is the basis of that need.[^V02-659][^V02-660]

Someone may ask: once a thing exists, does it not become more likely to exist than not? This greater likelihood either belongs to existence itself or is an added feature. If it belongs to existence itself, then as soon as something exists, this greater likelihood is present; once present, it makes the thing independent of a cause. But the cause is needed for the thing to exist. Its existence would then remove its own cause, which is impossible. If the greater likelihood is an added feature, it must have a cause. The thing depends on that feature, and the feature depends on a cause, so the thing is not independent of a cause.[^V02-661]

Here is another proof. An effect might be said to need a cause because it exists now, because it was once nonexistent, or because its existence was preceded by nonexistence. It cannot need a cause because of nonexistence itself, since nonexistence is nothing and needs no cause. Nor can it need a cause because its existence was preceded by nonexistence. Being preceded by nonexistence is a feature that necessarily belongs to the event once it exists. A thing may be possible in itself, yet a feature that belongs to it can be necessary: four can be possible, while being even is necessary for it. In the same way, an event may be possible, while having come after nonexistence is necessary for it. What needs a cause, then, is existence alone.[^V02-662]

Here is another proof. God has attributes and features that follow from Him.
