Different schools understand them in different ways: as relations or negative descriptions, as positive realities, or as states and forms within divine knowledge.[^V02-663][^V02-664][^V02-665] None of these attributes is necessary in itself, since there cannot be more than one Necessary Being. They are possible in themselves but necessarily belong to God. So a cause can produce an effect without that effect first having been nonexistent.

Someone might reply that these attributes are not acts, and that only acts need to be preceded by nonexistence. I answer: even if we agree that something not preceded by nonexistence should not be called an act, we have shown that something possible in itself can depend on a cause that exists eternally alongside it. Once this is accepted, there is no reason to forbid it in some cases. The only remaining objection would be to the word “act,” and that has no value in an argument about reality.

Here is another proof. Features that belong to a thing by its nature depend on it, but do not come after it in time. Features that follow existence also never leave it. There is no time when four is not even, a triangle does not have three angles, or fire is not hot. We can go further: a cause and its effect can exist together. Heating and burning happen together; pain follows a harmful imbalance or a separation in the body. A clearer example is that knowledge gives rise to being knowledgeable, and power gives rise to being able. These effects exist along with their causes; they do not wait until later. So a cause and its effect being present at the same time does not remove the effect's dependence on the cause.[^V02-666]

Here is another proof. A thing, considered as existing, must exist; considered as absent, it must be absent. This is a kind of necessity that follows from the condition we set. Coming into being is the order of these two states. If we consider a thing only under these conditions, its existence and its nonexistence are both necessary in their respective states. Necessity leaves no room for dependence on a cause. So coming into being, considered just as coming into being, cannot be what makes a thing need a cause. If we do not consider the thing only under those conditions, it still has necessary existence while it exists and necessary nonexistence while it is absent. But considered in itself, it still needs a cause. We can see, then, that what makes a thing need a cause is its possibility, not its coming into being.[^V02-667][^V02-668]

Here is another proof. The basis of a thing's dependence must not remain unchanged when its cause is present. Otherwise, it would still need another cause. But coming into being remains true of an event whether or not its cause is present. If coming into being were what made the event need a cause, the problem would remain. If possibility is the basis of its need, however, it does not remain unchanged once the cause is present: the essence, as an effect of that cause, becomes necessary. So possibility, and nothing else, is what makes a thing need a cause.

These are ten proofs that a possible essence needs a cause because it is possible. A caused existence itself depends on its giver by its very nature, not because of some added feature. Even so, we have also proved that the whole universe and everything in it came into being in time. I will return to this, if God wills.

Those who reject this principle offer weak arguments. First, bringing about what already exists is impossible, so the need for a cause must come before existence.[^V02-669] Second, if two things have both existed from eternity, neither has a better claim than the other to depend on its companion, since neither has an advantage over the other.[^V02-670] Third, it is established that the Creator of the universe acts by choice. But choice and intention apply only to bringing something into being; we find that we cannot intend to create something that already exists.[^V02-671] Fourth, once a building exists, it no longer needs a builder; once writing exists, it no longer needs a writer.

The first argument can be answered in two ways. We can challenge it by pointing out that being able requires power, and being black requires blackness, among other examples.[^V02-672] Or we can answer it directly: bringing about something that already exists is not impossible; in some cases it is necessary. What is impossible is giving existence a second time to something that already exists. Besides, the argument assumes the very point it is meant to prove.

The second argument fails because a cause is not a cause simply because it has existed from eternity. Nor is an effect an effect simply because it came into being in time. That would make neither of two things that came into being more fit to cause the other—for example, the motion of a hand and the motion of a key it turns. A cause is a cause because of what it is. Its very nature gives it priority over its effect, as the sun is prior to its light: light comes from the sun, not the other way around. The related objection—that if two things are inseparable, the absence of either must mean the absence of the other, so neither has more claim to be the cause—has already been answered.[^V02-673][^V02-674]

As for the third argument, choice and intention bring about the beginning of an action, while their continuation goes with the action's continuation. If intention and choice continue, they can continue to direct an action as well. To claim this is impossible simply assumes what is in question.

As for the fourth argument, builders and writers cause bodies to move and change place. They are not the causes that preserve a building's shape, keep things in place, or make them last. Other, nearer or more distant causes do that. I will explain this in its proper place.

#### Chapter 39 — Why every event in time depends on an unbroken circular motion

Know[^V02-675] that a complete cause cannot come before or after its effect in time. So the immediate causes of events must themselves come into being. If they had always existed, their effects would have always existed too. Whenever a cause exists while its effect does not, the effect is possible in relation to that cause: what is impossible in itself cannot be produced by a cause. So when the effect does come into being, something else must tip the balance toward its existence rather than its nonexistence. That something also needs a cause, and the same question arises again, until we reach what makes the effect necessary and brings it into being.

The immediate cause of an event, or at least one part of its cause, must therefore come into being along with it. We can ask the same question about that new cause. This leads either to an endless chain or to an event whose very nature is to change and come into being, such as motion, or a nature that renews itself.[^V02-676] But a nature that comes and goes, whose absence belongs to an earlier time and motion, must be preceded by another nature that keeps its time. This time-keeping nature has two sides. One is known to God: His eternal knowledge and the form of His decree, which is not part of the created world. The other belongs to the created world and comes into being anew each day.

The philosophers accepted an endless chain because they had not found this principle. They said the chain must either exist all at once or be arranged so that one thing comes before another. The first option is impossible, as we have already shown, so they chose the second. But then the events in the chain must either be separate instants or unfold over time. If they are separate instants, they must follow one another without end. That is impossible. And even if it were possible, each instant would be separate from the next, so the earlier instant would not have to end in the later one. It could not be the cause of the later instant, contrary to what they assumed. If the events unfold over time, they are motion.

Here is the point. When something new arises in matter—for example, a human form in semen—the cause gains a relation to that matter that it did not have before. The matter must move and change so that what was far from receiving the form comes closer. The semen undergoes a connected series of changes. Its powers and conditions shift until it becomes suited to receive that form from its active cause.

To explain this, a cause may either prepare something or bring about its effect. A preparing cause may come before the effect because it does not produce the effect itself. It only brings the recipient close to the point where the acting cause can produce it. The acting cause, by contrast, must be present with its effect. Preparation consists of a real process of change, linked step by step: each new state can arise only after the previous one has passed away.

Consider a heavy body falling. It does not reach any point along its path without becoming ready to move from there to the next point. Reaching each point prepares it for the next part of its fall. Its weight is what moves it. But until the body has reached a given point, its weight cannot move it from there. Once it reaches that point, its weight can move it onward. The earlier motion brings the body closer to the next effect; this is what philosophers mean when they say that motion brings causes closer to their effects.

The same thing happens when someone walks through a dark place by the light of a lamp. As the person steps onto a patch of ground lit by the lamp, the light falls on the next patch, which the person can then reach. The lamp directly causes the light on each patch; walking prepares the next patch to be reached. Likewise, someone who intends to make a pilgrimage has one general aim that gives rise to a sequence of smaller intentions. Reaching each point along the way makes it possible to form the next intention and move toward the next point. The general aim causes these smaller intentions and the movements that follow them, and remains present throughout.

With this in mind, we can see that one acting cause remains present with all the distinct effects that arise one after another. This is what makes it a cause and gives rise to its effects. The individual events have their own earlier and later order, but that order is part of their very nature; no cause makes one event earlier or another later. The cause brings about the events themselves, not the fact that one comes first and another comes after. The stages of a falling body's path are like the forms taken by gravity; the patches of light on the ground are like the forms taken by the lamp's light; and the smaller intentions are like the forms taken by the single general aim.

In the same way, each changing natural kind has an eternal cause that gives rise to its changing forms. But the cause's effect on each particular thing comes at its proper time, when matter has become ready and strongly disposed to receive it. This readiness arises through motion and change, and through the distinct forms that follow one another in matter: the earlier forms prepare matter to receive the later ones.

Nothing, then, can come into being without earlier events linked to it. There must be an unending sequence of events, each following another, with no first event. There must also be something that can continue to renew itself without interruption, so that time itself does not stop. This must be a body with one nature that keeps changing without a break and another aspect that remains in God's knowledge. Among bodily substances, the simple body created directly by God can renew itself without interruption. Among bodily qualities, the circular motion can continue without end; other motions and changes stop at a boundary that separates them from what follows.

You will learn that what causes this motion is not a body. It longs without end for the divine realm. From God it begins, and to God it returns, riding a celestial ship: “In the name of God be its course and its anchorage.”

Then comes the second section of the First Journey, followed by the third, which begins with Stage Seven. This completes the First Journey of *The Transcendent Wisdom in the Four Intellectual Journeys*, by the philosopher of the East, the wonder of his age, the divine sage Mulla Sadra (Ṣadr al-Dīn Muḥammad al-Shīrāzī)—may God bless his pure soul. We praise God for this success and ask Him to help us publish the remaining parts of the book soon. He is the Guardian, the Almighty. We also owe deep thanks to all the learned people and printing-house staff who helped us print and correct the book. We pray that God grant them success and happiness in both worlds. Amen, Lord of all worlds.

Publisher (Qom Press): Despite our great effort to produce this book without serious errors, circumstances—from the printer to the proofreader—combined to leave this page of corrections at the end. Perhaps a few minor errors escaped our notice. Readers are asked to correct the book using this table before they begin reading.

## Notes

[^V02-1]: Scholars use this account of essence, but it is not a strict definition; it explains the word. The concept of essence is not one of the external essences placed under the ten broad categories used to classify things. It is a general feature that can be said of all the categories and their kinds. This definition also differs from the later one, “that by which a thing is what it is.” Here, saying that essence answers “What is it?” shows that the thing being defined is a concept that can exist in the mind. Of things outside the mind, this applies to their essence, not to their existence or their being outside the mind. The second wording can apply to both a thing’s essence and its existence, so it is broader. What, then, does the first definition mean? From the arguments in the earlier discussion of existence, we learned that external realities—or most of them—have two connected aspects: existence, which cannot enter the mind, and essence, which is united with external existence in the world and with mental existence in the mind. There is no third aspect. So concepts that describe external things as existing, but do not directly fit those things, are not essences. They are ideas made by the mind and exist only there. Outside the mind there are only existence and the essences that exist through it. Other ideas formed by the mind are not present there as ideas. When we say, literally, “it is,” we mean what is present outside the mind: its real existence and the essence that expresses what it is. Other concepts, even features that follow from essences—such as the evenness of four and the oddness of three—are made by the mind and are not present outside as concepts, though they may have examples there. Essence, as what a thing is, therefore matches the thing’s real identity. If we ask what an intelligible thing is, we seek a concept identical with its external identity, one that cannot be separated from it; only essence answers. Thus essence is what answers “What is it?” — ط مد

[^V02-2]: A thing becomes particular when features foreign to its essence are added to it, so particularity belongs to it only by accident. The nature itself is universal. Particularity in the sense of being an individual, however, belongs to actual existence. Essence, both in itself and as the answer to “What is it?”, is universal, but universality is a feature outside it that necessarily belongs to it in the mind. In itself it is neither universal nor particular. So there is no conflict between saying that essence is a universal concept and saying that it is not, in itself, either universal or particular. — ط مد

[^V02-3]: Both wordings seem circular because the Arabic word for essence is related to the question “What is it?” This is like defining quantity as what answers “How much?” or quality as what answers “How is it?” The commentator explains that these are verbal definitions. — س ره

[^V02-4]: The denials in the claim that “humanity, considered in itself, is neither existent nor nonexistent, neither one nor many” use “is” to say what a concept means: the concept of human is the concept of human, not the concept of existent or nonexistent, and so on. This is self-evident. One might object that the same form of denial also applies to concepts that are not essences. The answer is that these judgments properly belong to essence; they apply to other concepts only by extension, because those concepts are drawn from essences in the mind. This also answers a related objection: universality and particularity apply to non-essential concepts too, but that does not make them essential features of those concepts. — ط مد

[^V02-5]: It is one thing for something to occur alongside another, and another for it to be that thing itself. Existence and nonexistence, unity and multiplicity, generality and particularity, and other such features are not the essence of one another. Nor are any of them identical with or part of any essence, even though an essence is never found without some of them or their opposites. — س ره

[^V02-6]: The earlier point denied that a thing could lack both opposites; here the author allows that it can lack them at one level. The note explains that the difference lies in the level being discussed: a thing can lack both at that level, though not in reality as a whole. This point is quoted from a note on *Shawāhid al-Rubūbiyya*. — ه ره

[^V02-7]: Someone may object that a level of reality is reality itself, so a denial at that level is a denial in reality. The reply is that a general nature is removed only when all its individual cases are removed. The denial described here is true only when it covers every level of reality, as the author explains later when discussing whether pure possibility exists in itself. — س ره

[^V02-8]: This qualification leaves out two cases: the relation between an essence and itself, and the relation between a cause’s existence and its effect’s existence. A cause’s existence includes its effect at a higher level, while the effect’s existence reflects and contains the cause at a lower level. — س ره

[^V02-9]: The opposite of a thing is its removal, not its removal with the same qualification added. A claim about something under a condition and the denial of that same claim under the same condition are not necessarily opposites. If a feature is false at a certain level, then “the feature is not true at that level” is true. But “the denial itself is true at that level” may also be false, because it is not the opposite of the original claim. If both claims can be false, they are not contradictories; true contradictories cannot both be false. And being attributed to something is broader than being identical with it. — س ره

[^V02-10]: Reality is wider than any one level, while the denial here applies only to a level. A general nature is removed only when all its cases are removed. Since essence is never without features such as existence, unity, and individuality—or their opposites—the philosophers put the denial before the qualification. — س ره

[^V02-11]: The denial applies to existence under the stated condition, not to existence in general. This makes the statement work for features that need no actual existence to belong to an essence; it is enough that the essence be a thing. The author shortens the explanation here because he gave it earlier in the book, although the commentator says it would have helped to expand it here. — س ره

[^V02-12]: An essence has two ways of standing in relation to its features. First, it is not described by them when considered simply in itself. This applies to features that belong to it only when it exists. Second, it can be described by features while still considered in itself, but these features belong to it from a different aspect, not because they are identical with its essence. These include features that accompany essence whether or not it exists, such as its necessary properties, possibility, and being a thing. The author’s statement that a thing can lack both opposites at one level concerns features tied to existence. His statement about answering questions with contradictories concerns features that belong to essence itself. In the last distinction, the level must qualify existence; if it is only the setting in which existence and nonexistence occur, the argument does not work. The commentator says the first explanation is the better one.

[^V02-13]: This is Ṣāḥib al-Mawāqif, who says that placing the qualification before the denial means the denial itself is required. The commentator says this is plainly mistaken.

[^V02-14]: That form of affirmative statement depends on the subject already existing, even though the subject here is essence considered at a level without existence. — س ره

[^V02-15]: The question is about essence considered in itself. We are not required to choose either side, even if essence is never without one of them in reality, because the alternatives only function like contradictories. If we answered with one attribute while essence is considered in itself, it could suggest that the attribute is identical with essence or part of it. This differs from a question that offers a true contradictory pair: there, we answer with one side, the negative one. The confusion does not arise in that case, because one question asks whether essence is described under the condition of being considered in itself, while the other denies that description. One might object that both questions concern essence under this same condition, so the same confusion should arise. The reply is that we do not say, “Essence in itself is not this”; we say, “Essence is not, when considered in itself, this.” That is another reason for putting the denial before the qualification: the level applies to the thing whose feature is denied, not to the denial itself. We must distinguish denying that something is present from saying that absence itself is present, and denying a thing under a condition from denying the condition itself. There is also a difference between ordinary predication and predication that says what a thing is. — س ره

[^V02-16]: When two affirmative descriptions are treated as contradictories, we cannot leave both aside: if a thing is not described by one, it must be described by the other. But this does not mean that the thing is identical with the description that applies to it. The fact that a thing cannot avoid one of two features in reality does not make either feature its essence or part of it. The commentator quotes Avicenna’s *The Healing*, where he distinguishes a question offering one affirmative and one negative answer from a question offering two affirmative descriptions that seem to form an either-or. In the first case, the answer must use the denial before the qualification, as in the main text. In the second, we do not have to choose: the essence may be free of both descriptions when considered in itself. Choosing one would wrongly imply, for example, that humanity means the same as existence or unity. — س ره

[^V02-17]: Many individual things sharing a universal nature does not mean that the nature is numerically one in itself. — ن ره

[^V02-18]: This is another question about essence. Does the universal nature found in the mind and the individual nature found outside the mind exist outside the mind as one and the same thing? Yes, in the sense that essence is joined with external existence, as explained earlier; it is not identical with existence. Does essence have a separate existence apart from the individuals in which it occurs? No. Is the essence in all its individuals one numerically, or does each individual have its own? The author chooses the second view: outside the mind, each essence belongs to a distinct individual and cannot be universally shared. Each individual has its own essence through its own existence. Essence is called universal only in the mind. — ط مد

[^V02-19]: Since universality is explained as one essence being shared by many, someone might think that one and the same universal exists numerically in all its individuals. That is impossible: it would mean the universal exists separately from its individual cases. Also, a mental universal exists in one mind, and existence makes a thing individual. Since the mind itself is one of the things that make the image particular, how could that same image be shared? The author explains sharing as matching, then limits what counts as the right kind of match. — س ره

[^V02-20]: Someone may object that an imagined image is also an ideal mental thing with a shadow-like existence, and so should be universal in relation to its matching individual cases. The reply is that “ideal” and “shadow-like” mean that something exists through another thing, as a universal exists through one of its individuals. An imagined image is itself one individual of a kind; the other thing it is linked to, like a shadow to what casts it, can be in the mind or outside it. — س ره

[^V02-21]: Although the wording is difficult, the point is that mental existence lacks the effects of existence in the world, such as individuality, position, and time. A mental concept can match many individuals: when we compare it with each one, each matches it. Its existence in one mind, or its lack of size or position, is not the external existence of the thing it represents. The author discussed this earlier in the section on mental existence. — ط مد

[^V02-22]: After discussing some questions about universals, the author turns to particulars. He uses “individuality” here to mean that a thing cannot be shared in thought. This is different from distinction, which can also apply to universals, and from signs that reveal a thing’s particular way of existing. — س ره

[^V02-23]: These philosophers say that universality and particularity come from the way we perceive things, through sensation or thought, rather than from differences in the things themselves. Their view does not reduce particularity to existence, since sensation does not grasp existence itself. It can, however, apply to direct awareness, where the thing is present to the knower. Another answer is that the senses provide evidence of a thing’s real individuality, while existence itself makes it an individual. Or they may mean only that sensation is a sign of individuality. The author’s cautious wording allows for these readings. — س ره

[^V02-24]: External existence has effects in the world, so it cannot enter the mind in that form. What we learn about an object through the mind is an image without those effects. Direct awareness is different: the thing is present to the knower through its own existence, so there is no problem in saying that we know existence by direct awareness. The author means this kind of direct witnessing. It is less clear how sensation itself could make something particular. A mental image, whether sensory or imagined, does not by itself prevent the mind from applying it to more than one thing. Another judgment is needed, such as that two identical things cannot exist, or that existence makes a thing individual. The image itself does not rule out more than one match, though a sensory or imagined image is narrower than a general idea such as humanity. Sensory awareness may work because sensible features take on a form in the sense organ like the form they have outside. The soul, joined to the body, becomes one with that form, then forms a mental image that matches the object. This connection may explain why the same sensory awareness cannot be shared among the outward senses. Inner awareness, such as knowing one’s own love or hatred, more clearly involves the thing being present to the soul. As for imagined images, it is the mind’s judgment that they represent one external individual that prevents sharing—not the image alone. — ط مد

[^V02-25]: This points to the way of the philosophers who see God as True Existence itself. It differs from the earlier view that the maker gives a thing its individuality: someone who sees God as an unknown necessary essence could accept that view, while this account treats God as the self-subsisting reality of existence. The earlier view can also mean that the maker gives an individualizing feature; this view says that existence itself makes the thing individual. — س ره

[^V02-26]: Matter already shaped by forms and other features helps explain why a thing comes into existence as this individual rather than another. Without such a condition, an individual would be selected without a reason. But if matter by itself cannot distinguish one individual from another, how can it alone make a thing particular? — س ره

[^V02-27]: “A position” here does not mean the general idea of position, or one individual position considered apart from the thing. Adding one universal idea to another does not make a particular. It means the actual position that belongs to the individual throughout its existence, like its particular temperament. Even this is not what truly makes the thing individual; it is a sign of its individuality and something that comes with its existence. — س ره

[^V02-28]: Knowing each of the two things requires knowing the other, but it does not depend on identifying the feature that distinguishes the other. It depends on the other thing itself, or on the cause of the relation between them. If someone sees a circle between the two acts of recognition, it is a circle in which both occur together, not a vicious circle. — س ره

[^V02-29]: The philosophers say that a thing cannot make another thing individual unless it is itself an individual. The Shaykh therefore says that position makes itself particular. The author reads this as meaning that position distinguishes one thing from another. Since time can also distinguish things, the claim that position alone makes something individual must be limited to cases where time is the same. If position is the same but time differs, time makes the difference instead. — س ره

[^V02-30]: “Position” here is the category that describes how parts of a body are arranged in relation to one another and how the whole body is placed in relation to what is outside it. Each thing in the world has its own place in the order of bodies and its own relation to what surrounds it. — س ره

[^V02-31]: Just as a thing’s real individuality is the same as its existence in reality, and the two ideas mean the same, so its being singled out is the same as its being distinct, both in reality and in meaning. — س ره

[^V02-32]: Distinction depends on existence; it requires something to exist. Things that do not exist are not distinct in themselves. — س ره

[^V02-33]: When features set things apart and make them seem numerous, those features are what truly differ and multiply, not the common subject in which they occur. The subject is called distinct or multiple only in a secondary sense. For example, if whiteness seems to vary with different subjects, times, or directions, those subjects, times, and directions are what truly differ; whiteness itself differs only because of them. Pure whiteness, taken by itself, has no distinction or multiplicity. — س ره

[^V02-34]: The statements that a species is its general kind and distinguishing feature are made at the level of what the thing is, with outside features—including actual existence—set aside. They mean, for example, “a human is an animal” and “a human is rational.” They are not claims about an actual person. This does not mean the general kind and the distinguishing feature are the same as each other. Each belongs to the species in a different way. If the feature were already part of the general kind’s definition, the distinction between them would collapse. If the general kind were part of the feature’s definition, explaining it would go on without end. Each is outside the definition of the other. The general kind is the broad side of the species; the feature makes it specific; the species is the complete kind. They are called parts because both appear in its definition. By itself, the general kind does not determine a feature; the distinguishing feature makes the subject definite enough for features to belong to it. It can also have some properties when it is itself an intermediate kind. — ط مد

[^V02-35]: Strictly speaking, it is matter in relation to the other part, which is form. In relation to the whole made from matter and form, it is the material cause. — ط مد

[^V02-36]: The mind cannot point to a general kind on its own. It can only treat it as an open question among several complete kinds. If the mind takes it as a separate idea, it becomes mental matter and the distinguishing feature becomes mental form; then they are no longer a general kind and its feature. Avicenna says that body taken as a general kind is still undetermined: we do not yet know what form it has or how many forms it includes. The mind seeks to complete it because no actual, fully determined body has yet been described. A general kind is shared by different complete kinds because it is joined to their distinguishing features. A specific kind, by contrast, is a complete nature shared by individuals of the same kind. “Incomplete” here means incomplete in its existence, not unclear in its meaning. — س ره

[^V02-37]: This means that it is complete in itself, not completed by adding a distinguishing feature, as in the previous case. — س ره

[^V02-38]: Each real kind has an immaterial, luminous individual that cares for its material individuals, except prime matter, time, and motion, which belong only to the bodily world. — ن ره

[^V02-39]: Its unity is instead the unity of one individual with different levels. Prime matter has different degrees, like one general nature divided into shares. Water in separate jars, taken from a spring, is more closely related to the spring than it is to the sea; so we say that the jars contain spring water, not sea water, even though matter is one in number. The commentators explain this in their discussion of matter. Darkness, like light, has different forms, but darkness comes from weakness and lack, while light comes from strength and fullness. All things bow before the Living, the Self-Sustaining. — س ره

[^V02-40]: This is like “existence in general,” which can be considered as unrestricted or as limited to a particular thing. It is also like water in general, which can mean pure water or water mixed with something, and like the different uses of a direct object in grammar. — س ره

[^V02-41]: One manuscript reads: “and divides it into itself, considered in this way, and itself, considered in the other two ways.”

[^V02-42]: Someone might say that this makes a category its own opposite. The answer is that the terms are broader or narrower depending on how we consider them. A nature taken with a condition in one respect can be taken without a condition in another. The text treats it as a specific kind in one place and as the same nature itself in another. — س ره

[^V02-43]: It is part of the essence when considered as different from the other part, and can be said of the whole because it is united with it. If we consider only that unity, it is right to call the whole a specific kind. — ن ره

[^V02-44]: The commentator says it would be better to add that matter, as matter, also comes first in thought: to imagine a house, we first imagine its roof and walls; to imagine a human being, we first imagine the soul and body. Avicenna allows definitions that name such external parts. — س ره

[^V02-45]: Put another way, the general kind is vague when compared with the complete forms found in specific kinds. In itself, however, it is already a kind of nature. Seen this way, it again serves as matter and as a part. — ط مد

[^V02-46]: The confusion comes from mixing up body as a material part with body as a general kind. — س ره

[^V02-47]: This answer follows the view held by Aristotle’s followers. Followers of Suhrawardī, who believe in forms of light, hold that each specific kind can exist apart from material features in a higher world of forms. General kinds, however, have no separate forms there. — س ره

[^V02-48]: The general kind and its distinguishing feature are each taken without condition in relation to the other part of the definition. Both can be said of the complete definition and of themselves, although each is vague or specific in relation to the other. Being taken without condition accounts for their unity and for their being said of the whole; their vagueness or specificity comes from what each is. — ط مد

[^V02-49]: “Distinction” here means distinction in the world. Accidental features can also set things apart there. A distinguishing feature is needed to complete the nature of a general kind, not simply to separate one existing thing from another. In logic, when people say that a distinguishing feature sets a thing apart and answers “What is it in itself?”, they mean that it completes the nature of the general kind. — ط مد

[^V02-50]: These are two ordered levels of existence, not two things on the same level. A bodily form does not exist without a specific form; the two forms are ordered, like matter and form in a compound. Color and the power to distinguish sight do not have two separate existences, even in an ordered way. This is why an external quality is simple. — س ره

[^V02-51]: This claim applies even to external compounds, when the general kind is considered by itself. That is why the author says “if it is correct.” — س ره

[^V02-52]: This is a false argument caused by mixing up the two meanings of “body” explained above. — س ره

[^V02-53]: The commentator says the clearer wording is “its joining with them,” as the context shows. — س ره

[^V02-54]: Do not read “having” as adding a separate relation just to make the sentence work. If “sensitive” and “moving” are not two distinguishing features, it does not follow that one thing has two features at the same level. They are two consequences of one feature: the animal soul. The true feature, which makes up a thing’s real nature and makes the general kind actual in the world, is not the concept of “sensitive,” nor the sensed quality itself, nor a feeling or relation. It is the soul that necessarily has these qualities. — س ره

[^V02-55]: An essential feature belongs to a thing when we set everything else aside. A feeling or a relation, by contrast, arises only when we consider something else. A feeling is a quality that happens to a thing; a relation depends even more on other things; and a sensed image is also a quality. How, then, could a substance be made up of one of these? Even if perception is an active process, as the author believes, an action is still a quality that happens to a thing. — س ره

[^V02-56]: The distinguishing feature contains the general kind in potential form; it does not merely imply it. — ن ره

[^V02-57]: These thinkers did not notice that if the source from which the idea of color is drawn may or may not be specific to one kind, then the scope of color itself would also be uncertain. Yet the mind does not allow color, for example, to be exclusive to black. One could reply that color has levels, each tied to a particular quality whose scope is uncertain. A single idea can be drawn from features whose scope is uncertain. On this account, color would be like heat, which some say is not a shared feature: the heat of living things, the heat of the elements, and other kinds of heat differ in kind. It would also be like light: sunlight lets a person with night blindness see, while other light does not. Even so, this answer would need to show that colors differ only through their distinguishing features, and that a careful thinker can tell when a feature is specific to one kind and when it is shared by several. — س ره

[^V02-58]: The composition is mental, but it is grounded in real features of thought, not imaginary, like the fabled tusks of ghouls. Otherwise, mental analysis of simple qualities would be a serious mistake. In truth, this kind of composition reflects reality because natures take shape in the mind and exist there in their own way. So when the author says it does not match reality, he means that it does not exist outside the mind. Also, the composition of two complete things in this way does not match reality; the composition of an incomplete, vague meaning with a meaning that makes it definite does. This resolves the tension with the earlier claim that the mind’s account can be true at one level of reality. — س ره

[^V02-59]: One might object that this does not solve the problem: if a thing is simple, how can the mind divide it into parts and draw several meanings from it without merely inventing them? The answer will become clearer in the later discussion of cause and effect, where the author turns to the insights of the mystics. These meanings have distinct forms in the mind, drawn from one thing as the mind becomes aware of fewer or more similarities and differences. In general, there are several views on how the mind forms parts from a simple thing. Al-Sharīf reports them after noting that everyone agrees the parts are distinct in the mind. The first view says they are distinct both in nature and in existence; this view is weak. The second says they are distinct in nature but not in existence, because it treats nature as prior to existence; on this view, the objection disappears. The third says they are one in nature and in existence; this makes the objection harder to answer. The answer is the one already given: the mind takes these meanings in different ways. Mulla Sadra rejects all three views and chooses a fourth, also suggested by al-Sharīf: outside the mind there is only a way of existing. The mind draws essential and nonessential meanings from it according to how many similarities and differences it notices. The author will explain this later. This is also what he means by saying that a distinguishing feature is a way of existing. He singles out the feature because the general kind is absorbed into it, and the kind gets its identity from that feature. — س ره

[^V02-60]: Someone might object: if the mind does not divide the general kind and its distinguishing feature, they are simply one, so neither can cause the other. But if the mind does divide them, they become matter and form, and then form—not the distinguishing feature—would be the cause. The author points to the answer by calling them two mental parts. There is a third option: they are neither wholly different, as matter and form are, nor wholly one, as they are in the outside world. They have something of both, as the author explained earlier when discussing the different ways a nature can be considered. This resembles the familiar problem of how we can learn something unknown. The answer is that what we seek is not wholly unknown: it is unknown in one respect and known in another. Someone may object that we cannot seek what is unknown or learn what we already know. But we are not seeking two things; we are seeking one thing with two aspects. The mistake comes from treating two ways of considering one thing as if they were two separate things. We can also take the opposite route and speak of causes in the outside world, which is the philosopher’s proper subject: there, the natural distinguishing feature is form, and the natural general kind is matter, so the question does not arise. Another view says the general kind and its feature are one in the world, in nature and in existence; on that view, too, the question disappears. The answer in the main text, however, rests on considering them in ways that are true within reality, as the author explained earlier and will explain again. Mulla Sadra accepts none of these views. He chooses a fourth, also suggested by al-Sharīf: outside the mind there is only a way of existing; the mind draws essential and nonessential meanings from it according to what it sees as shared or distinguishing. This is what he means by saying the distinguishing feature is a way of existing. He singles out the feature because the general kind is absorbed into it, and the specific kind gets its identity from it. — س ره

[^V02-61]: In real compounds, if we treat the general kind as matter and the distinguishing feature as form, the explanation is fuller: form works with matter as a cause. This approach can also be applied to simple things in the world by extending “matter” and “form” to include mental matter and mental form. — س ره

[^V02-62]: More clearly, one should say that the feature causes the particular animal nature by its own specific character, without some further step that first makes it specific. The author’s reply addresses the worry that animal nature must already be made specific before rationality can cause it. He says animal nature did not bring about this particular kind; the distinguishing feature did. Its causal power and specific character come from the feature, which comes before its effect, even though the relation between cause and effect exists only once both are there. — س ره

[^V02-63]: If all the manuscripts read this way, it is a slip of the pen. The correct word is “specified.” — س ره

[^V02-64]: The phrase beginning “from the feature” is not the grammatical subject of the passive verb. The unstated subject is the pronoun that refers back to the relative clause. — س ره

[^V02-65]: The author of *The Eastern Discussions* forgot that the distinguishing feature causes the general kind, and that the kind’s specific character comes from the feature. He treated the body as already specific and then asked what cause made it take this particular form. But the general kind is contained in the distinguishing feature and has no definite form apart from it. In the same way, matter has no definite form apart from form. — س ره

[^V02-66]: “Form” can refer to a thing’s kind, its general kind, or its distinguishing feature, especially when the mind turns it into a mental image; in that case, two technical uses overlap. Earlier, the author explained that a general nature may have to be specified by one of its individual cases, or may be joined to one only by chance. Such discussion concerns things that have forms in the world. This is what Avicenna means when he says that form can refer to a thing’s kind, general kind, distinguishing feature, or all of them together. The form that gives matter its reality is bodily form, and the matter is first matter. The form that shapes a thing into a natural kind is its specific form; the thing receiving it is matter already made into a body. The author has not listed every use of “form,” though Avicenna does so near the end of his discussion of natural bodies, gathering them under one meaning: what makes a thing actually be what it is. The same applies to the parts of things, and to terms such as priority, potential, and possibility. Or perhaps the author means that these uses share a meaning, rather than being unrelated uses of the same word. Avicenna says in *The Healing* that form can mean any actual meaning fit to be understood, including separate intelligences; any state or activity in one thing or in a compound, including motion and qualities; what makes matter actual; what completes matter without making it what it is, such as health or a natural tendency toward it; a special shape made in matter by craft; or a thing’s kind, general kind, distinguishing feature, or all of these. He adds that the whole can also serve as the form of its parts. — س ره

[^V02-67]: The wording may seem contradictory because “its matter” suggests that matter is part of the thing’s essence. The phrase “what bears its form” resolves this: the bearer lies outside the form it bears. — س ره

[^V02-68]: First matter must be only a receiver and pure potentiality. If it were also an actual thing that receives a form, its own actuality would either depend on something it receives, creating a circle, or on something that comes before it, leading to an endless chain. — س ره

[^V02-69]: As explained above, matter’s nature is indeterminate between one thing and another. Potentiality and indeterminacy are not actual things in themselves. The phrase “serves as” appears twice because the author is speaking first of first matter and bodily form; other matter and form have similar relations, including body and separate soul, and existence and essence. — س ره

[^V02-70]: But that is not right. The ultimate distinguishing feature completes a thing and contains all its essential perfections, but that does not mean it spells those perfections out. A definition is meant to set out the essential features in detail, not merely to contain them. — ط ره

[^V02-71]: This does not mean that the arrival of the ultimate distinguishing feature makes earlier general kinds and distinguishing features stop being what they are. That would be impossible. The point is that the earlier kinds and forms can remain if the ultimate form is removed. This discussion also draws on the idea that things can change their essential nature through substantial change, moving from one kind to another by degrees. The subject will return later. — ط مد

[^V02-72]: Having explained that form, rather than matter, makes a thing what it is, the author now shows that this depends on the final form, not on the earlier ones. The earlier forms and distinguishing features are conditions and preparations, not parts of the final nature, though they may be parts of other separate natures or of different stages within one existence, such as the human being considered as body and soul. The commentator sees four claims here: a thing is what it is through its ultimate form; each thing has one main form, with earlier forms serving as its branches; a distinguishing feature is a way of existing; and a shared nature exists in reality in a middle way. — س ره

[^V02-73]: The earlier forms exist in sequence: first before the final form, as preparations; then alongside it, as dependent branches; and finally within it, gathered together without remaining separate parts. The final form contains and possesses them at a higher level, so they do not have to remain scattered and separate. — س ره

[^V02-74]: “True nature” here means the reality from which a thing’s characteristic effects follow. Its essence is what the thing is in itself. — ن ره

[^V02-75]: The author does deny a “natural universal” when considering it precisely as a shared nature, but not when considering it in another way. — ن ره

[^V02-76]: If “composite” is used broadly, then “matter” and “form” must also be used broadly enough to include their mental counterparts. In that case, the difference between what is taken and what it is taken from is also a difference of viewpoint in external composites. For example, the natural general kind is the same as the matter itself. But external composites have matter and form, so the mental general kind is what is taken, while the external matter is what it is taken from. The distinction is real in this sense. — س ره

[^V02-77]: One might say: Yes, but form has something actual beyond its being a substance, whereas matter has nothing beyond it. This would make form like natural powers, which may be called “plant powers” even when they exist in an animal, since there is no plant in an animal apart from those powers.

[^V02-78]: One might object: If this is so, then what makes a thing what it is must be its distinguishing feature. The general kind is absorbed into that feature, as will be explained later. But matter’s substantial nature is absorbed into its potentiality, so substance cannot be assigned to matter in a composite body. The answer is that the distinguishing feature here is not an actual thing that can absorb something else. In this case the relation is the reverse. This is like an exception to the rule, not because the rule is being narrowed, but because the case does not fall under it: when a distinguishing feature or form is fully actual, the general kind is absorbed into it, and the thing is what it is through that form. Matter, by contrast, has no form or distinguishing feature of its own. It is what it is through the general kind, just as a number is what it is through its matter, since each level of number has no form other than the matter made up of its units. — س ره

[^V02-79]: In other words, nothing in the outside world corresponds to the general concept as such; that concept cannot itself be included in “rational.” A particular thing does exist, but it is a specific substance or a specific accident, such as a quality. The word “rational” does not tell us which it is. We know from other evidence that it belongs to the category of substance, but only in a secondary sense; substance as such, the general kind, or matter, lies outside the distinguishing feature or form. — س ره

[^V02-80]: There is a deep point here. The general kind is a feature that necessarily accompanies the true distinguishing feature, while the true distinguishing feature is the complete reality of a thing: a thing is what it is through that feature and no other. Everything else lies outside its essential reality. It is said that the final definition in a chain of definitions is the same as the middle term in a proof. The true distinguishing feature is the reality of the true cause. God, exalted be He, is the highest completion of all things. Understand this. — ن ره

[^V02-81]: Once it is established that all distinguishing features—from the first feature in the most basic composition to the last feature in any composite, and all forms from the first to the last—are existence and have no substantial or accidental essence of their own, it follows that the whole world is existence. This is a lofty idea, understood by a mature philosopher. The claim may seem to conflict with familiar principles: that a distinguishing feature is part of an essence, helps compose it, is one of the five predicables, and can be used with a general kind to define a nature. But the conflict is only apparent. The author’s view rests on the fourth account of mental composition discussed earlier. If a distinguishing feature is understood as a mode of existence, as the author holds, these principles do not contradict it. If instead it is understood as a simple essence, as others hold, then the instruction to keep it from outsiders may reflect their failure to see how a possible essence used as a distinguishing feature can be neither a substance nor a quality, even though the argument leads to this conclusion. A distinguishing feature of a substance is not itself a substance in the essential sense in which a general kind is; otherwise it would itself be composite. The same applies to distinguishing features of qualities: they do not belong essentially to the general category of quality, and likewise for the other categories. Saying that every possible essence is either a substance or a quality requires only that it be classified in some way, not that the classification be essential. — س ره

[^V02-82]: The author’s argument is not conclusive. Although general kind and distinguishing feature correspond to external matter and form in a substance, and although the mind considers the general kind and distinguishing feature as distinct, that does not mean the mental relation must hold in the outside world. Relations can differ depending on whether we consider something in the mind or outside it. For example, whether a statement can be affirmed varies with the standpoint. A final form can exist without the earlier matter and forms, and a kind can exist without its matter once its form is separate. This is possible in the outside world, but not if we look only at mental parts. There are other cases where the two standpoints yield different judgments. The argument, then, does not establish its conclusion, even though the conclusion may be true for another reason. — ط مد

[^V02-83]: The author has not said here that these forms are existence, but he has already said that distinguishing features in general are modes of existence. Later he will use this principle to argue that the whole world is existence. One might object that not being a substance or quality does not prove that something is existence: unity, a point, and motion, for example, may be outside the categories without being existence. The answer is that a thing must be either existence or an essence. If it is not an essence, it is existence. Existence is either unbounded and infinitely intense—God—or a particular mode of existence, which is possible existence. Nothing can be neither existence nor essence. Unity is existence; a point is a privation; and, as the author argues through substantial motion, motion is a mode of existence in the natural world. This is the author’s position: distinguishing features are existence, not essences, because essences are mental abstractions and cannot make a general kind actual. Others may instead take these features to be simple essences, neither substances nor qualities in themselves, though they can be called substances or qualities in a secondary sense. The author appears to follow this latter view in the next argument: he says the soul is a substance when considered in itself, but not when considered as the body’s form. — س ره

[^V02-84]: The highest general kinds themselves are simple essences that do not fall under a kind. But the end of the author’s argument, especially his claim that some things are understood directly rather than by definition, suggests that by “simple essences” he may mean things like existence, unity, multiplicity, priority, and posteriority. That reading is mistaken: these are mental concepts drawn from essences, not essences in their own right. If they were essences, they would themselves have existence, and existence would then have another existence. That is clearly wrong. The better reading is the one given above, though it takes some effort to reconcile it with the author’s later examples of existence and immediate awareness. Those examples may be only comparisons. — ط مد

[^V02-85]: The true distinguishing feature, which is real existence, is neither substance nor quality. As explained earlier, all things perish and fade in the one true reality of existence. Existence is the one, ultimate truth: He is Himself; there is no other than He. God bears witness that there is no god but Him. All distinct forms are levels of the reality of existence, and their appearance as separate things is only figurative and dependent. He is the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden; all things are mirrors of His manifestation, and as mirrors they vanish in what is reflected in them. Nothing stands between Him and you but your own self. Let go of what occupies you, and what truly matters will be revealed. — ن ره

[^V02-86]: How could the existence of a separate substance depend on a particular readiness in matter at a particular time, when it is equally related to every time and separate from matter? Also, what matter is ready to receive must be a state belonging to it and connected to it, so that matter can be completed by receiving it. But a separate reality is distinct in its essence from matter and bodies. How, then, could it be a state of matter? This objection applies to anyone who holds that the soul begins to exist but is immaterial, including Mulla Sadra, who says that it becomes spiritual in its later life. It does not apply to Platonists who hold that the soul has always existed. The details will come in the discussion of the soul, God willing. — س ره

[^V02-87]: That is, not in itself. The body may call for it indirectly, and this allows the soul’s immaterial essence to begin to exist in time. Otherwise, if its essence came about through itself, then the existence of the First Principle and its own possibility would be enough to explain it, and the soul would have existed from eternity. — س ره

[^V02-88]: The point is not that the source of the soul’s existence here and in time is the bodily existence itself. First, the soul’s bodily existence is its active, material existence—not its sacred essence. As the Persian verse says: “You are too great to appear in this little mirror.” Second, if matter’s readiness were not required here, and divine generosity alone were enough, then a rational soul would have been given even to an ant. The point is that the body calls for something that acts through matter, and that this active aspect is connected to its source and inner reality: the Active Intellect, whose existence comes directly from divine generosity and from the possibility in its own essence. Intellects exist through their own essential possibility, without a need for matter’s readiness or any other condition. The author’s possible interpretation of what is reported about Plato’s view helps clarify this. In the discussion of the soul, he says that Plato means the eternity of the universal intellect, which is the inner reality of the soul. He says it is untouched by the defects of material things. His phrase “only indirectly” indicates that the soul’s reality is a delicate connection to that source, a flash from it. The beginning of this level of existence is therefore like the beginning of the soul itself, and the body’s readiness is like the readiness of its source. This is the subtle point the author has in mind. — س ره

[^V02-89]: The previous answer relies on the view that the soul is a substance in its own existence. That view belongs to thinkers who hold that the soul—and even the intellects—has an essence of its own, a substantial nature different in kind from others. The author now points to another approach, closer to his own and to that of Suhrawardi, the Illuminationist master: intellects and souls are simple lights, without darkness, and pure existence, without an essence. They are not substances because they are beyond the category of substance, not beneath it. — س ره

[^V02-90]: The author will explain later that possible existence, which is subject to causes and effects, cannot be without an intellectual essence. As the saying goes, every possible thing is made up of two aspects. How could the mind judge something to be possible if it had no essence? Possibility understood as an equal openness to existence and nonexistence belongs to essence, though possibility understood as dependence belongs to possible existence. — ط مد

[^V02-91]: “Incidental light” here does not mean what Illuminationist philosophers usually call accidental lights, such as the visible rays of stars or lamps. Although these too are, in their view, joined to the true lights—the dominant lights and the governing lights of the heavens and earth—it would not make sense to say that such visible light is “light upon light.” Here, “incidental light” means the features of existence that are attributes in concept, but not separate existences. Alternatively, “the world” may mean the natural world, since Mulla Sadra has just said that bodily and natural forms are lights. The “incidental lights” would then be the governing and dominant lights, in keeping with his view that the soul is united with the Active Intellect, a view he shares with some earlier thinkers. — س ره

[^V02-92]: Just as the stars do not shine by their own power when the sun’s light fills the sky by day. — س ره

[^V02-93]: A receiver must be empty of what it receives. If what is received contains every actuality, the receiver must lack them all and have, in their place, absences and potentialities. This is why the human being is created weak, and why matter is the weakest and lowest of things. — س ره

[^V02-94]: “The realm of divine dominion” is called “the divine realm” because it is close to God’s rule. There, the facts of existence prevail, while the mere possibilities of things fade into the background. — س ره

[^V02-95]: Logical division requires two distinct beings in every self-standing substance, as will become clear later. These philosophers deny that accidents, general kinds, or parts of a thing—such as legs or wings—have separate rulers of their own. — س ره

[^V02-96]: Their claim is like saying that a human being has a fixed side and a flowing side, or that place and quality each have fixed and changing sides. “Humanity” here means the shared nature that can be said of a human being, of what is fixed, and of what is flowing. “The First Adam” is the fixed human being spoken of in the words of our master and leader, Ali. — س ره

[^V02-97]: There are two reasons for the name. First, the rulers of kinds are images of what is above them, as God’s names and the forms of His names are. Second, they are models for the natural individuals below them. — س ره

[^V02-98]: The mind reaches these divine Forms when it understands general ideas. As explained earlier, understanding a general idea is like the soul seeing a luminous Form from a distance. — س ره

[^V02-99]: They believed that general knowledge and certain proofs aim at these Forms and reach them. General premises are about general things; individual cases are not learned through proof in the same way. These Forms and their states are lasting realities, reached through definitions, descriptions, and proofs. But people reach them to different degrees. — س ره

[^V02-100]: This does not mean an “abstract essence” in the technical sense, since no such essence exists, even in the mind. It means the nature considered without adding any condition. — س ره

[^V02-101]: The author is giving this interpretation the benefit of the doubt. At first, Avicenna explained the view as an essence considered apart from every feature, including oneness. Here he describes it as one, but only in the general sense, not as one individual. — س ره

[^V02-102]: On Avicenna’s reading, Plato would have thought that when philosophers say “humanity always exists,” they mean that one numerically identical human nature lasts forever. Avicenna says it means that humanity continues through successive people. A shared nature relates to its individuals as parents to children, not as one parent to all children: it exists through them, and its existence is their existence. — س ره

[^V02-103]: This later thinker’s argument is confused because it mixes up a shared nature and a general idea in the mind. Some of his claims fit the shared nature: considering it in itself and saying it of individuals. Other claims fit a mental idea: its being made into a distinct object of thought and stripped even of its abstractness. He says that if the general idea existed outside the mind, the features that make it an individual would be added to the essence from outside. That could apply to a shared nature, where individual features are analyzed as added; it does not fit a mental general idea. If he meant the mental idea, he should have concluded that it is not general but individual. The stronger reading is that he means a mental idea, made distinct by the mind, and then said of individuals because the general idea is the truth and the individuals are its lower-level forms. On this reading, his argument is different from Avicenna’s and comes close to Mulla Sadra’s point that understanding general ideas is like seeing Forms from a distance. But the argument does not establish that these Forms exist on their own. The author’s criticism is that the speaker seems to reach this conclusion by chance, without understanding it, and that his reasoning does not prove independent existence. — س ره

[^V02-104]: “The Intellects” means the separate Intellects. There is no difficulty in saying they are real beings: their contents are lasting, shared understandings. They are ways the Intellect itself exists. So it is more reasonable to say that they exist as originals, with individual things in nature as their shadows. — س ره

[^V02-105]: This objection is misplaced. These philosophers do not give accidents their own separate rulers. — س ره

[^V02-106]: This is a clear opening onto the subject. — س ره

[^V02-107]: A better reply is that natural kinds depend on higher principles and all are connected to God. The sensory world is where the luminous Forms appear, like the broad base of a cone of light; it is not the point where all that light is gathered into its simplest and highest form. Everything the author says about the lasting existence of things in the timeless order, and their freedom there from the limits of matter, can be accepted. But in that respect, these things are the display and many-sided appearance of the luminous Forms, not the Forms in their highest, most unified state. The Forms also have a higher state, where they are beyond separation and gathered together, and where the many are held within the one. Material things depend on God even in the timeless order. Still, the existence of particular material things is at the lower level of that timeless order, while the luminous Forms exist at its higher levels. As for the author’s objection that material individuals have many separate existences, the teacher he quotes says they are one insofar as they are related to the one source. The wise have compared times and timed events to a point, and places and things in places to a point, when compared with higher principles. — س ره

[^V02-108]: This objection applies to the first two arguments. Followers of Aristotle need not deny that changes in matter depend on a separate intellect. But a separate intellect relates equally to all such changes. To bring about a particular change, something must determine which one occurs. If that determiner is also separate, the same problem returns. So there must be a material determiner for each particular event, working together with the separate intellect. The argument establishes no more than this. — ط مد

[^V02-109]: The earliest philosophers treated what exists in a bearer and an accident as the same. That is why they called the forms of natural kinds dependent features. — س ره

[^V02-110]: This is questionable. The fact that a thing can exist without a power does not make the power an accident. An accident is something that exists in a bearer that can exist without it both as a being and as a kind. Aristotle’s followers call the forms of natural kinds substances. Avicenna defines a substance as something that exists in no other thing in this way: it is not a part of that other thing and cannot be separated from it. — س ره

[^V02-111]: What preserves and maintains the body is the power that continues through time. It cannot remain all at once; it lasts through an unbroken sequence, and its effect lasts in the same way. — س ره

[^V02-112]: This phrase either connects to the point just made or gives the reason that comes before the claim it explains. It introduces the explanation beginning “These different actions.” — س ره

[^V02-113]: I would say the growth power does not make a painful break. A break in continuity causes pain. Instead, it produces one small amount of growth, then another, in a continuous increase. Just as the added amounts join to form one continuous quantity, each stage of growth follows continuously from the one before it. The process does not involve separate movements in different directions. — س ره

[^V02-114]: There is no need to deny the existence of powers within bodies or of natural forces in order to establish separate governing intellects. The powers in bodies can be channels through which separate causes act; those higher causes are too noble to perform each limited physical act directly. The powers within bodies are placed under them, and all are under the Creator of powers and abilities. Also, remarkable actions do not always require knowledge or awareness of their causes. Look at the honeycomb’s hexagons, the spider’s triangular webs, or nature’s conical flames and round droplets, bubbles, and foam. — س ره

[^V02-115]: If the author means that we lack a full, connected understanding of these processes, I agree—but such understanding is not required. If he means that we have no awareness of them at all, even a simple, direct awareness, that is not right. He himself later affirms that the soul has direct awareness of its powers and actions in the discussion of divine knowledge. — س ره

[^V02-116]: Granted, these powers are not made of parts. But why could they not have different levels, like light, which has one simple reality yet varies in completeness? One level is the most intense light; another is the light of the sun and moon. The governing light has one level in which it acts willingly, another in which it acts by choice, and another in which it acts by nature. Yet it remains one simple light, whose unity is real but dependent, like a shadow. As the Persian verse says: “How the shadow draws the forms of saints— / a sign pointing to the light of God’s sun.” — س ره

[^V02-117]: That is, the kinds would have ceased to remain stable, and then something other than a human might have come from a human, and so on. Since the kinds do not cease or change, they must have rulers and Forms that are their principles and ends. Their stability does not mean that they have no beginning or no end. The argument also leaves out another premise: the kinds are many and distinct, while God is one in every respect, so their differences cannot result from depending on God directly, without intermediaries. But this is open to question. If they depend on distinct aspects of the Active Intellect, as Aristotle’s followers say, then they need not be alike. The author’s stronger point is not only that the kinds have causes and ends, but that they share the same kind as their Forms. He argues that if the form of a kind were not based on a luminous model, it would be like an individual form produced by chance from matter and its features. There would be no order or stability; in the world of motion and chance, it might not appear at all. Since the human form, for example, is preserved, it must have an eternal model in the creative world. The bodily form is its shadow, and the two are alike in kind. This differs from saying that bodily kinds depend on aspects of the Active Intellect, such as possibility and necessity, or its knowledge of itself and of near and distant principles. These are clearly not of the same kind as particular creatures. — س ره

[^V02-118]: There is no problem in leaving the exact cause unidentified. The soul does not gain completeness by knowing these individual details. It is enough that each body has a particular mixture and readiness to receive a certain color, along with the way light enters and blends. — س ره

[^V02-119]: The image and its ruler each serve as a model for the other, though the word “model” is more often used for the ruler. Here it refers to the ruler. You might ask how their view leads to an endless chain of models. Suhrawardi explains this in *The Wisdom of Illumination*: a particular form cannot exist without a meaning. If the image that serves as ruler is itself a particular form, it must have another model, and so on. Here, the author uses “mold” to mean something like a body for a spirit. Later, when he says that the rulers of kinds were not made by the First Creator to be models and molds for what lies below them, he means models in the sense of a design or plan. — س ره

[^V02-120]: That is, “universal” in the sense that the outpouring of life reaches all individuals equally, not a concept that can be said of many. In *The Wisdom of Illumination*, the ruler is called universal because its care reaches all these individuals equally. It is the universal and the original, but not a shared concept. — س ره

[^V02-121]: The proof given so far establishes only that a nobler level of existence must be real if a lower one exists. It does not prove that both levels belong to the same kind. Material kinds call for, or point to, earlier immaterial beings that match them in perfection. But that does not show that each immaterial being is a model of the same kind as the material things that match it. The author sees this problem too: after giving three arguments, he says they do not prove that an intellectual Form belongs to the same kind as its bodily image. The fact that one being has the perfection of another does not mean they share its kind. Higher Intellects contain the perfections of material kinds, yet differ from them in nature. God contains all perfections but has no essence of his own.

    Mulla Sadra also says in other places that the same concept can have different forms of existence in different things. Knowledge, for example, is a quality in the human mind; the soul’s direct knowledge of itself is a soul-substance; the Intellect’s knowledge of itself is an intellectual substance; and God’s knowledge of Himself is identical with His existence, which has no essence. The concept is the same in each case, but its reality differs. — ط مد

[^V02-122]: The proof is also given in the metaphysics section of this work. — س ره

[^V02-123]: That is, if a lower being came into existence after it was already possible in itself. This is what the author means by saying “How could it be otherwise?” and pointing to the marvelous order. The reasoning is not circular. — س ره

[^V02-124]: They do not claim that accidents have separate rulers of their own. If they did, an accident would not belong to a subject, and the subject would need a capacity to receive it. This would mean there was potentiality and matter in the creative world, and the accident would become a self-subsisting substance—like a scent existing on its own. That follows only if the Forms are exact counterparts. If they are images only in the looser sense of correspondence, then, as the Illuminationist master says, there can be luminous intellectual forms corresponding to these qualities, such as love and pleasure. — س ره

[^V02-125]: The third proof, based on the possibility of a nobler being, is meant to establish that Forms and their images share one nature. Those who accept this principle usually require the higher and lower beings to have the same essence. Otherwise, if they were different kinds, someone could say that the higher kind did not exist before the lower because it was impossible. This follows from the fact that possibility, necessity, and impossibility belong to an essence: if one individual of a kind is possible, then all are possible; if one is necessary, all are; and if one is impossible, all are. The author reports this condition in the metaphysics section of this work. He has also used sameness of kind in his earlier argument against chance: wheat does not come from barley.

    The author himself noticed the difficulty. After presenting the three proofs, he says they do not establish that an intellectual Form belongs to the same kind as its image. The fact that a perfection found in one essence exists in another being does not make that other being part of the same kind. Higher Intellects contain the perfections of material kinds, but differ from them in essence. God contains every perfection and has no essence. — س ره

[^V02-126]: They name the ruler of a kind after that kind, with “Yazad” as another name for Haoma, not as a word of possession. — س ره

[^V02-127]: Suhrawardi means to deny that these rulers are material, or have features that depend on matter. He does not deny that they resemble their images. — س ره

[^V02-128]: This is a stronger objection than the first. It asks how, even on Suhrawardi’s account of images, a simple luminous essence could represent an animal whose legs and wings are parts of its nature.
