The objection can be answered only by Mulla Sadra’s principles: existence is primary, its degrees differ, and substance itself can change. — س ره

[^V02-129]: That is, “either bodies or dark forms.” The word “or” here divides a whole into its parts; it does not divide a general concept into separate kinds. — س ره

[^V02-130]: These lights belong to a level whose members are equal in rank; unlike the higher, ordered levels of Intellects, there is no chain of causes and effects among them. The main objection is that Suhrawardi’s view of the rulers of kinds conflicts with his view that essence, not existence, is primary. If the worlds and their models correspond, their differences should be differences of degree—greater or lesser light—not differences of kind. That would fit the view that existence is primary: the same reality of existence would be present in the bodily images at different levels, while the differences in kind would come from differences in the levels of existence. But then the worlds and their images would not correspond in the way described, or the view that all lights are degrees of one reality would have to be abandoned. In *The Witnesses*, Suhrawardi says that all Intellects are one kind, and that they cannot be true individual members of many different natures. — س ره

[^V02-131]: This goes further than the point made earlier. Earlier, the author seemed to treat everything except the final distinguishing feature as a true part of the kind. Here he points to what he established before: what makes a kind what it is, in the fullest sense, is its final distinguishing feature and its source; the rest are conditions and tools. The true forms of bodily kinds, as images of their rulers, are therefore their final distinguishing features and simple forms. Even in a natural human being, matter, bodily form, growth, and sensation through separate organs are not what ultimately define the kind. — س ره

[^V02-132]: Mental images play opposite roles in the two comparisons. As features that depend on the mind, they are compared first to forms that exist in matter. As images free of matter in the intellect, they are compared later to the separate Forms in the creative world. — س ره

[^V02-133]: This claim does not fit the stature of Aristotle, the First Teacher, through whom and others like him the lights of true knowledge spread. A report even says that he was a prophet, though people of his time did not recognize him. The rulers who spent time with him, such as Alexander, were among the better kings and leaders. — س ره

[^V02-134]: This is the life of existence that flows through all things, not life in the sense of awareness and action, as when we say that a living thing perceives and acts. — س ره

[^V02-135]: This is praise phrased to sound like criticism, like the saying, “I am the most eloquent of people, yet I am from Quraysh.” — س ره

[^V02-136]: This is the basis of the reply: the First Creator is simple in reality and contains all things in a higher way, with true and complete unity. The higher world flows from Him and contains all things as shadows, with a unity that is real but dependent. — س ره

[^V02-137]: “Words” in the teacher’s phrase “words of the Teacher” means active principles, whether separate from matter or joined to it. This includes the Forms of kinds. The author often uses “words” to mean existences. This use also agrees with sacred books and the sayings of the Imams: Jesus is called a Word; the Qur'an says, “If the sea were ink for the words of my Lord”; and a saying calls the Imams “the complete Words.” A word expresses what is within; existences express what is hidden in the unseen; and active powers express the all-encompassing power. — س ره

[^V02-138]: The soul reaches its full development by traveling through the lands of general realities and swimming in their seas. A general reality includes all its endlessly many particulars, and the soul can grasp that general reality. Particular things are only flashes from it. Knowing them does not complete the soul or give it any lasting gain. The restless soul values them only because it is tied to the body. For example, a particular fruit may appeal to you because of the heat of your liver; without that bodily condition, you would not seek it, and it would have no special appeal. Do not become attached to things that seem beautiful only because of the body and its powers. The words “although each is particular” form an exception that sounds like criticism but is meant as praise. — س ره

[^V02-139]: That is, the plant in the world of imagination, where it has size and shape. — س ره

[^V02-140]: A similar relation appears among the higher Intellects and in the hearts of people of knowledge and insight: their beliefs and direction are one. The perfected ones, from Adam to the Seal of the Prophets, share one reality. As the saying goes, “The souls of God’s lions are united,” and “the believer is a mirror to the believer.” Each sees himself and all things in the other. — س ره

[^V02-141]: In this short summary, the author does not repeat the earlier interpretations of Plato’s Forms because he considers them far-fetched. If he meant an essence without any qualification, that would return to Avicenna’s interpretation and be just as far-fetched. If he meant a general idea in the mind, that would return to the interpretation the author gives here. — س ره

[^V02-142]: This is another proof for the existence of intellectual Forms, beyond the three already given by the Illuminationist philosopher. The general ideas we understand must exist somewhere. They either exist on their own or in something else. If they exist in something else, they must be in a part of our material body, in our soul, or in a separate intellect. The argument rules out these alternatives, leaving Forms that exist on their own. Since they correspond to bodily kinds, each would then be a distinct, nonphysical individual of that kind.

    But there is another possibility: one immaterial being could contain all the qualities and capacities of the material world, while the Forms seem different because of the way we perceive it. By sensing different individuals, the mind becomes ready to connect with this intellectual being and know it directly. Our readiness makes the one being appear to us under different concepts. This is like how we form ideas of knowledge, power, life, hearing, and sight by noticing different qualities in ourselves, then attribute each idea to God. His reality is one, with no separate attributes. The concepts differ because our understanding changes, not because His reality is divided.

    The same could be true of human, horse, sheep, and other Forms: we connect with one intellectual reality that contains all the qualities and capacities found in the material world. This does not mean one undivided being contains several different natures. Instead, we form each general idea by relating that being to the material individual we have perceived. — ط مد

[^V02-143]: The same problems raised earlier about things existing in the mind arise here too. A form would have to count both as a thing in its own right and as a feature of something else, among other conflicts. In short, those problems remain whether the forms are said to exist in the soul or in a separate intellect. — س ره

[^V02-144]: These problems arise whether the forms exist in a separate Intellect, as Aristotle’s followers say, or on their own, as the author holds. Either way, many forms would come from one undivided source, or exist within God’s own being. If someone answers that the separate Intellect has many aspects—such as its acts of illuminating, knowing, loving, and ruling—those aspects could explain the forms. But then the forms would be that Intellect’s knowledge of its own principles. They could not come before its own active powers, so they would not be separate causes producing many forms within it. If someone says the forms exist there in a chain of causes and effects, as Aristotle’s followers do, the Illuminationists reject this because many of the forms stand at the same level. — س ره

[^V02-145]: In the author’s view, forms that exist on their own are general in the same way that forms in the intellect are general in Aristotle’s school. This does not mean there are two sets of forms, as some theologians claim when they distinguish between forms existing “in” the mind and forms existing “through” it. — س ره

[^V02-146]: As the saying goes, “Whoever loses a sense loses the knowledge it brings.” — س ره

[^V02-147]: If we draw a straight line of limited length, fix one end, and turn the other end until it returns to its starting point, we make a true circle. Many shapes have been proved in this way. — س ره

[^V02-148]: The assumption is that the material and separate cases share the same nature. If the material cases could not exist without an added feature, then the separate cases could not exist without it either; both would depend on that feature. Or the point may be that if an added feature makes a cause necessary, that cause must still exist before the thing that bears the feature, since an effect depends on its cause by its very nature. — س ره

[^V02-149]: I would say the reverse. There is a great difference between human nature as it exists in the divine realm, where it is at once knowing, knowledge, and known, and the same nature as it exists in bodily life. There is also a great difference between the powers that cause kinds to exist—such as necessity, possibility, and essence, whether considered alone or together with spatial position—and the complete patterns of those kinds. The higher natures are understood as they exist in a world where all things are joined, not as bare concepts or outlines. — س ره

[^V02-150]: The earlier reply shows that Avicenna’s argument fails for natures whose instances differ in degree, a distinction everyone accepts. Here the answer goes further, using the Illuminationist view that a nature itself can vary in degree: its level is both what differs and what makes the difference. Avicenna’s argument would work if every instance of a nature had the same level, like one fixed shade of white. It does not work if the nature can be stronger or weaker, like the difference between the whiteness of ivory and straw. — س ره

[^V02-151]: First, being limited or unlimited usually applies to quantities. Here the author has assumed that an image in the world of imagination is separate from matter, like an intellect. One might answer that it is still an extended image, so it can be limited or unlimited in its size, though not by its nature as an immaterial being. Second, if Avicenna’s argument worked, it would also rule out extended images in the world of imagination. But the Illuminationists say such images exist apart from ordinary matter. They also allow there to be endlessly many of them. The proof against infinite quantities does not apply to these images: they are not moving bodies or parts of one continuous, infinitely long space. They are simply unlimited in number, with no order or chain of causes among them. — س ره

[^V02-152]: This conflicts with what the philosophers—and the author himself elsewhere—say: only God and His complete Words exist before time. Perhaps the author means that each part of time comes into being anew, with nonexistence before and after it, even though God’s giving of existence never stops. — س ره

[^V02-153]: This is especially true of the borrowed beloved who is a fully perfected human being. The way this image is real is that we see it only as something that disappears into the One who appears through it—like wine without a cup. This may be why the Prophet said, “I was made to love three things from your world.” The soul’s turn toward the true Beloved is real in truth, even if it outwardly seems to be drawn to an image. Few people see the sun in water and recognize the sun itself without being distracted by the water. — س ره

[^V02-154]: The veils of light are realities that exist apart from bodies, such as intellects and souls. The dark veils are bodily things. The number points to a meaning discussed elsewhere. — س ره

[^V02-155]: Rumi puts it well: “If this is your fire, what must your light be? If this is your grief, what must your form be?” — س ره

[^V02-156]: This describes an individual image held in the imagination. What, then, of someone whose whole attention—or most of it—is fixed on general truths and whose aim rises above passing particulars? How far apart are understanding something through the intellect and picturing it in the imagination; how far apart are their kinds of lasting life. — س ره

[^V02-157]: The images we sense also have no lasting faithfulness or stability. Intellectual forms, by contrast, remain without change. They are like the meaning behind the world’s many appearances, or like the preserved root of a tree in its branches. Their change is like the changing appearance of a person’s body, while the person’s identity remains. When this lasting reality becomes a form for the soul, and the soul receives it as its matter, the soul is completed by that form and gains its lasting, self-sufficient life. — س ره

[^V02-158]: The commentator sees two readings of “identity” here. It may mean unity in general, whose kinds include predication, sameness in kind, and related forms. Or it may mean predication, divided into what applies in itself and what applies incidentally. It does not mean the other familiar divisions of predication into primary and common, direct and derivative forms; those are not investigated here. The first reading is supported by the author’s detailed discussion of sameness in kind later on and his lack of a detailed discussion of types of predication. The second is supported by his listing sameness in kind and related forms alongside identity here. — س ره

[^V02-159]: This names a specific kind after a general one. Two things that differ either share a nature—in which case they are alike—or they do not. If they do not, they may still be able to exist together in the same thing; then they are merely different. If they cannot exist together in that way, they are opposites. — س ره

[^V02-160]: The word “collection” directly means a gathering, and the word “units” implies a group. A group is an example of the general idea of gathering, which is the same idea as multiplicity. — س ره

[^V02-161]: This is a reminder, not a definition. A definition explains a general idea grasped by reason, not a particular thing sensed or pictured in the imagination. Nor is saying what something is not a definition: we already know that every thing is not every other thing. For example, we can define a human as a rational animal through a general idea, then draw attention to that idea by pointing to a particular person, such as Zayd. — س ره

[^V02-162]: This divides a thing into categories that can overlap. “One insofar as one,” which is the same as unity itself, is also genuine unity. Later, the author uses “genuine unity” in a broader sense. — س ره

[^V02-163]: That is, it is not a settled feature that remains in its subject. It may still be a feature that does not stay fixed, such as facing or touching. The philosophers also say that division can result from two features, whether these remain fixed or not. — س ره

[^V02-164]: The thing made up of several things is “one by kind”; this does not mean that the general kind itself is the basis of unity. The example of a human being and a horse makes this clear: “animal” is one general kind, while humans and horses are one by kind. The same applies to the other examples. — س ره

[^V02-165]: Humanity is the shared feature of a writer and a person who laughs. It is outside their definitions, yet can be said of both. In the technical use here, an attribute is something said of a subject but not part of what the subject is. Humanity is an attribute of being a writer or a laugher in this sense. — س ره

[^V02-166]: Some copies of the metaphysics section of *The Healing* read *kasīr* here, with an unpointed sīn. — س ره

[^V02-167]: “As a whole” refers to the celestial sphere. Being divided in two ways means being divided into individuals of a general kind and being divided into matter and form. — س ره

[^V02-168]: Unlike celestial bodies, sizes can be divided into individual examples but not into matter and form, because quantities are simple in the outside world. — س ره

[^V02-169]: The fact that one occurs in every number is also an example of creation disappearing into God in the Great Resurrection. — س ره

[^V02-170]: Numbers are repetitions of unity. Repeating something is a way for it to appear; if its appearance were separate from it, it would be another thing, not its appearance. Each number is made of repeated ones and has no form beyond them: two is one and one; three is one and one and one; and so on. This is true of numbers as signs. Created beings, by contrast, have their own distinct parts: a house is made of a roof and walls, not a roof and another roof. Although unity is the basis of every number, the numbers themselves have different natures, qualities, and effects. Any two different numbers are entirely distinct: two is not three, and three is not two. Among examples such as the ocean and its waves, a moving flame, a circle, and different kinds of motion, the relation between unity and number is the clearest example of divine oneness. Perhaps this is why Zayn al-Abidin prayed, “My God, the oneness of number belongs to You.” — س ره

[^V02-171]: *Al-Kafi* says, “We have states with God: we are He, and He is us.” It also says, “He is within things, but not by mixing with them.” — ن ره

[^V02-172]: The identity of unity and individual identity follows from the identity of unity and real existence, since the author has already said that being an individual thing is real existence. The author raises this objection and reply as he turns to unity and identity. The next objection concerns unity being the same as real existence. — س ره

[^V02-173]: The mistake may be that the objection treats a feature of a thing as part of what the thing is. The identity that remains is the identity of the underlying matter, just as its vague unity remains. The unity of continuity disappears, and so does the identity of that continuity. Or the mistake may come from using “body” in two senses: the extended form that first appears to us to be the body, and the body as matter together with form. The identity of the first disappears; the second remains. — س ره

[^V02-174]: The earlier principles show that existence in general divides into one and many, and that the many exists just as the one does. Yet every thing is one insofar as it exists. So existence in general always goes with unity in general. When we compare one existent thing with another, however, we can divide them into one and many. The author is discussing this particular kind of unity, which is opposed to multiplicity. Its opposite, multiplicity, exists, though it is not itself one. This is like other divisions of existence in general: into actual and potential, or into things outside the mind that have effects and things that exist only in the mind. Existence applies to both sides, and being actual applies to all that exists outside the mind. The reason is that existence has different degrees: strong and weak, pure and mixed. Features such as unity and actuality apply to all these degrees, but when we compare them, the weaker lacks what the stronger has. Avicenna’s argument therefore proves that particular unity differs from existence, but it does not show that unity in general differs from existence. — ط مد

[^V02-175]: This rejects the first claim in the argument. — س ره

[^V02-176]: That is, if we exclude unity in general. But multiplicity, when opposed to a particular kind of unity, exists; it is one of the two divisions of existence in general. — ط مد

[^V02-177]: This is not a third option, since the argument gave only two. It chooses the second: multiplicity exists in reality, not only in the mind. The disagreement is about how the word “many” applies. The reply treats multiplicity as a feature that can coexist with unity in the underlying thing. The objection instead makes being many part of the thing itself. — س ره

[^V02-178]: Here “sameness” means ordinary predication—saying one thing of another—not a definition in which the subject and predicate mean the same thing. — س ره

[^V02-179]: The commentator is referring to the philosophers’ claim that a shared existence is the main basis for saying one thing of another. They do not mean that predication is the same as judging a statement to be true. A conditional statement, for example, has an “if” part and a “then” part, and the shared existence described here does not make either part a judgment by itself.

    Nor do they mean that shared existence is the only thing needed. Possibility and human nature share an existence; on the accepted view, so do blackness and a body. Yet we do not say “a human is possibility” or “a body is blackness.” We need a link that makes the feature belong to the subject. English shows this link in words such as “possible” and “black.”

    To understand this, we need two distinctions. Something can exist in itself, like a body, or exist as a feature of something else, like blackness in a body. It can also exist for itself or for another. Two natures cannot be joined in every respect, because then they would be one thing, not two. They must differ in one respect and be joined in another. Their difference lies in what each is in itself; their unity lies in one being a feature of the other.

    Take a body and blackness. They share one existence, but the body exists in itself while blackness exists as a feature of the body. The predicate “black” says that the body has that feature. In this way, blackness removes from the body the lack of being black. This is what logicians mean when they say that a statement has two parts: one identifies the thing we are talking about, and the other says what feature belongs to it. At heart, a statement has a subject, a predicate, and a judgment. Any separate link between them belongs to the predicate, not to a fourth part of the statement.

    From this we can see that, in philosophical terms, a statement joins something that exists in itself—the subject—to a feature that exists in it—the predicate. The familiar formula “same in existence, different in meaning” is not quite exact. More precisely, the subject and predicate differ in the way each exists in itself, and they are joined because the predicate exists as a feature of the subject.

    Predication takes different forms according to how the feature belongs to its subject. One form is when a thing is related to itself in the mind, as when we say “a human is a human.” This is an essential definition. Another form joins two levels that differ in completeness, such as a thing and the causes on which it depends. This is called predication between a higher reality and its lower reflection. A third form links a feature to the thing it belongs to, as when we say “the body is black.” This is ordinary predication. Finally, sameness in this sense is not limited to ideas in the mind. A statement that includes a judgment exists in the mind, but the relation between things described here can also hold outside it. — ط مد

[^V02-180]: This is only a possible view, not one known to have been held. One might suppose that Democritus’s tiny, hard bodies—the basic parts from which he thought larger bodies were made—differed in kind and therefore differed in how they join and separate. But Democritus himself did not hold this view. — س ره

[^V02-181]: A definition must match what it defines. Also, if shared existence alone were enough for ordinary predication, an effect could not differ from its cause, since an effect cannot exist without its cause. So this is not a mistaken reversal of the definition. — س ره

[^V02-182]: The point is that saying one thing of another requires both some unity and some difference. Things may share a kind, a quality, or another feature, but that alone does not make predication possible. The conjunction here means “even though both are present,” not that the passage has moved to a separate point. — س ره

[^V02-183]: This challenges the scholar who says that ordinary language restricts predication to shared existence. He says that resemblance and related forms of unity cannot support predication because their shared feature is a common nature, not existence. The commentator replies that a shared nature can also support sameness. He then asks how someone who treats a thing’s nature as primary can also say that identity is the same as existence. If “existence” means only that the nature is related to its maker, that relation alone does not make the thing an individual. — س ره

[^V02-184]: In an earlier note, the commentator said this point applies to things made of separate parts, but not to simple things. Color, for example, is not a separate thing within whiteness. A better account is that essential parts, such as animal nature and rational nature, differ in what they are; that is why we can divide a definition into a general kind and a distinguishing feature. Parts of a measured length, by contrast, share the same nature. Cutting them apart is not the same kind of division. This is why a measured part differs from its whole more fully than two things that share a definition. — س ره

[^V02-185]: This is the state called fanāʾ, or self-effacement, which brings joy to those who know God. It does not mean that two things become one. — س ره

[^V02-186]: Even so, the numbers that divide into a given number are close, natural features that arise from that number itself. In this sense, they are like essential features. Many questions in arithmetic and some less familiar sciences depend on them. — س ره

[^V02-187]: That is, number is made only from unity, and number comes about through repeating unity. This supports the author’s point. — س ره

[^V02-188]: Unity applies to itself, as existence applies to existence and knowledge to knowledge. It also applies to multiplicity, because every many has unity in some respect. Here “belonging to a subject” means being connected to, or attributed to, something, even as an idea. Unity is not something added only after its subject exists. — س ره

[^V02-189]: At first, the author’s wording seems to include likeness, while otherness in individual identity is called “other.” To understand “difference in a feature,” use the logical sense of a descriptive feature: it may be the subject itself, part of it, or something outside it. In another classification, things that share an essential feature are alike; if they do not share one, then they are either contraries that cannot coexist or different things that can. Here “contrary” means a difference in an essential feature. — س ره

[^V02-190]: The words “same” and “other” are used in different ways in different technical systems. In one, “other” means different in individual identity; in another, it means likeness. — س ره

[^V02-191]: The author may mean that some differences between kinds make coexistence impossible, not that this is always so. This is a claim about particular cases, not a universal rule. Many kinds under one broad category can coexist, such as sweetness and yellowness in honey. Some philosophers also count bodily matter and form as two kinds of substance. — ط مد

[^V02-192]: “Subject” means anything that can serve as a bearer, even one assumed only in thought. This includes a statement considered as the subject of affirmation and denial. The time condition applies only to things that exist in time; timeless cases do not need it. A shared subject is essential to opposition: each side excludes the other from the same bearer. — ط مد

[^V02-193]: The phrase “in the same respect” is needed for paired relations such as father and son. It does not exclude the case of Zayd, who can be both a father and a son, because he is a father in relation to one person and a son in relation to another. The two general relations themselves are opposites.

[^V02-194]: The objections apply to the second way of dividing opposition, not the first. In the first division, one side is already defined as the absence of the other. — س ره

[^V02-195]: The two meanings do not even apply to exactly the same things. “Not blind” can be said of something that does not exist, because denial does not require a subject. Sight, however, requires an existing subject. — س ره

[^V02-196]: If the first account is correct, then opposition outside affirmation and denial is indirect rather than direct. — ط مده

[^V02-197]: The objection assumes that “removal,” in the phrase “the opposite of each thing is its removal,” means denial in the usual sense. The author points out that this would make only one side of a contradiction the true opposite. He then proposes using “removal” in a broader sense that can describe either side. — ط مده

[^V02-198]: Affirmation does not deny its opposite; it is what the denial rejects. — ه ره

[^V02-199]: The opposite of “not human” is not simply “human”; it is the denial of “not human,” which implies “human.” — ه ره

[^V02-200]: The better account mentioned here is the one the author develops later in the section on God and creation: “removal” includes both the idea of removing something and the real instance of its being removed. — ن ره

[^V02-201]: This is like the Arabic word for “praise” in “Praise belongs to God”: it can mean the act of praising or the state of being praised. The general word for “removal” can likewise mean either removing or being removed. — س ره

[^V02-202]: In other words, we can say either “its opposite is what removes it” or “its opposite is what is removed.” — ه ره

[^V02-203]: The contrast is between Zayd’s being white and his not being white. These are not complete statements. — س ره

[^V02-204]: If we add “not” to an idea simply as an idea, we get another idea, separate from the first just as any two ideas are. But if “not” means that something does not exist, the idea concerns whether a real thing exists, and the contrast becomes one between statements. This shows that contradiction in the full sense is between statements. The apparent contradiction between single terms such as “human” and “not human” depends on the positive and negative statements in which they appear. At its root is the rule that either the affirmation or the denial is true, but never both or neither. — ط مده

[^V02-205]: The author says this because the opposition between affirmation and denial applies both to statements and to single terms. — س ره

[^V02-206]: The two statements cannot both be true of the same case. The author later explains that they cannot both be true, but may both be false when there is no subject. — س ره

[^V02-207]: I say that al-Tusi, as explained in *Al-Shawāriq*, means that this opposition does not concern things existing outside the mind. In a contradiction, affirmation and denial concern relations that exist only in the mind, when we think a statement, or in speech, when we utter it. Opposition is itself a relation: a relation between relations. Indeed, the negative relation is the denial of the relation. It is surprising that the author himself acknowledges this a few lines later when he says that their opposition exists only in the mind, or figuratively in speech. That was all al-Tusi intended. Perhaps the author took him to be explaining what produces the opposition, or which side is opposed directly and which indirectly. But al-Tusi was identifying what the opposition applies to. — س ره

[^V02-208]: Calling universal affirmative and negative statements “contraries” stretches the usual meaning of the word. The author’s own example shows that this is only a label, not a strict fit with the definition. — ط مد

[^V02-209]: “Relation” here means an explanatory connection, not a feature that describes one thing in terms of another. An essence and a necessary result may be connected, yet neither exists at the same level as the other. The same applies to paired relations. — س ره

[^V02-210]: The passage may seem to repeat itself, but it makes two different points. First, a subject cannot be without either affirmation or denial in reality. Second, at a particular level of reality, it may be neither present nor absent. The author also adds that the statements cannot both be true. — س ره

[^V02-211]: Paired relations such as fatherhood and sonhood are opposites because each is understood by reference to the other. Neither can be understood alone. Their opposition is part of what they are. Some of the author’s other remarks may suggest that relations such as knower and known can coexist, and that they fail to do so only for an outside reason, as with cause and effect. The commentator rejects that view. — ط مد

[^V02-212]: Do not confuse a concept with what it describes. The idea of “relation” is not itself a relation, but the things it describes may be related. In the same way, the idea of “opposition” is an idea about relations, while its examples may include other kinds of relation. — ن ره

[^V02-213]: The author offers three answers. The first distinguishes a feature from the things that bear it. The second distinguishes relation in general from the narrower kind of relation that is opposition. The third distinguishes defining an idea from applying it to actual examples. In that sense, the concept of opposition is opposition by definition, while an actual case of opposition may also be a paired relation. A mental concept can belong to one group and serve as an example of another when considered in different ways. — س ره

[^V02-214]: The author repeatedly says that the definition of contraries, and some of the rules connected with it, rest on convention. These include the claims that one thing has only one contrary, that contraries must belong to the same nearest general kind, and that substances cannot be contrary to one another. This suggests that he regards only the conditions produced by the division of opposites as essential to the definition. On that account, contraries are two existing, positive things that are not relative to one another and cannot be present in the same subject in the same respect. Anything added to this would rest on convention.

    But it would be very strange to build philosophical conclusions on convention alone. If we add conditions to a definition that follows from a division, we create further possible kinds of opposition. We must either recognize and name these kinds or prove that they cannot exist.

    The usual definition says that contraries are two positive things, neither defined in relation to the other, that take turns in one subject, belong to the same nearest general kind, and differ as far as possible. Here is how its conditions can be understood.

    The first conditions follow from the earlier division of opposites. Either one opposite is the absence of the other, or both are positive. If both are positive, they are either understood through their relation to each other or they are not. The second pair are contraries: two positive things that are not relative terms.

    Why must they have a subject? To call two things opposed, and different from each other in their very nature, we must consider a third thing with which either one can be united. The presence of one then excludes the other from that same third thing. Otherwise, the idea of opposition does no work. Since both contraries are positive, the third thing must be something in which a positive feature can exist. Here *subject* is used broadly. It may mean the matter that receives a substantial form or the subject that bears an accidental property.

    Yet if we also require the greatest possible difference, substantial forms will not count as contraries. We are left with subjects that bear accidental properties. This is one reason for saying that contraries must be accidents, that they come in pairs, and that the two members take turns in a single subject.

    Why, then, must both belong to one general kind? A substantial form is united in existence with its matter. Together they make one definite thing. If substantial forms could be contraries, that union itself would be enough for each to exclude the other from the matter with which it becomes one.

    An accidental property is different. Its subject can exist without it. The subject and the property do not become a single reality in that same way. Simply saying that the two properties belong to one subject does not explain why one must exclude the other: one accident does not become identical with another merely because they share a subject.

    We therefore need something else with which either contrary can be united so completely that it makes a single kind of thing. In that unity, each excludes the other, because allowing both would amount to making something exclude itself. What meets this description is a general kind, or *broader kind*, which includes its species. The two contraries must therefore come under one broader kind.

    More precisely, it must be their nearest broader kind. A species is made definite by the distinguishing feature that divides and completes that nearest broader kind. A more distant broader kind does not have the same direct relation to the distinguishing feature. This was explained earlier in the discussion of essence. It follows that genuine contrariety occurs within a kind of accidental property divided into two species, with no third species between them.

    Finally, why must the difference be the greatest possible? Philosophers first recognized opposition in such pairs as black and white. They then found intermediate colors, including yellow and red. These could be called white in comparison with black, or black in comparison with white. They were therefore treated as combinations lying between the two extremes, leaving the extreme ends as the real contraries.

    On this account, *the greatest possible difference* means that the opposition is not a matter of comparison or changing points of view. The two sides differ in themselves. This last condition makes explicit what their opposition already means. — Ṭ. M.

[^V02-215]: The definition would be clearer if it said that contraries are positive things whose meanings do not depend on each other, that take turns in one subject, and so on. Paired relations can also take turns in one subject, but they are not contraries. — س ره

[^V02-216]: If good and evil were contraries, their opposition would be a paired relation. But they are not contraries: suitability and conflict are relations. — س ره

[^V02-217]: A better account is that virtue and vice are evaluative labels: they describe what should or should not be done. Vice also means a lack, as its definition makes clear. — ط مده

[^V02-218]: This assumes that there is no middle state between health and sickness. Some physicians say there are intermediate conditions, such as the state of a child, a young person, or an old person. — س ره

[^V02-219]: The author is returning to his earlier definition, which requires a shared subject, not merely a place where things exist. — س ره

[^V02-220]: The fact that kinds under a broad general kind do not oppose one another does not prove that sharing a nearest general kind causes opposition. It is possible that a shared kind plays no part at all. The author would need to show that contraries must share some kind. Also, the list of ten highest categories comes from observing examples; it is not a conclusion proved by reason alone. — ط مده

[^V02-221]: The earlier problem may have been about how a general kind and its defining features fit together. The defining features divide the general kind into its specific kinds; this does not mean that several separate causes act on one result. That is a different issue from whether two features can take turns in the same subject. The first reading is more precise. — س ره

[^V02-222]: Contrariety is a feature of things as they exist outside the mind. This does not rule out opposition between ideas in the mind, where the mind considers them in different ways.

[^V02-223]: The phrase “whether or not” refers to the positive feature: the subject may have the potential for it in itself, at a certain time, or as a member of a kind or broader group. — س ره

[^V02-224]: “Blindness” includes cases such as a scorpion’s or a person born unable to see. Hair loss from disease occurs after the subject had hair; hairlessness from birth occurs before that. Darkness is mentioned because some treat it as a positive contrary of light. — س ره

[^V02-225]: It is strange that he reverses the two established uses of “having and lacking.” Metaphysicians use the broader meaning and logicians the narrower one, as anyone who reads the *Categories* section of *The Healing* can see. In this book, the relevant lack is the loss of a capacity at the time when the subject should be able to act—for example, blindness after the power of sight has been lost. Having the capacity can give way to lacking it; once this particular capacity has been lost, the lack itself cannot simply become the capacity. By “this book,” the author of the quoted discussion means the *Categories*, the ancient title for the section on the ten categories of things in logic. Other sections were also given Greek titles, such as *Isagoge* for the five universals and *Bārminās* for propositions, as noted in the author’s commentary on *The Healing* and elsewhere. — س ره

[^V02-226]: This definition is only one part of the division. It focuses on whether the things can exist together in one subject. The different definitions add other conditions to separate contrary qualities from paired relations and from having and lacking. These conditions include being positive features, differing as much as possible, and having a subject that can move from one side to the other. — س ره

[^V02-227]: This supports the point that the terms are used in the reverse way described above. To include a tree’s lack of sight under contrariety, we need the broader, familiar meaning, which does not require both sides to be positive. A tree cannot see as an individual, as a kind, or through a nearby general kind; only a very broad underlying kind such as matter could be called capable of sight. The tree is not a problem for affirmation and denial, which also cover it. — س ره

[^V02-228]: Since the broader, familiar meaning does not require both sides to be positive, it can include these examples. They can also be included under the broader meaning of having and lacking. — س ره

[^V02-229]: As noted earlier, the familiar definition of contrariety is simply that the two sides cannot exist together in one subject. — س ره

[^V02-230]: On closer inspection, odd and even belong to affirmation and denial. The name “the One” used for God also points to this use of “one.” — س ره

[^V02-231]: In the earlier philosophers’ account, every shade of black is black in itself; it does not become white when compared with a darker shade. The claim that a middle shade is black relative to a lower shade and white relative to a higher one does not fit that account. — ن ره

[^V02-232]: The true unity found in existence does not oppose the different levels of multiplicity, which vary in how complete they are. This is unity within multiplicity and multiplicity within unity. As we have seen, multiplicity is nothing but unity repeated. The author’s difficulty is that he assumes one and many are opposed, then tries to decide which kind of opposition applies without first showing that they are opposed at all. What is clear is only that their meanings differ. — س ره

[^V02-233]: This follows from the usual rule about general ideas: each thing’s nature, considered in itself, is simply what it is. But in Mulla Sadra’s account, the particular existence of a possible thing is what is truly caused and brought into being; it is not the same as this general concept. — س ره

[^V02-234]: As they say, paired relations do not cause one another. Unity, by contrast, is a cause and comes first in its own right, while multiplicity is its effect and comes after it. — س ره

[^V02-235]: One could defend the author’s point by saying that the difference between the two unities comes from the different things in which they appear. Unity itself does not differ, and the size of the thing does not change the unity it receives. So the unity that contributes to a new multiplicity does so in the same way as unity contributes to multiplicity generally, once we set aside its link to a particular thing. Like cases should be treated alike, whether the result is allowed or not. Also, if the author’s opponent appealed to the mutual relation between measuring and being measured, this reply would not apply. — س ره

[^V02-236]: That is, the two opposing things must take turns in the same individual thing. — س ره

[^V02-237]: That is, two opposing things do not have to take turns in one individual thing. — س ره

[^V02-238]: Movement in a human being and rest in an inanimate thing illustrate opposition across a very broad shared kind. By contrast, the movement and rest of this same stone plainly take turns in one individual thing. Likewise, good and bad can refer, for example, to the goodness ascribed to God and to basic matter. — س ره

[^V02-239]: Defining features and necessary qualities oppose their negations in what we say about them, not in the way they exist, as the author explained earlier. — س ره

[^V02-240]: This refers to the appeal to desire in the argument. — س ره

[^V02-241]: This suggests that the four familiar kinds of opposition are not a fixed list proved by reason alone; the usual definition of contraries rests on accepted usage. What we can say for certain is that both one and many exist, and that they conflict in some way. Existence itself divides directly into one and many. These two ideas come from existence itself and are not outside it; otherwise, they would be false, since existence is what is fundamental. Unity and multiplicity are different features of existence, yet both belong to it. As explained earlier, unity goes with existence and applies to both one and many. Existence is shared by them and also differs in each. This is how one reality can have stronger and weaker levels. Many is the weaker level because the unity that goes with existence is weaker in it. This resembles other divisions: what exists outside the mind and what exists only in the mind; what is actual and what is only possible; what stays fixed and what changes. Different levels of one reality do not oppose one another in the strict sense, because their differences depend on what they share. So one and many, simply as one and many, are not opposites. The fact that one can appear when many disappears, and vice versa, shows only that they occupy different levels of one reality. — ط مده

[^V02-242]: Someone may object: a complete cause must include the absence of anything that blocks the effect. But absence is nothing; how can it help bring something into existence? Also, if a complete cause contains all the incomplete causes, it is not one thing but a collection made only in thought. The reply is that a complete cause can be simple, as the First Cause is for its first effect, or made up of several things, as causes in nature are. An agent that brings something into existence must itself exist. If “the complete cause exists” means that the agent exists along with all the conditions it needs to act, there is no problem. The effect may depend on something negative, such as the absence of a barrier, but that absence does not bring existence about; the agent does. Or the phrase may mean that each part of the cause is present or absent in the way needed: the agent, matter, form, and a condition contribute by being present; a barrier contributes by being absent; and a preparatory cause may contribute through either its presence or its absence. The author of *The Mawāqif* says that the absence of a barrier reveals a positive condition. For example, an open doorway reveals a space through which one can pass, and the absence of a support reveals a space where a roof can fall. We may know a positive condition only by noticing that something negative is absent, which can make it seem that the absence itself causes the effect. The same objection and reply apply to the next chapter’s claim that a cause must exist when its effect exists, and that an effect must exist when its complete cause exists. — س ره

[^V02-243]: The author avoids defining a complete cause as “everything a thing needs,” because that would make it sound as if a complete cause must be made up of parts. The First Cause of its first effect, for example, may be simple. “In the first sense” may qualify “complete cause,” or it may mean that this is an added meaning beyond the first definition: the first definition also applies to the last part of a complete cause, which can be called a cause in a narrower sense. So a complete cause is what brings an effect into existence, with no further cause beyond it. — س ره

[^V02-244]: Conditions and the removal of obstacles are also incomplete causes. They are often not listed separately, because they may be counted as part of the agent, understood as the complete cause that acts independently. Sometimes they are counted as part of matter, since a thing cannot receive a form until the right conditions are present and obstacles are removed. In his commentary on *Al-Mulakhkhas*, al-Jurjānī counts tools as part of the agent and the other factors as part of matter. The same applies to the second division: conditions and the removal of obstacles are causes that are not parts of the thing. The thing that receives an accidental feature is counted with matter because it is much like matter. If we consider a general kind and its distinguishing feature separately from the whole, the kind plays the role of matter and the feature the role of form. — س ره

[^V02-245]: Al-Jurjānī gives a more detailed division. An incomplete cause is something on which a thing depends in order to exist. It is either part of that thing or outside it. If it is outside, it is either what receives the effect—the underlying thing that bears an accidental feature, or the matter that receives a substantial form—or it is not a receiver. If it is not a receiver, it is either the agent, the purpose, or neither. In the last case it may be something positive, such as a condition, or something negative, such as the absence of an obstacle. A part is either a part in thought, such as a general kind or its distinguishing feature, or a physical part, such as matter or form. The phrase “it may be positive or negative” divides these cases without leaving any out; it also includes the preparatory cause. — س ره

[^V02-246]: That is, the same is true of the other one, form or matter. — س ره

[^V02-247]: The purpose, as understood in the mind, explains why the agent acts as an agent. Just as something’s being a purpose is not itself caused, an agent’s being an agent is not itself caused. — س ره

[^V02-248]: “Human form” here means the form that makes a human being the kind of thing it is, not the body’s shape. The form in the father brings about the same kind of form in the child. The ultimate agent is God, or, by God’s power, one of the beings close to Him. The passage does not mean the father’s physical shape. For Avicenna, the natural cause is the shaping power; for the Illuminationists, it is the intelligence that governs the species. It is not the father’s bodily form. — س ره

[^V02-249]: This objection applies to the view that the world began after a period of nonexistence, as the Ashʿarites held. They imagined that the world was preceded by empty stretches of time. In their view, freedom and necessity conflict, so they rejected the rule that a thing cannot bring about an effect until it becomes necessary to do so. Other thinkers say that necessity through a free choice does not conflict with freedom. God may freely create the world even if creation is necessary from then on and impossible in eternity. — س ره

[^V02-250]: A choice must be preceded by knowledge, will, intention, and power. In this account, God’s knowledge helps bring what He knows into being, and His will takes effect. The action would also depend on the chosen time being present. There is another problem: will is the final part of the complete cause, so the effect should follow it without delay. If a choice occurs at one time but the action only happens later, that choice is more like a general plan—such as deciding in winter to build in summer—than a specific will to act then. — س ره

[^V02-251]: These thinkers compare the will’s relation to the power to act with the relation between necessity and possibility. — س ره

[^V02-252]: That is, it is impossible for the event to happen before its appointed time. — س ره

[^V02-253]: The claim is that will follows knowledge and carries out what knowledge judges possible—that is, something that can happen later, not before. But this has already been rejected. — س ره

[^V02-254]: This differs from the earlier view that God wills what He knows will happen. That view makes His knowledge the cause of the event’s beginning. This view says instead that having a beginning belongs to the event itself. — س ره

[^V02-255]: Someone may object that allowing a choice without a reason denies a purpose that explains the action and blocks the proof of an agent that brings things about. The reply is that if an option can win without a reason, it also seems to be favored without a cause. The issue is which option becomes real, not merely whether the agent prefers it. Also, for those who accept a purpose as what makes an agent act, denying that purpose is like denying the agent itself. — س ره

[^V02-256]: Someone may say that the other thinkers do not claim God’s knowledge follows what happens; they only say He knows which things will and will not happen. But the Ashʿarites are known to say that knowledge follows what is known. If instead they say God’s knowledge is active and comes before the thing, they face the problems of treating it as eternal and compelled. There is also a direct argument: knowing a thing as it is requires that it exist. We cannot know a nonexistent thing as it is. If it had no existence or nature for the knower, how could it be known and distinguished? This argument also assumes from the start the very timing it needs to explain. — س ره

[^V02-257]: This means God’s eternal knowledge of a thing as it is. If that knowledge depends on the thing’s existing, the explanation goes in a circle. If it depends on the thing’s essence, it treats nonexistent essences as real. Another account says that essences are fixed through their dependence on God’s existence, but the thinkers under discussion have not reached that view, much less the idea that all things exist within the simple reality of God in a higher and more unified way. In any case, God’s eternal knowledge is active; it does not single out one particular time for a thing to begin. — س ره

[^V02-258]: This alludes to a reply to the view that time itself determines when an event begins. — س ره

[^V02-259]: The argument proves that God’s creative activity and causing continue without end; it does not prove that the things He creates are eternal. This is what the author means below by “the universal act” and “creation in general.” He began from the view of the most accomplished philosophers. But saying that everything besides God and His attributes is eternal would be disbelief and error, according to the thinkers of *Maʿārij al-Kamāl*, among the leading philosophers. This is how the argument should be stated, though the point is difficult to grasp. — ن ره

[^V02-260]: The argument does apply to events that continually renew themselves: the world of nature is always changing and cannot be eternal in the sense of remaining fixed. Its place at the receiving end of God’s giving is part of its nature, yet God’s giving does not cease. — س ره

[^V02-261]: There is a difference between the possibility of existing without a beginning and the possibility itself having no beginning. — س ره

[^V02-262]: This is remarkable: what explains why events begin also explains why God’s activity continues. — س ره

[^V02-263]: That is, what connects events to the Eternal is one continuous reality, not something preceded by a definite or imagined time. — س ره

[^V02-264]: This objection likely comes from misunderstanding Avicenna. He means a complete cause. An effect cannot be causally prior to its complete cause, and something that accompanies a cause cannot be earlier than its effect in time, since cause and effect exist at the same time. Priority by nature is not relevant here either. Priority, posteriority, and being together by nature concern incomplete causes; an incomplete cause cannot be considered alongside a complete cause, whether inside or outside it. So Avicenna is right that there is no priority in this case. — ط مد

[^V02-265]: For example, the outer sphere that exists with the second intellect is ahead of the inner sphere in rank and honor. But the priority by nature mentioned by the objector is the role of the last part of a complete cause, often called “the cause,” and of the removal of an obstacle. Both exist together with the complete cause. — س ره

[^V02-266]: This kind of priority does not follow just because two things happen together. Sunrise may coincide with a hand moving, and that hand movement may come before a key moves; the sun does not therefore come before the key. By contrast, the outer sphere and the second intellect exist together in their own order because both are effects of one cause. — س ره

[^V02-267]: Someone may ask how a feature can exist together with the thing that has it. The answer is that this is a matter of rank within the order of kinds. We can choose a broader kind, such as substance, or a specific kind, such as human being, as our starting point. Being together in one sense does not rule out priority or posteriority in another. — س ره

[^V02-268]: Al-Rāzī argues that if what accompanies something prior can be prior in one case, it should also be prior in the other. But this compares two different claims: whether one effect can have two causes, and whether one cause can have two effects. — س ره

[^V02-269]: Al-Rāzī may have a point. His objection concerns priority by nature, not only priority through causation. Something accompanying what is prior by nature may itself be prior by nature when the two are essentially linked, as animal and rationality are in relation to a human being. Or they may merely happen to be together, as sunrise, or Zayd’s arrival, may coincide with a hand movement that comes before a key moves. Perhaps the difference lies in what “together” means. — س ره

[^V02-270]: Causality is a feature a thing can have only once it exists. But in this proposed circle, the thing’s existence would come from something else. So it would first be an effect of that other thing and only then a cause of it. This is how the circular argument should be set out. — ن ره

[^V02-271]: That is, even though the impossibility of the conclusion is already plain, another argument is offered for it. — س ره

[^V02-272]: A difference that exists only in how we think of things is not enough to make opposites coexist. Priority and posteriority, and cause and effect, are unlike the knower and the known: the latter can be related in both directions, while the former cannot. Nor is the author’s denial here self-evident, so questioning it does not violate the rules of debate. — س ره

[^V02-273]: Since the argument is stated without qualification, it seems to claim that possibility and necessity conflict both in meaning and in reality. The author denies that they conflict in reality. Had the argument been offered only as a pointer to the proof, this loose wording would have been common enough. — س ره

[^V02-274]: When it is said that a final cause is a cause by its essence of what causes the thing to exist, this does not mean that its essence, considered on its own, is a cause. It causes through its existence in the mind. — س ره

[^V02-275]: For the two matters; and it was for the two matters. — خ

[^V02-276]: The point seems to be that a group’s existence adds nothing to the existence of its members. For that reason, its being caused is no different from the members’ being caused. The group would then need a first cause, just as its members do. But some writers describe the group as having an existence added to the existence of its members. On that account, the group’s cause would be its members, each caused by the one before it, and there would be no need for a separate first cause of the group. This would undermine the proof. Even so, we can still question whether an endless group forms a whole in the same way as a finite group. — ط مده

[^V02-277]: For example, the links of a chain. But that example concerns finite lengths and dimensions.

[^V02-278]: The reply tries to make the objection seem like a denial of an obvious truth, so that it can be dismissed as stubbornness. But the shorter series need not fall behind only at its final end; it might fall behind at infinitely many points, while the longer one gains infinitely many members. The sound reply is narrower: the proof assumes that one series has a finite number of extra members. That cannot be denied. The first reply works only if linked lengths and connected causes cannot break off in the middle, and if the mind can grasp the full order at once. — س ره

[^V02-279]: One could challenge the claim that this is self-evident, and deny that equality or difference in size applies to endless quantities. — ط مده

[^V02-280]: This is especially so for those who say that multiplicity is only an idea, as do some who hold that existence and what exists are one. On their view, numbers are only levels of multiplicity. — س ره

[^V02-281]: The point is that only a finite number of counted things exists, and the numbers we derive from them are finite too. But the same reasoning would apply to the motions of the heavenly spheres, since only finitely many of those exist at one time. — س ره

[^V02-282]: The word “some” may be out of place here: all philosophers say that souls are without end, since the flow of existence never stops. In their books on logic, they use the rational soul as an example of a general kind with endless individual members. The souls’ endlessness is simultaneous, not successive. Otherwise, it would not be limited to human souls, since every kind—human beings, horses, cattle, and all other earthly kinds—has endless individual members over time. One answer is that the author is contrasting this view with that of those who say that souls become one after leaving their bodies. They compare this to water in many jars: when the jars break, the water joins together. — س ره

[^V02-283]: Al-Kubasāt objects that the matching proof cannot be trusted. Two endless series may differ in a way that concerns their finite limit, not the respect in which they are endless. For example, an endless series of hundreds and an endless series of thousands both go on forever, but their members differ in size. Matching their ends in imagination does not move the whole difference out of the finite part and into the endless part. As the mind keeps matching them, the extra amount shifts from the end toward the middle and keeps moving; it never reaches one fixed point or the last member. When the mind stops, the difference remains at the point where it stopped. In short, the difference never moves into the endless part. It always remains at a finite point, either at the end or somewhere in the middle. So, he says, hold to the argument and do not be confused. — ه ره

[^V02-284]: This is because the levels of number do not all exist, unlike the motions of the heavenly spheres. The point is not that numbers are only ideas, as in the theologians’ view. Many philosophers hold that numbers are real, separate quantities. — س ره

[^V02-285]: This is the strongest argument, because the whole series has no existence beyond the individual events, as the author will show. So this does not apply a claim about each member to a separate whole. The whole is nothing but the events, each of which is finite; it is not one event that is itself finite. The same applies to a general kind, which exists only through its individual members. The supposed beginninglessness and permanence of a general kind come from confusing what exists in the mind with what exists outside it. — س ره

[^V02-286]: If the two series cannot be equal, how could one be greater? “Equal” here means equal in their pattern of growth. Al-Tusi’s point is that after matching, the series that starts one year ago must be equal to, greater than, or smaller than the one that starts now. The first two options are impossible, so the third follows—and that means the two series are finite. — س ره

[^V02-287]: Do not think this argument simply changes the proof. It does bring in the argument from reciprocal relations, but that argument counts paired things; it does not need an order or two series, which the matching argument does need. The author makes the work easier by saying that the two series already match in reality, without asking us to imagine a match. On the disputed side, the earlier events must then outnumber the later ones, because their counts must balance. The two series are like two lines that agree at every point except their ends, so both must have ends. The series are taken as what gives rise to these relations and makes them real.

    The claim that an earlier event at one end has a later event at the other end that is not itself earlier depends only on how we look at them. In reality, whatever event we choose as the latest is also earlier than what follows it. This is especially clear in time and motion, which have no first or last part. The same is true of a continuous line: no later part fails to be earlier than another part. So the argument does not apply to the motions of the heavenly spheres or to time as a continuous length. It differs from a chain of causes and effects, where there is a final effect that needs a cause, whose cause needs another cause, and so on without end. There, the number of earlier and later causes and effects must balance, so the argument requires them to be finite. Also, events that occur one after another do not all exist at the same time; only one exists at any moment. So they do not form an actual series that can be matched. The author presents the argument in a way that fits the theologians’ view. — س ره

[^V02-288]: This depends on the series stopping in the future, so that there would be a later event that is not earlier than anything else. That would make the later events in the past more numerous than the earlier ones. But why could the series not be impossible to stop, and so continue forever into the future? Then the earlier and later events in the series would be equal. — ط مده

[^V02-289]: If number is only an idea, then only the things we count really exist. The ordered numbers themselves do not. What actually exists has no order of number built into it. For example, which of the separate souls that exist together from eternity is the one counted as three, and which as four? There is no reason to choose one rather than another. So how could this order apply to them? This is really a point against the claim that each number is made up of the numbers below it. — س ره

[^V02-290]: The author does not use an argument some people offer: that a group of people captured a city, though no one person captured it alone. That proves only that the group differs from each person; it does not prove that the group has a separate existence. The group might be an idea drawn from its members, as fatherhood is drawn from Zayd and his child. The later example of several people moving a heavy stone will make this clearer. — س ره

[^V02-291]: “The whole” here means the whole in relation to what lies outside it. A whole ends when all its parts end, just as it ends when any one part ends. — س ره

[^V02-292]: When people say that a whole has an existence beyond that of each individual part, they mean the members taken together as a whole. They do not mean one thing made up of the members plus the description “together.” The fact that many things are arranged as one is a separate description of them. Or they mean the whole under the condition that its parts are arranged together. They do not mean that the arrangement itself is one of the parts: that arrangement is only an idea, or it would itself need another arrangement, and so on forever. — س ره

[^V02-293]: On this view, the whole exists, even if it exists only through the same existence as its individual members. This is like existence and essence, or broader kind and difference, in simple things. A chain of causes and effects is a whole of this kind; on their view, it still needs a separate cause. — س ره

[^V02-294]: In fact, the opposite was established. The members taken together come first as causes, while the whole under the condition that they are arranged together comes later as an effect. The details are discussed in *Shawāriq* by the scholar Lāhījī. — س ره

[^V02-295]: One might object that all the infinitely many chains taken together also form a whole that exists in its own right and must have a cause. Is that cause the whole itself, one of its parts, or something outside it? The author will address this shortly by defining the whole as all existing possible things. — س ره

[^V02-296]: For example, some hold that there are infinitely many motions, times, and souls, all of which depend on the Necessary Being. — س ره

[^V02-297]: Some also use this objection to argue that a thing can cause itself; the objection and answer are much alike. The argument works only if the whole has an existence beyond the existence of its members, which the author will deny. I would add that if the Necessary Being and all possible things formed one independently existing whole, the Necessary Being would be part of something else. Yet the Necessary Being has no parts and cannot be a part of another thing; that would also make God limited. God has no equal, rival, or second, and encompasses all things. If by this “whole” we mean only the concepts of the Necessary Being and the natures of possible things, then it plainly does not exist as a real thing. The concept “Necessary Being” is necessary only by definition; as something in the mind, it is possible. Have they not heard that God’s unity is absolute, not numerical; that God is not one item among other existing things, and is not detached from them? In any case, even setting aside these points, their own books state that the Necessary Being is not part of anything else. Yet on their view, this proposed composite would exist as a distinct thing. — س ره

[^V02-298]: The remaining parts would then depend on this cause, directly or through other causes. This is the least degree of independence, since the remaining parts depend on it through intermediaries. The greatest degree is when they all depend on it directly. — س ره

[^V02-299]: This objection applies to the premise only if it is taken as a universal rule. In the case under discussion, all the parts of the chain exist together, since the argument concerns a chain of causes and effects that exists all at once. But the author may be giving an example of a later effect taking the place of an earlier cause in the order of rank—for instance, the tenth intellect taking the rank of the first intellect in relation to the Necessary Being—or of an effect lagging behind its cause. — س ره

[^V02-300]: Rank is considered only from the end where the series has a limit; from the other side, only causal order is considered, because there is no fixed beginning there. — س ره

[^V02-301]: This feature belongs on the side of the effect, not the cause. In the same way, possibility and whatever else the first effect depends on are considered on the effect’s side; they do not compromise the simplicity of its complete cause. Also, the discussion here concerns the independence of an efficient cause, while the final effect is considered on the side of the material cause. — س ره

[^V02-302]: This reply changes the argument instead of answering it, which is not proper in a debate. — س ره

[^V02-303]: This is the point made when the original proof was stated, as explained earlier. — س ره

[^V02-304]: A condition may be necessary for something to exist: if the condition is absent, the thing is absent too. But the thing does not necessarily exist whenever the condition is present. The same is true of an incomplete cause. A complete cause, however, does produce its effect. — س ره

[^V02-305]: One might object: if the whole depends on each individual part, then its cause is the individuals taken together, not the whole itself. That would avoid saying that something causes itself. Besides, no single individual part is a complete cause. We reply that saying the whole depends on each part does not mean that each part is its complete cause. On this view, the whole itself is the complete cause. The point is that it does not depend on a complete cause outside itself. It is enough that an effect depends on every part of its complete cause and on that cause coming before it. In this view, “what an effect depends on” describes causes short of the complete cause. — س ره

[^V02-306]: Examples include a thing causing its own cause, as in a causal circle, or depending on itself. This does not mean that it is its own condition or one of its own parts; those would amount to saying that it causes itself. — س ره

[^V02-307]: The group exists only in the mind. — س ره

[^V02-308]: They may say that each whole is another existent, but they do not mean that it is a third, single thing counted as one. With a walnut and an almond, for example, the whole is not a third item beside the walnut and the almond, like a pistachio would be. Nor is it a separate arrangement that exists on its own. Instead, it is an existent that is both walnut and almond: one existent is only the walnut, another is only the almond, and a third is the walnut and almond together. This third is “another” because the walnut alone is one, the almond alone is another, and their whole is many things with parts, not one thing.

    This differs from the author’s view. He says that a whole is not a separate existent, because it is many, and existence goes with unity. These thinkers do not accept that existence must mean unity. As Avicenna says, many things can exist as many, even though they are not one. On their view, the repeated parts are clear: if the walnut-and-almond whole is a third thing, as the critic claims, and its arrangement exists only in the mind, then the walnut and almond are repeated inside the three-part whole. They say that number exists in its own right in this sense. The author says that number exists through what the mind draws it from: the units that make it up. Here, “all the units together” means each individual unit; the proponents of a separate whole use the phrase in the way just described.

    One might ask why the author raises the problem of an endless chain at every division, as before. The answer is that his objection is a proof, not just a way of forcing an opponent to accept a consequence. If the two sides of a division were a separate existent and existence necessarily meant unity, there would be another one beyond them. But these thinkers do not accept that existence must mean unity. For them, existence is broader, so the whole can exist as many. A general kind, for example, exists through its many instances. It is distinct from its particular features through its generality, not through another special feature. This point has been explained at length because it is subtle. — س ره

[^V02-309]: The earlier discussion of unity and multiplicity allows us to say both that each number is simply its units taken together and that it is more than that, since “units taken together” is a general kind shared by every number. That apparent difference may be only in how the mind arranges the units. In the outside world, numbers are just fewer or more units. — س ره

[^V02-310]: Both ways of putting the argument can be challenged by denying that an effect can exist without itself causing something further. This objection applies whether the chain is made up of complete causes or incomplete ones. In other words, one can deny that the chain stops at the end where future effects would follow. — ط مده

[^V02-311]: Sayyid Dāmād did well in *Qabasāt* not to rely on intuition alone. He gave this rule: if a general judgment applies to every individual in every way that individual can exist—either on its own or considered together with others—then it also applies to the whole. But if the judgment applies to each individual only when it is separate from the others, the whole may have a different judgment. For example, “possible” applies to every possible thing, and “changing” applies to every natural event. Those judgments also apply to the whole. But one loaf may be enough to satisfy each person individually; that does not mean it is enough for a whole group. — س ره

[^V02-312]: The group whose size matches the number of thousand-groups may not lie neatly at the finite end or the infinite end. It may be woven through the other group, with no clear point where the second group begins. Also, choosing units so that one group has a certain multiple of the other has no basis outside the mind. — س ره

[^V02-313]: Think of bricks leaning on one another, or bodies resting on one another. Unless something supports them, there are no leaning bricks or supported bodies. Remove one, and all the ones after it fall because of the order of dependence between them. — س ره

[^V02-314]: Here, infinitely many possible things are treated like one possible thing: two equally possible options do not become actual unless something outside them makes one take precedence, and either may fail to exist. Only a necessary cause can remove every way an effect might fail to exist; a possible cause cannot. So the chain cannot enter reality if each member’s existence depends on a condition that has not itself been made actual. — س ره

[^V02-315]: There is another way to argue against an endless chain. We have established that bringing things into existence and causing things belong to existence itself. A caused thing does not exist on its own; its existence depends on its cause. The self-standing existence that supports it is the existence of its cause. If the chain went on without end, there would be dependent existences linked to one another, with no independent existence to support them. That is impossible. This link between cause and effect is not just an idea in the mind, so it is not enough to say that each is independent of the other in some limited way. The link reflects real degrees of stronger and weaker existence. This argument also rules out a circle of causes. It resembles the eighth proof, but the two arguments use different starting points. — ط مده
