[^V02-316]: The author limits this proof to things that exist together, so it does not apply to things that occur one after another: such things are not all distinct in the outside world at the same time, and their number in the mind is finite. I would add that the proof is challenged by things some thinkers regard as actually infinite: rational souls that have left their bodies, according to philosophers; forms in the imaginal world, according to Illuminationist thinkers; the particular names of God and their mental forms, called “fixed realities” by mystics; God’s words that never run out or perish; divine manifestations, the joys of the people of Paradise, and the punishments of the people of Hell, according to religious thinkers; mental forms in God’s essence, according to Anaximenes; fixed natures, according to the Muʿtazilites; and the horizontal intellects, if perfected souls join them after leaving their bodies, according to Illuminationist thinkers. As their master says in *The Philosophy of Illumination*, perfected souls that have left their bodies join the dominant lights, increasing the number of sacred lights without end.

    This also gives the beginning of a proof for God’s oneness. If these actual infinities were truly many, they would form numbers. Numbers are even or odd, and either way the chain would have to be finite—contrary to the claim that it is infinite. So the multiplicity here cannot exist in reality. — س ره

[^V02-317]: The Sayyid’s clarification may have three meanings. First, he may mean that an infinite chain toward effects is impossible in itself, as shown by proofs that the downward flow of existence ends in a vertical order, even if it continues across different kinds. But the arguments used against an infinite chain of causes—comparison, reciprocal relations, and different causal roles—would not apply to it. Second, he may mean that such a chain is possible in itself but has not occurred. Third, he may mean that it is possible in reality, which cannot be the point: if it were, the chain of simple beings would not end in basic matter, nor would the higher worlds end in the world of nature. Illuminationist philosophers say that the highest lights descend to a highest light that produces no further light below it, much as physical light reaches a point where it produces no further light because of distance and obstructions. Philosophers in Aristotle’s school say that the intellects end with the tenth intellect. The first interpretation also seems unlikely, because the proof establishes that the chain of simple substances ends; it does not show that a thing cannot have an essential property, which in turn has another essential property, without end. At most, such a chain has not occurred. The second interpretation therefore remains. — س ره

[^V02-318]: That is, the real cause and the means through which an effect exists. The proofs are the means of establishing that the chain is impossible. So there is no contradiction between saying that the chain does not exist in reality and assuming it for arguments such as one-to-one comparison. Those arguments begin by considering the possibility of infinitely many causes, each existing with its effect. — س ره

[^V02-319]: Just as a cause exists at its own level, which is prior to the effect, it also exists at the level of the effect itself. — س ره

[^V02-320]: The distinction is valid, but it does not prevent the proofs from applying to a descending chain of effects. We are discussing real causes, not causes that merely prepare the way for an effect. When a real cause that necessitates its effects exists, all those effects exist with it, since a true effect cannot be delayed beyond its true cause. Each thing may exist at its own level, but if those levels do not form a real ordered series of existence, then there is no chain in either direction. In that case, one-to-one comparison and the other proofs apply, because the individual things exist together when their causes do. I wonder why the author has said neither yes nor no. Perhaps he remained silent out of deference. God knows best what his servants mean. — س ره

[^V02-321]: An effect has no feature that is not also present in its cause, but the cause has features that the effect does not. What has been shown so far is that the effects in a descending chain do not all exist together; it has not been shown that they are not ordered, unless the author's point is that the proofs require both features at once. — س ره

[^V02-322]: This conclusion follows from everything said earlier, not only from the claim that form also counts as an efficient cause. That is why the author can speak of all the levels of causes. — س ره

[^V02-323]: In other words, matter can mean either what receives a form or what bears the potential for the thing whose matter it is. “Receiver” and “matter” can also refer either to what exists together with a form or to what exists before that form is present. — س ره

[^V02-324]: One might object that this assumes what it is trying to prove: the question is whether the parts of an essence—its matter and form—can be infinite. The reply is that this is not circular. The parts at issue in the conclusion are parts of the essence understood by the mind. Their being finite is already established, since otherwise the essence could not be understood, its definition could not be complete, and there could be no highest kind. — س ره

[^V02-325]: Neither kind of element, taken universally, needs matter in the sense of a bearer of potential; those general kinds are created directly. Only particular instances that arise in time need matter. There is no problem with an endless succession of material things here, because they occur one after another. — س ره

[^V02-326]: This proves that an endless chain is impossible only as it rises from what is less complete to what is more complete. It does not prove that an endless chain is impossible as it descends from the more complete to what came before it, as the previous clarification explained. — ط مده

[^V02-327]: By defining matter as the subject that bears a thing's possibility, the author makes clear that this division concerns matter that bears potential and comes before a thing in time, not prime matter, which receives form and is prior to it by nature. Otherwise, the claim that a new quality does not remove anything would not apply: prime matter receives a form that was not there before. The definition also rules out matter considered without any form at all. — س ره

[^V02-328]: This does not apply to successive levels of development that do not work by replacing one form with another. In these cases, matter takes on one form and then another; the plant and animal forms, for example, are substantial forms, not accidents. The deeper answer is that there are not several forms here, as discussed in the Journey of the Soul, but one form that grows stronger and moves from incompleteness toward perfection. — س ره

[^V02-329]: This is equivalent to saying, “or if it does not remain.” — س ره

[^V02-330]: A false belief, considered as a mental image and state of the soul, can perfect the soul, even though it fails to match the outside world. Its being harmful is a relative matter. This applies to blameworthy qualities too. The point follows from the view that evil is relative: nothing is evil in itself simply because it exists. — ط مده

[^V02-331]: The Arabic leaves out an implied word: “the judgment that change can be reversed applies only to this kind.” — س ره

[^V02-332]: Elemental forms remain present in compound things. — س ره

[^V02-333]: This is true if we imagine one simple element moving toward something opposed to it. But if it combines with another element that is active against it, and their powers are moderated, a balanced mixture arises. The mixture is not opposed to what it becomes. That is how things stand with compound kinds, so the claim in the main text can be challenged. — ط مده

[^V02-334]: Just as thinkers say that a mixture has a balanced quality after the elements have acted on one another—cooler than pure heat, warmer than pure cold, and so on—we say that, alongside the pure forms of the elements, the mixture has an intermediate substantial form. It is earth in relation to pure water and water in relation to pure earth, and likewise for the other elements. Just as pure earth is earth, this intermediate form is a degree of earth; the difference is one of greater or lesser intensity. The same is true of water, air, and fire. From this, the commentator draws the idea of substantial motion: substance itself can grow stronger or weaker. This account reconciles the view that the elemental forms remain with the view that they are shed: their pure, fully distinct forms do not remain, but their intermediate forms, unified in the mixture, do. That is why this is called an Eastern wisdom. — س ره

[^V02-335]: The feature must be something that also describes the active thing, as evenness describes the number four. This rules out an action that is not a feature of what it acts on, and a feature that is not an action, such as God's true attributes. A thing's being described by something is not always the same as receiving it. In the passive sense, receiving means that an outside cause gives a feature to a thing through matter, as water receives heat. Fire's being hot by nature is different. — س ره

[^V02-336]: If acting and receiving are both effects produced by an active and a receptive cause, this would make the receptive cause a kind of active cause, against the distinctions established earlier. If “effect” means only that a feature belongs to something, the argument against many effects from one simple cause does not apply. That argument uses “effect” in its proper sense of something caused by an agent. — ط مده

[^V02-337]: The claim is that existence exists in itself, unity is one in itself, and causing causes in itself. But if causing were a real feature, it would be something possible and would need a cause. Saying that it causes by its own nature does not remove that need, just as saying that an event begins by itself would not explain how it came to exist. — س ره

[^V02-338]: The speaker uses a noun where a pronoun is expected: “what it produces” instead of “what is produced by it.” The commentator finds this surprising, since being affected is itself a kind of receptivity. — س ره

[^V02-339]: This is even more surprising than the previous point. The fact that two ideas have different meanings does not explain why both are true of the thing being described. What matters is whether the thing itself has different aspects that make both descriptions true. An intellect can know itself without needing two separate aspects, but a thing that moves and is moved must differ in the relevant way. The same applies to claims about God's knowledge, power, life, and will: the truth of all these descriptions cannot be explained by saying that the concepts differ, since all of them describe the same single divine reality. — س ره

[^V02-340]: Even if this were granted, the thing would not be simple: it would act by virtue of its existence and receive by virtue of its essence. — س ره

[^V02-341]: It would be clearer to replace “necessity and possibility” with “actuality and potentiality.” A recipient has its effect only as a possibility: it lacks what it may receive. An active cause has its effect in actuality: it has the perfection it gives. If the source and recipient were one, the thing would both have and lack the same effect, which is impossible. Strictly speaking, necessity applies to a complete cause, not to every one of the four kinds of cause by itself. It applies directly to a cause whose only cause is the agent, such as an immaterial effect that follows from its own possibility and the active cause that is also its goal. — ط مده

[^V02-342]: As explained earlier, possibility in itself is a kind of lack, so it is not a necessary feature in the strict sense. There is no active and passive relation here unless “possibility” means that either of two outcomes remains open. — س ره

[^V02-343]: If these abstract attributes are only ideas in the mind, they do not need to be made or caused, just as the ideas of acting and being affected do not. — س ره

[^V02-344]: This is not the only argument for the point. The unity of God's real attributes with His essence is one of the best-known proofs among philosophers, as in *The Beginning and the Return* and other works. — س ره

[^V02-345]: The cause of bodies and their natures must be something separate from matter, not the powers, natural processes, or souls that work through matter. First, these can start natural movement but cannot give existence itself; only what is free from potentiality can give existence. Second, they are themselves in motion, and something in motion is between potentiality and actuality, so it cannot be the source of existence. Third, bodily powers affect things only through their position relative to matter. But the question here is what brings matter itself into existence, and position cannot relate an agent to something nonexistent or to prime matter. An immaterial cause is different in all these ways. — س ره

[^V02-346]: That is, the sources are thoughts or acts of knowing. This follows once we add that every immaterial thing is an intellect: it knows and can be known. — س ره

[^V02-347]: “Thought” here means a firm judgment, not a passing image. What tips the choice is the belief that the action serves some end. — س ره

[^V02-348]: This is close to the first example. In the second, the point is that thought gives rise to the will, or strong desire. In the first, thought makes one of two opposite actions more likely than the other. The fact that desire and resolve come between thought and movement does not matter: the argument is that no bodily tool intervenes. — س ره

[^V02-349]: There is room to challenge this example. We will return to it when discussing the six kinds of agent, God willing. — ط مد

[^V02-350]: If “general” has its usual meaning, the objection is clear. If it means that God's knowledge and will extend to everything, which seems to be what the author intends, the problem is that they relate equally to all particular things and do not by themselves single out a limited number of them. — س ره

[^V02-351]: This may mean a particular intellect, or intellect in general, depending on the view held by Aristotle’s followers that separate intellects do not directly know particular things outside themselves. — س ره

[^V02-352]: That is, its own possibility alone is enough. Although such a thing is particular because it cannot be shared by many, it is separate from matter, time, place, and the readiness of a material recipient. So eternal knowledge and will can grasp it. — س ره

[^V02-353]: In short, there is no problem with eternal will relating to things created without matter or brought into being directly. Nor is there a problem with its relating to things that begin in time, because the complete cause of every new event includes both an enduring first principle and a new condition. — س ره

[^V02-354]: The two motions are alike, though their subjects—the surface of the celestial sphere and the surface of fire—are different. Both can receive motion that is faster or slower. The commentator points to the rising and setting of comets and similar heavenly bodies. — س ره

[^V02-355]: The subjects differ in kind, even though the Sun and Moon are both celestial bodies. Each heavenly kind has only one individual. — س ره

[^V02-356]: Perhaps Avicenna bases his view on the claim that, for these philosophers, strength and weakness belong to qualities, while existence is neither a property belonging to something else nor a substance existing in its own right. But the author’s reading seems better, since Avicenna describes existence as stronger or weaker. His phrase “existence as existence” alone does not prove that he meant the mind’s general idea of existence. — س ره

[^V02-357]: Some thinkers deny that a complete cause comes before its effect because the cause’s matter and form are present with the compound effect and seem to be the effect itself. The reply is that matter and form are counted as parts of the cause, not as parts of the single effect. The effect considered on its own is one thing: the form that makes it what it is. So the matter and form in the compound do not make the cause and effect identical, as the objection assumes. Once the simple form exists, the compound made from it and its nearby matter follows at the same level, gathering in a higher way the perfections already present in the form. The compound exists as a by-product of the form and needs no new cause of its own. This explains how the complete cause comes before the effect in its own right, while existing alongside the compound that follows from it only as a by-product. The commentator says this also helps with the question of resurrection. He adds that there is no need to use al-Lāhījī’s different but reasonable answer: the cause comes first as all its parts taken together, while the effect comes after when those same parts are joined. — س ره

[^V02-358]: The form is free from having to carry out material, earthly actions, but not from the immaterial forms of those actions in the afterlife—such as a body made entirely of form, sensing, nourishment, and sexual union. These follow from it by nature, even though it does not need them, just as a mature plant no longer needs to grow. The reason is that a simple intellect contains, in a higher way, the realities of the lower powers. It directly knows what is seen, heard, and otherwise sensed, without sense organs, and acts with full power, without bodily movement-powers. This applies to the simple intellect; other cases differ, as noted earlier. — س ره

[^V02-359]: Philosophers agree that there are four kinds of cause. They argue for goals as causes in their discussion of ends, and for matter and form in their discussion of matter and form. They treat active causes as self-evident. Some natural scientists today build their work on the claim that matter is the only cause. They therefore accuse philosophers of believing in chance, since philosophers say that the world depends on an agent without matter—God. The commentator says the philosophers are right. When physical things interact, their active and receptive effects differ. If fire ignites cotton, the fire contributes one effect and the cotton another. Fire’s effect is already present in it and passes to the cotton, though not like a liquid being poured out. The cotton’s effect is not present before the two meet; it receives that effect from the fire. This is how physical events work in general. Matter can therefore be seen in two ways: as something that has an effect and gives it to something else, or as something that lacks the effect and receives it. The first is an active cause; the second is a receptive cause. — ط مده

[^V02-360]: This kind of cause is called luck or chance. It occurs in the world of physical things, not in the universal order of creation. Luck and chance apply only when we look at a small part of the order, not at the whole order of the universe. — ن ره

[^V02-361]: Here matter means the bearer of a thing’s potential, as explained earlier. — ن ره

[^V02-362]: This applies to voluntary actions, which depend on a prior thought that includes belief in a goal. The author calls this a goal in a loose sense. Strictly speaking, a goal is the perfection an action reaches or the perfection the agent wants, and for which the action is done. — ط مده

[^V02-363]: The careful thinker seems to mean by “simple” that the thing is not made of distinct mental parts such as matter and form. On this reading, his argument has a problem: philosophers agree that accidents are simple in the outside world, and each kind of thing causes the accidents that belong to it. That cause would either be simple, which would make the kind eternal, or compound, which would mean that a simple accident comes from a compound cause. But the author later says that the soul is not simple. This suggests that he uses “simple” to mean “not made of distinct parts that exist”—whether those parts are in the mind or outside it. On that reading, the objection from accidents fails, because accidents depend on a subject for their existence, just as the soul depends on the body. A further problem remains: the parts and combinations of things that arise in nature must eventually end in simple things. Otherwise, the chain of parts would never reach anything that could exist, and new things would be made from eternal matter and forms—an impossible result. The author later answers that the coming into being of the formal part is built into its nature, so it needs no cause. But if this depends on the view that substance itself is in motion, it does not prove either that the cause of a new thing is compound or that every new thing is compound. — ط مده

[^V02-364]: The compound cause is made in thought from two parts: one lasting part, the unchanging face of God that shines on every matter ready to receive it—or the separate intellect that lasts through God’s lasting, one of His active names and attributes, such as the Tenth Intellect that brings about compounds in this world—and one new part, a portion of the turning motion of the heavenly sphere. — س ره

[^V02-365]: The commentator suggests a better reply: a part of a newly formed thing is not a separate new thing that needs its own separate cause; the cause of the whole compound is enough. The author’s claim that coming into being is built into the part’s nature, and so needs no cause, works only if “coming into being” means continuous self-renewal. It does not work if it means beginning to exist after not existing. A part of a new thing comes into being in that second sense too, so something must determine that it begins to exist. That cause also begins to exist, and so on without end. — س ره

[^V02-366]: That is, motion like a change in place. The motion of nature is also a kind of motion, but it is motion in substance itself. The two accounts agree, except that the first locates the conditions for coming into being in portions of the heavenly sphere’s changing position, while the author locates them in portions of the continuously flowing celestial nature that keeps time. Other natures do not continue in this way. — س ره

[^V02-367]: This is the Illuminationist Shaykh, who discusses the argument in his book *The Wisdom of Illumination*. For him, each whole exists as a unity over and above its individual parts. He therefore allows some intellects to come from a group of other intellects, and some to come from a whole made up of two aspects, such as two features within the intellects. His view will be explained later. The cause of every affected being has a real unity. One might object that if the simple effect begins to exist after not existing, and if, as already stated, a simple new thing cannot come from a compound, this would lead to an endless chain. The reply is that the Active Intellect is its active cause, the source from which it receives existence: it is one and simple, but it acts under an outside condition, namely a portion of the heavenly motion. The Shaykh’s point is that the active cause cannot itself be compound, a whole made up of two or more things. He says the whole is the cause, not each part on its own, even though the coming together of the parts does not itself exist as a thing. The complete cause of a new event, however, may be compound—for example, something lasting together with a new condition. Other philosophers say the complete cause of a new event is a group of conditions, some of which are absences, such as removing an obstacle or preparing matter. The author has also cited the writer of *The Mawāqif*, who treats removing an obstacle as the presence of something. A true unity in the world of events, though simple in one respect, may also be compound in another, as the author will explain. — س ره

[^V02-368]: The commentator groups the chapter’s claims this way: a possible thing must exist when its complete cause is present and cannot exist when that cause is absent; without either condition, it remains possible in itself. A cause need not coincide in time with the effect’s earlier nonexistence. Coming into being after nonexistence is part of the nature of what comes into being, just as possibility is part of an essence. These features are not separately made by the agent. Nor does being new explain why a thing needs a cause; what makes something need a cause must itself be dependent in its own nature. — س ره

[^V02-369]: In other words, matter serves as an intermediary through its readiness to receive a form, and this role of matter explains why the result begins in time. — ن ره

[^V02-370]: In the earlier case, two opposites—being new and being eternal—were combined in one respect. Here they are combined in two different respects. Yet this still means combining being necessary or impossible in itself with coming into being in time. — س ره

[^V02-371]: This is why some say that the necessary features of something brought into being are caused only indirectly: they follow once the thing exists, but are not separately made. This kind of necessity differs from absolute necessity, which is one of the alternatives to possibility. — ن ره

[^V02-372]: Just as it is wrong to say that a thing depends on a cause only because it is new, it is wrong to deny that being new belongs to the thing itself, and wrong to say that being new is what makes it depend on a cause. — س ره

[^V02-373]: This refers to the philosophers’ view that a thing depends on a cause because its essence is possible. That view is right in one respect. The author’s stronger claim is that existence itself is what makes a thing depend on a cause: existence is both what depends on the cause and the basis of that dependence. — ن ره

[^V02-374]: The commentator explains two points. First, possibility can show that an essence depends on a cause, but it is not the real source of that dependence. Second, on the author’s view, existence itself can be the source of an essence’s dependence. The objection says that existence depends on being brought about. The reply is that real existence—not the idea or act of “making”—is itself a relation and dependence, and this belongs to existence in itself; it does not need another cause. The idea of producing something does not produce anything. A thing comes into being through real existence, which removes nonexistence. Considered apart from particular essences, existence depends on God. Considered as appearing in particular essences, each essence exists through its own act of real existence. This is the existence brought about by the cause, not the idea of bringing it about. — س ره

[^V02-375]: Being the source of one thing is not the same as being the source of what is not that thing. This difference in sourcehood is why the cause would seem to have two distinct aspects. — ن ره

[^V02-376]: Fire’s specific form is the feature that makes it cause heat. Water has a different feature, the power to cool. These powers are additional to the matter and bodily form that fire and water share as bodies. If the heating or cooling power existed on its own, rather than in matter, that feature would be the thing’s own reality. — س ره

[^V02-377]: A simple effect is truly simple, but it does not have to be as simple as its cause. — ن ره

[^V02-378]: One reality can be joined to one thing and described as one of its parts, or joined to another thing and described as a part of that. That is different from the case here, where the difference is said to belong to the cause itself. It would be relevant only if the difference came from a relation that appeared after the joining. — ن ره

[^V02-379]: If we mean unity in general, there is still just one unity; a pair is made by repeating “one.” If we mean a particular unit, each pair has another unit alongside it, so the pairs differ without the shared unit becoming two. A point can likewise be the endpoint of many lines because each line meets it from a different direction. The point is not made of infinitely many parts; these are different ways the mind considers it. — س ره

[^V02-380]: The commentator disagrees with the author’s claim that contradictory descriptions cannot apply to one thing even from different aspects. In the usual account, a contradiction requires the same subject, time, and respect. “Zayd is learned through his soul but not through his body” is not a contradiction, because the descriptions concern different aspects. But a kind exists if any one of its instances exists; to say the kind does not exist, all its instances must be absent. So denying a feature at one level does not always mean the whole thing lacks it. Zayd is not at rest simply because he is not moving in the marketplace if he is moving at home. — س ره

[^V02-381]: A subject that bears changing features is described by them only indirectly; the feature itself is what is truly described. In that way, different features can be attributed to one underlying subject without contradiction. — ن ره

[^V02-382]: Otherwise, an affirmation would be the same as a denial. A thing, considered in itself, is only what it is. — س ره

[^V02-383]: That is, the claim concerns a description that applies to the thing itself. If the description applies only indirectly, the result may differ. — س ره

[^V02-384]: Two descriptions may contradict each other when applied directly to a thing, yet not when they describe features that coexist in it. Black and nonblack directly contradict each other. But blackness and motion can both be present in ebony; motion is not black. So these features differ in what they are, but they are not opposites as descriptions of what inheres in the same subject. — س ره

[^V02-385]: Here “its absence” means that the relation or state is absent, not that the thing itself does not exist. The opposite of “this thing is produced by a cause” is that it is not produced by that cause; it is not the separate description “this thing is not produced.” The same applies to existence and nonexistence. This does not affect the author’s proof, which treats sourcehood as part of the simple cause’s own reality, not as a relation that appears after cause and effect exist. The author says he will explain this distinction further later. — س ره

[^V02-386]: An effect in its own right is, by its very being, connected to its cause. A relation changes when one of its ends changes. So for the same specific effect to depend in its own right on different causes would mean that the relation changed while the effect remained the same. An effect that belongs to a general kind is different. It can come from many causes because the kind exists through many particular examples, as with the elements. — ن ره

[^V02-387]: The wording is brief. The intended meaning is that a special feature of one cause or of the other may help bring about the effect. If one feature acts on its own, the effect cannot also come from the other cause on its own. If both features contribute, then both causes are needed together. — س ره

[^V02-388]: This holds even when the effect must exist through both causes together, because together they form one complete cause. — س ره

[^V02-389]: Avicenna’s wording is condensed. “Form in general” means a substantial form fixed at the first level but still open to more specific forms. He is not speaking about one fully specified form, such as the form of a particular kind. — ن ره

[^V02-390]: “Possibility” here may mean dependence or need, which follows actual existence. Or it may mean that a thing’s nature is equally able to exist or not exist. In that second sense, possibility is simply the absence of necessity, so it is not an effect that a cause brings about. — س ره

[^V02-391]: Someone might object that difference belongs to different things by their own nature, and that such a feature is not made. The maker does not make white white or black black, nor make them different; they differ by their own natures. We reply that an essential feature needs no maker other than the thing that has it. That does not mean it needs no maker at all. The author’s point is that differences in existence—by kind or by number—come from the natures that have existence. Existence itself becomes many because the things that exist are many, not because existence is divided in itself. — س ره

[^V02-392]: The effect’s being possible means that it needs some cause. What makes it depend on one particular cause is that cause’s own special nature. This feature comes before the effect, since the effect does not yet exist. The effect’s general need for a cause does not by itself tell us which cause it needs. — س ره

[^V02-393]: “Preparer” here is used in its everyday sense, not as a strict technical term. In technical usage, a preparer is something that must first exist and then cease to exist for the effect to occur. A natural philosopher’s agent, by contrast, must exist at the same time as its effect; most people use the word in the same way. For example, when listing the causes of a bed, they call the carpenter its active cause. The carpenter truly is one cause of the bed, alongside the cutting of the wood, the bed’s shape, and its use for sitting. The same applies to a builder and the wood, bricks, and clay that make up a house, its shape, and its use as shelter. Still, these workers are active causes only in the sense used by natural philosophers, who focus on motion and change. They call the cause the source that changes or moves existing matter. The philosopher who asks about existence looks for what gives being: what brings something out of nonexistence, and gives it matter, form, action, and purpose. The carpenter and builder move matter that they did not create, toward a form that God gives it. For the philosopher of existence, God is the true giver of being. — س ره

[^V02-394]: The person with this mistaken belief has no sense at all of the world’s constant dependence on God, and is far from belief in one God. The person who holds the opposite view has found the spirit of nearness—or at least the fragrance of hope. — س ره

[^V02-395]: How could it be otherwise? God is the very foundation of the world’s existence, and the world’s existence is His manifestation. It is not separate from Him in a way that would let it stand apart. If the foundation were removed, what depends on it would not remain. — س ره

[^V02-396]: The first verse points to God’s preserving and sustaining the heavens and the earth. The second points to their destruction and passing away. The source presents the ideas in this order. — ن ره

[^V02-397]: Taken without qualification, “neither is necessary” is not true of reality, because denying a general necessity denies all its individual cases. But it is true at a particular level of reality. If Zayd is moving in the marketplace, for example, he is not moving in the house. It is therefore true to say that he is at rest there, even if he is not moving at all. So the claim that possibility has vanished from the argument does not follow. — س ره

[^V02-398]: This is one case of the same general rule. The comparison may turn on the difference between describing something in relation to another thing and describing it in itself. — س ره

[^V02-399]: Someone might object: if an agent acts because of its existence, then the agent must be God, which is the claim we are trying to prove. But if it acts because of existence added to the essence of a possible thing, then possibility helps give existence. The reply is that there is a third option: the agent acts because of the particular existence it directly brings about. Existence has levels. The existence caused in a possible thing is not the same as the complete existence that has no cause. The distinction makes the point clearer, even though existence is not really divided into separate kinds. — س ره

[^V02-400]: The pronoun refers to form. As Avicenna said earlier, a form in general is a condition for the Active Intellect’s effect, whether the effect is in matter or elsewhere. It cannot refer to prime matter, which can receive an effect but cannot produce one. Nor can bodily form itself produce an effect; active power belongs to natures and specific forms. The note may also point to a disagreement: philosophers in Aristotle’s school say that forms joined to bodies—powers and natural qualities—can act, while Illuminationist philosophers say that effects come from separate forms, the luminous exemplars. — س ره

[^V02-401]: The author has already raised several objections: possibility is not real in the same way as existence; it may be involved only as a condition, not as a part of the cause; and a form may act without matter being the direct agent. He now points out that these philosophers themselves give possibility a role in the way the celestial spheres come from intellects. Since possibility is a negative feature, how can they keep it from sharing in the giving of existence? They can answer that the intellect causes the sphere’s nature, not its existence. The sphere’s existence is made necessary by the intellect, together with its nature and its limited possibility. The intellect, as a light that receives its being from God, gives rise to the next intellect. What is lower comes from what is lower; what is higher comes from what is higher. — س ره

[^V02-402]: This note answers the argument by pointing out that existence is real, while essence and possibility are ideas the mind forms about it. Since possibility is only an idea added to the real nature of a possible thing, nonexistence does not help bring anything into being. And since existence has different levels, it is fair to speak of agents of existence other than God: these are the different levels of existence. — س ره

[^V02-403]: More precisely, the First Agent cannot have any extra matter or goal, either in its unqualified action or in its action toward the first effect. Since the same reality gives it both its own being and its power to act, what belongs to it in this respect is not something separate from it. The same applies to the other features. — س ره

[^V02-404]: The point is that fire burns while it is hot, and the sun gives light while it is moving. Heat and motion may necessarily belong to fire and the sun, but that necessity is not eternal. — س ره

[^V02-405]: The actions we see in the world fall into two broad groups. In the first, knowledge has no role in producing the action, as with the actions of natural forces. In the second, knowledge does play a role, as when an animal walks. The first group may fit the agent’s nature; this is action by nature. Or it may go against the agent’s nature; this is action by force, as when a heavy body moves upward.

    When an agent’s knowledge helps produce its action, that knowledge is part of the kind of being it is. It allows the agent to recognize what fulfills its nature and to choose it over what does not. An animal seeking food moves toward something it sees. If it finds food, it eats; if it finds a stone, it leaves it. If the animal already knows an action is good for it, it does not need to think further. But if it is unsure, it compares the thing’s features with what would fulfill it. Once it decides the action is good—or decides that it is not—it acts or leaves it alone. This turn toward acting or not acting is what we call choice. Deliberation helps the agent reach a judgment and end its uncertainty. If the judgment is clear from the start, no deliberation is needed. The agent chooses what it already knows to be good, as a speaker says each letter of a sentence without stopping to consider whether to speak each letter or remain silent. As soon as the agent reaches a clear judgment, whether by thinking or without thinking, it acts without delay.

    This shows that voluntary action and compelled action are not two different kinds of action in the world. So we should not divide agents that act with knowledge into those that act by choice and those that act under compulsion, as if their actions worked in completely different ways. A person who compels someone makes one option seem impossible. The person then chooses the other option because they believe it is the only one left. Without the threat, they might have chosen the same option for some other reason. In both cases, the person’s will and choice work in the same way; the difference is in how we describe the situation. In ordinary choice, the person believes they cannot refuse because of some reason they accept. Under compulsion, the reason is a threat or some other pressure that makes refusal seem impossible. For example, someone who moves away from a wall that is about to fall on them acts in the same way as someone who moves because a tyrant threatens to drop the wall on them unless they do. In both cases, the person believes they must move, and that belief leads them to choose the action.

    Society still distinguishes between these cases to protect people and enforce its laws, which bring reward, punishment, praise, and blame. But this is a practical distinction, not a deep difference in how the actions themselves work. Compulsion does not erase the agent’s will; in either case, the action still involves the agent’s will.

    All this concerns agents whose actions depend on the ordinary, mental kind of knowledge. The author also lists two more kinds: acting through satisfaction and acting through providence. He will question the second kind later. Strictly speaking, there are four kinds if we leave aside both compelled action and providence. But the author’s account of God’s knowledge requires another kind. He seems to include it under providence, because the action comes from knowledge alone, without an added motive. The difference is that this knowledge is direct awareness, not a picture or idea in the mind. Some call this way of acting self-disclosure.

    I say: we can sort the kinds of agency by asking first whether the agent knows what it does. If not, its action either fits its nature or goes against it: these are action by nature and action by force. If the agent does know what it does, ask whether the action is chosen. If it is not, the agent acts under compulsion. If it is chosen, ask whether the agent’s knowledge of its action is the same as its action and comes from its knowledge of itself; if so, it acts through satisfaction. Otherwise, the agent knows the action in detail before it happens. If that knowledge is joined to an added motive, the agent acts through intention. If the knowledge itself produces the effect, then either the knowledge is different from the agent’s being, in which case it acts through providence, or it is identical with its being and fully reveals the effect, in which case it acts through self-disclosure. I have also explained this way of dividing the kinds in my poem *Gems of Insight*. Anyone who wishes to remember it can turn to that poem. — س ره

[^V02-406]: It is doubtful that acting through providence differs from acting through intention. The same idea of falling is present in someone who is frightened and loses their balance on a high beam and in someone accustomed to standing on high buildings or walls who does not fall. If the idea alone caused the action, both would fall. It seems instead to be intentional action. The person on a high wall must choose either to stay or to fall. Fear and shock may hold their attention on the thought of falling, so that no other option remains in their mind; they fall. The person who is used to being there has both options in mind and chooses to stay. Similar things happen when someone is terrified by danger: they may see only the option of escape and run without thinking, or see only the danger and throw themselves into it, as when someone lets a wild beast attack. — ط مده

[^V02-407]: This is why people are sometimes said to be compelled while seeming to choose, and God to choose while seeming to be compelled. God’s choice makes one of the possible outcomes necessary, but His choice itself is not forced. — س ره

[^V02-408]: The soul does not perceive its faculties by receiving images of them inside those faculties; otherwise the faculties would perceive themselves, and two identical images would have to exist together. Nor does it perceive them by receiving their images inside itself, since then it would perceive only general ideas, while it uses particular powers. — س ره

[^V02-409]: This example is meant to show only that knowledge can cause an action. It does not mean that knowing what action one is about to perform causes it. In the example, the soul knows that something is sour, and the action is the tongue’s tightening and squeezing. — س ره

[^V02-410]: “Reasons” here means the proofs used to establish a feature. They are called the “middle” in an argument because they are joined to the conclusion by the word “because.” Most features, apart from basic possibility, need to be proved. On the common view that things have an essence, possibility is basic too. On the view that they have no essence, possibility means dependence on the Necessary Being. The author does not mean that we must think about matter and a subject in order to understand that the soul is independent, or about nonexistence and what comes before and after in order to understand beginning and continuing. Those ideas are already part of what these qualities mean. — س ره

[^V02-411]: The soul’s qualities are its own existence, since they become actual in the soul itself. The one who perceives is joined with what is perceived in the act of awareness. What the soul imagines, senses, and pictures within itself also begins in it, and is either in harmony with it or against it. The soul contains its powers in two ways: as a complete whole that holds many parts together, and as a whole present within each of its parts. — س ره

[^V02-412]: “They forgot God, so He made them forget themselves.” Though the soul knows itself directly, this knowledge has levels, from broad and unclear to detailed and clear. There are also degrees of direct insight, reaching all the way to certainty through direct experience. Think of people looking at a fully formed human being from very far away, from very close, or from somewhere in between. Some see through dusty air, others through clear air. Some can make out the person’s qualities and way of life, others cannot. All of them see, but each sees to a different degree. The soul’s direct knowledge of itself also has levels: from the infant’s awareness, through reason in full use and the active intellect, to self-effacement before God. All these are forms of direct awareness. — س ره

[^V02-413]: Pure potentiality has no feature that distinguishes one kind from another. Saying that the first matter of the heavens differs in kind from the shared matter of the elements is like saying that simple, particular existences differ wholly by their own nature, as the philosophers in Aristotle’s school are said to claim. But first matter is simple, and there is no basis in it for a division into kinds: the general kind would have to include the very feature that distinguishes each kind, while that feature would itself have to be included in the general kind. Such a distinction is hard to make sense of even in things that are fully actual and distinct. It makes even less sense in first matter, which is pure potential. Potentiality, simply as potentiality, has no distinguishing feature, just as nonexistence has none. — س ره

[^V02-414]: The philosophers set these distinctions aside more often than they observe them. — س ره

[^V02-415]: “One in a general sense” means matter can be considered as the broadest kind when we do not specify a form. Considered in itself, however, it is one particular thing. It remains the same material through changes. When philosophers say that its unity is general and open-ended, they mean it is pure potential and can take on any form, or that it has levels that do not destroy its unity as one particular thing. This is why they say the water in two bowls came from the same pitcher, not from the sea: in the first case, a particular level or source is preserved; in the second, it is not. — س ره

[^V02-416]: Why could matter not move itself, given that substantial change is possible? — س ره

[^V02-417]: The disagreement is not only about longing, which includes lacking something in one way; it is about love more broadly. Those who affirm this teaching affirm both love and longing, so an opponent must reject both. Ibn Sina’s argument does not rule out love, since love can exist alongside possession. The philosophers say that the First Cause loves itself and that separate intellects love the higher lights. Saying that matter loves form does not conflict with Ibn Sina’s argument. His own treatise on love uses the word “longing,” so the commentator says that the two positions contradict each other. — س ره

[^V02-418]: The text should also say “or earthly souls, human and jinn”; this phrase may have been dropped by a copyist. — س ره

[^V02-419]: Matter’s love can also be explained this way. The verse says, “Wherever you turn, there is the Face of God.” His Face appears in the throne of intellect and in the lowly world of matter. The face of the One is one wherever it appears. So the light in matter is the same light that appears in intellect. The single face present throughout creation is God’s creative will and love, which contains the will and love of created things, but not their individual selves or the features that belong to them. This will and love has one nature in every lover and beloved; only the degree of its appearance differs. — س ره

[^V02-420]: Existence has no parts, and it has no separate instances. What makes its levels different is the same as what they share. The existence in matter, simply as existence and as the subject of philosophy, is the same as the existence in beings that know and feel. So the love and longing in matter are real, not merely comparisons. Matter’s existence is weak because it is only the potential for existence. But potential existence is still existence when compared with absolute nonexistence, just as a shadow is light when compared with total darkness. The same is true of matter’s love and longing. Someone might say that the love belongs to matter’s existence, not to matter itself. But every possible thing is made of two aspects: its bright aspect, which is its existence, and its dark aspect, which is its nature considered apart from existence. The existence of matter is basic. So it is true to say that matter’s existence loves itself, that it loves form, or that it loves the First Cause through the existence it receives from Him. Existence is one in kind, and all things praise God. — س ره

[^V02-421]: Matter is the source of longing because it is the potential part of things. Its potential comes from the limitations of souls and natural kinds in the downward order of existence, just as matter’s readiness to receive forms comes from the basic possibility in the Active Intellect. Basic possibility belongs to souls and natural kinds in the higher, unified world; first matter and its readiness to receive forms belong to souls and natural kinds in the lower, changing world. Their changing powers and readiness, and their continuous motions, are their desires and efforts. They are mixed with lack in one way and possession in another because they are joined to matter.

    Without matter, nothing would have the potential to move toward completion, and no action or desire—whether natural or belonging to a soul—could take place. Imagined forms without matter cannot act on one another, mix, join, or rise toward God as earthly matter does. Nor can they move or long for the lasting truth as heavenly matter does. Matter is the source of longing in all who long. How could it have no longing, when it is longing itself? — س ره

[^V02-422]: The wording means “to refute the denied premise.” The source leaves out the word “premise,” or uses a descriptive phrase in its place. I have supplied it because Ibn Sina is the one denying the premise, not the one proving it. — س ره

[^V02-423]: Philosophers can make sense of an afterlife, whether bodily or spiritual, only if they accept both series of desire described here. Without both, they cannot explain the next life. Those who accept only an endless series of changes over time cannot prove an afterlife without simply relying on religious teaching. — ن ره

[^V02-424]: This refers to the passage about matter longing for the completion of the kind of form it already has, and to the later passage about rising through successive perfections. The writer will later quote Ibn Sina on two kinds of change: one form may replace another, or a thing may grow by adding one level of perfection to another, as a child becomes an adult, then a purified soul, then an intellect. The second kind is the upward series described here. — س ره

[^V02-425]: The objection is that belief in an afterlife cannot fit with an endless series of changing conditions: if every goal has another goal after it, there is no final goal or next life. The answer is that the upward series of perfection does have an end, even if the horizontal series of changing forms does not. This is one of the difficult points in philosophy of God. — ن ره

[^V02-426]: Love helps every being hold on to the first and second levels of perfection it has, and seek the further perfections it lacks. The order of souls and other knowing beings is held together by longing and fear. Fear also serves love, because avoiding harm protects what one loves—oneself. Every being loves its own nature and inner reality. Intellect loves and is loved by itself, and its love exists in itself, just as its separate existence, will, and knowledge exist in themselves, without a material subject. These qualities are distinct from its nature considered as an abstract idea. God has no matter, even in the broadest sense, and has no abstract nature apart from His existence. That is why the Second Teacher says that there must be knowledge that exists in itself and will that exists in itself; otherwise, there could be no knowledge or will that depended on another thing. Love and longing are therefore a good guardian and a close attendant, entrusted with the world. Some have said: when love is complete, it is God. — س ره

[^V02-427]: A word can have several separate meanings in technical philosophy even if each meaning shares a broad idea with the others. A language scholar asks whether the same concept applies to two things at all. A philosopher asks whether they share the same defining features. If one word is used for two things that each have their own defining features, philosophers treat the word as having two separate meanings. For example, “form” can mean the thing that receives a quality, such as a body that receives blackness; the form that makes matter actual; or a quality itself. These are different meanings, even though each has to do with making something actual. “Matter” and related terms work in the same way. — ط مده

[^V02-428]: As with the word “possibility” near the start of the book, “nature” can be used as a name for different things. This does not mean that exactly the same feature exists in each one. It is like giving someone the nickname “Black.” — س ره

[^V02-429]: By “the second answer,” the author means the first of the two answers left after he has ruled out the first one.

[^V02-430]: At first, the author’s choice seems to conflict with his later account of substantial change, where a form makes matter more complete through gradual growth rather than by replacing one form with another. In that account, an earlier level of actuality becomes the matter and potential for a later level. An actuality resists another actuality only when both compete at the same level. A form that is complete in one relation can still be matter, or potential, in relation to a higher form. So earlier levels remain within the later form, though they are folded into it. The commentator says the three views may be brought together in this way. — ط مده

[^V02-431]: The one true form is the final form, which gathers its earlier stages into one. The other forms count as separate branches or conditions only when we look at them as many. On the author’s account of substantial change, they are stages of one continuing form, each becoming the next while an underlying reality remains. This is how he understands unity, rather than as a collection of separate forms. — س ره

[^V02-432]: The rational soul that makes a human being what it is does not differ from the powers that came before it. Those powers are early expressions of the soul and are contained within it. Here, “making something what it is” means bringing it into actuality and completing it, as a form makes matter actual. — س ره

[^V02-433]: It would be clearer to say that a definition of a human being as a substance that can have dimensions, grow, sense, move by choice, and reason is not adding something outside the thing being defined. Nor is it listing mere conditions or branches. It is listing the parts or levels of the human being when each is considered on its own. When considered together, they form one simple reality. — س ره

[^V02-434]: The movement may have a goal in relation to another movement—for example, Zayd may go from home to a gym to exercise—or in relation to the power of desire, rather than the moving power itself. The moving power’s goal is always the point to which it moves, as with natural movement. Otherwise every movement would be a kind of seeking, whether natural or voluntary, and seeking itself is never the thing sought. — س ره

[^V02-435]: When we consider the active moving power by itself, apart from desire and perception, it is like the natural powers. We may call it the muscular power, or the natural mover found in simple things. In either case, the only thing sought through its movement is the endpoint. — س ره

[^V02-436]: Avicenna also uses “or” here to mean “and.” Otherwise, mere imagination without thought would not differ from aimless action as described by the critics. In this technical sense, aimless action has no rational, thought-based source and so no rational goal. The point reached by the movement—which is always the goal of the active power—is also a goal of imagination and desire. If the active power and imaginative desire do not share the same endpoint, and there is no rational desire, the source of the action must be imagination alone or imagination joined with another source. — س ره

[^V02-437]: I do not like this term. It would be better to call the action a failure in relation to that goal, since what is void does not exist, while this action does. — س ره

[^V02-438]: Democritus held that his tiny bodies shared the same nature, and argued for this from the fact that all bodies have three dimensions. This does not mean that the word “body” is used for several unrelated things, or that one idea points to different real natures. He explained the tiny bodies’ different shapes by denying qualities we feel through touch. He said the tiny bodies in fire are triangles with sharp points that pierce the skin and seem hot; the others are cubes, squares, or spheres, as he thought their simple nature required. He also believed in empty space. All of this is false. — س ره

[^V02-439]: Once the heavenly spheres and planets came together, their positions and rays produced these formations for a purpose. So they did not arise by chance—that is, without an agent, without a goal, or without both. — س ره

[^V02-440]: Empedocles did not claim that the heavenly spheres or the basic elements came about by chance, because he regarded them as brought into being directly. He may have meant that strange features and accidental changes in compound things happen by chance. Some Muslim scholars also called the world of growth and decay “the realm of chance” in this sense. — س ره

[^V02-441]: The mind rejects this claim because “chance” means either that an event has no agent or that it has no goal. Both claims conflict with reality: events have agents and goals. The possibility that something could be otherwise is what makes it need a cause, and a goal is a cause that moves an agent to act. Chance also refers to what is unexpected and rare, while events that happen always or most of the time are not rare. — س ره

[^V02-442]: Necessity rules out chance. No one would say it happened by chance that four is an even number. The material in an embryo’s hand, when it meets every condition for forming an extra finger and the active power acts on it, stands to that extra finger as four stands to evenness. The discussion here concerns goals; the existence of active causes has already been settled elsewhere. It has been shown that when two alternatives are equal, neither occurs until an outside factor tips the balance. At the level of what a thing is, neither of two opposites applies to it. Once causes necessarily bring about their effects, how can the result be by chance? — س ره

[^V02-443]: A Persian couplet says: “Speak of the One; turn from all the rest. Keep one heart, and turn your face toward one goal.” — س ره

[^V02-444]: The words “As for the obstacles” may have been omitted from the original manuscript. The same point applies to obstacles. The author may have thought that the following discussion of withering covered this second case, since obstacles also amount to something missing. — س ره

[^V02-445]: This is the point mentioned earlier: everything has its place in the order of the whole, and the whole order serves as a goal for the causes at its beginning. — س ره

[^V02-446]: If “heavenly arrangements” means physical positions, these are the immediate causes. The deeper causes are the higher souls that move the heavens, especially the sun’s soul, and the higher intellects that guide their movements toward their goals. God encompasses the whole order from beginning to end. Rain is an incidental outcome, not something forced by matter. — س ره

[^V02-447]: The cause that ultimately tips the balance is with God, since He is the true agent. The commentator explained this in the opening of his notes. — س ره

[^V02-448]: This raises an obvious objection: does this not rule out an outcome being favored without a reason, rather than ruling out choice itself? We reply that choosing means one option becomes more likely than another. If one of two equal options becomes more likely because of a further choice, the same question applies to that choice, and the chain goes on forever. If there is no further choice, then one option becomes more likely for no reason. Also, since the goal is what moves an agent to act, without a goal that favors one outcome the agent is not actually acting. To say there is an agent while denying what makes it act is to affirm and deny the same thing. — س ره

[^V02-449]: The theologians say that will stands to power as necessity stands to possibility. They miss that will makes an action necessary only because of a prior motive: the judgment that the action will be useful. Will does not do this by itself. — س ره

[^V02-450]: That is, the two alternatives are equal in reality, and one is singled out only on the opponent’s assumption. — س ره

[^V02-451]: This argument conflicts with the one before it. The first assumes that will is directed toward only one thing; this one assumes that will relates equally to several things. — س ره

[^V02-452]: Here, “rather” corrects what came before; it does not mean “yes.” — س ره

[^V02-453]: If someone asks what tips the balance in favor of this particular motion, the question returns to the second argument. The two points are therefore not separate arguments but one. — س ره

[^V02-454]: As we have learned, each cause has a particular relation to its particular effect. That relation rules out the effect taking a different form. — س ره

[^V02-455]: The commentator says he has not seen the treatise mentioned here. He thinks it may refer to arguments in *The Throne of Divine Wisdom* and elsewhere: particular features may belong to the identity of an individual thing, even though they are accidental to its general kind. The objections may confuse the individual with the general, or treat what necessarily follows from a thing as if it had to be caused separately. For example, someone might ask why the letter alif is straight and dal is curved, without seeing that an alif without straightness would not be an alif, nor a dal without its curve a dal. These features need no separate cause. If the question is about the letter in general, it has no separate existence; the question comes back to why alif is alif and dal is dal, though what belongs to a thing’s nature needs no cause. This is the point behind the saying that the blessed was blessed from eternity and the wretched was wretched forever. — س ره

[^V02-456]: If someone found two equally strong motives, they would stop and not act. Even a donkey does not move toward water or fodder until it sees them; its desire favors movement over rest only when there is a reason to do so. — س ره

[^V02-457]: Someone might ask how this fits with the sacred saying that God created creation so that He might be known, and the Qur'anic verse “I created jinn and humankind only so that they may worship Me,” which commentators and philosophers explain as “so that they may know Me.” They say that the goal of creation is God Himself. The answer is that His being known is identical with His essence, just as His other attributes are identical with His essence. In another sense, His being known takes the form of a relation to the perfect human being. The goal is direct witnessing: that those who know God know Him through immediate presence, until the knower and the act of knowing no longer remain distinct, as in complete erasure, utter annihilation, and the passing away of annihilation itself. — س ره

[^V02-458]: Some contemporary scholars who follow the traditionist school say that will is an attribute of action and means only willing something other than oneself. The commentator disagrees. The arguments, he says, show that a willer ultimately wills himself: people seek food, drink, clothing, homes, writing, sewing, family, and friends because they seek their own good and love themselves. — س ره

[^V02-459]: In some versions, another line comes first: “I pass by Salma’s home and kiss this wall and that one.” The poet first speaks of the houses generally, then names the particular beloved’s home, as if it stood for them all. A similar couplet says: “I kiss the ground her beauty crossed—what of the house where her beauty dwelled?” — س ره

[^V02-460]: This passage needs some clarification. I will distinguish three points. First, these outcomes are grouped by when they occur in relation to the main goal: before it, as hardness is needed for cutting; alongside it, as a dark color accompanies iron; or after it, as earthly events follow the motion of the spheres. Second, hardness helps cause the cutting, while the dark color merely happens to accompany the iron that is needed. Third, someone might ask how hardness and darkness can be goals if they last as long as the knife does. The reply is that the relevant hardness is the hardness after the iron has been softened and shaped, and the relevant color is the color of the finished knife, not of the iron before it is polished. Another answer is that these qualities may be goals of the powers and natural processes that act on matter and form minerals. Among the names by which God is invoked is: “O You whose treasures are in the mountains.” — س ره

[^V02-461]: The proper goal lies within the hidden side of the world, at the end of the upward sequence of beings, as will be explained in the discussion of the afterlife. The first cause also lies within the hidden side, at the start of the downward sequence. One moves from hiddenness into view; the other moves from view back into hiddenness. — س ره

[^V02-462]: The claim that the defining forms of kinds exist forever rests partly on old assumptions from astronomy and natural science, and partly on the idea that each kind has its own eternal, separate cause. Later work in mathematics and natural science showed those old assumptions to be mistaken. Earlier discussions also questioned whether these separate causes exist. In addition, general rules drawn from nature depend on the conditions we observe now, and we have no proof those conditions will remain the same forever. So we cannot prove that nature must always produce human beings, animals, plants, earth, or the same elements. This differs from the continuing flow of existence as God wills it: that has a complete cause and an unrestricted agent who exists forever and is not limited by conditions or blocked by obstacles. The commentator says the full discussion belongs elsewhere. — ط مده

[^V02-463]: “The first” and “the second” refer to a goal that exists in what receives it and a goal that exists in the agent. — س ره

[^V02-464]: This is true when we look at motion as a kind of change that stands opposite to rest. But when something moves from one place to another, it passes through a series of temporary positions before coming to rest in one fixed place. That final place is the endpoint of the movement. — ط مده

[^V02-465]: Having distinguished good and generosity as terms used for goals, the author now explains what good and generosity mean more broadly. — س ره

[^V02-466]: Suppose an agent performs an action for a goal. If there were no goal, the action would not happen. So the act’s being brought about depends on the goal, just as the agent’s ability to act depends on it. Since the agent acts by its own nature, the goal must be present in some way within that nature, not wholly outside it. The agent has a general, undirected state and a particular state directed toward an action. The action, too, has two levels: the action itself and the fuller state it reaches. A thing can therefore be viewed as the basic fact that it exists and as the fuller state toward which it moves. This is how each created thing has a goal through its cause. What has no further goal and is itself the goal is not made for something else. — ط مده

[^V02-467]: Put another way, in every action the agent seeks itself. For agents that can change, an incomplete self seeks its more complete state, even if it only imagines that state. — س ره

[^V02-468]: The goal that exists in what receives an action, or in someone other than the agent, need not be the final goal. It may be the goal of one action and then serve another goal in a later action, until the chain returns to the agent. For example, a carpenter may make a bed so that a ruler can sit on it. That does not prevent the ruler’s sitting from having its own goal, or the carpenter from also seeking payment or some other benefit that serves his own good. — س ره

[^V02-469]: The author seems to mean that one kind of goal is both a goal and an endpoint in the strict sense. This specific use agrees with the wider, everyday meaning of the word. — س ره

[^V02-470]: Consider their familiar division: “A thing either requires existence through itself, and so is necessary, or it does not.” Yet the Necessary Being is pure, simple existence, standing by itself. His existence does not come from a maker or depend on a subject in which He exists, for He has neither matter nor an essence distinct from existence. So “requires” here means that He does not need another to give Him existence. Similarly, when they say that a substance “stands by itself,” they mean that it does not exist in another thing as an accident does.

[^V02-471]: A final cause is knowledge of the good result of an action. In this case, the ultimate goal is God Himself, so the knowledge in question is God’s knowledge of His own essence and, through it, of all that depends on Him. The philosophers describe this as general knowledge of effects; Mulla Sadra holds that God knows them all in a single, undivided act of knowledge. Since God’s essence is the source of the action, this knowledge gives rise to creation without deliberation.

[^V02-472]: This is natural, created worship: the obedience built into the way each thing is made. It is not the worship required by religious law. The commentator distinguishes commands addressed to people, which leave room for choice, from God’s creative command, through which things and matter take on their nature and powers.

[^V02-473]: The author specifies earthly, animal worship because human worship, considered at that level, is nothing like the worship of the heavenly spheres and their inhabitants. They neither sleep nor grow weary. They worship God without interruption or exhaustion. And what is ordinary human knowledge beside that of the angels who are protected from error, especially those nearest to God? As the verse says:

    > Where is the Beloved, and where are you, deceiver?
    > What has the light of eternity to do with those still farther astray?

    But among human beings there is a group superior even to the angels, let alone the heavenly spheres. Of them another verse says:

    > Neither the heavens nor the angels possess
    > What lies in the deepest heart of Adam's child.

    These are the finest of God's servants and the choicest beings in creation, especially those who follow Muhammad most completely. It is said of them:

    > The Holy Spirit, in the gardens of the highest sanctuary,
    > Has tasted the earliest fruit from our gardens.

    And of their leader it is said:

    > If Ahmad were to unfold that majestic wing,
    > Gabriel would remain overwhelmed forever.

    The words of Farīd al-Dīn ʿAṭṭār of Nishapur apply to this whole group:

    > My child, day and night these seven turning circles
    > Carry out their work for your sake.
    >
    > The worship of the spirits is for you;
    > Paradise and Hell reflect grace and severity toward you.
    >
    > The holy ones have all bowed before you;
    > Part and whole are immersed in your existence.
    >
    > Do not keep looking down on yourself in contempt;
    > None greater than you is possible in this order.
    >
    > Your outward being is a part; inwardly you contain the whole.
    > Even in humility, do not think yourself incapable.
    >
    > When the time comes for the whole to rise in rank,
    > The ranks of creation rise through your existence.

    The reason is that a human being who has reached full perfection stands under the Greatest Name, the name *Allah*. Angels stand under names that express God's freedom from imperfection, such as the All-Holy and the All-Pure. The heavenly spheres stand under such names as the Everlasting, the Exalter, and the Lord. The complete human being knows all the divine names: both those that express God's being beyond comparison and those that describe how His qualities appear in creation.

    Consider the difference. The spirit of a heavenly sphere remains bound to its sphere. But the spirit of a person in this highest human group becomes free—not only from the natural body, but even from the body of the world of images and, beyond that, from both worlds of form. Such a person casts off the two sandals and leaves the two worlds behind. An angel close to God may be a spirit free of bodily limitation, yet still does not possess knowledge of all the divine names in both of these ways.

    These complete human beings bring the rising chain of existence to its highest point. Their intellects have risen beyond the need to use the body and its organs. It is as though they have already taken their bodies off while still wearing them. They stand opposite the pure intellects that mark the beginning of the descending chain. If any veil remains, it will be lifted entirely. This is why ʿAlī said, as his earthly life ended, “By the Lord of the Kaaba, I have succeeded!”

    Their worship is therefore nobler in quality than the constant worship of the heavens. A small amount of perfectly sincere action can outweigh an immense quantity of other actions. Their knowledge likewise surpasses the angels' knowledge, because the complete human being knows God through all His names. Perhaps, then, the author's statement concerns human beings simply as earthly human beings, not those who have reached this complete state. — S. R.

[^V02-474]: A thing can be the true goal only through its own independent perfection. Created beings do not exist independently; their existence depends on God. So God is their true goal.

[^V02-475]: More precisely, God is first through His self-subsisting existence and last as the goal toward which we turn. The commentator says that nearness to God means taking on divine qualities; Mulla Sadra calls this resembling the First Principle.

[^V02-476]: This explains why some goals that seem lower than the person can still be pursued. A poor writer seeks payment, which is a better state than being in need. Someone who is generous only in possibility seeks to become generous in action. In general, people seek a fuller state of themselves. The commentator adds that the intention itself is higher than the thing intended, because the intention gives rise to the action.

[^V02-477]: The lower goal exists only in the person’s mistaken picture of it. For example, someone may imagine that giving in to a shameful desire is a sign of greatness. The person thinks their whole self seeks it, but in fact the imagination and bodily appetite seek a passing pleasure.

[^V02-478]: You may object: This answer clearly belongs with the preceding question, not with the present one. I reply: it belongs here too. The present objection was that if a goal makes someone act, and the agent is higher than the result it produces, the agent would have to gain some perfection from a goal beneath it. But the soul, considered in its higher nature, does not perform a particular act to gain a passing, lesser goal; such a goal cannot complete it in that respect. When the soul acts through its connection with matter, however, it can act for a particular goal, because in that respect it can gain what it lacks. If the answer also fits the earlier question, that is no difficulty: the earlier question then has two answers.

[^V02-479]: The commentator says that the world’s true existence depends on God; created things have existence only through Him. In the Persian verse, the word for “or” separates two ideas. It does not show doubt. The heedless may think that some things are enemies and others friends, but all things display the qualities of the true Beloved.

[^V02-480]: God’s knowledge of Himself is active: it brings things into being.

[^V02-481]: An action has a goal in the sense that it reaches a fitting result, the fuller state toward which it tends. God’s own goal, in this sense, is Himself.

[^V02-482]: Harm and loss are called side results because they arise indirectly within the order of material things. God does not directly aim at them.
