[^V02-483]: Nature has a kind of prior awareness because a source must in some way hold the basis for what it produces. The power of fire to heat contains the basis of heat; the power of water to cool contains the basis of cold. The result does not come from a source wholly unrelated to it.

[^V02-484]: The commentator reads the Qur'anic verb as “they understand,” so the verse says that people do not understand the praise of other things.

[^V02-485]: The commentator argues that awareness and love appear in every kind of existence, each according to its level. If bodily matter and a higher intellect share the same basic kind of existence, though at very different levels, then even a very faint form of existence has some degree of awareness and love. This is how the author earlier described matter’s natural longing for form.

[^V02-486]: The saying “God is Zayd’s existence” means that God alone has existence in the fullest and independent sense. Created things do not have existence by themselves; God gives them being and holds them in being. There is no second, separate source of existence. The distinction between a thing and its existence points to this dependence; it is not a separate object added in the mind.

[^V02-487]: The claim that orderly action proves planning is not a proof. It may persuade people who do not see how natural results follow from the powers of things. Nature can bring about an ordered result without thinking or choosing it.

[^V02-488]: The two senses are the goal that gives rise to an action and the end at which the action arrives.

[^V02-489]: The commentator explains three connected senses of the world’s final goal: God’s knowledge of the good order before creation; God Himself as the true end of creation; and God as known and met by those who seek Him. The second is the end reached by creation itself; the third is the end as experienced by creatures who come to know God. These differences matter while the seeker and the One sought are viewed as separate. They disappear in complete awareness of God.

[^V02-490]: This refers to both kinds of command: the commands of religious law and the commands built into the way creation works.

[^V02-491]: The commentator says that people attached to worldly things will not be comforted in the next life by imagining those things. When someone loses money or a child, simply imagining them does not ease the grief; it may deepen it. In the next life, people face the real results of their actions: wrongful gain becomes a form of fire, while generosity becomes a good and welcome reality. The next life is the harvest of what people do here.

[^V02-492]: This conclusion is the finest point in this stage, indeed in all these stages. Understanding it joins the book’s ending to its opening and brings the work to completion. It brings together proof and direct spiritual experience—the kind taught by the All-Merciful when joined with awareness of God—as well as reason and transmitted teaching. May God reward the efforts of the one who brought it to completion.

[^V02-493]: An intermediary in existence is one that makes a subject truly have the property it mediates; that property cannot truthfully be denied of the subject. Fire, for example, makes water hot. An intermediary in attribution works differently: the subject is described by association, so the description can truthfully be denied of it. There are several kinds. In the first, the intermediary and the subject exist separately and occupy different places, as with a ship and the passenger it carries when both are called moving. In the second, they exist separately but are not apart in place, as blackness and ebony when we call the ebony black; blackness is what is truly black. In the third, they exist as one, as broader kind and distinguishing feature in a definition, and as essence and existence when we consider what is real. Because an individual plays this third kind of role in the realization of a natural universal, the universal exists only through individuals, not separately from them, as the thinker from Hamadan who lived in Avicenna’s time held. Nor does it exist in a separate world as the archetype of its kind, as some have imagined. Such a creative archetype would itself be an individual of that kind; its greater perfection would not prevent it from sharing its nature with other individuals, just as a perfect human being is still human like an imperfect one. In short, the universal does not act as something that gives existence. It has no existence apart from individuals, and its nature is neither existent nor nonexistent in itself. Existence is neither identical with nor part of it. The universal nature is immersed in the existence of its individual; its real existence is that individual’s own existence. So it is not an intermediary in existence but is attributed to the individual by association. This attribution is metaphorical in ordinary thought, though it counts as demonstrative when seen from a high level of spiritual insight.

[^V02-494]: Scholars disagree about how the parts of a definition, such as a general kind and its distinguishing feature, are joined, although they agree that the mind distinguishes them. One view says that these parts are separate in both essence and existence in the external world. A second says that they are distinct in essence but united in existence, since they are parts that can be predicated of the whole; predication means that they share one existence, while essence is prior to existence in what a thing is. A third view says that they are united in both essence and existence. The author takes a fourth view: these parts have no external reality as separate or unified essences, even though the natural universal exists externally in an incidental way. What exists in itself outside the mind is one simple mode of existence. Depending on its capacities and on which similarities it notices, the mind draws distinct ideas from it. When the author says here and in his other books that the true distinguishing feature is a particular existence, he is expressing this fourth view.

[^V02-495]: “Relation” here means the way God’s existence shines through the world, not a separate relation between two independent things. The commentator distinguishes the world as God’s shadow from the essences where that shadow appears: existence is light, while essences have no independent being. Saying that the world exists through God does not mean God created a second, separate reality. This is why the Qur'anic verse says, “Have you not seen your Lord?” rather than, “Have you not seen your Lord’s shadow?”

[^V02-496]: Every image that appears where the shadow falls is a sign of the original reality from which it spreads. The greater world is like a mirror of God’s high attributes and beautiful names, which are the roots of all things. To know the realities of things is, in the end, to know God and His attributes. This also explains “He taught Adam all the names”: the complete human being knows the realities of the world through God, and this knowledge is itself knowledge of God. Readings of the verse in terms of the realities of the world, God’s attributes, or the names of Muhammad and his family support one another.

[^V02-497]: The commentator says that the verse does not mean God could have held back the shadow but chose not to. God willed the world and brought it about through His generosity and wisdom; all that happens was already known and decreed. The verse uses a conditional form like “If He wills, He can replace you.”

[^V02-498]: “Universal concepts” here explains “natural universals”: an essence considered just as it is, before the mind considers it as universal.

[^V02-499]: The commentator explains the familiar saying that possible essences and fixed entities are not beings in themselves but still have effects. They have no existence of their own; they receive a dependent status when existence gives them its features. This lets us speak of possibility, limitation, and multiplicity without treating these ideas as separate beings. Possibility means that neither existence nor nonexistence is necessary for an essence; limitation belongs to essence, not existence; and existence does not multiply by itself, but through the things in which it appears.

[^V02-500]: The comparison shows how concepts such as life, knowledge, and power are drawn from the existence they describe. They are like possible essences drawn from individual beings. The same concept can describe God or an angel, while the relation tells us whether that life or knowledge is eternal or began in time.

[^V02-501]: Every individual fully has the nature of its kind. The universal’s relation to its individuals is like a parent’s relation to children: the whole nature is present in each individual, with that individual’s features making it particular. The nature is not split into parts or multiplied with the individuals; the difference lies in how the mind considers each one.

[^V02-502]: The commentator explains the unity of existence: existence has one shared meaning, and what distinguishes each being is not something added from outside. The same existence appears in different particular forms.

[^V02-503]: The commentator praises the mystery of how God links universal essences with individual existences without a shared part between them. The universal is the particular understood in a different way; the difference lies in how we perceive it. God’s names bring opposites together—the First and the Last, the Visible and the Hidden. The same existence is present in the highest intellects, though it remains free of possibility and change. This is another example of God joining opposites without a common feature between them.

[^V02-504]: In Sufi language, the “secret of destiny” is God’s eternal knowledge of each fixed essence and of the states it will display when it exists. God judges each thing according to what He knows of it. Some say that knowing this secret frees a person from striving and toil. The commentator notes that this is different from the question of why some evils arise in the world.

[^V02-505]: The commentator cites Qur'anic verses saying that good comes from God, while evil comes from oneself; that all things come from God; that people will only will what God wills; and that God erases and confirms what He wills. Here, “from yourself” means “from your own fixed essence.”

[^V02-506]: “Union” here includes all kinds: predication, likeness, sharing a kind, equality, and other forms of sameness, including cases where we do not usually describe them through predication.

[^V02-507]: An effect is not a separate thing alongside its source; it is the source appearing in another way. The commentator says that all created beings depend entirely on God. Their beings, qualities, and actions are links to the One who sustains them; their appearance is held within His appearance.

[^V02-508]: The commentator compares this with the idea that God brings things into being by appearing through them: He reveals them and is hidden in their appearance. At the resurrection, His unity removes their separate forms. This does not mean that God changes His nature or that He has children. It means that created things only seemed to be separate from Him; their reality is His ongoing act, as the Qur'an says, “Each day He is at work.”

[^V02-509]: The objection can be stated in two ways. If the Necessary Being falls under the broad category of relation, it would need a distinguishing feature and would be composite. If it were identical with that broad category, it would be caused by it. The highest category itself is simple, so only the first version leads to composition.

[^V02-510]: You may say that this strengthens the objection: if the Necessary Being is a cause through His very essence, and a cause precisely as cause ... What was intended is that the apparent separation assumed at first is not real. We may affirm causation, but understand it as the unfolding of His acts, not as an independent union of the kind the Muʿtazilite proposes. For God “neither begets nor is begotten”; rather, “Each day He is engaged in a matter.” — س ره

[^V02-511]: If a proof is a judgment of the mind, why does the author say that the mind is not the judge? The commentator answers that he means reason by itself, without God’s light. A proof has authority because it is a light from God. The mind recognizes the truth through a light given to it, just as the saying goes: “Know God through God.”

[^V02-512]: The point is that every category of relation is a kind of essence, but not every identity that depends on a relation belongs to that category. The argument confuses a relation in a thing’s existence with the mind’s separate idea of relation.

[^V02-513]: The commentator objects that the category of relation is still an essence and cannot be drawn from existence alone. A relation in existence, however, is not a quality that rests on a subject; it is the link by which one thing’s existence depends on another. The mind forms the separate idea of relation by considering the two things and the link between them.

[^V02-514]: Relation is a concept formed by thought. It follows the way things exist, but the relation itself is not a separate thing in the external world.

[^V02-515]: The commentator gives two further answers to the problem of time. First, before and after in time are links in existence itself, not the formal category of relation; not every kind of priority or delay belongs to that category. Second, as the Sheikh explains elsewhere, the mind creates the idea of relation. It can consider the earlier and later parts of time together, even though they do not exist together outside the mind.

[^V02-516]: The author offers another answer: time is one continuous reality, not a set of separate pieces. Its parts are joined in one flowing existence. In that way, the earlier and later moments are together in the continuous whole, while each keeps its own place in the sequence.

[^V02-517]: The point is that these things have relational existence, not independent existence, as explained earlier.

[^V02-518]: The alternative would create a circle: possible beings depend on God’s life, knowledge, power, and other perfections. The argument also applies to a possible perfection claimed to belong to God.

[^V02-519]: This note compares the argument with a later proof in the section on divine things. A possible being cannot depend on something impossible in itself. This also answers al-Dawani’s objection to the principle that the noblest possible being must exist: its supposed cause cannot be a perfection in God that is itself impossible.

[^V02-520]: The note cites mystics who say that all things are one reality in different forms. The world’s “attributes” are not qualities added to God; they are features of existence considered in relation to its manifestations.

[^V02-521]: The note lists images that can help explain this relation: a moving flame tracing a circle, motion giving rise to the idea of a path, a flowing present giving rise to time, a mirror and its reflections, and the sea and its waves. It also invokes the Qur'anic image of a lamp within a niche and the sign of unity found in every thing.

[^V02-522]: Ordinary compounds, such as a body made of matter and form, have distinct parts. Numbers, by contrast, consist of repeated ones. The note also compares this kind of predication to statements about possibility or “thingness,” where the predicate is not an added quality in the subject.

[^V02-523]: “Existence without restriction” means existence itself, not a general concept. Its all-pervading reach is an act of the Real, not the Real’s essence considered in itself.

[^V02-524]: The distinction allows two approaches to be reconciled: denying limitations and possible boundaries does not mean denying existence as a positive reality.

[^V02-525]: Eternal necessity means that a thing exists through itself, apart from every distinct external condition or cause.

[^V02-526]: Number offers a comparison: its kinds are simple in meaning, and any distinction between broader kind and defining feature in them is a distinction the mind makes.

[^V02-527]: Fractions belong to the category of relation, not quality, because they are not settled qualities inhering in a subject.

[^V02-528]: This follows one account given by the mystics. Abd al-Razzaq al-Kashani explains the 'Cloud' mentioned in a report about God as the level of absolute oneness, because no one knows that level but God. It is veiled by divine majesty.

    Others take the Cloud to mean the level of unity in which the divine names and attributes appear. A thin cloud hangs between heaven and earth; likewise this level stands between the heaven of undivided oneness and the world of created multiplicity. Kashani rejects this reading of the report. The Prophet was asked where the Lord was before creating the world and answered, 'In a Cloud.' But the level where names and relationships are first distinguished already involves a determination, and Kashani takes everything determined to be created.

    I do not accept that every determination must be created. This is the appearance of God's attributes, not yet His acts in the created world. The fixed realities related to the names and attributes appear as objects of divine knowledge. They are not thereby created things existing independently of that knowledge. Even the first active giving of existence is called the world of command rather than the world of creation in this terminology. On the author's account, the separate intellects belong to the divine realm and endure through God's own enduring reality.

    Moreover, the question concerned the *Lord* and the level of lordship. That is precisely the level of unity in which the names and attributes are distinguished, as the author states elsewhere.

[^V02-529]: The source seems to omit a second “either.” The note supplies it: existence taken with one attribute is a particular name; with all attributes it is the name “Allah”; with a particular created determination it is a particular possible being; and with all such determinations it is the “Great Human,” the universe as a whole.

[^V02-530]: The note quotes Jami on the fixed entities: before the sea of generosity stirred, they were distinct neither in knowledge nor in external existence; when it stirred, the Necessary and the possible became distinct and the pattern of duality began.

[^V02-531]: By this he means the universal intellect, which embraces the intellects arranged both in higher and lower ranks and alongside one another. It is the enduring reality at the heart of the world. When philosophers say that ‘substance remains,’ substance can mean what everything else depends upon. In this sense the enduring heart of the world is God's abiding face, even as created natures pass and change. It is also the existence that spreads through all things. This reality is later called the ‘Breath of the All-Merciful.’ That name usually refers to the existence that extends through the whole world. It is applied here to the intellect that contains all other intellects because this intellect receives that existence in its fullness.

    It is its first dwelling place, before it divides into many distinct levels—the mother of all places and of the pens. It still remains in the heaven of its own unity. At this level there is no need to treat its essence as something separate from its existence; on the view being defended, intellect has no essence apart from the fact that it exists.

[^V02-532]: God’s self-sustaining power appears at every level: in the concept of substance in the mind, in matter as the subject of material forms, and in the universal intellect as the form of forms and source of all agents.

[^V02-533]: Compound divine names, such as the Living, the Self-Sustaining or the Most High, the Magnificent, can have a unity that produces an effect not found in either name taken by itself.

    An objection: any two names can be put together. What makes this combination a real unity? The answer is that a being must genuinely display both names, either by its created nature or through the qualities it develops. In its created nature, an animal is both hearing and seeing; the heavens display enduring motion and elevation. In conduct, a person may acquire both wisdom and generosity.

    If someone is generous but lacks wisdom, merely calling that person 'the servant of the Generous and Wise' does not bring the two qualities together. In this way, a person who is strongly shaped by one of the divine names may be called its servant even without bearing that name outwardly. The servant of the Guide leads others; the servant of the Giver gives to them.

    As explained earlier, things may appear to a seeker as signs in which the divine names are displayed. From a higher point of view, they may be described as the names expressed in action, because the sign has no independent standing apart from what it displays. At the highest level of sincerity, even the distinction of attributes falls away.

[^V02-534]: The primary names include Living, Knowing, Willing, Powerful, Speaking, Hearing, and Seeing, with “Allah” as their chief. The main names are finite, while their many derivatives are not. “Relation” here includes both pure relations and things described through relations.

[^V02-535]: Once “name” is understood in the mystics’ sense, sayings such as “the world is a manifestation of the names” or “the name is the named” need not be taken as ordinary identity claims. They are the same in existence but distinct in meaning.

[^V02-536]: God's essential names belong to the unseen, and His essence—the hidden treasure—is the unseen beyond every unseen thing. This is one reading of the words, “With Him are the keys of the unseen.”

    Some writers find a symbolic meaning in the letters of the divine name. The letter *ha* can stand for pure divine identity rather than for an ordinary pronoun. It may be drawn as a circle, with no starting or ending point and with its center equally related to every part of its edge. Some draw two circles instead, to suggest beauty and majesty. The letter *waw* arises when the sound is extended. Since *ha* is formed in the throat and *waw* at the lips, their joining can suggest the divine breath embracing what is hidden and what is outward. The sound is only a sign pointing toward the unlimited divine existence; it does not contain it.

    Alternatively, the 'keys of the unseen' may refer to God's names expressed in action: the intellects through which beings come forth and those through which they return. On either reading, only God knows them fully. Knowledge requires a likeness between the knower and the known. Whatever belongs wholly to the divine realm exists and remains through Him; only He knows its innermost reality.

[^V02-537]: Each divine name has a corresponding fixed entity: the human entity corresponds to the all-gathering name “Allah,” an angel to “the All-Holy,” and an animal to “the Hearing, the Seeing.” These are knowledge-forms that exist with their names, not later effects.

[^V02-538]: The “most holy outpouring” is the essence appearing through names, attributes, and their fixed entities. The “holy outpouring” is its appearance through possible essences in the active world. The first is the self-disclosure of attributes; the second, the self-disclosure of acts.

[^V02-539]: Here “separately” means distinct in essence or concept, not in existence: the names and their corresponding realities share one existence, that of the named. The divine knowledge level is the realm of distinct knowledge, where each thing is known as it is.

[^V02-540]: The note points to Jami and Husayni, who speak of bringing the fixed entities from knowledge into visible reality—from the unseen into direct witness.

[^V02-541]: The mistake comes from confusing a thing's nature with its actual reality. These thinkers hold that existence is fundamental. How, then, could they mean that actual beings are only ideas? The aspect of each thing through which God's action and light appear—His face, power, and will—is not an empty mental invention.

    When mystics say that angels, heavenly spheres, humans, animals, and other creatures are 'considered' or 'conceptual,' they are speaking about the natures of things taken by themselves. Such natures have no independent existence. They are not saying that these beings, as they actually exist, are unreal. The mistake lies in moving from a statement about a nature considered in thought to a statement about a living, existing reality.

    Consider a philosopher who says that a human nature, taken by itself, is equally open to existence and nonexistence. A reader might suppose that an actual human being, while existing, could just as well not exist at that very moment. But while something exists, its existence is certain; it cannot be both itself and not itself in the same respect. Even a thinker who holds that natures are fundamental means the nature as connected with existence, not a bare nature considered in isolation. The poet Shabistari's statement that distinctions are matters of consideration makes the same point.

[^V02-542]: The commentator says the first and second ways of considering a possible being ultimately both concern existence. The first is a general, undivided awareness of its existence; the second considers existence and unity in more detail.

[^V02-543]: An objection: calling Necessary Existence 'existence on condition of absolute freedom' seems to make it existence with a condition attached. Yet the accepted account is that it is existence *without* conditions. How can both be right?

    The answer is that 'absolute' here means the boundless reach of real existence, not the generality of a concept in the mind. All three expressions used by these thinkers can point to the same pure, simple existence, which excludes nonexistence from itself and is the source of existence for other things.

    'Without condition' means that no limit is imposed on it. 'On condition of being unrestricted' means that unrestricted reality itself; the supposed condition merely says that there is no condition. Even 'on condition of nothing else' can refer to the same reality, because the word 'nothing' removes the limits, natures, and absences we might otherwise add to our thinking. The descriptions differ, but the reality described is one.

    The word 'absolute' will also be used in a different sense: God is true existence, while the existence that flows from His act may also be called 'absolute.' The same word is then used of two different levels, which the author will distinguish later.

[^V02-544]: If reason’s judgments could fail at a level beyond reason, then even the judgment that the world has an eternal Creator could fail. Reason is the highest human power for knowing. Some claims are beyond a limited or confused use of reason, but not beyond sound reason itself.

[^V02-545]: Possible beings need not have independent existence to be real. Their existence may be only dependent and relational: their whole being is need and dependence on the true reality. This does not conflict with unity.

[^V02-546]: The mystic looks beyond the many essences and images that draw attention in different directions to the one existence that gives them life. The note compares this to a mirror and its many reflections: the source remains one while its appearances differ.

[^V02-547]: A human being is only an imperfect comparison for God. A human is limited and made of parts; body and soul are joined in a way that develops the body, but the soul is not its cause in the same sense that God is the cause of all existence.

[^V02-548]: “Balanced” means free from extreme anger, fear, or desire. Such states make the many forms of the imagined world more prominent and distract from the vision of unity.

[^V02-549]: An effect shares the nature of its cause and is not a separate thing beside it; otherwise, it would not truly be an effect. In this account, existence itself is God’s power. All sources of action are levels of that power, while actions considered apart from their existence are like mirages.

[^V02-550]: The note reads “the world is God’s book” as an image of creation as writing: intellects are complete words, souls are names, changing natural forms are actions, and accidents are like grammar and marks in writing. The world’s many forms are written by one author.

[^V02-551]: The note quotes a mystic who says he is like someone who has seen a dream but cannot speak of it, while the world cannot hear. He cannot put the secret into words, and most people cannot receive it.

[^V02-552]: The objection has two possible targets. If it concerns Unity (*wahidiyya*), that level includes many names, attributes, and fixed entities. If it concerns Oneness (*ahadiyya*), there are no names or distinctions there, and so no duties or created beings. Multiplicity, duties, and actions appear at later levels. The unity described here is the unity that gathers many things together.

[^V02-553]: The author calls the third level “universal,” but it is not a general idea shared by many things. The commentator notes that “universal” can mean a natural kind, a rule that applies to a whole class, or a word such as “a man.” The author uses the term loosely because this level is long to explain and easy to confuse with the abstract idea of existence.

[^V02-554]: Philosophers often use “numerical unity” to mean that something is one individual. They also sometimes use it for the kind of oneness found within numbers, which the mystics take as a sign of the special unity of God.

[^V02-555]: “Timeless” here means without beginning in time, not uncreated in essence. The all-pervading existence is described as the Throne of the All-Merciful, a created reality, and an extended shadow.

[^V02-556]: All-pervading existence differs from basic matter and a general kind. It is fully real and includes all beings. Matter and a general kind, by contrast, are only potential and vague until they appear in particular forms. One is full and complete; the others are marked by lack.

[^V02-557]: The author makes this distinction because readers often mistake “absolute existence” for the simple mental idea of existence. The same words can refer either to that idea or to the real existence that appears through beings.

[^V02-558]: This note refers to philosophers who say that essences are real in themselves and that existence is only a mental idea.

[^V02-559]: The mystics call all-pervading existence “matter” by comparison. They do not mean that it is material or something that can be received by a subject. It is higher than both matter and receptivity.

[^V02-560]: The note links the name “Breath of the All-Merciful” with sayings attributed to the Imams, who used “dust” or “matter” for this all-pervading existence.

[^V02-561]: The note explains why al-Qunawi calls this existence “general.” In other fields of study, “general” can be a figure of speech for what is broadest and first noticed by the mind. The Real is the first reality and the most evident at every level.

[^V02-562]: The fixed identities of possible beings have no independent existence, though their features and effects depend on all-pervading existence. “No existence” here means no separate, self-standing existence. The note contrasts God’s complete independence with the possible beings’ total dependence.

[^V02-563]: The commentator stresses how closely the levels of existence are related. Even restricted existence, represented by the Universal Intellect, belongs to the divine realm in a deeper sense. All-pervading existence, whose first manifestation is the First Intellect, belongs to that realm even more fully. It is the source of all that comes into being; the reverse is not true.

[^V02-564]: All-pervading existence is not a separate product made by God. It is God's own act of flowing forth and appearing; nothing stands apart from Him as a second source. In thought, the First Intellect is the first distinct being to proceed from God, though it is preceded by the act of proceeding itself. This does not limit God's power or suggest that He hands creation over to another. All lower acts and beings are contained within this one act, especially in all-pervading existence. Its appearance is God's own self-disclosure, not something separate from Him; the First Intellect is the active expression of His power and continues to exist through Him.

[^V02-565]: The word “existence” may be used for God if it means real existence as it applies to its source, not merely as an abstract idea in the mind. In that case, the word points to the reality that gives rise to the idea. This is the existence that rules out nonexistence.

[^V02-566]: Rumi's “nothings” means the natures or identities of created things, not their real existence. The mystics sometimes call a nature “nothing” because, on its own, it is neither existence nor nonexistence. It appears to exist when joined with existence, but it does not exist independently. This is not the claim that a particular person is God. For the mystics, existence is primary and has different levels: one level is cause, another effect. The created nature is dependent on existence and is only an aspect of what appears. In this sense, God is the root of our being, and we are wholly dependent on Him.

[^V02-567]: Some theologians allow “existing” as a description of God but not as one of His names. The dispute concerns how the word is used.

[^V02-568]: A real thing is either existence itself or a particular nature. A nature, considered on its own, is neither necessary in existence nor necessary in nonexistence, so it cannot be the Necessary Being, who is pure reality and existence. God is not the abstract idea of existence, but the reality that makes existence known. Those who deny any shared meaning between God's existence and the existence of created things to avoid likening God to creation go too far: every act of knowing depends on God's light, and every being appears through Him.

[^V02-569]: The commentator's objection is verbal rather than a real disagreement. He distinguishes true existence, all-pervading existence, and restricted existence, but also explains that all-pervading existence is God's manifestation, while God is hidden through the intensity of His manifestation. A manifestation is not separate from what it manifests. There is one reality with different levels, not a second or third God. None of these levels implies that God enters created things, becomes joined to them, or takes on their defects. Restricted existence is connected to a created nature only in the sense that the nature describes it.

[^V02-570]: At the inward level, the scriptures do not differ in their central teachings. All things are signs of the One who has no equal, and each reveals God's oneness and unity. God's words in the world and in the soul do not run out or change; His light does not fade, and His giving does not cease. What belongs to God is timeless, while matter and nature are changing and passing away. The mystics also distinguish three levels of unity: knowing about a fire from its light, seeing the fire itself, and being consumed by it. In the last two, the one who knows is lost in the simple, all-pervading reality.

[^V02-571]: “Turn toward” means that they earnestly seek this unity. For most people, it can be reached only through spiritual discipline and moral fairness: treating others as oneself and preferring their good to one's own until the sense of separation falls away. Not reaching this deepest experience does not invalidate a person's direct or reasoned knowledge of unity.

[^V02-572]: The wording means that the signs or descriptions point toward the same thing, even if the words differ.

[^V02-573]: As long as there is someone pointing and something being pointed to, there are still two. True unity cannot be captured by that kind of description. This is why Ali speaks of “lifting the veils of majesty without a gesture of pointing.”

[^V02-574]: A real nature, such as humanity, can exist both in the world and in the mind. Abstract ideas such as existence, nonexistence, possibility, and thingness cannot exist as separate things in the world. But the mind does not invent them from nothing. It forms them by reflecting on other ideas. So real natures are drawn from things that exist; abstract ideas are drawn from the mind's ideas about those things.

[^V02-575]: A particular nature, such as humanity, is a basic idea that can refer to a real person. Ideas such as possibility and thingness are formed by thinking about those basic ideas. They do not point to a separate object in the world. The same word “thing” may also be used for the nature itself, but that does not give it an independent existence.

[^V02-576]: The first appearance of existence is one with the later appearances. The sense of many separate acts comes from the many forms that reflect it, not from any division or repetition in the source or in its act. Once we set aside the many mirrors, there is no second source or repeated act.

[^V02-577]: Some sayings from the Imams identify the “Face” that remains after all things perish with the Imam himself. This does not conflict with the author’s use of “Face” for all-pervading existence. The note says that the Muhammadan and Alawite realities are one light in the realm of spiritual authority.

[^V02-578]: The note explains how scripture can speak of God's “hand,” coming, or sitting without treating Him as a body. Such words refer to His acts and their effects, which are His way of appearing, not to body parts or qualities added to His essence. The perfect human being, who acts through God rather than through an independent self, can be called God's hand or eye. The same is true of the powers of intellects and souls. All these are expressions of one divine power, not separate parts of God.

[^V02-579]: The “point of direct insight” means hearing the speaker within the words. Al-Sadiq is said to have repeated a verse until he heard it from the One who speaks it. This is why some mystics prefer reciting the Qur'an to many prayers: in reciting it, they feel that they speak God's words on His behalf.

[^V02-580]: The commentator says the mistaken Sufis accept unity within the many forms of the world, but deny that multiplicity can also be gathered within God's unity. The complete view includes both: God's hidden oneness and His appearance through many forms. This is more complete than treating God as only the sum of the world. All serious thinkers agree that God's essence cannot be fully known. It is the unbounded reality of existence, not the limited form of a “Great Human” or any other created being. If this divine reality did not exist, there would be no reason for the agreement the author describes.

[^V02-581]: The commentator reads the opening words as “while they think this,” signaling that the claim he describes is false.

[^V02-582]: These disputes arise because “existence” has several meanings: God's true existence, all-pervading existence, and the general idea of existence that the mind applies to particular things. When the mystics say that God is beyond existence, they mean He is beyond created, limited existence or the mind's abstract idea of existence. They do not mean that God is beyond reality itself. Denying one limited sense of existence does not deny existence in every sense.

[^V02-583]: “True existence” can mean God, who exists through Himself and for Himself. It can also mean real existence, as opposed to an abstract idea in the mind. Here it has the second meaning. The claim is obvious and easily stated, yet important enough to remember constantly: we need to see existence flowing through all things before we can call even a rose or a thorn a manifestation of God.

[^V02-584]: The phrase “fixed identities” may sound as if the natures are separate from existence, and it may sound like the Muʿtazilite teaching. But the two views differ. For the mystics, fixed identities depend on God's knowledge and are never separate from it. The Muʿtazilites treated them as independent of every kind of existence, both in the world and in knowledge.

[^V02-585]: The author grants that a nature must be able to receive existence, but it need not already exist in order to do so. Its natural readiness is enough. He adds that possible beings may have earlier forms in nature or in God's knowledge, before their later forms appear in the world.

[^V02-586]: God's existence is simple and complete, yet it contains every level of existence. In that sense, His knowledge is one and undivided, while also knowing every being and nature in detail. His names and qualities include the fixed identities that belong to the beings He knows.

[^V02-587]: “Time” here means a level or stage, not ordinary time. The verse's denial that the human being was a “thing” refers to a particular existence in the physical world, while its affirmation of humanity refers to the nature. The saying about God and nothing being with Him concerns the same distinction. There is no contradiction.

[^V02-588]: The note compares natures to basic matter in a compound thing. Matter is where imbalance and weakness appear, which keeps these defects from being attributed to God. This does not mean God fails to give what He can; it means that the nature of what receives existence also shapes what it can receive.

[^V02-589]: The fixed identities can be seen as a display of God's hidden name, because they are known within His hidden unity before they appear in the world.

[^V02-590]: Indeed, most minds trained in reasoning—perhaps all—find this bewildering. What makes a nature the nature it is does not exclude nonexistence, light, or darkness. It is therefore unlike pure existence or pure light. How, then, could such natures be God’s own states when He is sheer manifestation and actuality, especially since the natures are not themselves created separately? Some investigators, however, approach the question from unity: if natures can in some sense be called things, they say that everything called a thing belongs to His states. They distinguish leaving existence out of consideration from treating nonexistence as a feature. Earlier in the book we quoted the view that natures are existences to which particular forms of existence are attached. There is also this consideration: natures are forms known to God, and His knowledge is His attribute.

[^V02-591]: The wording from al-Qunawi is unclear on its own and does not prove the author's point. The commentator says there may be more in the surrounding passage that would make the meaning clear, but he does not have it at hand.

[^V02-592]: The word “knowledge” may seem unnecessary here, since the note is discussing what appears to things. But the author includes it because in divine knowledge, knower and known are one.

[^V02-593]: A feature belongs to something only under certain conditions. For example, an intellect has a certain level of existence when it is separate from matter, while a plant grows only in a suitable place and season. A quality that belongs to something under one condition does not automatically belong to it—or cease to belong to it—when that condition changes. Existence appears in endlessly many ways, even though its basic patterns are limited.

[^V02-594]: The note alludes to Qur'an 13:17: water flows through valleys according to their measure, while foam washes away. Here, existence is like the water, and natures or particular determinations are like the foam carried on it. What makes a thing appear is existence; its nature shapes the form that appearance takes.

[^V02-595]: Natures differ partly through their own character: just as the maker does not make blackness black or whiteness white, their difference is not something separately added to them. Their differences also reflect the order of the most holy outpouring. The different levels of existence mirror one another: differences in the natural world reflect differences in the imaginal world, which in turn reflect differences in the higher spiritual and divine levels, ultimately corresponding to God's names and qualities. These names and qualities are not caused by anything beyond God. As a saying in *Nahj al-Balāgha* puts it, “Whoever asks ‘Why?’ has made Him subject to a cause.”

[^V02-596]: Defects and blameworthy features include both limits recognized by reason and faults defined by religious law. A thing’s limits belong to it insofar as it is a bounded nature. Its existence and its luminous aspect, by contrast, come from existence and from the face of God.

[^V02-597]: The Qur'an makes the same point in the story of Moses and Khidr. Khidr says of the damaged boat, “I wanted to damage it.” Later, he says of the boys, “Your Lord wanted them to reach maturity and bring out their treasure,” and “We wanted their Lord to give them a better replacement.” The defect is attributed to the human agent; the good outcome is attributed to God.

[^V02-598]: “All good is in Your hands” includes both God's names of majesty and beauty, and the manifestations of His names. “Evil is not attributed to You” is a simple denial: when we look for what evil is in itself, nothing remains but absence, as explained earlier. A dualist who sees good and evil in the world and concludes that evil must have its own real source mistakes a swelling for healthy flesh. He has failed to see that evil, in itself, is a kind of nonexistence.

[^V02-599]: The commentator says God's second appearance takes place at a level within His own reality. He compares all-pervading existence to a connecting word: it only has meaning in relation to something else and cannot stand alone. In that sense, it is neither God Himself nor something separate from Him. Another view treats the fixed identities as part of God's hidden aspects. A further reading is that, because natures are ideas in the mind, God's appearance is through actual beings, whose existence depends on Him.

[^V02-600]: Strictly speaking, these terms describe different ways the divine reality shows itself.

[^V02-601]: The commentator links this wording to the level at which God is known.

[^V02-602]: If God's self-disclosure is one, the divine essence must be even more one. Differences appear only in the mirrors that receive it. Even those differences belong only to the second appearance, which cannot equal the first.

[^V02-603]: A Persian poet asks: “If You are all there is, what is this world? If I am nothing, why this cry?”

[^V02-604]: The note explains the claim that God's self-disclosure is not repeated in two senses. First, there is only one self-disclosure; saying it repeats would suggest that there are two. Second, the same claim supports the philosophers' view that what has ceased to exist cannot return as exactly the same thing. The Qur'an says, “Each day He is at work.” God's self-disclosure may seem to repeat as different mirrors and receivers follow one another, but each moment brings a new appearance. A Persian poem puts it this way: “Spiders dry flies; people of insight celebrate twice in a single breath.”

[^V02-605]: Here, “image” does not mean a separate mental copy of a thing. The commentator's point is that the same nature is present to the mind and to the world, but in two different ways. Outside the mind, it has real effects; in the mind, it does not. If what we knew were only a separate copy, we would know an image of the thing, not the thing itself.

[^V02-606]: Here “the opposite way” does not mean that the soul receives a mirror-image of things, as the first view suggests. It means that the light from the active source reflects from the soul back toward that source, like rays from a lamp reflected by polished objects. The first phrase in the text explains the first view; the phrase about seeing things within the active source explains the second. The author may also be giving two ways to describe the same view.

[^V02-607]: This means the light that comes from God and appears in things. This wording avoids the idea that God is made more complete by something outside Himself.

[^V02-608]: Ibn Arabi expressed this idea in a Persian poem: “Be a tablet for all beliefs; see Him in every form. On the Day of Resurrection, when the Real appears to all people in every form, He appears in a way that fits each person's belief.” Another poem says, “Your beauty moves through every reality; only Your majesty hides it.” The veil does not mean that God is a body, substance, or quality. It means that He is beyond these things. A clear-sighted person can affirm that God is beyond them and still see His beauty shine through every atom. In a body, for example, that person sees existence and self-standing being. Wherever existence is found, it is God's light; self-standing being belongs to God. We deny only the body's limits and need. The same applies to substance and quality: God's perfection does not depend on any limited nature. His beauty is His majesty, and His majesty is His beauty. The veil is either nonexistence, the overwhelming brightness of His appearance, or the limits of our understanding.

[^V02-609]: The first point is indicated by the Qur'anic words, “We will show them Our signs in the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that this is the truth.” The second is indicated by, “Is it not enough that your Lord witnesses all things?”

[^V02-610]: Many copies say “the mirrors differ,” but the commentator thinks the text should read “the images differ.”

[^V02-611]: This passage favors the idea that God is the mirror in which things appear. God is their source, and His relation to them is active and direct; their relation to Him is dependent. He shows them as they are, while they do not show Him fully. The mystics often prefer the reverse image for the spiritual traveler: a mirror helps you see, so God should be what is seen; and the mirror disappears behind the image, just as natures should fade from view rather than God. These views can be reconciled. The author is describing how things are in themselves; the mystics are describing how the traveler should see them. A mirror both shows the image, as God's light does, and hides itself, as natures do.

[^V02-612]: A created being sees God only through the veil of its own limits, though it may not notice that veil while seeing Him.

[^V02-613]: That is, “you are still yourself.”

[^V02-614]: This means complete self-forgetfulness: God is seen without the veil of individual limits, and both the one who sees and the one seen are God. Everything else is erased.

[^V02-615]: A saying expresses this idea: “He appears to the imagination through it, and through it He remains hidden from it.” Al-Ḥallāj also says, “Between You and me stands my ‘I,’ which struggles with me; by Your grace, remove my ‘I’ from between us.”

[^V02-616]: The commentator limits the claim about what thought can know. A philosopher does not merely collect words or ideas; through definitions and sound judgments, the mind can ask what a thing is, whether it exists, and why. Thought can map general truths that match reality, even though it cannot directly grasp a thing as it exists outside the mind. For example, a philosopher's account of the heavens describes a simple fifth kind of body, unlike earthly elements, living and moving without end. An ordinary person may know only vaguely that the heavens are the body above us, or may even mistake them for turquoise or glass. Even the basic idea of a body is subtle: it is one continuous thing, with matter and form, not a pile of indivisible pieces or tiny hard particles, and open to endless division. The same is true of the ideas we form about immaterial realities. Direct awareness, by contrast, comes through the knowledge of the highest source itself. A person who knows realities in this way, as the most complete souls do, comes close to ruling over the thinking mind. A Persian poet says: “The wise are the compass-point of existence, but Love knows how they wander within its circle.”

[^V02-617]: Other creatures serve particular names of God: an angel serves the Holy; an animal, the All-Hearing and All-Seeing; a spirit, the Subtle; and the devil, the One who misleads. In the same way, an incomplete person may serve one name through their actions.

[^V02-618]: This refers to the view mentioned earlier that the fixed identities of things are among God's own aspects.

[^V02-619]: The story goes that the Muʿtazilite scholar Qāḍī ʿAbd al-Jabbār met the Ashʿarite scholar Abū Isḥāq al-Isfarāyīnī at the home of Ṣāḥib ibn ʿAbbād. The Qāḍī said, hinting at their disagreement, “Glory to God, who is far above doing evil.” The other scholar replied at once, “Glory to the One in whose kingdom nothing happens except what He wills.” The author combines the two sayings, keeping their shared truth and leaving aside their conflict. Do not use the story to attack either scholar.

[^V02-620]: The author treats this subtle question in most of his books, even short works such as *al-ʿArshiyya* and *al-Mashāʿir*. Mulla Sadra believes hardly anyone apart from Aristotle grasped it. I think many mystics have reached it too, speaking of “the many held within the one.” One striking feature is that the greatest opposite becomes evidence for the other: because the Necessary Being is perfectly one and simple, He is all things at their fullest level, a fullness with no greater multiplicity beyond it. A point that seems to create a problem can itself resolve it, as in Aristotle's argument against two first principles. A mystic said, “I knew God by His bringing opposites together.” This teaching is one example. Another puzzle is the claim that “the simple reality is all things and is none of them.” This means that it includes every perfection but none of their limits or defects. The careful scholar sees no real contradiction. The Qur'anic words “God knows all things” point to this.

[^V02-621]: The illusory nature of a mirage, with its limits and defects, is outside the reality of the Real. The proof can be stated directly: nothing is outside that reality. Or it can be stated as the author does, by assuming the opposite and showing that it leads to a contradiction. Both forms of the proof are sound.

[^V02-622]: One may object that nonexistence is not a thing, so it cannot be one of the parts that make up a reality. But this objection also applies to the claim that the Necessary Being is made up of essence and existence, since essence is not an existent thing either. If you say that essence still has its own kind of “thinghood,” then the same can be said of nonexistence. Unless essence is allowed this status, the possibility of things and their capacity to receive existence make no sense. And unless nonexistence is allowed a similar status, possibility itself makes no sense: what would be balanced with existence, or what would make existence unnecessary? Philosophers say that a thing may be existence, essence, or nonexistence. Nonexistence can be considered in the world or in the mind; this does not mean that it exists as a separate thing. The worst kind of composition is existence joined to nonexistence, or affirmation joined to denial, when denial means loss of a perfection. But an absence has no separate positive reality to make a real pair with existence. Composition of existence and essence also comes down to considering existence and nonexistence together.

[^V02-623]: In the short proof, the author assumes that what makes a denial true is different from what makes an affirmation true; that would make the Necessary Being composite. In the detailed proof, he also rules out the other option: that the very same basis makes both affirmation and denial true. There are three possibilities: the basis of denial is the same as that of affirmation, different from it, or there is no basis at all. The author rules out the first two. The third may seem stronger, since a denial can be true even when its subject does not exist. But the argument concerns an existing subject. When the subject exists, a simple denial amounts to saying it has a limiting feature or lacks something. The author signals this with “not a horse” and by saying that denial is not pure negation. Different ideas can come from one reality when they do not oppose one another, as with existence, unity, individuality, knowledge, and power. But opposing ideas cannot have the same basis, as with cause and effect, mover and moved, and affirmation and denial.

[^V02-624]: “Nature” here means whatever makes a thing what it is. The term is broad enough to include existence itself, which is what the argument is about.

[^V02-625]: One might ask why the author moves from “a human is not a horse” to the claim that what makes a thing itself differs from what makes a denial true of it. The point is that he is speaking about the thing's reality and existence, not only about its name. If the same existence made both an affirmation and its opposite true, the thing would be itself and not itself at once. The author explains this same point in *Secrets of the Verses*.

[^V02-626]: Even a division made only in the mind is a serious kind of composition. It would be like analyzing a possible thing into essence and existence, or a simple kind into a general kind and its distinguishing feature. The mind cannot allow any such parts in the Necessary Being, who is necessary through Himself. This is the composition meant by the saying, “Every possible thing is made of two parts.”

[^V02-627]: The wording is compressed in Arabic. It means that nothing real is denied of God; only denials of lack, nonexistence, defects, and possibility apply. To remove a lack is to affirm existence.

[^V02-628]: The first two verses show more clearly how God's unity appears within many things. The verse about God's being the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden, points to the further claim that the utterly simple reality is all things.

[^V02-629]: A critic may object that the possibility belonging to an essence is not one denial but two together, so it does not contradict the thing that is denied of it. If we treat the two denials as one quality of the essence—namely, that neither side is necessary—and take this as a positive feature, then we are no longer making a pure denial. We are saying that something is present but lacks a feature. The opposite of necessity is a pure denial, not this kind of denial. And if possibility remains a pure denial, then it is one denial in the wording but two in reality.

[^V02-630]: One branch of the argument concerns the existence of a single, complete cause, which gives rise to the First Emanation. The other concerns the unity of all action, as in the argument quoted earlier from Bahmanyār.

[^V02-631]: The commentator raises three objections. First, the argument would prove too much: it would also rule out other accepted parts of a cause. Second, when a cause is able to act because of what it is made of, that concerns its makeup and dependence; it is not a new act of bringing something into existence. The rule that independence in action means independence in existence applies to a cause that gives existence and to an effect that depends on it for existence. It does not apply to a mere part of the cause, so the comparison with the earlier proof does not fit. Third, being a cause is a relation we understand; it is not a new thing that must itself be caused. A subject's sharing in a part of a cause is simply part of its role in the first effect, not a second causal act with a new participant. The commentator concludes that this proof does not hold.

[^V02-632]: Put another way, the First Intellect's existence, seen as light or as related to God, gives rise to the light of the second intellect. Seen as mixed with the shadow of possibility or as related to itself, it gives rise to the outermost sphere. In both cases, the cause is existence viewed in two different ways.

[^V02-633]: An acting cause gives its effect what is active and complete in it. But material things are not pure activity: because of matter, they also contain potential and limits. The matter in the agent makes it a particular kind of cause and limits the range of its effect. The matter in what receives the effect helps it receive, but also keeps it from becoming fully complete. This is likely what the author means when he denies that bodily things can give existence in the way a divine cause does. He does not mean that bodies in nature cannot cause anything at all. We observe causes in nature and use them to find the rules that govern natural events. The philosophers explain the special features of kinds of things by their forms, and their motions by what moves them. The point is that bodies do not create the underlying matter or the substantial forms, as a divine cause does.

[^V02-634]: That is, active bodily powers cannot be divine causes that give existence, even though they can cause changes in nature. This is even less possible for powers that are merely passive or preparatory.

[^V02-635]: The expression “makes reception possible” answers an objection. We may grant that a bodily power does not bring the later form into existence, but suppose it produces the matter’s readiness to receive it. Would the same difficulty not return? No: the power makes it appropriate to describe the matter as ready; it does not separately bring that readiness into existence. Keep this distinction in mind.

[^V02-636]: This refers to the teaching that nonexistence is one of the starting points of things that come into being. Aristotle named three: matter, form, and nonexistence. Here, nonexistence means that matter has shed its earlier form; without that, it could not receive the new form.

[^V02-637]: The commentator offers another account of nonexistence as a starting point. In the earlier account, it is part of what makes up a thing; here, it is part of what allows existence to begin. In one case, nonexistence accompanies matter; in the other, it stands against existence. The discussion becomes clearer if there is change within substance itself. Another way to explain it is that possibility could not exist without nonexistence: possibility means that existence and nonexistence are both open to the thing. Possibility is one reason a thing needs a cause. More broadly, unless we recognize nonexistence at the levels below God's own complete existence, there can be no levels or degrees among beings. Every level below the highest is incomplete in relation to what is above it, even if it is not made of matter and form, or of a general kind and its distinguishing feature. It is made up of being and a lack when compared with what is above it. Only God is simple reality without qualification.

[^V02-638]: Here “matter” means matter formed into a body, as the word usually does. Prime matter itself has no spatial position. Position comes two stages after it.

[^V02-639]: The power's action in these two cases is not the same. When it affects something else, it acts as a natural cause. When its effect is in its own matter, it only serves as a channel through which a higher cause's influence first passes. The author confirms this at the end of the chapter: the power is a condition for the matter to receive an effect, not the cause that gives it existence. This does not mean that natural powers and forces do nothing.

[^V02-640]: The author now gives three results of this principle. First, a bodily power cannot produce an immaterial being, such as a soul, let alone a power that can only be acted upon. Second, bodily powers cannot produce prime matter, which has no spatial position and is so low in the order of things that it nearly resembles nonexistence. Third, they cannot produce bodily form, because a body needs form in order to exist and act, yet a bodily power has no spatial relation to the form in its own body.

[^V02-641]: An immaterial being has the same relation to all the things it affects, since it has no spatial position. Things that exist in time are therefore timeless in relation to separate intellects, just as timeless things are eternal in relation to the Eternal.

[^V02-642]: The soul begins as bodily and tied to a spatial position. Through change within its very being, it becomes spiritual. The author also answers the objection by saying that the body's mixture of qualities is a condition for the soul to come into being.

[^V02-643]: The author explains this later, in the middle of the Journey on the Soul.

[^V02-644]: This is a fallacy. The fact that the concept of existence is one and applies in the same way does not mean that all the things it describes have the same nature. That would follow only if existence were part of what they are, as “rational animal” is part of the definition of a human being, so the features of that definition apply to humans. But this self-evident concept of existence does not make up the different levels of existence, as we explained near the start of this book.

[^V02-645]: He seems not to have considered that things can differ in their whole nature, without that difference being made up of a general kind and a distinguishing feature. The most general kinds and the final distinguishing features are examples.

[^V02-646]: That conclusion does not follow. On this assumption, the cause's existence would itself be composite, so more than one effect could issue from it. Besides, broader kind and distinguishing feature have one existence, especially in simple things. The mind's distinction between them is like its distinction between existence and essence.

[^V02-647]: We raised this objection earlier, in the discussion of causation: if essences themselves were caused, there would be a problem with individuals that share the same specific essence. Recall that argument.

[^V02-648]: If you object that al-Razi says God has an essence, I reply: if this objection is meant as a proof, the problem is clear. If it is meant to force us to accept his own assumptions, we can answer that the cause of something that exists must itself exist. So this essence would need existence in order to have an effect. Yet al-Razi treats existence as one and the same in all things.

[^V02-649]: More precisely, the second of the two senses leads to the first.

[^V02-650]: “Before” here means before in time. This is enough to refute the claim, because it would mean that an effect comes after its complete cause.

[^V02-651]: What is described as possible is not limited to a particular time. The universe means everything other than God, and its possible natures are not confined to a time.

[^V02-652]: This would also make possibility depend on something else, which is impossible on the view being discussed.

[^V02-653]: The commentator adds a third argument for possibility being eternal. If possibility were not eternal, its opposite—impossibility—would be eternal, which leads to an impossible result. Once that is rejected, possibility must be eternal. He then argues that eternal possibility allows the possibility of eternal existence: a perfect source can always give, and an unrestricted act of giving needs only that a thing be possible in itself. Theologians reject this conclusion, as the objection that follows will show.

[^V02-654]: One might say that the condition is the prior nonexistence of each event: every event's nonexistence existed from eternity and ended when the event began. But that creates a circle. The end of Zayd's impossibility depends on the end of his nonexistence; his nonexistence ends only when he exists; his existence depends on his possibility; and that possibility depends on the end of his impossibility.

[^V02-655]: The author is not speaking about things whose very nature is change, but about enduring kinds—such as humans, horses, and cattle—whose individuals replace one another. A clearer example is a person such as Zayd, who plainly came into being even though his possibility is eternal. The answer is that the argument concerns existence in general, which needs only possibility in itself. Zayd's particular existence also needs matter ready to receive him and a link to the Eternal, such as a part of the heavens' motion.

[^V02-656]: Nothing comes into being while it is already continuing to exist. Coming into being means existing after nonexistence; continuing to exist means existing after already existing.

[^V02-657]: This would also make possibility depend on something else, which is impossible on the view being discussed.

[^V02-658]: You may say that the possibility of possibility belongs to it by itself, just as existence belongs to existence by itself. But that would mean possibility itself is an essence, and so one of the possible things; it would still need another possibility. Every essence has its own possibility. Also, this possibility that comes from something else must belong to essences and make them ready to receive the possibility that comes from beyond them.

[^V02-659]: Otherwise, one option would prevail for no reason, which is impossible. The author does not discuss the other option—that a thing remains possible but becomes independent of a cause—because its problem is clear.

[^V02-660]: While it continues to exist, the effect remains equally open to either side and needs something to determine which side occurs. Possibility is the basis of that need. This explanation also answers the charge that the argument assumes what it is trying to prove.

[^V02-661]: This question is meant to show that an effect no longer needs a cause once it exists, even if it remains possible. The commentator replies that one option being more likely does not make the other impossible. The author also gives another answer.

[^V02-662]: You may object that the question is about what creates the need, not about the thing that needs a cause. The same applies to the earlier claim that nonexistence cannot explain this need. I reply that the real basis of dependence must be what truly depends. As the author explains, coming into being is not itself something that depends; and possibility, taken as an abstract idea, does not depend either. The real sense of possibility is need and dependence. So this argument shows that coming into being is not the basis of dependence, but it does not show that the abstract idea of possibility—the idea that either side may occur—is that basis. The author rejects this sense of possibility elsewhere, unless it is meant only as a way to recognize dependence, not as what makes the thing depend.

[^V02-663]: Pure relations cannot be identical with God's essence, since they are relations. Negative attributes cannot be identical with it either, since they refer to absence, not to the reality of existence.

[^V02-664]: More precisely, this is the view of the philosophers in Aristotle’s school, who say that God's knowledge involves forms impressed in the divine essence.

[^V02-665]: This is the view that the fixed forms of all things, which follow the divine names and attributes, are detailed forms of knowledge within God's essence. Unlike the forms described by the Aristotle’s followers, these fixed forms do not come after their source; they are like the meanings of God's names and attributes.

[^V02-666]: In this view, being knowledgeable and being able are states caused by a feature in the subject. They cannot be separated from that subject.

[^V02-667]: The commentator objects that necessity is one kind, whether something is necessary in itself or because of something else. Since necessity makes a thing independent, the necessity of an event at the time it exists would also seem to make it independent of a cause. How, then, can coming into being be what makes it need a cause?

[^V02-668]: Rather, the cause brings about the event: an event is something that exists after not existing, and its existence comes from the cause. Why would the event stop being an event after its first moment? The effect can be necessary in one respect and possible in another. Its existence is necessary once its cause is present, while its essence, considered on its own, remains possible. After the cause acts, possibility is not true of the effect in every sense; it remains true only when we consider the essence by itself. This explains how an effect can continue to depend on a cause while it exists.

[^V02-669]: Yes, but priority in nature and order is enough; priority in time is not required.

[^V02-670]: Not necessarily. Two things may both be eternal, yet one may be stronger and the other weaker. The weaker may depend on the stronger, not the other way around. Consider the sun and its light if both are eternal.

[^V02-671]: This argument rests on a mistaken assumption. Who says that God acts by intention or by a motive added to His essence? The philosophers say He acts through knowledge and willing; the mystical view says He acts through self-disclosure. Even if “intention” means ordinary purpose, a motive may come from within or from outside, and need not apply only to things coming into being. God's action is related to His own essence.

[^V02-672]: In the theologians' language, an essential attribute is what makes similar things alike and different things unlike. Blackness makes two black things alike, and humanity makes two humans alike; blackness and sweetness differ because one is black and the other sweet. They also use “attribute” for a feature that exists in something else. The subject gives rise to that feature, yet it exists along with the subject for as long as the subject exists.

[^V02-673]: In the mind's ordering of events, one could insert “then” or “so” between them.

[^V02-674]: As explained earlier, one of the two absences reveals the other, while the other is what brings it about.

[^V02-675]: The difficult problem here is how an event that begins in time can be linked to an eternal cause through something between them. It has been called an almost incurable difficulty. For the theologian who holds that the universe as a whole began in time, the difficulty arises in two ways. First, how can God's act begin later if God has always existed and is a complete cause, from which the effect cannot fail to follow? The Necessary Being is necessary in every respect. Philosophers need not face this problem in the same way. Second, how are the events that occur each day connected with God? Philosophers, the Ashʿarites, and the leading thinkers of the religious community agree that nothing gives existence except God: there is no power or strength except through God, the Most High, the Almighty.

    The brief answer to the second difficulty is that every new event depends on an eternal source together with a newly arising condition. God also gives existence to that condition, since He alone gives existence in the fullest sense. There are three main accounts of these conditions, none of which the author sets out explicitly here. On the first, they are successive changes in elemental matter, through which fresh states of readiness arise without end. On the second, they are the successive positions of the heavens and the stages of their circular motion. On the third, they are stages in the flowing nature of the heavens and in the continuous change of their very substance. This third account is the author's.

    How, on this account, does a changing nature depend on the eternal and unchanging God? The author points to a nature that preserves time and has two aspects: one continually renewed, the other abiding with God. The two are like a cone of light, its tip with God and its base with matter. The tip depends on the eternal God, while successive stages at the base are linked with events and natural things that begin and end. The flowing of this nature belongs to what it is and does not need a further explanation. On the other philosophers' account, the heavens' motion, considered as being between its starting and ending points, is simple and stable in itself though its relations change; thus it depends on the eternal God. Its successive stages, considered as a continuous course, are linked with the events that come to be.

[^V02-676]: Change belongs to the way a natural thing exists, not to its nature considered on its own. A thing's reality is its nature together with its existence.
